Use confidence re-encoding to produce coordinate-wise confidence scores and a directional displacement estimation module to predict per-coordinate deviation directions. A post-hoc calibration framework that can also aggregate coordinate-level confidences to box-level localization to remain compatible with prior calibration approaches.

Result: Experiments in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings show that ReDC expresses coordinate-wise localization more precisely than existing calibration methods, and its coordinate-level scores can be aggregated to reproduce or align with box-level localization metrics.

Conclusion: Coordinate-level calibration with directional information enhances the expressiveness and reliability of localization confidence, expanding calibration coverage while remaining compatible with traditional box-level calibration through aggregation.

Abstract: Deep learning based object detectors require trustworthiness beyond competitive detection performance, but deep neural networks are prone to overconfident predictions, assigning high confidence scores to predictions that are likely to be inaccurate. To improve the alignment between confidence scores and prediction accuracy, existing methods calibrate confidence scores based on box-level localization, such as precision or intersection over union with the ground truth bounding box. However, box-level localization reflects only a measure of agreement between the predicted box and the ground truth, resulting in calibrated confidence scores for box-level accuracy failing to capture the localization accuracy of coordinates of box. To tackle this issue, we propose a novel post-hoc calibration framework, rethinking detection calibration (ReDC), which provides reliable coordinate-level confidence scores, including directional information. The proposed framework defines coordinate-wise alignment and deviation direction between predictions and ground truth. Based on the alignment measure, confidence re-encoding produces reliable coordinate-level confidence scores, while directional displacement estimation predicts coordinate-wise deviation directions. Extensive experiments under in-domain and out-domain scenarios demonstrate that the proposed approach expresses the coordinate-wise localization of detected objects more precisely than existing methods. Furthermore, our method covers the representational scope of prior calibration approaches by aggregating coordinate-level confidence scores into box-level localization.

</details>


### [27] [Adaptive Emotional Video Captioning via Affective Heterogeneous Graph Reasoning and Multi-task Joint Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29045)
*Junbo Wang,Liangyu Fu,Yuke Li,Xuecheng Wu,Zhiyong Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SAGML proposes an adaptive affective heterogeneous graph for emotional video captioning to replace tree-structured priors, enabling multi-emotion representation with soft gating and joint captioning + emotion distribution learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EVC methods rely on global emotion vectors or rigid hierarchical priors; tree-structured emotion priors with hard masking may suppress correct lexical emotions when coarse categories are inaccurate and struggle with mixed/overlapping emotions. A flexible, recoverable representation of emotions is needed.

Method: Build a soft affective heterogeneous graph with catalog-level emotion nodes and lexical-level emotion word nodes. Inject a soft gate into video-to-emotion graph attention as a continuous bias. Fuse the affective representation with visual tokens into a causal language decoder. Use dual catalog and lexical heads to impose explicit emotion distribution learning on prompt hidden states. Train with a joint objective combining autoregressive caption generation and emotion distribution supervision.

Result: Frames SAGML as an error-resilient and multi-emotion-aware baseline for EVC; concrete quantitative gains are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: An adaptive framework that overcomes hard masking and rigid priors by using a soft heterogeneous graph and multitask supervision to achieve nuanced, emotion-aware video captioning.

Abstract: Emotional video captioning (EVC) aims to describe a video with both factual correctness and affective expressiveness. It requires a model to perceive subtle, ambiguous, and temporally varying emotional cues and translate them into natural language without weakening objective visual content. Existing methods have progressively introduced contextual attention, emotion interpretation, emotion priors, dynamic emotion perception and emotion-cause reasoning. Nevertheless, most of them still depend on either global emotion vectors or rigid hierarchical priors. In recent methods, the tree-structured emotion prior establishes a coarse-to-fine connection between psychological emotion categories and daily emotion words, but its hard subordinate masking may irreversibly suppress correct lexical emotions once the coarse category prediction is inaccurate. It is also limited in representing mixed or overlapping emotions that frequently occur in real videos. To address the issues, we propose SAGML, an adaptive EVC framework via affective heterogeneous graph and multi-task language modeling. Instead of treating the emotion prior as a discrete tree, SAGML constructs a soft affective heterogeneous graph containing catalog-level emotion nodes and lexical-level emotion word nodes. The soft gate is injected into video-to-emotion graph attention as a continuous bias, allowing visually supported lexical emotions to remain recoverable rather than being removed by a hard mask. The resulting affective representation is fed together with visual tokens into a causal language decoder, while dual catalog and lexical heads impose explicit emotion distribution learning on the prompt hidden states. The overall model is trained with a joint objective that combines autoregressive caption generation and emotion distribution supervision. SAGML provides an error-resilient and multi-emotion-aware baseline for EVC.

</details>


### [28] [Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning for Spiking Point Cloud Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29048)
*Zihao Guo,Jihua Zhu,Yiding Sun,Lin Chen,Danwei Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SpikePEFT enables parameter-efficient fine-tuning of spiking point cloud models by selectively adapting intrinsic neuron dynamics while freezing synaptic weights, and by recovering task signals from silent states. It achieves high accuracy with only ~5% trainable parameters and maintains SNN energy efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High storage and parameter overhead in fine-tuning pre-trained spiking point cloud models; binary spike propagation loses sub-threshold information; need efficient adaptation for neuromorphic vision on resource-constrained devices.

Method: Two modules: (1) Intrinsic Dynamics Tuning (IDT) dynamically modulates membrane decay and firing thresholds, enabling neuron-intrinsic adaptation with frozen synaptic transformations. (2) Silent-State Disambiguation Adaptation (SSDA) extracts task-relevant information from informative silent states to enrich downstream signals. Overall, parameter-efficient fine-tuning targets ~5% of trainable parameters.

Result: Gerneralization demonstrated across benchmarks with 92.4% on ModelNet40 and 85.6% on ScanObjectNN (PB_T50_RS), while updating only about 5% of parameters, preserving energy efficiency of SNNs across experiments.

Conclusion: SpikePEFT offers a promising, practical pathway for parameter-efficient adaptation of neuromorphic vision models, balancing accuracy, model footprint, and energy efficiency on 3D point-cloud tasks.

Abstract: Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) offer energy-efficient solutions for point cloud analysis on resource-constrained devices through event-driven computation. However, existing pre-trained spiking point cloud models rely on full fine-tuning for downstream task adaptation, incurring substantial parameter and storage overhead. Furthermore, binary spike propagation suppresses task-relevant sub-threshold information. To address these issues, we propose SpikePEFT, the first parameter-efficient fine-tuning framework for spiking point cloud models. Specifically, Intrinsic Dynamics Tuning (IDT) adaptively modulates membrane decay and firing thresholds, enabling efficient neuron-intrinsic adaptation while keeping the pre-trained synaptic transformations frozen. Moreover, Silent-State Disambiguation Adaptation (SSDA) recovers task-relevant information from informative silent states, thereby providing richer evidence for downstream adaptation. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and efficiency of SpikePEFT. In particular, our method achieves 92.4% accuracy on ModelNet40 and 85.6\% on the most challenging classification split ScanObjectNN(PB\_T50\_RS) while updating only about 5% of the trainable parameters and preserving the energy efficiency of SNNs. This work provides a promising step toward parameter-efficient adaptation of neuromorphic vision models.

</details>


### [29] [Learning from Adversity: Semantic-Aware Mask Refinement through Adversarial Perturbation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29059)
*Beomyoung Kim,Sung Ju Hwang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Phoenix combines adversarial mask perturbations and contrastive refinement learning to model realistic segmentation errors and improve refinement, outperforming prior methods and boosting SOTA models; code available online.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current mask refinement relies on simplistic synthetic noise that does not capture the complex, semantically structured error patterns produced by real segmentation models.

Method: (1) Adversarial Mask Perturbation via embedding attacks to generate semantic-aware noise resembling real errors; (2) Contrastive Mask Refinement Learning with a tri-directional framework that enforces feature consistency within semantic regions while maintaining separation between classes.

Result: Phoenix significantly outperforms existing methods across diverse tasks and consistently enhances state-of-the-art segmentation models; code and project page are publicly available.

Conclusion: Phoenix provides a semantically meaningful noise model and a robust refinement learning framework, yielding substantial improvements in segmentation quality and generalization across tasks.

Abstract: Despite significant advances in image segmentation, even state-of-the-art models produce masks with imperfect boundaries, semantic inconsistencies, and structural errors. Mask refinement addresses these limitations, yet current approaches rely on simplistic synthetic noise that fails to capture the complex error patterns of real segmentation models. We introduce Phoenix, a novel framework that leverages adversarial learning to generate semantically meaningful noise patterns and contrastive learning to model refinement relationships. Our approach consists of two key innovations: (1) Adversarial Mask Perturbation, which employs embedding attacks to create semantic-aware noise that mimics real segmentation errors, and (2) Contrastive Mask Refinement Learning, which establishes a tri-directional framework that ensures feature consistency within semantic regions while maintaining separation between classes. Experiments demonstrate that Phoenix significantly outperforms existing methods across diverse tasks, while consistently enhancing state-of-the-art segmentation models with substantial improvements. Our code and project page are publicly available at https://phoenix-eccv26.github.io.

</details>


### [30] [MHRGait: Gait Recognition from Momentum Human Rig Pose](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29083)
*Huiran Duan,Qian Zhou,Xianda Guo,Hua Zou,Guoying Zhao,Zhongyuan Wang,Yingli Tian*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes MHRGait and MHRGait++: a rig-space, 184-parameter per-frame pose representation (Momentum Human Rig) for gait, plus modality-balanced fusion with silhouettes; achieves top model-based results on CCPG and SUSTech1K with a compact network (~2.76M params, 0.69 GFLOPs for 30 frames) and good cross-dataset transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gait input representations (silhouettes, skeletons, 3D meshes) are limited by appearance factors like clothing or body shape. The work investigates whether compact articulated controls (rig-space) can serve as an effective standalone gait cue and as a complementary signal to silhouettes.

Method: Introduce Momentum Human Rig (MHR) pose: 184 semantically organized body/hand parameters per frame estimated from monocular video; MHRGait models intra-frame coordination and temporal evolution to produce compact descriptors. MHRGait++ fuses MHR with silhouettes via modality-balanced distance fusion to avoid modality dominance. Network: about 2.76M parameters; 0.69 GFLOPs for 30 frames.

Result: On CCPG and SUSTech1K benchmarks, MHRGait achieves best overall performance among compared model-based methods and transfers well across datasets. MHRGait++ consistently improves silhouette-based recognizers and preserves favorable accuracy-efficiency trade-offs.

Conclusion: Rig-space articulation is a viable standalone gait representation and a useful complement to projected body shape; the approach yields strong model-based performance with a compact, efficient network and supports cross-dataset transfer. Code is available.

Abstract: Gait recognition is shaped by its input representation. Silhouettes encode projected body shape, skeletons encode sparse joint coordinates, and 3D meshes encode dense surface geometry. In each case, identity-bearing articulation is observed through geometric carriers that also vary with clothing, skeletal scale, or body shape. We investigate whether gait can instead be recognized from compact articulated controls. We introduce Momentum Human Rig (MHR) pose as a gait representation, describing each frame using 184 semantically organized body and hand parameters estimated from monocular video. MHRGait groups these heterogeneous controls by anatomy, models their intra-frame coordination and temporal evolution, and produces compact body and hand descriptors. We further introduce MHRGait++, which combines MHR pose with silhouettes through modality-balanced distance fusion, preventing descriptor count from determining modality importance. Experiments on four benchmarks show that MHRGait attains the best overall performance among compared model-based methods on CCPG and SUSTech1K and transfers effectively across datasets, while its recognition network requires only 2.76M parameters and 0.69 GFLOPs for a 30-frame input. MHRGait++ consistently improves silhouette recognizers with a favorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off. These results establish rig-space articulation as an effective standalone gait representation and a complementary cue to projected body shape. Our code is available at https://github.com/duanhuiran/MHRGait.

</details>


### [31] [Forwardrobe: Garment-Aware Gaussian Avatars from a Single Image](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29106)
*Daisheng Jin,Shuyun Wang,Ying He*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Single-image reconstruction of garment-aware 3D avatars by separating clothing from the body in Gaussian canonical space, enabling animation and independent garment editing; improves loose garment reconstruction (skirts/dresses) and supports virtual try-on.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Loose garments pose geometry and motion representation challenges for body-aligned skinning; a garment-aware, separable representation is needed to achieve realistic animation and editing from a single image.

Method: Forwardrobe is a feed-forward framework that disentangles clothing from the body in canonical Gaussian space. It introduces a garment layer with continuity-aware geometry and skinning initialization, pose-conditioned non-rigid deformation, and appearance adaptation, yielding animatable, garment-aware Gaussian avatars from a single image.

Result: Experiments show improved garment reconstruction quality and greater flexibility in garment manipulation compared with existing single-image avatar reconstruction methods.

Conclusion: The garment-separated Gaussian avatar framework enables independent garment editing, transfer, and 3D virtual try-on, improving animation quality for loose garments.

Abstract: Reconstructing animatable 3D human avatars from a single image remains particularly challenging for loose garments, whose geometry and motion cannot be adequately represented by body-aligned topology and skinning. We present Forwardrobe, a feed-forward framework for reconstructing garment-aware Gaussian avatars from a single image. Forwardrobe explicitly separates clothing from the body in canonical Gaussian space and equips the garment layer with continuity-aware geometry and skinning initialization, pose-conditioned non-rigid deformation, and appearance adaptation. These designs improve garment reconstruction and visual quality during animation, particularly for skirts and dresses. The separated garment layer additionally forms an independently controllable 3D asset, enabling garment editing, transfer, and 3D virtual try-on. Experiments demonstrate improved garment reconstruction quality and greater flexibility in garment manipulation compared with existing single-image avatar reconstruction methods.

</details>


### [32] [A Frozen Pixel-Space Diffusion Model Can Guide Itself with Its Own Samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29122)
*Zixuan Fu,Chong Wang,Lanqing Guo,Kailai Zhou,Jiahao Nie,Bihan Wen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Synthetic Self-Guidance (SSG): freeze a pretrained pixel diffusion backbone, train a lightweight head on synthetic samples, and use intermediate-final prediction discrepancy as self-guidance to achieve large FID gains with minimal compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce training cost for pixel-space diffusion models by leveraging frozen backbones and self-generated supervision; exploit the hierarchical structure of intermediate vs. final predictions.

Method: Attach a small prediction head to an intermediate layer of a frozen pixel diffusion Transformer. Use the discrepancy between the intermediate head's prediction and the final output as a self-guidance signal during sampling. Train the head using only model-generated samples (no real images). Evaluate across ImageNet JiT variants with and without classifier-free guidance (CFG).

Result: SSG yields consistent generation improvements across multiple pixel diffusion models. It reduces FID by over 50% in the no-CFG setting and further improves strong baselines with CFG (e.g., JiT-H/16: 1.86→1.67; PixelREPA-H/16: 1.81→1.59). Adapter training uses <1% of full-model compute.

Conclusion: Synthetic Self-Guidance provides a low-cost, effective strategy to augment pixel-space diffusion by self-supervised guidance from an intermediate representation, with substantial performance gains and minimal training overhead.

Abstract: Pixel-space diffusion models aim to learn an end-to-end generator directly over raw pixels. This is challenging because a single model must capture both global structure and local texture in the same high-dimensional space. While recent work improves pixel diffusion through alternative prediction targets, training objectives, and architectures, these advances typically require training a new model from scratch. We show there is a cheaper, complementary strategy: \textbf{a frozen, pretrained pixel diffusion model can guide itself}. Our key observation is that intermediate layers of a pretrained pixel diffusion transformer can be decoded into coarse predictions that capture the main low-frequency structure, while the final layers progressively refine local, high-frequency details. We therefore attach a lightweight prediction head to an intermediate layer, keep the backbone frozen, and use the discrepancy between the intermediate and final predictions as a self-guidance direction during sampling. To train this head, we further find that real images are not necessary. Instead, model-generated samples suffice and even outperform real images for training the head, especially in enhancing the high-frequency components that pixel diffusion tends to underfit. Across multiple pixel diffusion models on ImageNet, our \textbf{Synthetic Self-Guidance (SSG)} consistently improves generation while adapter training requires less than 1$\%$ of full-model training compute: it reduces FID by over 50$\%$ across the evaluated JiT variants without classifier-free guidance (CFG) and further improves strong baselines with CFG, e.g., JiT-H/16 from 1.86 to 1.67 and PixelREPA-H/16 from 1.81 to 1.59. Our code is available at https://github.com/zfu006/SSG.

</details>


### [33] [SciFigPlag-Bench: A Benchmark for Provenance-Aware Scientific Figure Plagiarism Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29124)
*Zhiying Cui,Minghao Yang,Linlin Gao,Jie Liu,Pengyuan Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces SciFigPlag-Bench, a provenance-aware benchmark for scientific figure reuse with a factorized taxonomy and four diagnostic tasks; demonstrates baselines and unresolved challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Figure plagiarism in scholarly communication is underexplored for provenance-aware, multimodal evaluation. General image benchmarks miss domain-specific reasoning about how and where evidence is reused in figures.

Method: Define a factorized taxonomy separating what content is reused from how it is transformed; cover material-preserving reuse (full-figure, subfigure) and abstract-content reuse (data re-expression, structural redraw). Build SciFigPlag-Bench with 2,582 positive pairs and 2,541 negative pairs, combining real-world cases, taxonomy-guided synthetic examples, and visually similar negatives. Four diagnostic tasks: pairwise detection, source attribution, hierarchical reuse-type classification, and reuse correspondence localization. Evaluate diverse vision-language models to establish baselines.

Result: Provided initial baselines; results reveal persistent challenges in fine-grained provenance reasoning, reuse-type understanding, and spatial evidence grounding, indicating gaps between current models and the task.

Conclusion: SciFigPlag-Bench offers a targeted benchmark for provenance-aware reasoning over scientific figures, enabling nuanced evaluation of reuse detection, attribution, and localization; the taxonomy and tasks guide future model development in multimodal provenance understanding.

Abstract: Scientific figures often encode the visual evidence behind scientific findings, yet figure plagiarism remains underexplored as a benchmarked multimodal evaluation problem. We present SciFigPlag-Bench, a benchmark for provenance-aware reasoning over scientific figures in scholarly documents. Unlike general image-similarity or image-forensics benchmarks, SciFigPlag-Bench evaluates whether a suspicious figure reuses evidence from a specific source figure, how the reused content has been transformed, and where the reused evidence appears. We introduce a factorized taxonomy that separates what is reused from how it is transformed, covering material-preserving reuse, such as full-figure and subfigure reuse, as well as abstract-content reuse, such as data re-expression and structural redraw. Guided by this taxonomy, we construct a hybrid benchmark with 2,582 positive pairs and 2,541 negative pairs, combining documented real-world cases, taxonomy-guided synthetic examples, and visually similar negatives. The benchmark supports four diagnostic tasks: pairwise detection, source attribution, hierarchical reuse-type classification, and reuse correspondence localization. Experiments with diverse vision-language models establish initial baselines and reveal persistent challenges in fine-grained provenance reasoning, reuse-type understanding, and spatial evidence grounding.

</details>


### [34] [First Investigation of Deep Learning for Intraoperative Gauze Segmentation in Minimally Invasive Abdominal Surgery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29132)
*Priya Tomar,Maximilian Broß,Philipp Feodorovici,Jan Arensmeyer,Philipp Leifels,Aditya Parikh,Hanno Matthaei,Christian Bauckhage,Helen Schneider,Rafet Sifa*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Deep learning gauze segmentation in robot-assisted MIS using real-world data; evaluates CNN-, transformer-, and hybrid-based architectures; introduces auto-tracked (pseudo-label) masks to mitigate data scarcity; real-world data helps balance blood visibility and gauze detection; auto-tracked annotations boost performance, especially in generic surgical scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prevent retention of gauze during surgery and reduce re-operation risk, while addressing the scarcity of annotated surgical datasets and the variability of real-world operative scenes.

Method: In-house surgical dataset from a university hospital with three gauze categories. Systematically compare CNN-based, transformer-based, and hybrid segmentation architectures. Investigate the use of auto-tracked (pseudo-label) segmentation masks to augment limited annotations and study their effect on performance in realistic surgical settings.

Result: Real-world training data mitigates the challenge of detecting gauze in the presence of blood; auto-tracked annotations provide additional performance gains, notably in generic surgical scenarios across architectures.

Conclusion: Effective segmentation approaches using real-world data and semi-supervised (auto-tracked) annotations can enhance robot-guided surgeries by precisely delineating foreign objects, improving patient safety and surgical outcomes.

Abstract: Surgical gauze is an essential part of surgical procedures, primarily used for controlling bleeding and absorbing bodily fluids. The post-surgical retention of gauze can lead to serious complications and necessitate additional surgery for its removal. Despite the clinical significance, research on gauze segmentation using real-world surgical data remains underexplored, owing in part to the scarcity of annotated datasets. In this work, we investigate the use of deep learning methods for gauze segmentation in robot-assisted minimally invasive abdominal surgeries, utilizing an in-house surgical dataset prepared at a university hospital. The training data reflects realistic surgical settings and captures extensive diversity in spatial, morphological, and visual attributes across three different gauze categories. We evaluate several widely used segmentation architectures, including CNN-based, transformer-based, and hybrid architectures, to establish a proof-of-concept for gauze segmentation in a realistic clinical setting. In addition, we investigate the influence of sub-optimally annotated, auto-tracked segmentation masks as a strategy to address data scarcity and improve performance. Our results demonstrate the efficacy of real-world training data in countering the main challenge reported by prior works, the trade-off between blood presence and gauze detection. The incorporation of auto-tracked annotations yields performance enhancements, particularly in generic surgical scenarios. The integration of effective segmentation approaches can benefit robot-guided surgical procedures and various downstream applications by providing precise delineation of foreign objects, thereby enhancing patient safety and surgical outcomes.

</details>


### [35] [On the Efficacy of Self-Supervised Point Cloud Encoders for Efficient 3D Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29136)
*Yao Zheng,Tian Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Systematic study of low-cost self-supervised point cloud encoders (PCP-MAE, Point-MAE) for 3D-LLMs using MiniGPT-3D. End-to-end training from random initialization is viable; encoder architecture and self-supervised objective interact strongly; purely geometric encoders lag on closed-set tasks; provides actionable design guidelines for cost-effective 3D-LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the high computational barrier of 3D-LLMs by assessing whether cheaper self-supervised point cloud encoders can match or approach performance of costly multi-modal encoders, and how architecture-objective interactions affect outcomes.

Method: 12 experimental groups derived from 7 encoder initializations (1 multi-modal baseline, 5 self-supervised, 1 random), evaluated under frozen and unfrozen fine-tuning (12 groups) across 2 architectures (MaskTransformer, PointTransformer), 3 objectives (PCP-MAE, Point-MAE, random init), and 2 datasets (Objaverse 660K, ShapeNet55-34 ~50K). Metrics include open-vocabulary accuracy, captioning score, and ModelNet40 closed-set accuracy.

Result: End-to-end training from random init yields 52.50% open-vocabulary accuracy and 44.45 captioning score. Best self-supervised config: PCP-MAE + MaskTransformer = 59.00% accuracy. Point-MAE + MaskTransformer reduces to 46.50% (pattern reversed for PointTransformer). Closed-set ModelNet40 accuracy remains ~13–18% with pure geometry, vs ~62% for multi-modal baseline, even after end-to-end fine-tuning.

Conclusion: The study offers practical guidelines for cost-effective 3D-LLM design and reveals notable interaction patterns between self-supervised objectives and encoder architectures. While random-init end-to-end training is promising, purely geometric encoders struggle on closed-set tasks, underscoring remaining benefits of multi-modal alignment for comprehensive 3D understanding.

Abstract: 3D point cloud-language models (3D-LLMs) enable 3D understanding by pairing point cloud encoders with large language models, but existing methods rely on costly multi-modal encoders (e.g., ULIP-2) that require image-text-point cloud alignment on 8x A100-scale compute, creating high barriers for research and deployment. In this work, we systematically investigate whether low-cost self-supervised point cloud encoders, specifically PCP-MAE and Point-MAE, can serve as effective alternatives.
  Using MiniGPT-3D as our testbed, we evaluate 7 encoder initialization/pre-training setups (1 multi-modal baseline, 5 self-supervised, 1 random init) under frozen and unfrozen fine-tuning (12 total groups), across 2 architectures (MaskTransformer, PointTransformer), 3 objectives (PCP-MAE, Point-MAE, random init), and 2 datasets (Objaverse 660K, ShapeNet55-34 approximately 50K).
  Our experiments reveal three key findings: (1) The four-stage MiniGPT-3D pipeline can effectively train a 3D encoder from random initialization: an end-to-end trained random init encoder reaches 52.50% open-vocabulary accuracy and 44.45 captioning score, approaching top pre-trained variants; (2) Architecture and pre-training objective show strong crossover interaction: PCP-MAE + MaskTransformer achieves 59.00% accuracy (best self-supervised), while Point-MAE + MaskTransformer drops to 46.50%, with the pattern reversed for PointTransformer; (3) Closed-set ModelNet40 classification remains a core weakness of purely geometric encoders, reaching only ~13-18% accuracy vs. ~62% for the multi-modal baseline, even after end-to-end fine-tuning.
  Our results offer practical guidelines for cost-effective 3D-LLM design and reveal interaction patterns between self-supervised objectives and encoder architectures.

</details>


### [36] [CorrelationFlow: A Training-Free Geometric Approach for LiDAR Scene Flow Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29237)
*Minh-Quan Dao,Yancong Lin,Julie Stephany Berrio Perez,Holger Caesar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A training-free, geometric framework (CorrelationFlow) for LiDAR scene flow using BEV occupancy, combining connected-component labeling with correlation maximization; offers dense and sparse variants and competitive unsupervised performance across diverse datasets, challenging the need for learned architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current scene flow methods rely on fixed architectures and self-supervised losses, inheriting shared assumptions that fail for sparse, distant, or fast-moving objects. A formulation-free approach could uncover a broader set of viable solutions.

Method: Dense: perform spatio-temporal connected-component labeling on bird’s-eye-view occupancy images; recover motion from correlation peaks; propagate velocities to member points. Sparse: operate on a single sweep pair by matching boundary key-point occupancy descriptors via lightweight correlations.

Result: On the Argoverse 2 2026 Scene Flow Challenge multi-domain test set (five datasets, heterogeneous sensors/platforms), CorrelationFlow ranked second among unsupervised methods and showed the most graceful degradation at long range compared to learned counterparts.

Conclusion: A substantial portion of the scene flow problem is solvable by classical computer vision. Progress may require questioning the formulation itself rather than merely scaling data or model capacity.

Abstract: LiDAR scene flow estimation has settled into a monoculture: nearly all recent methods share the same feed-forward architecture and the same family of self-supervised losses, inheriting each other's assumptions, and each other's blind spots. When those assumptions fail, as they do for sparse, distant, or fast-moving objects, every method built on them fails together, and adding parameters or simulated training data does not fix what the formulation itself gets wrong. This paper takes the opposite path. We present CorrelationFlow, a training-free geometric framework that reduces scene flow to two textbook operations: connected-component labeling and correlation maximization on bird's-eye-view occupancy images. Objects are isolated as spatio-temporal connected components, their motions recovered as correlation peaks, and the resulting velocities propagated to all member points. However, this dense correlation evaluates every candidate displacement of every cluster and requires a window of past sweeps; therefore, we develop a sparse counterpart that operates on a single sweep pair by matching lightweight occupancy descriptors at boundary key points. Because nothing is trained, nothing is inherited: on the multi-domain test set of the Argoverse 2 2026 Scene Flow Challenge, spanning five datasets with heterogeneous sensors and platforms, CorrelationFlow ranked second among unsupervised methods and degrades most gracefully at long range, where the shared assumptions of learned methods break down. Our results suggest that a substantial share of the scene flow problem is solvable by classical computer vision, and that progress may require questioning the formulation, not scaling it.

</details>


### [37] [Have I Seen You? Embedding Behavior Signals Synthetic Face Dataset Membership](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29144)
*Paweł Borsukiewicz,Daniele Lunghi,Wendkûuni C. Ouédraogo,Jacques Klein,Tegawendé F. Bissyandé*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Synthetic face data can leak information about real training data via dataset-level membership inference; an attack recovers the synthetic training dataset in all cases and sometimes identifies the generator's real source dataset, highlighting privacy risks and the need for stronger leakage mitigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess privacy risks of using synthetic face datasets trained on real faces and quantify potential leakage of real training data through model and generator training traces.

Method: A dataset-level membership inference attack that first identifies the synthetic dataset used to train a face recognizer, then infers the real dataset used to train the generator. Evaluated over 11 face recognition models, 11 synthetic datasets, and 7 real datasets.

Result: The attack recovers the synthetic training dataset in 100% of cases and identifies the generator's source dataset in 54.5% of cases.

Conclusion: Synthetic data retain traces of real training data; privacy-preserving deployment requires stronger leakage mitigation and possibly improved data sanitization or leakage-resistant generation methods.

Abstract: Synthetic face datasets are increasingly used to reduce privacy exposure and data access constraints in biometric recognition. Yet the generators that produce these datasets are trained on real faces, so synthetic data may still reveal their real source data. We study this risk through a dataset-level membership inference attack that first identifies the synthetic dataset used to train a face recognizer and then infers the real dataset used to train the generator. Across 11 face recognition models, 11 synthetic datasets, and 7 real datasets, the attack recovers the synthetic training dataset in 100% of cases and identifies the generator's source dataset in 54.5% of cases. These results show that synthetic data can retain dataset-level traces of real training data and that privacy-preserving deployment requires stronger leakage mitigation.

</details>


### [38] [Progressive Decision-Making for Localizing Open-Ended AI-Generated Image Forgeries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29156)
*Jingyi Hou,Xiaoxia Chen,Leyu Zhou,Zhichuang Wang,Zhijie Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Adaptive sequential decision-update for robust AI-generated forgery localization. Treats localization as an intermediate state and uses an Evidence-Guided Mamba (EG-Mamba) with a decision evidence projector to iteratively update the forgery mask, improving generalization especially on unseen forgeries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-generated forgeries exhibit diverse, evolving traces with weak and regionally uneven cues. Fixed one-shot localization struggles to exhaustively learn all manipulation appearances; a robust approach should leverage evidence and uncertainty to update localization iteratively.

Method: Convert mesoscopic traces into compact decision evidence via a lightweight decision evidence projector. Apply Evidence-Guided Mamba (EG-Mamba) to perform uncertainty- and boundary-aware state updating. Treat the localization map as an intermediate state and progressively update it rather than producing a final one-shot mask. Preserve reliable manipulated/background regions and cautiously revise ambiguous regions based on available evidence, uncertainty, and boundary conditions.

Result: Extensive experiments on conventional and AI-generated manipulation benchmarks show the method’s effectiveness. Notably, when trained only on conventional manipulation data, the approach yields larger gains on unseen AI-generated forgeries, indicating that progressive decision-updating generalizes better to heterogeneous traces.

Conclusion: Adaptive sequential decision-updating is a promising paradigm for robust forgery localization, enabling better handling of incomplete and ambiguous evidence and improving generalization to unseen AI-generated forgeries by treating localization as a state that is progressively refined.

Abstract: AI-generated image forgeries are becoming increasingly realistic and difficult to characterize with fixed manipulation patterns. As generative models continue to evolve, it is impractical to expect a localization model to exhaustively learn all possible forgery appearances from large-scale training data alone. Nevertheless, many AI-generated forgeries still leave subtle forensic traces, although these cues are often weak and unevenly reliable across regions. Therefore, robust localization requires not only extracting informative forensic traces, but also making reliable decisions from incomplete and ambiguous evidence. In this paper, we move beyond static one-shot prediction and reformulate final forgery localization as an adaptive sequential decision-updating process, where the localization map is treated as an intermediate state rather than a fixed output. Rather than producing the final mask via one-shot pixel-wise prediction, our method progressively updates the localization state guided by available evidence, uncertainty, and boundary conditions. Specifically, we first transform mesoscopic traces into compact decision evidence via a lightweight decision evidence projector, and then introduce Evidence-Guided Mamba (EG-Mamba) to perform uncertainty- and boundary-aware state updating. This design allows reliable manipulated and background regions to be preserved, while ambiguous regions are cautiously revised according to the available evidence. Extensive experiments on both conventional and AI-generated manipulation benchmarks validate the effectiveness of the proposed method. Notably, even when trained only on conventional manipulation data, our method brings larger gains on unseen AI-generated forgeries, indicating that progressive decision-updating is especially useful for heterogeneous and hard-to-exhaustively-learn manipulation traces.

</details>


### [39] [MoRAE: Flow-Friendly Self-Supervised Latents for Text-to-Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29180)
*Yifei Zhu,Mingyi Shi,Yangyang Cai,Miao Cheng,Yoshifumi Kitamura,Taku Komura*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MoRAE tackles two motion-specific bottlenecks hindering transferring Representation Autoencoders to motion spaces: spectral ill-conditioning of JEPA features and decoder-sensitive residuals. Through a compact bottleneck and motion-coupled training, it yields a flow-friendly latent, enabling a non-autoregressive Flow-Matching DiT to reach state-of-the-art.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-motion generation requires semantically correct, temporally coherent, and physically plausible motions. While RAEs have succeeded in image generation, naively applying motion-space encoders like Motion-JEPA leads to dramatic failure due to geometric issues in the latent space and how decoder sensitivities amplify latent errors.

Method: 1) Bottleneck distillation to remove weak/redundant directions and stabilize the latent spectrum; 2) motion-coupled training to align latent geometry with the decoder so that latent errors map to small motion artifacts; 3) train a standard non-autoregressive Flow-Matching DiT on the resulting latent.

Result: MoRAE produces a flow-friendly latent representation that enables a standard Flow-Matching DiT to achieve state-of-the-art performance in text-to-motion generation.

Conclusion: Addressing motion-specific bottlenecks by latent bottlenecking and decoder-aware training makes representation-based text-to-motion generation viable and superior, delivering stable transport and high-quality motion outputs.

Abstract: Text-to-motion generation must produce motions that are semantically correct, temporally coherent, and physically plausible. A natural approach is to first project motion data into a structured semantic space and then train a generative model within that space. Such a paradigm has been highly successful in image generation through Representation Autoencoders (RAEs), where a frozen self-supervised encoder provides semantic features for diffusion or flow models to learn from. However, direct transfer of such a paradigm to motion space using Motion-JEPA as the frozen encoder fails dramatically. We diagnose this failure geometrically and identify two motion-specific bottlenecks: (1) the JEPA feature space is spectrally ill-conditioned, making the Gaussian-to-data transport unstable; and (2) even with a well-conditioned spectrum, flow residuals tend to align with decoder-sensitive directions, where small latent errors are amplified into large motion artifacts after decoding. Based on these insights, we propose MoRAE. MoRAE addresses the two bottlenecks separately. A compact bottleneck distills the structured JEPA representation while removing weak and redundant directions, bringing the latent spectrum into a transport-stable regime. Motion-coupled training then aligns the retained latent geometry with the decoder, making characteristic flow errors less costly after decoding. With this flow-friendly latent, a standard non-autoregressive Flow-Matching DiT achieves state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [40] [Locally Consistent Transductive Information Maximization for Few-Shot Remote Sensing Scene Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29192)
*Karim El Khoury,Benoît Gérin,Benoît Macq,Christophe De Vleeschouwer*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LC-TIM extends TIM++ with a locally consistent transductive objective for few-shot RS scene classification by adding a neighbor-consistency regularizer; demonstrates state-of-the-art performance, especially in low-shot settings; includes a multi-source graph fusion and an open-source benchmark with ten datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve transductive few-shot remote sensing scene classification by effectively leveraging local neighborhood structure and multi-source information from foundation models to surpass zero-shot baselines.

Method: Extend TIM++ with a local consistency regularizer that enforces agreement between each query sample and its κ nearest feature-space neighbors; the regularizer is a multiplicative factor in the closed-form q-update with negligible overhead. Propose a multi-source extension that fuses affinity graphs from multiple RS foundation models.

Result: Transductive methods outperform zero-shot baselines across the benchmark; LC-TIM achieves state-of-the-art accuracy, with the largest gains in low-shot regimes where neighborhood cues are most informative.

Conclusion: LC-TIM effectively exploits local neighborhood information and multi-source affinities to boost transductive few-shot RS classification, with minimal computational cost and strong empirical gains; an open-source benchmark is provided for comprehensive evaluation.

Abstract: Remote sensing scene classification is increasingly relying on foundation models pre-trained on large-scale Earth-observation data. Moreover, transductive inference, which exploits the collective statistical structure of the entire unlabeled query set, appears to naturally match remote sensing pipelines where large images are routinely split into patches and inferred as a batch. In this work, we introduce LC-TIM (Locally Consistent Transductive Information Maximization), which extends the state-of-the-art Transductive Information Maximization for Few-Shot CLIP (TIM++) objective with a local consistency regularizer that enforces prediction agreement between each query sample and its $κ$ nearest feature-space neighbors. The regularizer enters as a single multiplicative factor in the closed-form $q$-update, adding negligible computational overhead. We further propose a multi-source extension that fuses the affinity graph from multiple remote sensing foundation model, further boosting classification accuracy. To assess these methods, we establish the first comprehensive, open-source benchmark for transductive few-shot RS scene classification, evaluating LP++, TransCLIP, TIM++, and LC-TIM across ten diverse datasets, two remote sensing vision-language models, and across various few-shot settings. Our experiments show that transductive methods consistently outperform zero-shot baselines, and that LC-TIM achieves state-of-the-art accuracy, with the largest gains in the low-shot regime where neighborhood cues are most informative. Code is publicly available at: https://github.com/elkhouryk/LC-TIM

</details>


### [41] [UltraSAM3: A Concept-Driven Foundation Model for Universal Ultrasound Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29200)
*Bo Xu,Quanhao Zhu,Rui Lin,Boling Zhu,Chenyuan Wang,Hongfei Lin,Feng Xia,Chenhua Ji*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UltraSAM3 is a concept-driven foundation model for universal ultrasound image segmentation that aligns ultrasound visuals with clinically meaningful concepts using ultrasound-specific image–mask–concept triplets; augmented by an instruction-guided agent to translate natural language queries into ultrasound concept prompts; trained on 37 public datasets across 13 anatomical categories; outperforms existing models and improves robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultrasound images exhibit speckle noise, low contrast, shadows, and ambiguous boundaries, making segmentation challenging. Existing methods are either task-specific or rely on expert-provided prompts, limiting generalizability and workflow flexibility.

Method: Train UltraSAM3 on a large-scale ultrasound segmentation corpus spanning 37 public datasets and 13 anatomical categories; adapt an existing foundation model (SAM3) to ultrasound-specific image–mask–concept triplets; develop an instruction-guided agent that converts complex natural language queries into concise ultrasound concept prompts; evaluate on multi-organ ultrasound benchmarks, external datasets, and in visual-prompt-enhanced settings.

Result: UltraSAM3 consistently outperforms representative concept- and text-driven biomedical segmentation models across multi-organ ultrasound benchmarks and external datasets; the instruction-guided agent increases robustness for complex user instructions.

Conclusion: Ultrasound-specific concept adaptation enables the creation of generalizable and interactive ultrasound segmentation foundation models.

Abstract: Ultrasound imaging has become increasingly widespread in clinical practice due to its portability, low cost and real-time capability, making ultrasound image segmentation important. However, ultrasound images differ substantially from CT, MRI, and other medical imaging modalities, as they are often affected by speckle noise, low contrast, acoustic shadows and ambiguous boundaries. Existing ultrasound segmentation methods are still mainly limited to task-specific models or visual-prompt-based foundation models, which are either tailored to particular tasks or require expert-provided visual prompts, making them inconvenient for flexible clinical use. To address these challenges, we propose UltraSAM3, a concept-driven foundation model for universal ultrasound image segmentation. Unlike conventional models, UltraSAM3 enables text-based target specification by adapting SAM3 to ultrasound-specific image--mask--concept triplets. The model is trained on a large-scale ultrasound segmentation corpus covering 37 public datasets and 13 anatomical categories, allowing it to align ultrasound visual patterns with clinically meaningful concepts across diverse organs and lesions. To further improve usability under realistic clinical interaction, we propose an instruction-guided agent that parses complex natural language queries into concise ultrasound concept prompts for UltraSAM3. Extensive experiments demonstrate that UltraSAM3 consistently outperforms representative concept- and text-driven biomedical segmentation models on multi-organ ultrasound benchmarks, external datasets, and visual-prompt-enhanced settings. Moreover, the agent improves segmentation robustness for complex user instructions. These results indicate that ultrasound-specific concept adaptation is effective for building generalizable and interactive ultrasound segmentation foundation models.

</details>


### [42] [Domain-Division based Progressive Learning for Source-Free Domain Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29202)
*Pan Liu,Jing Li,Meng Zhao,Wanli Xue,Qinghua Hu,Shengyong Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes DPL, a source-free domain adaptation method that divides the target domain into easy and hard subdomains and applies a two-stage progressive learning strategy with neighborhood-based pseudo labeling; achieves state-of-the-art results and releases code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Privacy and portability drive source-free DA; existing self-training often ignores difficult samples; deep networks learn clean samples faster, motivating a division-based, progressive approach leveraging local structure.

Method: Two alternating stages. Stage 1: partition target into easy-to-adapt and hard-to-adapt; perform neighborhood-based pseudo labeling and uncertainty-aware self-training; align class mappings across subdomains. Stage 2: apply subdomain-specific learning; easy samples use consistency regularization; hard samples leverage local structure to mine intrinsic data properties; iterate between stages.

Result: Extensive experiments on standard benchmarks show DPL outperforms state-of-the-art methods; ablation confirms the benefit of domain division and progressive learning; code available at the provided GitHub link.

Conclusion: Domain division and progressive, subdomain-tailored learning effectively harness target structure under source-free DA, delivering superior performance and broad applicability.

Abstract: With growing privacy and portability concerns, source-free domain adaptation requires only a source pre-trained model and an unlabeled target domain, allowing for effective adaptation to the target data. Most existing self-training methods focus on selecting and exploiting samples with reliable predictions, often neglecting others. Inspired by the finding that deep models learn clean samples faster than noisy ones, we propose a domain-division based progressive learning method named DPL. Specifically, our approach consists of two alternating stages, each beginning with the division of the target domain into easy-to-adapt and hard-to-adapt subdomains based on adaptation difficulty, followed by neighborhood-based pseudo label assignment. In stage one, we enhance classification accuracy through uncertainty-aware self-training and alignment of corresponding classes between subdomains. Stage two then applies tailored learning strategies to each subdomain, starting with consistency learning on the easy-to-adapt samples and progressing to utilizing local structural information for the more challenging ones, thereby mining the intrinsic properties of the target data. Extensive experiments on several widely used benchmarks validate the effectiveness of our approach, demonstrating superior performance compared to state-of-the-art methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/iamjingli/DPL.

</details>


### [43] [Multi-Modal Object Re-Identification with Dual Semantic Guidance and Global-Local Mutual Modulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29207)
*Weixiang Zhou,Xingguo Xu,Yuhao Wang,Cong Wang,Yang Yang,Zhixun Su,Jinshan Pan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A robust multi-modal ReID framework with dual semantic guidance and global-local mutual modulation, featuring Text-Semantic Injector (TSI), Masked Global-Local Modulator (MGLM), and Hierarchical MoE Fusion (HMF); validated on three benchmarks with public code upon acceptance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-modal ReID methods struggle with background clutter and cross-modal misalignment due to weak semantic priors, and neglect the synergy between global and local representations due to holistic feature modeling.

Method: The framework comprises three components: (1) Text-Semantic Injector (TSI) to inject clean textual features into visual tokens for enhanced semantic awareness; (2) Masked Global-Local Modulator (MGLM) enabling part-aware cross-modal interaction via joint guidance from soft masks and global context; (3) Hierarchical MoE Fusion (HMF) to adaptively fuse multi-spectral features under local semantic supervision, yielding discriminative representations.

Result: Extensive experiments on three multi-modal ReID benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method; code will be publicly available at the provided repository link upon acceptance.

Conclusion: The proposed dual semantic guidance and global-local mutual modulation framework yields discriminative and robust cross-modal representations for ReID by integrating text-derived semantics, fine-grained part interactions, and adaptive fusion.

Abstract: Multi-modal object Re-Identification (ReID) aims to retrieve target instances by leveraging complementary information across modalities. However, existing methods suffer from two challenges. First, they often fail to exploit well-aligned and reliable semantic priors, making them vulnerable to background clutter and cross-modal misalignment. On the other hand, they typically rely on holistic feature modeling, overlooking the synergy between global and local representations. To overcome these limitations, we propose a robust multi-modal ReID framework with dual semantic guidance and global-local mutual modulation, which mainly consists of three key components, namely the Text-Semantic Injector (TSI), the Masked Global-Local Modulator (MGLM), and the Hierarchical MoE Fusion (HMF). The TSI enhances semantic awareness by integrating clean and coherent textual features into visual tokens. The MGLM enables part-aware cross-modal interaction through joint guidance from soft masks and global context, improving fine-grained feature alignment. Finally, the HMF adaptively aggregates multi-spectral features under local semantic supervision, yielding discriminative and robust representations. Extensive experiments on three multi-modal ReID benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method. The code will be made publicly available at https://github.com/zw-absin/DSGM upon acceptance.

</details>


### [44] [Is It Time for the Renaissance of Salient Object Detection in the Era of MLLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29222)
*Wenzhuo Zhao,Xiuzhi Li,Zhongkuan Mao,Ronghao Xian,Yao Jiang,Zhao Gao,Keren Fu,Qijun Zhao,Jian Cheng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SaliLLM diagnoses a gap in MLLMs for SOD: strong localization but weak segmentation; introduces FOCUS, a training-free framework using Gestalt-inspired collaborative attention and Bayesian surprise to organize foreground and prompt a general segmenter, achieving strong zero-shot SOD across 13 RGB, RGB-D, and RGB-T benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle SOD into localization and segmentation, evaluate zero-shot SOD beyond mask-based metrics, and diagnose mismatches between MLLMs and annotations that hinder segmentation performance.

Method: Develop SaliLLM as a diagnostic benchmark with phrases, boxes, and attributes to probe saliency perception; introduce FOCUS (Foreground Organization via Gestalt-inspired Collaborative attention) which combines top-down Bayesian-surprise calibration of foreground granularity with bottom-up propagation of MLLMs evidence over entity-centric perceptual manifolds derived from self-supervised features to produce prompts for a general segmenter; all training-free.

Result: FOCUS generally outperforms SOTA without training; reductions in mean absolute error of 11% (vs fully supervised), 34% (vs weakly supervised), and 48% (vs self-supervised) across 13 RGB, RGB-D, RGB-T SOD benchmarks.

Conclusion: Signals a renaissance of SOD from task-specific supervision to zero-shot foreground organization; code is provided in supplementary material.

Abstract: The zero-shot capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are pushing salient object detection (SOD) beyond task-specific supervision. To disentangle MLLMs beyond conventional mask-based evaluation, we decompose SOD into localization and segmentation, and re-engineer datasets with phrases, boxes, and attributes, establishing a diagnostic benchmark for MLLM saliency perception (SaliLLM). SaliLLM uncovers a striking capability mismatch: MLLMs outperform state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods in localization, yet remain substantially weaker in segmentation. Further analyses attribute this gap primarily to mismatches between MLLMs and annotations over foreground cardinality, granularity, and extent. Motivated by this diagnosis, we recast zero-shot SOD as protocol-aligned Foreground Organization and introduce the first training-free framework that leverages Gestalt-inspired Collaborative attention for Unified SOD (FOCUS). FOCUS couples top-down Bayesian-surprise calibration of protocol-conditioned foreground granularity with bottom-up propagation of MLLMs evidence over entity-centric perceptual manifolds induced by self-supervised features, yielding coherent object extents as prompts for a general segmenter. Across 13 RGB, RGB-D, and RGB-T SOD benchmarks, FOCUS generally surpasses SOTA methods without training, reducing mean absolute error by 11\%, 34\%, and 48\% compared with fully, weakly, and self-supervised methods, respectively. Our findings signal the renaissance of SOD: from task-specific supervision to zero-shot foreground organization. Code is available in the supplementary material.

</details>


### [45] [When Model Priors Conflict with Visual Evidence: Mitigating Commonsense-Driven Hallucinations by Selective Prior Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29240)
*Kesheng Chen,Yamin Hu,Wenjian Luo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SPC selectively calibrates prior preferences in vision-language models to reduce commonsense-driven hallucinations, improving counterfactual image accuracy while preserving accuracy on matched commonsense images.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CDH occurs when a model's commonsense prior overrides clear visual evidence. A method is needed to dampen this prior only when it conflicts with image data, without harming valid commonsense-consistent predictions.

Method: Estimate candidate-level prior-preference for each answer, subtract it from image-conditioned scores with an instance-dependent strength, and revise the prediction only if the resulting score pattern strongly supports an alternative. Evaluate on counterfactual and matched commonsense images, across CDH categories, candidate-answer permutations, and other conflict benchmarks.

Result: SPC substantially improves accuracy on counterfactual (CF) images while largely preserving accuracy on matched commonsense (CS) images. Gains generalize across CDH categories, candidate-answer permutations, and other conflict benchmarks, and SPC rarely alters predictions on benchmarks without such conflicts.

Conclusion: Selective Prior Calibration is an effective, generalizable approach to mitigate CDH in vision-language models, with potential applicability to a broad range of conflict scenarios beyond CF/CS tasks.

Abstract: In vision--language models, commonsense-driven hallucination (CDH) occurs when a model's commonsense prior overrides clear visual evidence of an atypical state. For example, a model may report that a visibly six-fingered hand has five fingers. We show that these errors are systematically directed: when a model answers a question about a counterfactual (CF) image incorrectly, its answer often coincides with the candidate it prefers without access to the image. Suppressing this prior indiscriminately can repair CF errors, but may also disrupt correct answers on matched commonsense (CS) images, where the same prior is helpful. We therefore propose Selective Prior Calibration (SPC), which subtracts candidate-level prior-preference estimates from image-conditioned scores with an instance-dependent strength and revises the original prediction only when the resulting score pattern strongly supports an alternative. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SPC substantially improves accuracy on CF images while largely preserving accuracy on matched CS images. Furthermore, these gains generalize across CDH categories, candidate-answer permutations, and other conflict benchmarks, while SPC rarely alters predictions on benchmarks without such conflicts.

</details>


### [46] [TAVI-TEC: An AI-Based Tool for Procedural Planning of Transcatheter Aortic Valve Implantation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29243)
*Alessandra Zerillo,Stefano Cannata,Diego Bellavia,Daniele Ciriello,Simone Manini,Salvatore Pasta,Caterina Gandolfo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AI-driven TAVI-TEC automates preoperative CTA analysis for TAVI planning, enabling rapid measurements and valve-size prediction with good agreement to manual measurements; 82% size-prediction accuracy; potential to standardize workflow, pending multicenter validation and broader valve compatibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rising TAVI volumes necessitate efficient, standardized preoperative planning to reduce operator variability and accelerate workflow.

Method: Fully automated pipeline integrating deep learning-based segmentation of cardiovascular structures, calcification detection, centerline extraction, landmark identification, annular plane definition; ML-based valve size prediction (multilayer perceptron); deployment via a web-based DICOM viewer.

Result: Measurements completed in ~2-6 minutes; strong concordance with clinician measurements (e.g., annular area CCC 0.934, ICC 0.935, R2 0.881; perimeter CCC 0.909, ICC 0.909, R2 0.854); valve-size prediction accuracy 82% with most errors between adjacent sizes.

Conclusion: TAVI-TEC has potential to reduce operator variability and streamline preoperative workflows, but requires multicenter validation and extension to additional measurements and valve platforms.

Abstract: Computed tomography angiography (CTA) is crucial for preprocedural TAVI planning, providing the anatomical information required for prosthesis sizing and vascular access assessment. As the volume of TAVI procedure increases, improving efficiency and standardizing annotations is becoming essential in clinical practice. This study presents TAVI-TEC, a fully automated artificial intelligence-based framework integrated into a web based DICOM viewer for routine preoperative TAVI planning. Pre-procedural CTA scans from patients undergoing TAVI with SAPIEN 3 Ultra (S3U) prostheses were processed using a fully automated pipeline. Deep learning-based segmentation of cardiovascular structures, calcification detection, centerline extraction, landmark identification, and annular plane definition was implemented to quantify key annular and aortic root measurements and color-coded maps of lumen reduction and vessel diameter for vascular access. A multilayer perceptron classifier was trained to predict prosthesis size prior to the TAVI procedure. Results revealed that TAVI-TEC enabled pre-procedural measurements in approximately 2-6 min. Strong agreement with clinician-derived measurements was observed for annular area (coefficient of concordance, CCC = 0.934; interclass correlation coefficient, ICC = 0.935; R^2 = 0.881) and perimeter (CCC = 0.909; ICC = 0.909; R^2 = 0.854). The valve-size prediction model achieved 82% overall accuracy, with most misclassifications occurring between adjacent prosthesis sizes. Though further multicenter validation and extension to additional measurements and valve platforms are required, the TAVI-TEC methodology may reduce operator variability in pre-TAVI measurements and streamline the preoperative workflows of the Heart Team for decision-making.

</details>


### [47] [OsteoCAD: A Human-in-the-Loop Cloud-Edge Framework for Bone Tumor Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29266)
*Maximo Rodriguez-Herrero,Dante D. Sanchez-Gallegos,Heriberto Aguirre-Meneses,Marco Antonio Núñez-Gaona,J. L. Gonzalez-Compean,Jesus Carretero*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OsteoCAD is a modular eHealth framework that makes deep learning for medical image analysis more accessible by providing end-to-end DL tools and remote GPU access, validated in a real-world bone tumor segmentation case in Mexico.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Health-care organizations face resource and expertise barriers to deploying DL; there is a need to democratize access and streamline deployment.

Method: Develop a modular platform integrating dataset creation, preprocessing, model training, and inference with a user-friendly interface, and enable secure remote GPU connections.

Result: Feasibility demonstrated in a Mexico case study; DL-powered eHealth solutions can operate without advanced technical expertise or complex local configurations.

Conclusion: OsteoCAD lowers barriers to DL adoption in clinics and supports practical, scalable deployment of medical imaging DL tools.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Deep Learning (DL) have notably advanced medical image analysis, yet many health- care organizations struggle to adopt them due to limited com- putational resources and specialized expertise. To address these barriers, we introduce OsteoCAD, a modular eHealth framework that democratizes access to DL tools in clinical practice. Osteo- CAD delivers end-to-end DL capabilities-from dataset creation and preprocessing to model training and inference-through an integrated and user-friendly interface. To mitigate local hardware constraints, the framework securely connects to remote GPU infrastructures. We validate OsteoCAD's feasibility through a real-world case study in Mexico focused on large bone tumor segmentation. The results demonstrate the framework's ability to enable DL-powered eHealth solutions without demanding ad- vanced technical expertise or complex local configurations.

</details>


### [48] [Training-Free Entity-Level Few-Shot Segmentation of Remote Sensing Images with Advection Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29278)
*Xueting Bai,Huan Ni*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A training-free, entity-level few-shot segmentation framework for remote sensing that uses SAM3 priors to form entity primitives, builds a multi-modal semantic potential field with foreground/background prototypes and textual responses, and applies an advection-based refinement to propagate category information and suppress texture noise, improving cross-domain adaptation without extra training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce training cost and noise in cross-domain few-shot segmentation, particularly for remote sensing, by moving from pixel-level to entity-level reasoning and leveraging robust priors.

Method: 1) Generate category-agnostic entity primitives via SAM3's geometric priors. 2) Reformulate inference to entity-level; construct foreground/background prototypes. 3) Combine with dense textual responses from SAM3 to form a multi-modal semantic potential field. 4) Apply advection equation-based refinement to propagate category-aware information across feature and similarity spaces, enhancing continuity and suppressing local noise.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple remote sensing datasets show reduced domain shift and local noise, improved adaptation capability of SAM3 for remote sensing few-shot segmentation without additional training; code to be released."

Conclusion: A training-free, entity-level framework leveraging SAM3 priors and advection refinement yields robust, domain-shift-resistant remote sensing few-shot segmentation without training.

Abstract: Existing cross-domain few-shot segmentation approaches suffer from high training costs due to source-domain episodic training and pixel-wise dense prediction, while often producing fragmented and noisy predictions. To overcome these issues, we propose a training-free entity-level few-shot segmentation framework for remote sensing images with advection refinement. Specifically, we first leverage SAM3's generic geometric priors to generate category-agnostic entity primitives. By reformulating few-shot inference from pixel-level prediction to entity-level reasoning, foreground and background prototypes are constructed and combined with dense textual semantic responses from SAM3 to build a multi-modal semantic potential field. Furthermore, an advection equation-based semantic refinement mechanism is introduced to propagate category-aware information across both feature and similarity spaces, enhancing semantic continuity and suppressing local texture noise. Extensive experiments on multiple remote sensing datasets demonstrate that the proposed framework effectively mitigates domain shift and local noise, substantially improving SAM3's adaptation capability for remote sensing few-shot segmentation without additional training. Our code will be publicly available at https://github.com/yu-ni1989/ELFSS-AR.

</details>


### [49] [FillGS: Filling Observation Gaps in 4D Gaussian Splatting via Viewpoint-Time Selection and Generative Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29284)
*Takashi Otonari,Toshihiko Yamasaki*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Active spatiotemporal viewpoint selection for 4D Gaussian Splatting (4DGS) to mitigate sparsity-induced artifacts. The approach computes rendering sensitivity and motion-aware observation density to select virtual viewpoints for generative enhancement, filters unreliable regions, and fine-tunes 4DGS on the reliable data. Evaluated on multi-view benchmarks with induced observation gaps, showing consistent improvements over prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 4D Gaussian Splatting struggles with artifacts in sparsely observed spatiotemporal regions due to limited viewpoint coverage. Existing generative-model-based refinements rely on heuristic viewpoint selection and cannot proactively explore sparse areas, limiting reconstruction quality in dynamic scenes.

Method: 1) Estimate rendering sensitivity and motion-aware observation density of 4D Gaussians. 2) Actively select spatiotemporal virtual viewpoints to alleviate observation sparsity and guide generative enhancement. 3) Apply generative refinement for chosen views. 4) Filter out regions conflicting with captured observations or likely to contain generative artifacts. 5) Fine-tune 4DGS using only the reliable regions. 6) Evaluate on multi-view video benchmarks with splits designed to induce observation gaps.

Result: The proposed pipeline yields consistent improvements over prior viewpoint selection strategies and fine-tuning methods, both qualitatively and quantitatively, while reducing artifacts in refined renderings.

Conclusion: Active, data-driven spatiotemporal viewpoint selection can effectively improve 4DGS reconstructions in scenarios with sparse observations, enabling higher fidelity dynamic scene rendering with fewer artifacts.

Abstract: 4D Gaussian Splatting (4DGS) can render dynamic scenes photorealistically. However, with limited viewpoint coverage, some spatiotemporal regions remain sparsely observed, leading to artifacts, particularly in scenes with large motion. Existing approaches leveraging generative models rely on heuristic virtual-viewpoint selection before refining rendered views. As a result, they cannot actively explore such sparsely observed regions. To address this issue, we propose a pipeline that actively selects spatiotemporal virtual viewpoints to improve 4DGS reconstruction. Our method selects virtual viewpoints for generative enhancement based on the rendering sensitivity and motion-aware observation density of 4D Gaussians, prioritizing views that alleviate observation sparsity. In the refined images, we filter out regions that conflict with captured observations or are likely to contain generative artifacts and then fine-tune 4DGS using only the reliable regions. We evaluate our method on multi-view video benchmarks using new train/test splits designed to induce observation gaps. Results show consistent improvements over prior viewpoint selection strategies and fine-tuning methods in both qualitative and quantitative evaluations, while reducing artifacts.

</details>


### [50] [CALM-AH: An ABAW11-Calibrated Multimodal Ensemble with Reliability-Gated Multi-Expert Consensus for Video-Level Ambivalence and Hesitancy Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29310)
*Wenzhuo Sun,Mingjian Liang,Richard Attfield,Zongyuan Ge,Xuelian Cheng,Pamela Carreno-Medrano*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Multimodal ensemble CALM-AH for A/H recognition; RG-MEC adds unanimity-gated corrections via AffectGPT and a GPT-based verifier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ambivalence/hesitancy labeling is subtle and benefits from robust, multimodal cues and ensemble methods that balance task-specific, affective, and semantic evidence.

Method: Construct 15 non-empty feature combinations from textual, acoustic, visual, and derived statistics; for each, select the best of three classifier families via validation binary cross-entropy and optimize decision threshold for validation Macro-F1. Use fixed hard-voting weights transferred from BROTHER. Introduce Reliability-Gated Multi-Expert Consensus (RG-MEC): an anchor-preserving ensemble that combines the initial prediction with three correction experts (CALM-AH, AffectGPT, GPT-based semantic verifier); override only if all three correction experts agree on the opposite class.

Result: On the ABAW11 dataset (participant-disjoint), CALM-AH achieves Macro-F1 0.7525; RG-MEC achieves Macro-F1 0.7771.

Conclusion: The proposed ensemble improves A/H recognition performance by leveraging diverse multimodal cues and a consensus-based correction mechanism, with RG-MEC providing a further gain through unanimity-gated corrections while limiting isolated expert errors.

Abstract: Ambivalence and hesitancy (A/H) are subtle behavioural states that may be expressed through language, voice, facial activity, and other non-verbal cues. The ABAW11 A/H Video Recognition Challenge asks systems to assign a binary A/H label to each naturalistic interview video. Performance is measured using Macro-F1 so that recognition of both A/H and No-A/H samples receives equal importance. We present CALM-AH, a multimodal ensemble that combines textual, acoustic, visual, and derived behavioural-statistical features. We construct 15 non-empty combinations of these feature branches. For each combination, we select the best of three classifier families using validation binary cross-entropy and optimise its decision threshold for validation Macro-F1. The resulting binary decisions are combined using fixed hard-voting weights transferred from BROTHER. We further introduce Reliability-Gated Multi-Expert Consensus(RG-MEC), an anchor-preserving decision-level ensemble that combines an initial prediction with three complementary correction experts: CALM-AH, AffectGPT, and a GPT-based semantic verifier. The initial system provides the default prediction. Its label is overridden only when all three correction experts unanimously support the same alternative class; otherwise, the anchor prediction is retained. This unanimity-gated design limits the influence of isolated expert errors while permitting bidirectional correction when task-specific, multimodal-affective, and semantic-pragmatic evidence are fully consistent. On the participant-disjoint ABAW11 dataset, CALM-AH achieves a Macro-F1 of 0.7525, and the complete RG-MEC system achieves 0.7771.

</details>


### [51] [DualDiT: A Conditional Dual-Output Diffusion Transformer for Joint OCT Image and Segmentation Mask Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29337)
*Fernando García-Torres,Rocío del Amor,Sandra Morales,Álvaro Barroso,Peter Heiduschka,Björn Kemper,Valery Naranjo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DualDiT is a conditional dual-output diffusion transformer that jointly generates OCT B-scans and upper retinal layer masks in ex vivo mouse retina, outperforming DDPM and LDM baselines in fidelity, perceptual realism, and utility for data augmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses scarcity of annotated OCT data in mouse retina by enabling joint image–mask synthesis with transformer-based diffusion to capture the joint distribution, reducing reliance on labor-intensive manual segmentation.
