Conclusion: 3D-aware RGB-NIR fusion without clean RGB data collection is effective for robust low-light imaging, offering improved robustness and broad applicability across noise conditions.

Abstract: Robust low-light imaging remains challenging for the community. Recent studies have explored fusing Near-Infrared (NIR) with noisy RGB to achieve improved enhancement, yet most methods depend on carefully curated training data pairs, with limited robustness under different scenarios. This paper offers a new perspective for RGB-NIR low-light imaging by incorporating 3D-aware neural modeling. Without using clean RGB supervision, a powerful model can be optimized to implicitly fuse extremely noisy RGB observations with NIR cues in 3D space, effectively recovering clean RGB images. The proposed model obviates the requirement for clean RGB data collection, generalizes across different noise levels. Extensive evaluations on synthetic and real data demonstrate its superiority. Codes available: https://github.com/MyNiuuu/3DarkFusion

</details>

### [76] [Arranging circles of radii 1,2,...,n around a central circle: a Supnick TSP and certified finite optima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28654)
*Maurizio Falconi*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Minimizing the central radius R for a fixed set of surrounding circles of radii 1..n that are externally tangent to a central circle, the optimal chain order is governed by a fixed Supnick/anti-Monge TSP ordering independent of R; full feasibility requires a circular angular-constraint system, and global optima are certified for 3≤n≤14 via branch-and-bound with a 50-digit verifier. The pyramid (Supnick) order is optimal when geometrically realizable, with unconditional lower bounds otherwise; floating-circle cascades occur beyond certified n, with conjectured asymptotics R*(n) = n^2/8(1+o(1)). The repository contains artifacts, verifier, and reproducibility commands.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand a discrete-geometric packing/packing-like optimization where surrounding circles of varied radii are arranged around a central circle. The study links TSP-type orderings (Supnick/anti-Monge) to geometric realizability, addressing both chain-order feasibility and the full circular constraint system, and providing computational certificates and conjectures for larger n.

Method: 1) Decompose the problem into a chain-ordering component with a fixed Supnick/anti-Monge TSP ordering, leveraging symmetry of the angular-separation matrix which makes the minimizing order independent of R. 2) Recognize that non-adjacent circle constraints can break full feasibility; formulate the complete problem as a circular system of pairwise angular constraints, equivalently a simple temporal network. 3) Certify global optima for 3≤n≤14 via branch-and-bound with independent 50-digit verification. 4) Observe floating-circle cascade heuristically beyond certified range and conjecture asymptotics.

Result: Chain-ordering component is governed by a fixed Supnick/anti-Monge order; Supnick’s theorem yields a single minimizing cyclic order, independent of R. The pyramid (Supnick) order is optimal whenever the chain necklace is geometrically realizable, providing an unconditional lower bound in all cases. Full geometric feasibility can fail due to non-adjacent constraints; global optima are certified for 3≤n≤14; floating-circle cascades appear to continue beyond; conjectured asymptotics R*(n)=n^2/8(1+o(1)).

Conclusion: The Supnick/anti-Monge structure robustly determines the chain-ordering, with a provable minimal cyclic order independent of R; full feasibility requires solving the angular-constraint system, for which exact optima are certified up to n=14 and lower bounds hold universally. The observed floating-circle cascade and conjectured asymptotics suggest a consistent large-n behavior, motivating further computation and theoretical work.

Abstract: We study a discrete-geometric optimization problem: circles of radii \(1,2,\dots,n\) are all externally tangent to a central circle, and the central radius \(R\) is minimized over cyclic orders of the surrounding circles. We prove that the chain-ordering component is governed by a fixed Supnick/anti-Monge traveling-salesman order. For every \(R\), the angular-separation matrix is symmetric anti-Monge, so Supnick's theorem gives one minimizing cyclic order, independent of \(R\). This proves the conjectured "pyramid" order optimal whenever the corresponding chain necklace is geometrically realizable, and gives an unconditional lower bound in all cases.
  Full geometric feasibility can fail because non-adjacent circle constraints are not captured by the chain equation; from \(n=8\) the smallest circle can become a floating circle tangent only to the central circle. We formulate the full problem as a circular system of pairwise angular constraints, equivalently a simple temporal network, and certify global optima for \(3\le n\le14\) using branch-and-bound plus an independent 50-digit verifier. We observe heuristically that the floating-circle cascade continues beyond the certified range, and we state the continuation and the asymptotic form \(R^\ast(n)=n^2/8(1+o(1))\) as conjectures. The repository contains the saved certificate artifacts, verifier, and reproducibility commands.

</details>


### [77] [Convex Approximation and the Hilbert Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28885)
*Ahmed Abdelkader,David M. Mount*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: An intrinsic Hilbert-metric framework for approximate membership testing in convex bodies in Rd, yielding economical Delone sets via volume entropy, an ellipsoid-covering/query structure with ray shooting, and finger searching guided by Hilbert distance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address efficient approximate membership testing for convex bodies by moving from external Macbeath-region constructions to intrinsic Hilbert-metric structures, aiming for simpler, locality-aware data structures and improved query times.

Method: Construct structures derived from the Hilbert metric on a convex body K in Rd. Revisit Delone sets and bound their size via volume entropy. Design a query structure using a covering by ellipsoids and answer membership queries via ray shooting. Leverage the intrinsic Hilbert metric to enable finger searching, with query time tied to the Hilbert distance traversed.

Result: A new intrinsic approach to approximate membership testing is proposed. The paper derives size bounds for economical Delone sets based on volume entropy, introduces a simple ellipsoid covering-based query structure with ray shooting for membership tests, and demonstrates that finger searching is facilitated by Hilbert-metric distances, yielding distance-sensitive query times.

Conclusion: The intrinsic Hilbert-metric perspective provides a compact, locality-aware framework for approximate membership testing in convex bodies, with competitive Delone-set size bounds, an efficient ellipsoid-covering query mechanism, and natural support for finger searching.

Abstract: The efficient representation of convex bodies in multi-dimensional spaces is a fundamental problem in computational geometry. Several key developments were recently brought about using a number of constructions utilizing Macbeath regions. In this paper, we present a novel intrinsic approach for approximate membership testing, where we carry out the entire development based on structures derived from the Hilbert metric associated with a convex body $K$ in $\mathbb{R}^d$. First, we revisit the construction of economical Delone sets, deriving the size bound based on the notion of volume entropy. Second, we design a new query structure based on a simple covering by ellipsoids, where queries are answered by ray shooting. As an added bonus, the intrinsic viewpoint facilitates finger searching, where the query time can be bounded by the distance traveled in the Hilbert metric.

</details>


### [78] [Differentiable Approximations for Distance Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28886)
*Ahmed Abdelkader,David M. Mount*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: This work provides a differentiable, multiplicative (1+ε) approximation to Euclidean distance to a point set P in R^d, differentiable off P, using a partition-of-unity to blend local approximations and a novel approximate Voronoi diagram based on a simple point-location structure; achieving storage/query bounds on par with state-of-the-art approximate nearest neighbor methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable gradient-based optimization in learning pipelines by providing a differentiable distance oracle with controlled smoothness and analytic guarantees.

Method: Introduce a regularized distance via a partition-of-unity over a covering of space to blend multiple local distance approximations into a smooth global function; develop a new approximate Voronoi diagram built on a lightweight point-location data structure that avoids lifting transformations and ray shooting.

Result: Attains a multiplicative (1+ε) approximation to Euclidean distance, differentiable on R^d ackslash P, with asymptotically optimal bounds on the gradients and Hessians; storage and query time match the best-known results for approximate nearest-neighbor searching.

Conclusion: Shows that differentiable distance queries with strong accuracy and smoothness guarantees can be achieved for point-set distances, with practical query-efficiency suitable for integration into learning systems.

Abstract: The widespread use of gradient-based optimization has motivated the adaptation of various classical algorithms into differentiable solvers compatible with learning pipelines. In this paper, we investigate the enhancement of traditional geometric query problems such that the result consists of both the geometric function as well as its gradient. Specifically, we study the fundamental problem of distance queries against a set of points $P$ in $\mathbb{R}^d$, which also underlies various similarity measures for learning algorithms.
  The main result of this paper is a multiplicative $(1+\varepsilon)$-approximation of the Euclidean distance to $P$ which is differentiable at all points in $\mathbb{R}^d \setminus P$ with asymptotically optimal bounds on the norms of its gradient and Hessian, from a data structure with storage and query time matching state-of-the-art results for approximate nearest-neighbor searching. The approximation is realized as a regularized distance through a partition-of-unity framework, which efficiently blends multiple local approximations, over a suitably defined covering of space, into a smooth global approximation. In order to obtain the local distance approximations in a manner that facilitates blending, we develop a new approximate Voronoi diagram based on a simple point-location data structure, simplifying away both the lifting transformation and ray shooting.

</details>


### [79] [Approximation Algorithms for Geometric Maximum Coverage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29160)
*Sujoy Bhore,Timothy M. Chan,Pasin Manurangsi*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: A study of the geometric maximum coverage problem delivering improved approximations across several geometric set systems, plus parameterized schemes for small k, a continuous-volume variant with an EPTAS for fat convex objects, and formal hardness results delineating the limits (APX-hardness, (1-1/e+ε) hardness, and PTAS-avoidance in 3D).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maximizing the covered points (or total weight/volume) by selecting k geometric objects is a fundamental optimization problem in computational geometry and has wide applicability. Existing guarantees (e.g., 1-1/e) are often tight for general set systems; this work leverages structural properties (linear 2-shallow cell complexity, constant VC dimension) to surpass those bounds for broad geometric families.

Method: Develops (i) a polynomial-time algorithm with a factor strictly better than 1-1/e for set systems with linear 2-shallow cell complexity or decomposable into a constant number of such systems; (ii) a (1-ε)-approximation for small k for set systems with constant VC dimension, with runtime exponential in ~O(k/ε); (iii) an EPTAS for the continuous-volume variant for fat convex objects in fixed dimension; (iv) hardness reductions to establish APX-hardness for fat axis-aligned rectangles in 2D, (1-1/e+ε)-approximation hardness for axis-aligned boxes in dimension depending on ε, and a lower bound ruling out n^{poly(1/ε)}-time PTASs for the continuous problem in 3D. 

Result: Algorithms achieve approximation ratios exceeding 1-1/e for several geometric families (e.g., pseudodisks 2D; fat axis-aligned rectangles 2D; similar-size fat triangles 2D; axis-aligned unit cubes 3D). For small k, a (1-ε)-approximation with time exp(~O(k/ε)) improves prior parameterized schemes (notably reducing dependence from exp(O(k^2/ε^5))). A continuous variant attains an EPTAS for fat convex objects in fixed dimension. Hardness results show APX-hardness in 2D fat rectangles, (1-1/e+ε)-hardness for boxes in a dimension depending on ε, and no PTAS for the continuous problem in 3D under standard assumptions.

Conclusion: The work broadens the practical and theoretical limits for geometric maximum coverage, offering stronger approximation guarantees for many natural geometric families, efficient parameterized schemes for small k, and a clear delineation of hardness boundaries, including when PTASs are unlikely in continuous settings.

Abstract: We study the maximum coverage problem for geometric set systems: given a set of points, a set of geometric objects, and a number $k$, select $k$ objects maximizing the number of points inside their union.
  - We present a polynomial-time approximation algorithm with approximation factor strictly better than $1-1/e$ for any set system with linear 2-shallow cell complexity (or any set system that can be decomposed into a constant number of such set systems). The result also holds for the weighted maximum coverage problem, where objects have weights and we want to select objects with total weight within a given budget. The result applies to many types of geometric objects, including pseudodisks in 2D, fat axis-aligned rectangles in 2D, similar-size fat triangles in 2D, axis-aligned unit cubes in 3D.
  - For small $k$, we obtain a $(1-ε)$-approximation algorithm more generally for any set system with constant VC dimension, running in time exponential in $\tilde{O}(k/ε)$. This simplifies and improves Badanidiyuru, Kleinberg, and Lee's parameterized approximation scheme [SoCG'12] running in time exponential in $\tilde{O}(k^2/ε^5)$.
  - A continuous version of the geometric maximum coverage problem asks for $k$ objects maximizing the volume of their union. We give better approximation algorithms for this problem for certain families of objects; e.g., we obtain an EPTAS for fat convex objects in any constant dimension.
  - We complement our algorithms with several hardness results, e.g., APX-hardness for fat axis-aligned rectangles in 2D, $(1-1/e+ε)$-approximation hardness for axis-aligned boxes in a dimension dependent on $ε$, and a lower bound ruling out $n^{\mathop{\rm poly}(1/ε)}$-time PTASs for the continuous problem for axis-aligned boxes in 3D.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [80] [Topology-Aware Data Movement for Disaggregated GPU Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28633)
*Sanjeev Rao Ganjihal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Topology-aware disaggregated LLM inference transfer orchestration reduces KV-cache transfer latency by exploiting interconnect hierarchy and overlapping transfer with computation, achieving 3–18x latency reduction over uniform RDMA in analytical models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disaggregated inference requires KV-cache transfers between prefill and decode GPU pools; bandwidth varies dramatically across interconnects (NVLink ~900 GB/s within domain, InfiniBand ~50 GB/s across nodes, TCP ~12.5 GB/s across data centers), making uniform RDMA suboptimal.

Method: Discover interconnect hierarchy at startup; implement three mechanisms: (1) pipelined layer-by-layer transfer overlapping transmission with prefill; (2) NVLink domain-aware placement for Mixture-of-Experts to co-optimize expert dispatch with KV-cache locality; (3) CXL 3.0 memory expanders as a shared overflow tier providing large capacity with low latency. Provide analytical bandwidth models, component implementations, and projected analysis across three architectures.

Result: Analytical bandwidth models and prototypes indicate 3–18x transfer latency reduction over uniform RDMA; full evaluation requires multi-node clusters with heterogeneous interconnects and CXL 3.0 hardware not yet widely available, limiting empirical validation.

Conclusion: Topology-aware transfer orchestration substantially improves disaggregated LLM inference performance by aligning data movement with interconnect topology; practical deployment depends on future hardware availability.

Abstract: Disaggregated LLM inference creates a datacenter networking problem that no existing system solves correctly. When prefill and decode run on separate GPU pools, the KV cache must be transferred between them. For a 70B model this is 2.6 GB per request, exceeding 100 GB/s aggregate at production scale. Yet DistServe, Splitwise, and Mooncake all use uniform RDMA, ignoring that bandwidth between two GPUs varies by 72x depending on their physical relationship: 900 GB/s via NVLink within a domain, 50 GB/s via InfiniBand across nodes, 12.5 GB/s via TCP across data centers. We design a topology-aware transfer orchestrator that discovers interconnect hierarchy at startup and selects optimal transport per transfer. Three mechanisms work together: (1) pipelined layer-by-layer transfer that overlaps transmission with ongoing prefill, hiding 60 to 85 percent of latency behind computation; (2) NVLink domain-aware placement for Mixture-of-Experts models that co-optimizes expert dispatch with KV cache locality; and (3) CXL 3.0 memory expanders as a shared overflow tier providing 6x capacity at 86x lower latency than NVMe. Full evaluation requires multi-node clusters with heterogeneous interconnects and CXL 3.0 hardware that is beyond academic resources and not yet available in GPU clouds. We present analytical bandwidth models, component implementations, and projected analysis across three architectures showing 3 to 18x transfer latency reduction over uniform RDMA.

</details>


### [81] [Sensitivity Analysis of GRU, LSTM and Transformer Encoder in Classification of Automated Driving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28665)
*Bidhya Shrestha,Christos Papadopoulos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Evaluates GRU, LSTM, and Transformer encoder for identifying Level 2 ADSs using telematics data; introduces a modular robustness framework with five corruption families across five severity levels, revealing strong clean-data performance but a critical vulnerability to temporal jitter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Independent verification of which automated driving system is active is essential for safety, regulatory compliance, insurance assessment, and anomaly detection in future SDVs that may host multiple native and aftermarket ADSs.

Method: Compare three sequence models (GRU, LSTM, Transformer encoder) on telematics sequences to classify active ADSs (Comma Openpilot, Tesla Autopilot, Cadillac Super Cruise) and manual driving. Evaluate on clean data and with threat-matched training. Develop and apply a modular robustness framework simulating telematics degradations: five corruption families (continuous-channel noise with AWGN drift, cross-channel noise, temporal jitter; burst loss, delayed transitions, spurious toggles, cross-feature inconsistencies) across five severity levels (L1-L5). Use macro-F1 as the robustness metric.

Result: Clean-data macro-F1: 0.92 (GRU), 0.90 (LSTM), 0.93 (Transformer). Threat-matched training yields macro-F1 0.904–0.916 with modest clean-data penalty. Under degradations, event-level corruptions maintain macro-F1 ≥0.87 at L5, while temporal jitter drives macro-F1 down to 0.44–0.50 across all models.

Conclusion: All three models perform well in clean conditions; robustness is highly sensitive to temporal jitter, revealing a sharp robustness gap. The proposed framework effectively differentiates loss mechanisms and can guide improvements for telemetry-based ADS activity verification.

Abstract: Automated driving systems (ADSs) are becoming ubiquitous. Future Software Defined Vehicles (SDVs) may be able to run multiple ADSs, both native and aftermarket such as Comma.ai's Openpilot. Monitoring systems to independently verify which automated driving system is active are important for safety monitoring, regulatory compliance, insurance assessment, and anomaly detection. In this paper, we first evaluate the effectiveness of three sequence-based classification models: Gated Recurrent Units (GRU), Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks, and a Transformer encoder model for identifying Level 2 automated driving systems using vehicle telematics data alone: Comma Openpilot, Tesla Autopilot, and Cadillac Super Cruise, along with manual driving. All three models achieve strong clean-data performance with macro F1-scores of 0.92 (GRU), 0.90 (LSTM), and 0.93 (Transformer encoder model) when trained on clean data; threat-matched training yields 0.904-0.916 macro F1 with only a modest clean-data penalty. Second, we introduce a modular robustness evaluation framework that simulates realistic telematics degradation through five corruption families at five severity levels (L1-L5). Continuous channels are perturbed using additive white Gaussian noise with cumulative drift, correlated cross-channel noise, and temporal jitter. Binary event signals are subjected to burst loss, delayed transitions, spurious toggles and cross-feature inconsistencies inspired by communication errors. Robustness is measured using macro-F1, which gives equal weight to each class and is suitable for imbalanced multiclass evaluation. Our evaluation reveals a sharp failure-mode split: event-level corruptions reduce macro-F1 only slightly (greater than equal to 0.87 at L5), while temporal jitter collapses macro-F1 to 0.44-0.50 across GRU, LSTM, and Transformer encoder model.

</details>


### [82] [Guarantees on Dynamical System Distinguishability for LLM Token Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28667)
*Mohamed Akrout,Dan Wilson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Classification of LLM responses can be understood via stochastic linear dynamical systems (DSs); fundamental accuracy limits exist even with dynamics differences; misclassification improves exponentially with sequence length; cross-embedding transferability is governed by an intertwining map's singular values.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide a theoretical understanding of why DS-based classification works, how performance scales with token sequence length, and when it generalizes across embedding models.

Method: Formalize the task as a binary hypothesis test between two stochastic linear DSs; analyze the total variation distance between stationary marginals to establish an accuracy floor; derive an exponential decay of misclassification probability with sequence length L governed by a dynamical discriminability quantity δ^2 (spectral distance between DSs); introduce an approximate intertwining condition between embeddings to bound transferable discriminability via the smallest singular value of the intertwining map.

Result: Demonstrates that the TV distance can be arbitrarily small even for substantially different dynamics, implying a fundamental accuracy floor for classifiers ignoring token dynamics; derives exponential misclassification decay with length L; defines δ^2 as a key discriminability measure; provides a bound on cross-embedding transferability based on the intertwining map's smallest singular value.

Conclusion: The work explains empirical gains of DS-based classification and advocates applying dynamical-system theory to AI analysis, offering a theoretical foundation for when such approaches succeed and how they generalize across embedding models.

Abstract: Recent work has shown that classifying large language models (LLMs)' responses can be distinguished by modeling token embeddings as trajectories of a black-box dynamical system (DS) and comparing prediction residuals of two DSs. Despite the empirical success of this dynamical approach, a theoretical understanding of why it works, how well it scales as a function of the token sequence, and when it transfers across embedding models remains lacking. We address these questions by formalizing the classification task as a binary hypothesis test between two stochastic linear DSs. We show that the total variation distance between the stationary marginal distributions of the two DSs can be arbitrarily small even when the dynamics differ substantially, which provides a fundamental accuracy floor for any classifier that ignores token dynamics. We then show that the misclassification probability of DS-based classification decays exponentially in the sequence length $L$, with the decay governed by a dynamical discriminability quantity $δ^2$ that captures the spectral distance between the two DSs. We also characterize cross-embedding generalization by introducing an approximate intertwining condition between embedding models and establishing a lower bound on the transferable discriminability in terms of the intertwining map's smallest singular value. Together, these results explain the empirical performance of DS-based classification and motivate further investigation into using DS theory to analyze AI systems, in contrast to the more common approach of using AI to model dynamical systems.

</details>


### [83] [LARA: Lightweight Adapters in the Residual Stream for Composable Adaptation and Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28669)
*Pascal Ekin,Hyosun Choi,Wei Jie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LARA introduces lightweight additive residual adaptation: it keeps the base model frozen, reads hidden states at select layers, and injects low-rank corrections into the residual stream. It matches LoRA at the same parameter budget, offers an inference-time scale γ for graded control, and supports multiple behaviors resident simultaneously with low overhead on a single frozen base.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient model adaptation without modifying base weights, enabling multiple behaviors and on-device deployment; overcomes limitations of weight-space adaptation by enabling residual-space, per-token routing, and inference-time interpolation.

Method: For each behavior, LARA reads a small set of hidden states from selected layers and adds a low-rank correction to the residual stream, leaving all base weights untouched. Behaviors are trained separately as small residual modules over a shared frozen base. Inference-time scale γ interpolates between base and adapted behavior. Multiple behaviors (seven in the report) can reside on a single frozen 1.5B model with ~33 MB overhead.

Result: On a code fine-tuning task and on preference optimization (DPO), LARA matches LoRA at equal parameter counts. Seven behaviors (six fine-tuned and one DPO-optimized) fit on a single frozen 1.5B model with ~33 MB overhead, compared to training a full model for each behavior. Behavior routing per token is supported, enabling hosting many behaviors on-device.

Conclusion: LARA provides efficient, flexible adaptation by operating in residual space on a frozen base: it enables multiple behaviors with low overhead, per-token routing, and graded control via a learnable inference-time scale, making on-device deployment and updating of behaviors practical.

Abstract: We present LARA (Lightweight Additive Residual Adaptation), a method for efficient adaptation that operates in the residual stream of a frozen model rather than in its weights. Where LoRA adds an update of low rank to weight matrices, LARA reads the hidden state at a small set of layers and adds a correction of low rank back to the residual stream, leaving all base weights untouched. On a code fine-tuning task and on preference optimization (DPO), LARA matches LoRA at equal parameter counts. Because adaptation is a frozen base plus a residual, LARA exposes a scale γ, applied at inference, that interpolates smoothly between base and adapted behavior, a form of graded control that adaptation in weight space does not offer. Finally, because each behavior is a small residual module over a shared frozen base, many behaviors can be held resident at once and routed automatically per token. We place seven behaviors, six fine-tuned and one optimized for preference, on one frozen 1.5B model for roughly 33 MB of overhead, against one full model for each behavior. Because the base is untouched, behaviors are trained separately and selected per token rather than loaded on demand, which suits hosting many behaviors, and adding new ones, on a single model on a device.

</details>


### [84] [Hierarchical Copula-Gumbel-Top-\texorpdfstring{$K$}{K} Routing: Two-Sided Dependence Control for Frozen Mixture-of-Experts at Fixed Per-Token Routing Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28670)
*Richard Yi Da Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Hierarchical Copula-Gumbel-Top-K to jointly configure MoE routing while keeping per-token routing fixed; introduces within-group Gaussian copula and cross-group antithetic coupling; proves invariance properties; analyzes load variance trade-offs; includes a trainable controller with score-function estimator; initial pilot validates the method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strengthen understanding and control of joint routing distributions in MoE with fixed per-token routing; enable tunable load coherence and dispersion via dependence structure without altering base routing laws; provide a practical training path using a lightweight controller.

Method: Two-level dependence design: within-group exchangeable Gaussian copula correlates Gumbel perturbations across experts; across-group antithetic construction introduces negative dependence; theoretical results show marginal distributions preserved; conditional expectations of traffic preserved; analysis of variance inflation/deflation; controller-based training with score-function estimator and frozen features.

Result: Proven that both operations preserve per-token routing marginals and conditional traffic; characterize trade-offs: positive within-group coupling increases load variance; cross-group nonnegative opposition reduces variance; two dials on invariance surface to control coherence and load dispersion; small-scale pilot validates the mechanism and training route but not task-level gains yet.

Conclusion: The Hierarchical Copula-Gumbel-Top-K framework offers a principled, tunable mechanism to modulate MoE routing behavior via dependence structure while preserving token-wise routing distributions; practical training approach via a lightweight controller; future work to assess task-level benefits and scalability.

Abstract: A stochastic Gumbel-Top-$K$ router defines, for every token of a mixture-of-experts (MoE) model, a \emph{routing law}: a distribution over ordered expert lists and mixture weights. We ask which \emph{joint} distributions over the routing choices of different tokens are reachable while every individual token's complete routing law is held exactly fixed. We give a two-sided construction, \emph{Hierarchical Copula-Gumbel-Top-$K$} (\CGA{}). Within a group of related tokens, an exchangeable Gaussian copula positively correlates the Gumbel perturbations at each expert coordinate, which can increase within-group expert-set coherence. Across disjoint pairs of groups, a tunable antithetic construction introduces a selectable amount of negative dependence. We prove that both operations leave each token's ordered Top-$K$ sample, mixture weights, and inclusion probabilities identical in distribution to independent routing \emph{at a routing layer conditioned on its pre-routing logits}; conditional expected expert traffic is preserved as a consequence. We characterize the resulting trade-off: positive within-group coupling can only inflate the variance of realized expert loads relative to independent routing, while nonnegative cross-group opposition can only reduce it relative to flat coupling at the same within-group strength. Coherence and load dispersion are thus controlled by two complementary dependence dials on the invariance constraint surface. Because the base model is untouched, the dials can be driven by a small controller over frozen features, trainable with a score-function estimator: the frozen network is evaluated only in the forward direction, and gradients are confined to the controller. An initial small-scale pilot validates the mechanism and the training route, but does not establish task-level fine-tuning gains.

</details>


### [85] [LAWFUL: Law-Aligned Witness for Faithful Use of Latents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28672)
*Kevin Chen,Kenneth W. Parker,Anish Arora*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LAWFUL provides a principled, coverage-aware framework to diagnose whether neural nets learn and internally use continuous-physics laws, addressing two key gaps (continuous counterfactual causal-consistency and domain-of-validity) and validating this on Mocap2Radar for the Doppler law even when f(t) and v(t) are not observed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To answer whether neural predictors that are accurate for physical systems actually encode governing laws as structured, formal knowledge, and whether such laws are used by the network across the law’s domain of validity; this requires interpretable, continuous-variable checks beyond mere predictive accuracy.

Method: Introduce LAWFUL to close the first two interpretability gaps: (i) a coverage-aware causal-consistency measure over continuous counterfactuals, (ii) a domain-of-validity test for the learned circuit. Apply the framework to the Mocap2Radar transformer, analyzing whether it learns and internally uses the Doppler law f(t)=2v(t)/λ from motion-capture and radar data in which neither f(t) nor v(t) appears. The work also lays groundwork for checks on invariants, forbidden behaviors, and quantity-flow through the circuit.

Result: LAWFUL closes the first two interpretability gaps and is validated experimentally on Mocap2Radar, demonstrating that the model can learn and internally utilize the Doppler frequency law even when the explicit variables f(t) and v(t) are absent from the data.

Conclusion: A foundational framework for verifying learned physical laws in neural networks over continuous variables; confirms that practical learning of a law is possible and measurable for at least the first two interpretability gaps, with a clear path for future verification of invariants, forbidden behaviors, and flow of derived quantities.

Abstract: When a neural network predicts a physical system accurately, has it learned the governing law as formal, structured knowledge, and if so, does the network's internal computation actually use that representation throughout the law's domain of validity? We identify four interpretability gaps that limit answering these questions for {\em physics laws over continuous variables}: the absence of a coverage-aware causal-consistency measure over continuous counterfactuals; of a domain-of-validity test for the identified circuit; of a verification of the law's invariants and forbidden behaviors; and of a quantification of how a derived physical quantity flows through the circuit. We develop a foundational framework, LAWFUL, that closes the first two and lays groundwork for the remaining two, and illustrate it on the Mocap2Radar transformer, validating whether it learns and internally uses the Doppler frequency law $f(t) = \frac{2 v(t)}λ$ from motion-capture and radar data in which neither $f(t)$ nor $v(t)$ appears.

</details>


### [86] [MPP-GNN: Subject-Adaptive Community Detection for fMRI-Based Alzheimer's Disease Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28681)
*Yang Zhang,Xiao Zhou,Jonathan Warrell,Avram Holmes,Xuan Zhang,Mark Gerstein*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A bilevel, meta-probabilistic pooling GNN discovers subject-specific brain modules and uses them as priors to refine edges and representation learning, improving Alzheimer's disease classification and aligning with canonical networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inter-subject variability in functional brain modular organization is not captured by fixed module definitions; modules should be learned per-subject and leveraged to guide learning of connectivity.

Method: Proposes MPP-GNN with coupled, bilevel optimization: adaptive graph partitioning to discover subject-specific modules, then uses discovered modules as priors to guide edge refinement and representation learning in a GNN; evaluated on two public AD datasets; achieves higher AUC than baselines.

Result: Achieves highest AUC vs baselines on both datasets; demonstrates alignment with Yeo atlas functional networks; reveals network-level dedifferentiation pattern in Alzheimer’s disease.

Conclusion: MPP-GNN accommodates inter-subject modular variability, yields improved AD classification, and demonstrates potential for linking data-driven modules with canonical network architecture.

Abstract: Functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) is a widely used technique for studying the brain. Recent methods that utilize graph neural networks (GNNs) for analysis of brain functional connectivity have shown great potential for the classification of brain disorders, such as Alzheimer's disease (AD). However, these methods often assume a preset number of functional modules across all subjects, which overlooks inter-subject variability. In addition, the discovered modules are rarely used to directly guide the learned connectivity patterns. Here, to address these issues, we propose a Meta Probabilistic Pooling GNN (MPP-GNN). We frame the model's task as a coupled, bilevel optimization that performs adaptive graph partitioning hierarchically to discover subject-specific modules and then uses the discovered brain modules as an explicit prior to guide edge refinement and representation learning. We validate MPP-GNN on two public datasets for AD classification, achieving the highest AUC in comparison to established baselines for both datasets. Furthermore, our analysis demonstrates that MPP-GNN shows significant alignment with the canonical functional-network organization defined by the Yeo brain atlas and reveals a network-level dedifferentiation pattern for AD.

</details>


### [87] [Technological Advances in Detecting and Managing Cognitive Impairment in Older Adults: Trends, Challenges, and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28687)
*Mohammad Asif,Azizuddin Khan,Mohd Azam,Anurag Rajkumar Bombarde*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multimodal AI-driven review on early cognitive impairment detection and management.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve early detection of cognitive decline from MCI to dementia and enable personalized care.

Method: Critical synthesis across EEG, MRI/PET, plasma biomarkers, digital markers; develops taxonomy, validation framework (subject/site-independent), integrative detection-to-intervention framework, and tables comparing methods, interventions, and risk/protective factors; surveys AI/ML/DL approaches (CNNs, LSTMs/BiLSTMs, transformers, self-supervised models) and cross-site validation issues.

Result: EEG markers and deep models show high accuracy but often lack external validation due to small single-site datasets; plasma p-tau217 achieved clinical utility; anti-amyloid therapies approved with contested modest benefits; multidomain lifestyle prevention matured; wearables/remote tools enable continuous monitoring; multimodal fusion improves sensitivity/specificity; barriers include standardization, explainability, data privacy, equitable deployment; near-term promise lies in multimodal, longitudinally validated systems linking early detection to actionable, personalized care.

Conclusion: Advances point to trustworthy, multimodal, longitudinal validation systems that connect early detection to actionable, personalized care, contingent on external validation, standardization, and privacy safeguards.

Abstract: As populations age, cognitive decline from mild cognitive impairment (MCI) to dementia is a defining health challenge of the coming decades, yet routine assessment often misses its earliest signs. This article critically synthesizes recent technological advances for detecting and managing cognitive impairment in older adults, spanning neurophysiological signals (chiefly electroencephalography, EEG), structural and molecular neuroimaging (MRI and amyloid/tau PET), blood-based biomarkers, and digital markers, integrated through artificial intelligence (AI), machine learning (ML), and deep learning (DL). Beyond summarizing, it contributes a cross-disciplinary taxonomy, a methodological-rigor lens foregrounding subject- and site-independent validation, an integrative early-detection framework linking tiered screening to intervention, and comparison tables of detection methods, interventions, and risk and protective factors. EEG markers (alpha/theta changes, P300 latency) and deep models (CNNs, LSTM/BiLSTM, transformers, self-supervised EEG foundation models) report strong accuracy, yet many rest on small, single-site datasets unlikely to survive rigorous external validation. Elsewhere, gains are tangible: plasma p-tau217 has reached clinical utility, with the first blood test cleared to aid Alzheimer's diagnosis in 2025; anti-amyloid therapies (lecanemab, donanemab) are approved despite modest, contested benefits; and multidomain lifestyle prevention has matured. Wearable, remote, speech, and virtual-reality tools enable continuous, ecologically valid monitoring, and multimodal fusion improves sensitivity and specificity. Barriers remain: standardization, explainability, data privacy, and equitable, externally validated deployment. The field's near-term promise lies in trustworthy, multimodal, longitudinally validated systems linking early detection to actionable, personalized care.

</details>


### [88] [SEDR-Seq2P: A Lightweight Dilated Residual Sequence-to-Point Network for Multi-Task Industrial NILM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28693)
*Hatem Haddad,Feres Jerbi,Issam Smaali*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SEDR-Seq2P is a lightweight Seq2Point-based NILM model for industrial loads that offers a favorable accuracy-delay trade-off, achieving similar or better accuracy than Seq2Point while significantly reducing latency compared with WaveNet, through dilated residual blocks and squeeze-and-excitation attention in a one-to-many multi-task setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial NILM faces high measurement noise and pervasive concurrent operation, which degrade models trained on residential data. There is a need for scalable, efficient models that maintain accuracy with lower inference cost and good latency characteristics for deployment in industrial environments.

Method: Introduce SEDR-Seq2P, a lightweight extension of Seq2Point incorporating dilated residual blocks and squeeze-and-excitation attention. The approach operates in a one-to-many, multi-task disaggregation setting and is evaluated under a unified IMDELD protocol against Seq2Seq, Seq2SubSeq, Seq2Point, GRU, and WaveNet. Performance is measured with energy-estimation metrics and an accuracy-delay criterion.

Result: Compared to Seq2Point baseline, SEDR-Seq2P reduces MAE by ~7%, increases R^2 by ~1%, and raises match rate by ~0.8%. Relative to WaveNet, it reduces inference latency by ~58%, yielding a better accuracy-delay trade-off. Seq2Point remains a strong accuracy-delays balance over Seq2Seq/Seq2SubSeq, while GRU and WaveNet achieve higher accuracy with substantially higher cost.

Conclusion: SEDR-Seq2P delivers a favorable accuracy-delay balance for industrial NILM in one-to-many settings, offering substantial latency savings over WaveNet without sacrificing much accuracy, making it suitable for scalable industrial deployment. The approach improves key metrics (MAE, R^2, match rate) versus Seq2Point and is more efficient than heavier autoregressive models while maintaining competitive accuracy.

Abstract: Industrial NILM remains challenging because measurement noise and widespread concurrent machine operation reduce the generalization of models tuned on residential data. This work adopts a one-to-many, multi-task disaggregation setting, in which a single network estimates multiple industrial machine loads from aggregate power. Under a unified evaluation protocol on IMDELD, we benchmark Seq2Seq, Seq2SubSeq, Seq2Point, GRU, and WaveNet using energy-estimation metrics and the accuracy-delay criterion. While Seq2Point offers a stronger accuracy-delay balance than Seq2Seq/Seq2SubSeq, GRU and WaveNet achieve higher accuracy at markedly higher computational cost. To close this gap, we propose SEDR-Seq2P, a lightweight Seq2Point extension with dilated residual blocks and squeeze-and-excitation attention. Relative to the Seq2Point baseline, SEDR-Seq2P reduces MAE by approximately 7%, improves the coefficient of determination by approximately 1%, and increases the match rate by approximately 0.8%. In addition, compared to WaveNet, SEDR-Seq2P reduces inference latency by approximately 58%, yielding a favorable accuracy-delay trade-off for scalable industrial deployment.

</details>


### [89] [Predicting Steel Fatigue Life from Micrographs Using Physics-Informed Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28695)
*Aryuemaan Kumar Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A vision-based pipeline (CV) estimates log N_f for lightweight steel from micrographs using physics-informed features and GNLL-trained CNN; fast, calibrated predictions on synthetic data; domain transfer to real samples needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rapid, non-destructive fatigue-life estimation for quality control without lengthy mechanical testing.

Method: Seven-stage OpenCV preprocessing, 28-dimensional physics-informed feature extractor (crack morphology, grain structure, porosity, texture), CNN regression with Gaussian negative log-likelihood loss to predict log N_f and per-sample uncertainty σ_hat; evaluation of SE-CNN, ResNet-50, and VGG-16 on synthetic micrographs; Grad-CAM for interpretability; open-source release including dataset generator; synthetic-domain validation.

Result: ResNet-50 achieved R^2 = 0.93, RMSE = 0.18 log-cycles, macro-F1 = 0.91 on synthetic data; GNLL reduced Expected Calibration Error from 0.089 to 0.021 (76% improvement); inference time < 65 ms per image; Grad-CAM maps align with metallurgical features.

Conclusion: The approach shows strong methodological soundness in simulated conditions and enables rapid, calibrated fatigue-life estimation from images; immediate next step is domain transfer to real-field samples.

Abstract: Here is the plain text version optimized for arXiv's submission form. Custom macros (like \CV and \SI) have been converted to standard text/math so they render correctly on the webpage: Evaluating the fatigue life of structural steels conventionally requires mechanical testing lasting tens to hundreds of hours, making it impractical for rapid quality control. We present CV, a computer vision framework that estimates the fatigue life ($\log N_f$) of lightweight alloy steels directly from optical micrographs without physical testing.The pipeline features a seven-stage OpenCV preprocessing routine to remove artifacts, a 28-dimensional physics-informed feature extractor (quantifying crack morphology, grain structure, porosity, and texture), and a CNN regression model trained with a Gaussian negative log-likelihood (GNLL) loss to jointly predict $\log N_f$ and sample-specific uncertainty $\hatσ$.Evaluating three architectures (SE-CNN, ResNet-50, VGG-16) on a synthetic micrograph benchmark, ResNet-50 achieves $R^2 = 0.93$, RMSE = 0.18 log-cycles, and macro-F1 = 0.91. The GNLL objective reduces Expected Calibration Error by 76% compared to a mean-squared-error baseline (ECE: $0.089 \rightarrow 0.021$). Grad-CAM maps confirm the network attends to metallurgically meaningful microstructural features.Running in under 65 ms per image, the pipeline and synthetic dataset generator are open-sourced. Because validation relies entirely on synthetic micrographs, these results demonstrate methodological soundness under simulated conditions; a domain-transfer study on real field samples is the immediate next step.

</details>


### [90] [Mitigating Class-Tail Undercoverage in Medical Vision-Language Models under Clinical Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28696)
*Mushir Akhtar,M. Tanveer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CALCoDe is a post-hoc reliability layer for frozen medical vision-language models that achieves robust class-tail aware coverage under distribution shifts by combining class-tail adaptive localized conformal deferral with an independently calibrated support audit, achieving 0.95 coverage across eight settings and outperforming baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing conformal methods either adapt to test neighborhoods or to source-frequency tails but do not model held-out class-wise undercoverage under domain shift in medical VLMs. There is a need for reliable per-class coverage guarantees that hold under shift.

Method: CALCoDe, a post-hoc reliability layer: (1) uses cross-fitted validation predictions to identify classes at risk of undercoverage; (2) estimates class-conditional tail thresholds using a disjoint calibration split; (3) combines a protected (class-tail) threshold with a localized conformal threshold via a one-sided maximum; (4) ensures the final set contains all labels admitted by the localized rule while adding protection for validation-identified classes; (5) adds an independently calibrated support audit to defer cases with insufficient inlier support; (6) assumes exchangeability among accepted examples within each protected class to guarantee finite-sample coverage at a prespecified guard level; (7) empirically evaluates coverage on shifted external cohorts.

Result: Across two dermatology shifts (HAM10000→ISIC 2019 and HAM10000→PAD-UFES-20) and four frozen backbones (BiomedCLIP, OpenAI CLIP ViT-B/32, PubMedCLIP ViT-B/32, MedSigLIP-448), CALCoDe achieves both observed marginal and worst-class coverage of 0.95 in all eight settings; average worst-class accepted coverage on HAM10000→ISIC 2019 is 0.970, beating sTACP (0.926) and LCP-VLM (0.864).

Conclusion: CALCoDe provides robust, class-tail aware coverage for medical VLMs under distribution shifts, outperforming standard conformal baselines and recent VLM-specific methods, and serves as an effective post-hoc reliability layer to ensure calibrated, safe deployment.

Abstract: Medical vision-language models (VLMs) can retain high observed marginal coverage after clinical shift while substantially under-covering an individual disease class. The affected class varies with acquisition protocol and backbone geometry, so source prevalence does not reliably reveal the failure. Existing localized and tail-aware conformal methods respectively adapt to test neighborhoods and source-frequency tails, leaving held-out class-wise coverage failure unmodeled. We introduce Class-Tail Adaptive Localized Conformal Deferral (CALCoDe), a post-hoc reliability layer for frozen medical VLMs. Cross-fitted validation predictions identify classes at risk of undercoverage, and a disjoint calibration split estimates their class-conditional tail thresholds. CALCoDe combines each protected threshold with a localized conformal threshold using a one-sided maximum. The resulting set contains every label admitted by the localized rule, with additional protection confined to validation-identified classes. An independently calibrated support audit defers cases with insufficient inlier support. Under exchangeability among accepted examples within each protected class, CALCoDe provides finite-sample coverage at the prespecified guard level and contains the corresponding localized conformal sets; coverage on shifted external cohorts is evaluated empirically. Among standard conformal baselines and recent VLM-specific conformal methods evaluated across two dermatology shifts (HAM10000 to ISIC 2019 and HAM10000 to PAD-UFES-20) and four frozen VLM backbones (BiomedCLIP, OpenAI CLIP ViT-B/32, PubMedCLIP ViT-B/32, and MedSigLIP-448), CALCoDe is the only approach whose observed marginal and worst-class accepted coverage both reach 0.95 in all eight settings. On HAM10000 to ISIC 2019, its average worst-class accepted coverage is 0.970, compared with 0.926 for sTACP and 0.864 for LCP-VLM.

</details>


### [91] [Flow Matching with Missing Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28698)
*Fairoz Nower Khan,Nabuat Zaman Nahim,Peizhong Ju*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Missing-Data Flow Matching treats missing coordinates as latent variables and integrates the loss over plausible completions; proves an exact correction, showing MCAR incomplete data yields the same objective as complete data (bias moved to the completion model). Finite-sample results reveal counterintuitive variance behavior, the optimality of one completion per example under a fixed budget, and a single irreducible bias from the learned completion bounded by a Wasserstein distance to the true completion law; empirically competitive with strong baselines on tabular data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world data often arrives with missing coordinates. Extending flow matching to incomplete data requires principled handling of missingness to preserve learning objectives and provide actionable design guidance.

Method: Model missing coordinates as latent variables and compute the expected flow matching loss over their support. Prove the correction is exact. Under MCAR with true completions, show incomplete-data objective equals complete-data objective. Perform finite-sample analysis: missingness affects estimator variance (not increases it); one completion per example matches complete-data variance; with a fixed evaluation budget, a single completion is optimal. If using a learned completion model, it contributes one irreducible bias bounded by its expected conditional Wasserstein distance to the true completion law.

Result: The theory predicts parity between incomplete and complete data objectives under MCAR, a variance-transfer phenomenon, and optimal single completion under budget; the learned completion introduces a bounded irreducible bias. Empirical results validate the predictions, show that deterministic—not frozen—imputation drives collapse of the generated distribution, and place the method competitively against classical and deep imputation baselines on real tabular data.

Conclusion: Missing-data flow matching provides a principled, theoretically-grounded approach to partial observations with clear guidance on completion design and its impact on bias and variance; the framework yields exact corrections under MCAR and achieves competitive empirical performance.

Abstract: Flow matching assumes fully observed training data, which many real-world applications rarely provide. We propose Missing-Data Flow Matching, which treats the missing coordinates of training samples as latent variables and averages the flow matching loss over the values they could take. We first prove the correction is exact rather than approximate. Under missing completely at random with true completions, the incomplete-data objective equals the complete-data objective, so missingness changes nothing about what flow matching learns and the entire difficulty relocates to the completion model. Our finite-sample analysis then answers design questions that the algorithm leaves open, and the answers are not the ones intuition suggests. Missingness transfers estimator variance rather than adding it, one completion per example already matches complete-data variance exactly, and under a fixed evaluation budget one completion is optimal. A learned completion model contributes a single irreducible bias, which we bound by its expected conditional Wasserstein distance to the true completion law. Experiments numerically validate the theoretical predictions, show that deterministic rather than frozen imputation is what collapses the generated distribution, and place our method alongside strong classical and deep imputation baselines on real tabular data.

</details>


### [92] [MMFGU: Multimodal Federated Graph Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28708)
*Haodong Lu,Zekai Chen,Weiwei Ji,Shihao Li,Xunkai Li,Xun Wu,Yinlin Zhu,Rong-Hua Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MMFGU presents a multimodal federated graph unlearning framework that leverages target-specific representation decoupling to handle fine-grained unlearning requests. It maps heterogeneous requests to unified carriers, decouples requested representations while anchoring retained semantics, exposes/repairs propagated residuals with lightweight probes, and selectively purges affected clients via compact prototypes and response signals, achieving 41.5x speedup over full retraining while preserving utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multimodal federated graph learning, unlearning requests can be fine-grained (e.g., delete an account, remove a specific image/text, or revoke cross-modal associations). Existing federated graph unlearning mainly handles entity/relation or client removal and cannot satisfy these multimodal requests, leading to privacy and compliance gaps. A framework is needed to support target-specific, selective, and efficient unlearning without compromising retained information or graph utility.

Method: Introduce MMFGU built around target-specific representation decoupling. Map heterogeneous unlearning requests into unified target carriers; decouple requested representations while anchoring retained semantics; expose and repair propagated residuals via lightweight probes; selectively purge affected clients through compact prototypes and response signals. The approach emphasizes preserving retained content, preventing recovery via remaining modalities, and avoiding re-entry of traces after aggregation.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows that MMFGU effectively removes requested information, preserves retained graph utility, and delivers a 41.5× speedup relative to full retraining.

Conclusion: MMFGU enables precise, efficient multimodal unlearning in federated graphs by decoupling targets, repairing residuals, and selectively purging affected clients, offering substantial efficiency gains and maintenance of utility.

Abstract: Multimodal federated graph learning enables clients to collaboratively train graph models over structural, textual, and visual signals without sharing private local data. However, the presence of heterogeneous multimodal content also makes unlearning requests more frequent and fine-grained: users may delete accounts or interactions, remove a particular image or text while retaining the associated entity, or revoke the learned correspondence between retained modalities or graph attributes. Existing federated graph unlearning mainly handles entity/relation or client removal and cannot directly satisfy these multimodal requests. They introduce three challenges: removing only the requested information without damaging retained content, preventing the target from being recovered through remaining modalities or graph neighborhoods, and stopping related traces on other clients from re-entering the global model after aggregation. To address them, we propose \textsc{\textbf{MMFGU}}, a multimodal federated graph unlearning framework built around target-specific representation decoupling. \textsc{MMFGU} maps heterogeneous requests into unified target carriers, decouples requested representations while anchoring retained semantics, exposes and repairs propagated residuals with lightweight probes, and selectively purges affected clients through compact prototype and response signals. Experiments show that \textsc{MMFGU} effectively removes requested information, preserves retained graph utility, and achieves a $\boldsymbol{41.5\times}$ speedup over full retraining.

</details>


### [93] [Mirror Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28737)
*Yunpeng Liu,Matthew Niedoba,Oluwanifemi A. Adekanye,Jason Yoo,Yingchen He,Berend Zwartsenberg,Frank Wood*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Mirror learning from third-person demonstrations: transform third-person observations into actionable, first-person-like data via a perspective transform (video diffusion) and inverse dynamics, enabling mirror data to train policies; improves BC with mirror data; suggests generative world models enable scalable, teleoperation-free imitation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Behavior cloning struggles to exploit rich third-person signals; collecting first-person data via teleoperation is costly. The paper seeks to leverage third-person demonstrations to learn policies safely and scalably.

Method: Learned perspective transformation using a fine-tuned video diffusion model to convert third-person footage into learner-like views; train an inverse dynamics model to map observed trajectories into the learner's control space; synthesize mirror data from these components; train policies on mirror data alone and as augmentation to BC.

Result: Mirror data alone yields effective policies; adding mirror data to first-person BC training improves downstream performance; results imply generative world models encapsulate structure enabling safe, scalable alternatives to teleoperation.

Conclusion: Imitation learning from third-person observations via mirror data is viable and effective; modern generative models can provide the necessary world-structure to obviate labor-intensive teleoperation-based data collection.

Abstract: We investigate imitation learning through the lens of third-person observation and propose a framework for mirror learning: acquiring actionable policies from passive observation. While behavior cloning (BC) excels under dense, well-aligned first-person data, it fundamentally fails to leverage the rich observational signals arising from third-person demonstrations that humans and animals routinely exploit. We introduce a method that composes (i) a learned perspective transformation that places learners in demonstrators' shoes using a fine-tuned video diffusion model and (ii) an inverse dynamics model that infers action trajectories in the learners' control space. This enables the synthesis of mirror data, pseudo first-person expert data generated from third-person observations of demonstrator behavior. Empirically, we show that mirror data alone can train effective policies, and that augmenting first-person BC training with mirror data further improves downstream policy performance. Our results suggest that modern generative world models implicitly encode sufficient structure to enable a scalable and safe alternative to teleoperation-heavy data collection.

</details>


### [94] [TAGTorch: A PyTorch Library for Geometry, Topology, and Symmetry-Aware Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28755)
*Brendan Kennedy,Tegan Emerson,Gregory Roek,Emilie Purvine,Henry Kvinge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TAGTorch is a new open-source PyTorch library that unifies topology, algebra, and geometry tooling for machine learning, addressing fragmentation by providing data preprocessing, architectures, training techniques, and model analysis tools; the paper discusses design, architecture, capabilities, and future priorities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The software ecosystem for topology-, algebra-, and geometry-inspired ML is fragmented, with many methods existing only as research prototypes in unmaintained repos; a unified, maintained library is needed to improve usability, interoperability, and reproducibility.

Method: Development and description of TAGTorch, including its design philosophy, current architecture, capabilities, and how it addresses gaps in the software ecosystem; discussion of future development priorities.

Result: Description of a new library (TAGTorch) and its architecture and capabilities; the abstract emphasizes design and future work rather than empirical experiments, implying a software paper introducing the library.

Conclusion: TAGTorch aims to unify and advance the software ecosystem for topology, algebra, and geometry in ML and outlines priorities for ongoing development.

Abstract: Over the last decade, neural networks have been applied to an increasingly diverse range of applications, including data with rich geometric, topological, or symmetry-related structure. As a result, researchers have increasingly drawn inspiration from topology, algebra, and geometry. Despite this rich algorithmic development, the supporting software ecosystem remains fragmented. Many important methods exist only as research prototypes in unmaintained repositories. We address this by introducing Topology, Algebra, and Geometry Torch (TAGTorch), an open-source, PyTorch-based library that unifies tools inspired by topology, algebra, and geometry, including data-preprocessing methods, architectures, training techniques, and model analysis tools. We describe the design philosophy of TAGTorch and then discuss its current architecture and capabilities, highlighting areas where it can fill gaps in the current software ecosystem. We conclude with a discussion of our future development priorities for the library.

</details>


### [95] [Feature Interaction Modeling for Physics-Informed Neural Networks and Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28762)
*Quan Gu,Hongxia Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FM-based feature interaction modules embedded into PINNs and neural operators improve expressiveness for parameterized PDEs, yielding notable gains in shock-like regimes but not consistently on smooth problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limited cross-field interaction representation in PINNs/Neural Operators; leverage second-order Taylor expansion intuition to model variable couplings across spatio-temporal, parameter, and boundary-condition features.

Method: Introduce FM-PINN to explicitly capture spatio-temporal interactions using factorization machines; partition features into independent groups (spatial coordinates, time, physical parameters, initial/boundary conditions) and learn cross-group interactions; extend to FM-Operator and FM-DeepONet to handle nonlinear conservation laws and sharp gradients.

Result: Numerical experiments show substantial accuracy gains for shock-dominated PDEs; FM-Operator/DeepONet outperform baselines in nonlinear conservation laws and problems with sharp gradients/discontinuities; no consistent advantage on smooth operator benchmarks.

Conclusion: Cross-feature coupling via FM modules is a promising direction for physics-informed learning of parameterized PDEs with strong cross-field dependencies, offering improved accuracy in challenging regimes though not universally beneficial.

Abstract: This work embeds feature interaction modules derived from factorization machines (FMs) into physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) and neural operator learning, to enhance model expressiveness for solution manifolds of parameterized partial differential equations (PDEs). Motivated by the second-order Taylor expansion of multivariate functions to characterize variable couplings, we first propose FM-PINN. It explicitly captures spatio-temporal variable interactions and improves the approximation accuracy for smooth high-order PDEs. We further group spatial coordinates, time, physical parameters, and initial and boundary conditions into independent feature sets and model their cross-group interactions. Based on this strategy, we develop FM-Operator and FM-DeepONet, which are particularly effective for nonlinear conservation laws and problems with sharp gradients or discontinuities, while offering no consistent advantage on smooth operator learning benchmarks. Numerical tests demonstrate that the proposed mechanism delivers substantial accuracy gains on challenging shock-dominated equations, indicating a promising direction for physics-consistent modeling of parameterized PDEs with strong cross-field dependencies.

</details>


### [96] [Representations from Pretrained Machine-Learning Interatomic Potentials as Coarse Coordinates for Material Generation and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28776)
*Paul Hagemann,Katharina Ueltzen,Simon Müller,Janine George,Philipp Benner*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Coarse-Fine Transport Distance (CFTD) to evaluate generative inorganic crystal structures using atom-averaged coarse MACE features, capturing quality, novelty, and memorization, and shows coarse MACE features can guide generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide a unified, distribution-based evaluation for crystal-structure generative models that captures both quality and novelty while mitigating memorization, leveraging pretrained MLIPs (e.g., MACE) as feature extractors.

Method: Define a two-tier distance metric (Coarse-Fine Transport Distance) using featurizers based on coarse and fine representations, with coarse MACE features for the quality component; compare CFTD to SUN metrics; demonstrate memorization detection; use coarse MACE features to guide a generative model.

Result: CFTD effectively captures both quality and novelty of generated crystal structures, detects memorization, and aligns with or complements SUN metrics; coarse MACE features can be used to steer material generative models.

Conclusion: Coarse MACE features provide a practical and versatile tool for evaluating and guiding inorganic crystal-structure generation, enabling unified assessment of quality and novelty while facilitating model guidance.

Abstract: Generative machine learning is increasingly used for inorganic crystal structure generation. Most models and the corresponding evaluation approaches rely on simple forms of crystal structure representation. In this paper, we showcase the power of atom-averaged features from pretrained Machine-Learning Interatomic Potentials (MLIPs), such as MACE, for such tasks. We first introduce a distance measure that assesses the output of material generative models by capturing both quality and novelty in a single distribution-based evaluation framework. In particular, we introduce the Coarse-Fine Transport Distance (CFTD) using two different featurizers, where the quality component is based on coarse MACE features. We showcase CFTD's versatility in capturing crystal-structure quality while also detecting memorization, and compare it with the recently introduced continuous SUN metrics. We further show that coarse MACE features can be used as guidance for a material generative model.

</details>


### [97] [Distilling Knowledge from Large Language Models into Lightweight Reinforcement Learning Agents for Autonomous Cyber Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28826)
*Konur Tholl,François Rivest,Mariam El Mezouar,Adrian Taylor,Ranwa Al Mallah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM-guided autonomous cyber defense (via prompt engineering) can outperform baseline RL in CybORG CAGE Challenge 2; subsequent online policy distillation yields a tiny RL agent while preserving defense performance; evaluation across 4–12 host networks shows teacher-guided stabilization does not consistently beat the optimized teacher policy; supports using cybersecurity-focused LLMs as expertise sources and policy distillation for deployable agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the instability and sample-inefficiency of reinforcement learning for autonomous cyber defense. Explore whether a cybersecurity-focused LLM can provide expert-like decision-making without fine-tuning, and whether its policy can be compressed into a lightweight RL agent for practical deployment.

Method: 1) Prompt-engineered 8B cybersecurity-LM (no fine-tuning) used to control defense in CybORG CAGE Challenge 2. 2) Propose online policy distillation to transfer the LLM policy into a compact RL agent (~64.9k parameters). 3) Evaluate across CybORG scenarios with 4–12 hosts and varying network configurations. 4) Test teacher-guided RL stabilization strategies and compare to the optimized teacher policy.

Result: The 8B LLM with prompts outperforms the baseline RL agent in the modified CybORG environment. Policy distillation yields a lightweight 64.9k-parameter RL actor that preserves effective defense capabilities. Transferability tests across 4–12 hosts show varying results; teacher-guided stabilization strategies do not consistently surpass the optimized teacher policy, indicating policy-alignment limitations between RL optimization and teacher-guided defense.

Conclusion: Cybersecurity-focused LLMs can serve as expertise sources for autonomous defense. Policy distillation provides a viable path to operationalize frontier models into efficient, deployable agents, though aligning RL reward-driven optimization with teacher-guided strategies remains challenging.

Abstract: Autonomous Cyber Operations (ACO) are increasingly important for defending enterprise networks as cyber threats continue to evolve in sophistication. ACO applications commonly employ Reinforcement Learning (RL) agents to learn defensive behaviors through interaction with environments. However, RL agents typically require extensive exploration during training, often resulting in unstable behavior and poor initial decision-making before converging toward effective defense strategies.
  In this work, we investigate the use of a Large Language Model (LLM) to improve autonomous defensive decision-making within an ACO environment. Through prompt engineering rather than fine-tuning, we demonstrate that an 8-billion parameter LLM pretrained on cybersecurity data can outperform a baseline RL agent in a modified CybORG CAGE Challenge 2 environment. We then propose an online policy distillation framework that transfers the LLM's defensive policy into a lightweight RL agent containing only 64,910 parameters, reducing model size by several orders of magnitude while maintaining effective defensive capabilities. This provides a pathway toward operationalizing frontier cybersecurity models within lightweight, deployable agents.
  To evaluate transferability, we construct CybORG scenarios ranging from 4 to 12 hosts and assess the approach across varying network configurations. We also evaluate teacher-guided RL stabilization strategies and observe that none consistently surpass the optimized teacher policy, suggesting policy-alignment limitations between reward-driven RL optimization and teacher-guided defense strategies.
  Our results demonstrate the potential of cybersecurity-focused LLMs as sources of expertise for autonomous cyber defense, while policy distillation provides a practical path toward operationalizing frontier cybersecurity models within efficient, scalable agents.

</details>


### [98] [Hypergradient-based Bilevel Reinforcement Learning with Improved Sample Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28849)
*Naman Saxena,Mudit Gaur,Vaneet Aggarwal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hessian-free hypergradient-based bilevel RL leveraging Boltzmann policy optimality for entropy-regularized discounted objectives; achieves O(1/ε) iteration and near-optimal O~(1/ε^2) sample complexity, without PL assumption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve scalability and sample efficiency of bilevel RL methods, which typically rely on Hessian-based hypergradients or penalty-based approximations, hindering applicability to meta-learning, hierarchical tasks, and RL from human feedback.

Method: Derives a hypergradient-based bilevel RL algorithm that uses the optimality of the Boltzmann (softmax) policy for the entropy-regularized discounted RL objective. The method is Hessian-free and comes with convergence guarantees under mild regularity; importantly, it removes the Polyak-Lojasiewicz condition on the outer objective. 

Result: The proposed approach attains iteration complexity O(ε^{-1}) and a state-of-the-art sample complexity of ~O(ε^{-2}) under mild assumptions. It achieves Hessian-free updates and improved convergence without requiring the PL condition.

Conclusion: The work advances scalable bilevel RL by delivering a Hessian-free, hypergradient-based algorithm with strong sample-efficiency guarantees and by relaxing a common assumption (PL) in the outer objective.

Abstract: Bilevel reinforcement learning (RL) is an important framework within the literature of RL that can be used to formalize various categories of problems, such as meta-learning, hierarchical task decomposition, and reinforcement learning from human feedback (RL-HF). Most of the bilevel RL algorithms are either not scalable because of using hypergradient with Hessian, or they suffer from high sample complexity because of using penalty-based approximation methods. In this work, we propose a hypergradient-based bilevel RL algorithm using the optimality of the Boltzmann policy for the entropy regularized discounted RL objective function. Our proposed algorithm is Hessian-free and obtains an iteration complexity of $O(ε^{-1})$ and state-of-the-art sample complexity of $\tilde{O}(ε^{-2})$ under mild regularity conditions. Further, in our convergence analysis, we are able to remove the assumption of the Polyak-Lojasiewicz (PL) condition on the outer-level objective function present in the prior state-of-the-art sample complexity work.

</details>


### [99] [An analysis of machine learning approaches for enhancing decision-making in complex discrete choice tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28854)
*Sheng Lun Christine Cao,Destenie Nock,Alex Davis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Semi-parametric and non-parametric ML models generally outperform parametric models in learning and predicting individual discrete choice rules under heterogeneity, across five behavioral rules, with improvements tied to training set size and determinism; a case study shows the Twin Neural Network performing best (BIC = 13.351) for real policy data, highlighting context-driven model selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limited understanding of how machine learning methods can estimate individual discrete choice rules under individual heterogeneity in policy elicitation, and to explore data-driven approaches for preference elicitation beyond traditional parametric models.

Method: A comparative Monte Carlo study evaluating four models (multinomial logistic regression, generalized additive model, twin neural network, Gaussian process) on five choice rules (linear strong utility, monotonic strong utility, ideal point, lexicographic semiorder, multiattribute linear ballistic accumulator).
