The experiments vary (a) the number of attributes, (b) the number of training choice sets, and (c) the determinism of the choice rule. A real-energy policy preference case study complements the simulations, with model comparison via information criteria (e.g., BIC).

Result: Across rules and contexts, semi-parametric and non-parametric models outperform the parametric MNL. Model performance improves with more training choice sets (≈6–96%) and with increasing determinism of the rule (≈0–55%). In the real-data case, the Twin Neural Network achieves the best fit (BIC = 13.351).

Conclusion: The study demonstrates the viability of semi-parametric and non-parametric models for policy-focused discrete choice modeling and emphasizes that the choice task context should guide model selection, while noting limitations and the need for further guidelines on model applicability across rules and data regimes.

Abstract: Discrete choice modeling is a common tool used for preference elicitation during policy-making, but this is typically done through parametric models. Machine learning can push the boundaries of discrete choice modeling for policy-based preference elicitation by adopting a data-driven approach or learning individual preferences. However, there is limited knowledge of how well machine learning methods can estimate individual discrete choice rules under individual heterogeneity, especially in the context of challenges often experienced during preference elicitation. This study evaluates four machine learning models (multinomial logistic regression, generalized additive model, twinned neural network, and Gaussian process) with respect to their capacity to learn and predict five choice rules that are important in the behavioral and social sciences (linear strong utility, monotonic strong utility, ideal point, lexicographic semiorder, and multiattribute linear ballistic accumulator). Monte Carlo experiments were performed to assess model performance when increasing a) the number of attributes in the choice alternatives, b) the number of training choice sets, and c) the choice rule's determinism. The simulation results demonstrated that semi-parametric and non-parametric models generally outperform parametric models across all choice rules and experimental contexts. Model performance also generally improves by 6% to 96% and 0% to 55%, respectively, with an increase in training choice sets and choice rule determinism. A case study using real energy policy preference data was also conducted, where TNN performed best with a BIC of 13.351. This work demonstrated the viability and limitations of semi-parametric and non-parametric models in the context of policy-centric discrete choice modeling and showed how the choice task context should drive model selection.

</details>


### [100] [Fast Rates for Swap-Agnostic Learning of Proper Losses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28856)
*Princewill Okoroafor*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Swap-agnostic learning allows per-level-set postprocessing of predictions; authors show joint control over prediction-level comparisons for proper losses, enabling offline/online guarantees without solving a separate problem per prediction value.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prediction-dependent postprocessing (swap-agnosticism) is a natural and important extension of agnostic learning, but previously required solving many separate agnostic-learning problems for each possible prediction value. The work seeks a unified, rate-optimal approach.

Method: Reduce swap-agnostic learning to a second-order multicalibration problem via Blackwell approachability with a Bernstein-style variance correction; derive offline and online rates for finite H and fixed smooth proper loss, and extend to entire families of losses; obtain improved rates for bounded, convex, 1-Lipschitz proper losses.

Result: Offline: excess risk scales as ~((log|H|)/m)^{2/3} for finite H and fixed smooth proper loss. Online: swap-regret scales as ~T^{1/3} (log|H|)^{2/3}. For all proper losses in [-1,1], online and offline rates are ~sqrt(T log|H|) and ~sqrt(log|H|/m). For convex, 1-Lipschitz proper losses, rates improve to ~T^{1/3} (log|H|)^{2/3} online and ~ (log|H|/m)^{2/3} offline. All bounds are tight up to log factors and improve over previous ~T^{2/3}(log|H|)^{1/3}. The main tool is a reduction to a second-order multicalibration problem via Blackwell approachability with variance correction.

Conclusion: This work provides the first unified, rate-optimal treatment of swap-agnostic learning for proper losses, enabling prediction-dependent postprocessing with provable guarantees; the technique connects swap-agnosticism to multicalibration and Blackwell approachability, offering a path to broader loss classes and online/offline regimes.

Abstract: Swap-agnostic learning strengthens classical agnostic learning by allowing the comparator to select a different hypothesis on each level set of the learner's predictions. This benchmark captures prediction-dependent postprocessing, but appears to require solving a separate agnostic-learning problem for every possible prediction value. We show that, for proper losses, these prediction-level comparisons can instead be controlled jointly. Our main result is an offline swap-agnostic learner for any fixed proper loss. For a finite hypothesis class $H$ and any fixed smooth proper loss, the excess risk from $m$ i.i.d. samples is $\widetilde{O}((\log |H|/m)^{2/3})$, with a corresponding online swap-regret bound of $\widetilde{O}(T^{1/3}(\log |H|)^{2/3})$. We also give algorithms whose predictions are simultaneously swap-agnostic for entire families of losses. For all proper losses bounded in $[-1,1]$, we obtain online and offline rates of $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{T\log |H|})$ and $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{\log |H|/m})$, respectively. For convex, $1$-Lipschitz proper losses, these rates improve to $\widetilde{O}(T^{1/3}(\log |H|)^{2/3})$ online and $\widetilde{O}((\log |H|/m)^{2/3})$ offline. These bounds are tight up to logarithmic factors and improve upon the $\widetilde{O}(T^{2/3}(\log |H|)^{1/3})$ rate implied by the swap-omniprediction guarantee of Luo et al. (2025). Our main technical contribution is a reduction from swap-agnostic learning to a second-order form of multicalibration, obtained via Blackwell approachability with a Bernstein-style variance correction.

</details>


### [101] [Adaptivity via a Parallel Architecture for Stochastic Gradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28902)
*Bin Fu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A parallel framework combines static gradient methods to achieve improved adaptivity by distributing the search for the optimal iteration budget T among p processors, each running a GD with a geometrically sampled T.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static gradient methods with fixed step/post-iteration budgets can be suboptimal in unknown environments. The goal is to enable automatic adaptivity without manual tuning by leveraging parallel exploration of candidate iteration budgets.

Method: Define GD(x0, T) with step size s = S(T) and floor(T) iterations; updates x_{i+1} = x_i - (η/s) g_i. Use p processors to search over T via a geometric sequence. At stage i, processor j uses T_{j,i} = h(j,i); each processor executes GD(x0, T_{j,i}).

Result: The framework provides a mechanism to adaptively select T across processors so that the resulting gradient descent satisfies convergence conditions, effectively improving adaptivity compared to a single fixed-T method.

Conclusion: Parallel assembly of static gradient methods offers a scalable pathway to automatic adaptivity in gradient-based optimization by distributedly testing candidate iteration budgets and selecting those that meet convergence criteria.

Abstract: We develop a parallel framework that assembles static gradient methods to achieve better adaptivity. A static gradient method, denoted by $\mathrm{GD}(x_0,T)$, takes as input an initial point $x_0\in\mathbb{R}^n$ and $T\in \mathbb{R}^+$ specifying the number $\floor{T}$ of iterations. The step size is chosen as $s=S(T)$, where $S(\cdot)$ is a predetermined function of $T$. The method then performs the iterations $ x_{i+1}=x_i-\fracη{s}\cdot g_i,$ where $g_i$ is a stochastic gradient evaluated at $x_i$, and $η$ is a scaling factor. For an integer $p\ge1$, the $p$ processors in the proposed parallel framework search for an appropriate value of $T$ according to a geometric sequence so that the resulting gradient descent satisfies the desired convergence conditions. Each processor executes an infinite sequence of stages indexed by $i=1,2,\ldots$. At stage $i$, processor $j$ is assigned $ T_{j,i}=h(j,i),$ where $h:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N} \rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}$ is a prescribed function. Processor $j$ $(j=0,1,\ldots,p-1)$ executes $\mathrm{GD}(x_0, T_{j,i})$ at stage $i$.

</details>


### [102] [Reflection or Re-Generation? Why LLM Revision Fails Where Human Revision Succeeds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28908)
*Yefan Tao,Gerald Friedland,Madhusudhanan Chandrasekaran,Luyang Kong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HRF introduces a controlled two-pass protocol to compare human vs LLM revision under identical conditions, revealing that LLM reflection suffers two failure modes: near-zero information gain on objective tasks and negative gain on subjective tasks, unlike humans who gain information in both. The failures arise primarily in the revision step, suggesting LLM reflection is better seen as conditioned re-generation rather than true error-driven revision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLM reflection yields genuine error-driven revision comparable to human revision, and to identify where in the process reflection fails (input quality vs revision) across different task types.

Method: A controlled two-pass (self-, peer-, cross-agent) protocol called the Human-LLM Reflection Framework (HRF). An information-theoretic analysis using per-iteration cross-entropy reduction assesses information gain (Delta I) during revision. Experiments compare human revisions to LLM revisions on objective (finite answer spaces) and subjective tasks, with diagnostic analyses including revision conditioned on first-pass correctness and oracle-guided revision against a random-reshuffle baseline.

Result: Two failure modes of LLM reflection were found: (1) objective tasks yield near-zero information gain (Delta I ≈ 0), resembling neutral re-generation rather than effective revision; (2) subjective tasks yield negative information gain (Delta I < 0), moving predictions away from the target. Human revision yields positive information gain in both settings. Cross-agent analysis localizes the defect to the revision step, not input quality, as LLMs degrade even high-quality human responses. Sub-step analyses show variability by task and model, with self-error detection present for objective tasks but weak for subjective ones; oracle-guided recovery sometimes exceeds baseline for some models but not others. The overarching interpretation is structural: without external information, self-conditioned revision cannot reduce target uncertainty, so LLM reflection is better described as conditioned re-generation rather than true error-driven revision.

Conclusion: The core insight is that LLM reflection without external information does not perform genuine error-driven revision; its improvements resemble re-generations conditioned on prior outputs. Effective reflection on complex tasks likely requires external signals or guidance to reduce uncertainty and achieve true revision.

Abstract: Reflection, the ability to revisit and revise prior reasoning, is central to how humans improve their answers. Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly prompted to "reflect," yet whether this resembles human revision remains unclear. We introduce the Human-LLM Reflection Framework (HRF), a controlled two-pass protocol comparing human and LLM revision under identical conditions across self-, peer-, and cross-agent settings. Using an information-theoretic analysis based on per-iteration cross-entropy reduction, we find two failure modes of LLM reflection. On objective tasks with finite answer spaces, reflection yields near-zero information gain (Delta I approx 0), behaving as neutral re-generation indistinguishable from re-sampling. On subjective tasks, it yields significant negative gain (Delta I < 0), moving predictions away from the target. Human revision, by contrast, yields positive gain in both settings. Cross-agent experiments localize the failure to the revision step, not input quality: LLMs degrade even high-quality human responses. Diagnostic analyses (revision conditioned on first-pass correctness, and oracle-guided revision against a random-reshuffle baseline) show that which sub-step dominates varies by task and by model rather than reducing to a single mechanism: self-error detection is present on objective multiple-choice tasks but weak on subjective ones, and recovery under an oracle error signal exceeds the baseline for some models and falls below it for others. The unifying account is structural: without external information, self-conditioned revision cannot reduce uncertainty about the target, so LLM reflection is better understood as conditioned re-generation than as genuine error-driven revision.

</details>


### [103] [Gated Q-learning: Add Off-Policy Bias to Taste](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28916)
*Brett Daley*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gated Q-learning introduces a state-action dependent gating mechanism to interpolate between fully off-policy and unbiased multistep updates, avoiding importance sampling. It preserves contraction guarantees, derives the exact fixed point, and empirically shows longer credit-assignments improve early learning without the high variance from IS-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the long-standing bias-complexity trade-off in off-policy multistep Q-learning (Watkins Q(λ) vs Peng Q(λ)); off-policy estimators struggle due to collapsed importance sampling under greedy targets, leading to either biased fast learning or unbiased but short horizons.

Method: Propose a continuous, state-action-dependent gating mechanism that attenuates eligibility traces rather than using importance sampling. Theoretical analysis proves the expected operator remains a contraction and yields the exact fixed point; empirically evaluate gating on learning speed and stability across horizons.

Result: The framework yields theoretical guarantees (contraction, fixed point) and empirical evidence that intermediate gating enables longer credit-assignment horizons with faster initial learning than the extreme, underlining its practicality as an alternative to importance sampling.

Conclusion: Gated Q-learning provides a simple, adaptable alternative to importance sampling for off-policy Q-learning, enabling customizable multistep horizons and controlled off-policy bias while preserving convergence properties.

Abstract: Multistep credit assignment is critical for sample-efficient reinforcement learning, yet managing off-policy bias in Q-learning remains a fundamental challenge. For 30 years, practitioners have been limited to a binary choice: eliminate the bias at the cost of severely truncated eligibility traces (Watkins' Q($λ$)), or ignore the bias to learn faster while injecting detrimental errors into the value estimates (Peng's Q($λ$)). Modern off-policy estimators fail to resolve this tension, as importance-sampling ratios collapse under Q-learning's greedy target policy. We introduce Gated Q-learning, a novel algorithmic framework that ends this dilemma by smoothly interpolating between the two historical extremes. Rather than relying on importance sampling, our approach employs a continuous, state-action-dependent gating mechanism to selectively attenuate eligibility traces in an exploration-aware manner. We provide a rigorous theoretical foundation for this mechanism, proving that the expected operator remains a contraction mapping and deriving its exact fixed point. Empirical evaluations verify that intermediate gating safely enables longer credit-assignment horizons, yielding faster initial learning than either extreme. Gated Q-learning offers a simple alternative to importance sampling while enabling customization of the effective multistep horizon and the amount of off-policy bias in Q-learning agents.

</details>


### [104] [Learning Optimal Dynamic Matching via Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28925)
*Genta Okada,Shunya Noda,Junpei Komiyama,Akira Matsushita*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Develops a value-based RL framework for dynamic matching on evolving graphs, introducing an event-time reduction and a post-decision residual-graph value representation learned via graph neural networks and TD learning, yielding improved matching policies over baselines in benchmark settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic matching markets face the trade-off between immediate value from matching and preserving participants for potentially better future matches, within evolving networks and exogenous exits.

Method: Infinite-horizon continuous-time model with stochastic arrivals, node-type transitions, edge realizations, and exits. Proves an event-time reduction (act after exogenous events). Derives that the optimal edge-wise Q-function reduces to a single continuation-value on post-decision residual graphs. Uses a graph neural network to approximate values and a forward-greedy matching heuristic guided by TD learning.

Result: The learned policy substantially outperforms immediate and threshold-based rules in a binary-type benchmark and performs comparably or better than baselines in kidney paired donation benchmarks depending on exit predictability and warning reliability, demonstrating state-dependent, adaptive policies.

Conclusion: Residual-graph value learning yields policies that adapt to realized connectivity and exit information, enabling improved dynamic matching decisions beyond simple greedy or threshold strategies.

Abstract: Dynamic matching markets require decisions about whom to match and when: matching now yields value but removes participants who may create better future opportunities. We develop a value-based reinforcement-learning framework for this problem on finite, evolving weighted graphs. We study an infinite-horizon continuous-time model with stochastic arrivals, node-type transitions, edge realizations, and exogenous exits. We prove an event-time reduction: without loss of optimality, the planner acts immediately after each exogenous event and then waits for the next one. We further show that the optimal edge-wise $Q$-function is characterized by a single continuation-value function on post-decision residual graphs, reducing the learned object from state-action values to graph values. Exact action selection still requires combinatorial matching optimization; we approximate the value with a graph neural network, train it by temporal-difference learning, and use it in a forward-greedy matching heuristic. In a binary-type benchmark, the learned policy substantially outperforms immediate and threshold-greedy rules by preserving common nodes for rare arrivals of valuable matches while forming lower-value matches only in thick pools. In a kidney paired donation benchmark, it performs similarly to immediate greedy when exits are unpredictable, recovers the logic of patient matching when warnings are reliable, and outperforms the better of Immediate Greedy and Patient Greedy across intermediate warning probabilities. These results show that residual-graph value learning yields state-dependent dynamic matching policies that adapt to realized connectivity and exit information.

</details>


### [105] [Latent Lie-Poisson Neural Networks (LLPNNs): Discovering the motion of Lie-Poisson systems through observable data and latent dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28939)
*Vakhtang Putkaradze*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLPNNs offer a structure-preserving approach to learn Lie–Poisson dynamics from partial observations by using a latent representation and Noether invariants, enabling long-term prediction for both regular and degenerate Hamiltonian systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable accurate, long-term prediction of Hamiltonian systems under partial observability (unobserved momenta and co-states) and potential degeneracy (no Lagrangian), preserving the Lie–Poisson geometric structure during learning.

Method: 1) Learn either a Hamiltonian decoder on active variables or a pseudo-Lagrangian encoder for latent variables. 2) Build latent trajectories via a universal Noether invariant from Lie–Poisson symmetry reduction. 3) Reconstruct observable and latent dynamics by Lie–Poisson flows augmented with Magnus-based Lie-group updates, ensuring structure preservation.

Result: Demonstrated on three systems (generalized rigid body on SO(3), Kirchhoff underwater vehicle on SE(3), multi-agent optimal-control on SE(2)^N). Shows strong long-term predictive accuracy, robustness to noise, and competitive performance with modest data and lightweight networks.

Conclusion: LLPNNs provide a practical, geometry-consistent framework for learning Lie–Poisson dynamics directly from observable data, applicable to regular and degenerate Hamiltonian systems, with reliable long-term prediction and robustness.

Abstract: Structure-preserving neural networks are essential for the long-term prediction of Hamiltonian systems from data. Many important Hamiltonian systems in mechanics and control admit symmetry reduction to Lie--Poisson systems, including rigid bodies, underwater vehicles, fluids, plasmas, and optimal control problems. A fundamental challenge in learning such systems is that their dynamics evolve in momentum variables that are typically unobservable, while available data consist only of observable quantities such as configurations and velocities. In optimal control applications, the situation is further complicated because the latent variables contain unobservable co-states and the Hamiltonian may be degenerate, preventing the existence of a corresponding Lagrangian and rendering the encoder-decoder approaches inapplicable.
  We introduce Latent Lie--Poisson Neural Networks (LLPNNs), a structure-preserving framework for learning Lie--Poisson dynamics directly from observable data. The proposed approach exploits three geometric ingredients: (i) learning either a Hamiltonian decoder or a pseudo-Lagrangian encoder on the active variables, (ii) constructing latent trajectories through a universal Noether invariant arising from Lie--Poisson symmetry reduction, and (iii) reconstructing observable and latent dynamics through Lie--Poisson flows combined with Magnus-based Lie-group updates. The resulting method preserves the geometric structure and is applicable to both regular and degenerate Hamiltonian systems.
  We demonstrate the method on three examples: a generalized rigid body on SO(3), Kirchhoff's underwater vehicle on SE(3), and an optimal-control problem for interacting vehicles on $SE(2)^N$. Numerical experiments show excellent long-term predictive accuracy, strong robustness to noise, and competitive performance using only modest datasets and lightweight neural-network architectures.

</details>


### [106] [FairDiffuseVQVAE: Sampling-Time Fairness in Tabular Diffusion via Conditional Refinement of Vector-Quantized Latents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28945)
*Nitish Nagesh,Mahdi Bagheri,Amir M. Rahmani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two-stage FairDiffuseVQVAE decouples fidelity from fairness in synthetic tabular data. Stage 1 uses a vector-quantized autoencoder with a row-level discriminator (no fairness terms); Stage 2 applies a DiffuseVAE-style diffusion refiner conditioned on the Stage-1 reconstruction and protected attribute via classifier-free guidance, with fairness enforced by sampling the protected attribute uniformly at inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the trade-off between distributional fidelity and fairness in synthetic tabular data by decoupling the objectives: achieve high fidelity without fairness penalties during training and enforce fairness through sampling at inference.

Method: Stage 1: Vector-quantized VAE with a row-level discriminator (no fairness penalties). Stage 2: DiffuseVAE-style continuous diffusion refiner conditioned on Stage-1 reconstruction and the protected attribute via classifier-free guidance. At inference, sample protected attributes uniformly to enforce demographic parity by construction.

Result: On the Adult, Bank, and COMPAS datasets, FairDiffuseVQVAE achieves Demographic Parity Ratio 0.702 ( +47% over FairTabDDPM) and Equalized Odds Ratio 0.686 ( +100%). It attains the lowest mean pairwise correlation error among published methods (0.034) at the cost of approximately 15 AUC points. Outperforms TabDDPM, TabSyn, and fairness-aware baselines (DECAF, FairTGAN, FairTabDDPM).

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates that decoupling fidelity and fairness via a two-stage diffusion framework with sampling-based fairness can deliver strong fairness gains with controlled utility loss, by making demographic parity a property of the sampling distribution rather than a training objective.

Abstract: Synthetic tabular data is increasingly used in privacy-preserving data sharing, data augmentation, and to mitigate downstream classifier bias. State-of-the-art tabular diffusion models such as TabDDPM and TabSyn achieve excellent distributional fidelity but offer no mechanism for fairness; conversely, fairness-aware tabular generators (DECAF, FairTGAN, FairTabDDPM) impose explicit fairness penalties at training time, yielding modest fairness gains at substantial cost to either sample quality or downstream utility. We introduce FairDiffuseVQVAE, a two-stage architecture that decouples fidelity from fairness: a vector-quantized autoencoder with a row-level discriminator (Stage~1, no fairness terms) is followed by a DiffuseVAE-style continuous diffusion refiner that conditions on both the Stage-1 reconstruction and the protected attribute via classifier-free guidance (Stage~2). Fairness emerges as a property of the sampling distribution -- uniform sampling of the protected attribute at inference time enforces demographic parity by construction, rather than from competing loss terms. On the Adult, Bank and COMPAS datasets, FairDiffuseVQVAE achieves the highest mean Demographic Parity Ratio ($0.702$, $+47\%$ over FairTabDDPM) and Equalized Odds Ratio ($0.686$, $+100\%$). It also attains the lowest mean pair-wise correlation error ($0.034$) of any published method, while explicitly trading $\sim$$15$ AUC points for these fairness gains.

</details>


### [107] [Shapley-Value-Based Feature Attribution for Data Masking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28946)
*Xinxue,Qu,Francis Bilson Darku,Hong Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A fair, feature-level Shapley-value framework for data masking that jointly optimizes disclosure risk and data utility, agnostic to masking and evaluation methods; experiments claim risk reduction with preserved utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address privacy concerns from data sharing by providing a principled, feature-level approach to data masking that transcends dataset-level tradeoffs and is broadly applicable.

Method: Adapts Shapley-value-based feature attribution to privacy, modeling both disclosure risk and data utility; operates at the feature level and is agnostic to masking methods, ML/DL models, and evaluation metrics.

Result: Experimental results indicate the method reduces disclosure risk while preserving data utility.

Conclusion: Proposes a versatile, model-agnostic framework to guide data masking design and policy, potentially improving privacy protection without sacrificing utility across diverse methods and metrics.

Abstract: Despite its many benefits, widespread access to individuals' personal data also causes severe privacy concerns for consumers, companies, and policymakers. This study proposes a novel framework that adapts the Shapley-value-based feature attribution approach to the problem domain of data privacy by capturing the two crucial dimensions of data privacy---disclosure risk and data utility. Our proposed framework takes a holistic view of data masking through a fair feature attribution approach based on Shapley values. Different from the existing literature that mostly focuses on the risk-utility tradeoff at the dataset level, the proposed framework addresses the tradeoff at the feature level. Furthermore, the proposed framework is agnostic to data masking methods, statistical and machine learning methods, and data utility and disclosure risk evaluation metrics. Experimental results show that our proposed method can effectively reduce disclosure risk while preserving data utility.

</details>


### [108] [Overcoming the Weakest-Link Effect in LLM-Driven Program Optimization via Heterogeneous Edit Recombination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28947)
*Jingwen Fu,Zhen Liu,Yuhan Liu,He Zhang,Nanning Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HERO introduces a zeroth-order LLM-based program optimizer that generates diverse, non-overlapping atomic edits and uses evaluator-guided selection to compose coherent improvements, mitigating the weakest-link issue and outperforming gradient-based approaches across multiple domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Guiding program search with textual gradients becomes unreliable as problem/score mappings grow in complexity. Explicit gradients are not strictly necessary; LLM priors can propose atomic edits directly. Zeroth-order search must overcome the weakest-link effect where a single harmful edit negates others.

Method: HERO prompts an LLM to generate diverse, non-overlapping atomic edits for the current program, then systematically selects and composes a subset of edits into coherent improvements using evaluator scores to guide selection.

Result: Across algorithmic problems, strategy games, design of LLM-based agentic systems, and robotic path planning, HERO consistently discovers higher-scoring programs, converges faster, and uses fewer tokens than prior LLM-based optimizers.

Conclusion: Explicit gradients are not essential for effective program optimization; a zeroth-order approach with diverse atomic edits and evaluator-driven composition yields robust improvements across diverse domains.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to solve complex problems by searching over program space, offering a general paradigm for scientific problems that can be naturally represented and solved as programs. Despite recent progress, identifying effective optimization directions for a candidate program remains challenging. By analogy with automatic differentiation, existing methods typically guide the search using a textual ``gradient'': a first-order update direction expressed as textual edits. Such gradients are inferred either from previously evaluated programs or from LLM-generated feedback on the implicit program-score mapping. However, these estimates become increasingly unreliable as the program--score mapping grows more complex, limiting their practical utility. We argue that explicit gradients are not essential for effective program optimization. Leveraging their prior knowledge, LLMs can propose plausible atomic edits directly from the current program, thereby enabling a zeroth-order optimization strategy. However, zeroth-order search suffers from a \textit{weakest-link effect}: when a bundle of edits is accepted or rejected as a whole, a single harmful edit can negate the benefits of all remaining edits. To address this issue, we introduce HERO, a program optimizer that prompts an LLM to generate diverse, non-overlapping atomic edits and then systematically selects and composes them into coherent program improvements using evaluator scores. We evaluate HERO across algorithmic problems, strategy games, the design of LLM-based agentic systems, and robotic path planning. Across these domains, HERO consistently discovers higher-scoring programs and converges substantially faster than prior LLM-based optimizers, while consuming fewer tokens.

</details>


### [109] [Mining Verdict Boundaries for Neural Network Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28954)
*Jiawei Ren,Guanqin Zhang,Zhenya Zhang,Yulei Sui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes monotonicity-aware exponential search for Branch and Bound (BaB) in neural network verification to locate verdict boundaries across subproblem paths, enabling simultaneous splitting of multiple activations and skipping irrelevant nodes; includes an enhanced version using quantitative estimates; empirical evaluation shows efficiency gains vs. state-of-the-art BaB methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BaB-based verification faces inefficiency due to sequential, node-by-node subproblem solving and costly bounds propagation along the tree path. Exploiting monotonicity along paths can accelerate boundary identification and reduce redundant work.

Method: Introduce search strategies that split multiple activation functions (e.g., ReLUs) simultaneously by leveraging path monotonicity, enabling an effective exponential search to find the verdict boundary. An enhanced variant uses quantitative information from solved subproblems to refine the boundary estimate and further prune boundary-unrelated subproblems.

Result: Empirical evaluation on standard benchmarks shows improved efficiency over recent BaB approaches, demonstrating faster boundary localization and reduced solver work by skipping irrelevant subproblems.

Conclusion: Monotonicity-guided, exponential-path search improves BaB efficiency for neural network verification; incorporating quantitative subproblem information further enhances boundary estimation and pruning.

Abstract: Branch and Bound (BaB) aims to achieve complete verification of neural networks by adaptively partitioning the problem and applying off-the-shelf verifiers to subproblems. Its problem-splitting history can be represented as a tree, where each subproblem corresponds to a child node. A key problem of BaB lies in searching for the verdict boundaries across all the paths that divide the verified and unverified subproblems. We observe that the existing BaB approach tackles this problem by solving each expensive subproblem sequentially along the tree path as its depth increases, requiring costly bounds propagation at every visited BaB tree node (i.e., subproblem), which is inefficient. To address this issue, we propose effective search approaches that leverage the monotonicity of each path to efficiently and precisely locate the verdict boundary by simultaneously splitting multiple activation functions (e.g., ReLU), rather than processing them one at a time as in the classical approach. Our approach performs an effective exponential search along each path, allowing us to skip many boundary-unrelated subproblems when identifying the verdict boundary. The enhanced version further improves this process by estimating the boundary's position using quantitative information obtained from subproblem solving. We perform experimental evaluation on commonly-used benchmarks to assess our proposed techniques, and compare them with recent BaB-based approaches.

</details>


### [110] [Efficient LLM Adversarial Training via Low-Rank Defense and Circuit-Guided Surrogates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28959)
*Weiyi He,Yuping Lin,Jiliang Tang,Yue Xing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes computation-efficient latent adversarial training (LAT) for LLMs using defense-side ReFT and attack-side surrogate modeling, achieving 48.1% per-step FLOPs reduction with only 0.0118% trainable parameters, backed by theory and experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adversarial training is highly costly on large models; while LAT reduces cost, it remains expensive at modern scales. The paper seeks additional efficiency from both defense optimization and attack optimization.

Method: Defense-side: apply representation fine-tuning (ReFT) within LAT and analyze potential mismatch between ReFT token selection and attack. Attack-side: during attack generation, extract only relevant circuits from the LLM to build a lightweight surrogate, avoiding full-model forward-backward passes.

Result: Across comparisons to standard LAT with full fine-tuning, the proposed approach reduces per-step adversarial-training FLOPs by 48.1% and uses only 0.0118% trainable parameters, supported by theoretical justifications and numerical evidence.

Conclusion: Computation-efficient LAT is viable by combining ReFT-informed defense optimization and circuit-based surrogates for attacks; the approach offers substantial speedups with minimal parameter updates, though careful alignment of defense and attack components is advised for robustness.

Abstract: Adversarial training is one of the most effective defenses against adversarial attacks, yet the computational cost remains prohibitive at modern scales, especially for large language models (LLMs). While existing mitigation strategies, e.g., latent adversarial training (LAT), have been developed, they still incur a high computational cost. In this work, we comprehensively investigate computation-efficient strategies to speed up LAT from two complementary perspectives: (1) Defense-side optimization: We explore the representation fine-tuning (ReFT) within LAT, and reveal a potential issue if there is a mismatch on which tokens to apply ReFT and the attack. (2) Attack-side optimization: When computing adversarial attacks in each LAT iteration, we extract only the relevant circuits from the LLM to construct a lightweight surrogate model, avoiding the computation in the forward-backward passes through the full model during the attack generation. For both perspectives, we provide theoretical justifications and numerical evidence to illustrate the effectiveness of the proposed strategies. Ultimately, compared to standard LAT with full fine-tuning, our method on average reduces per-step adversarial-training FLOPs by 48.1% while requiring only 0.0118% trainable parameters.

</details>


### [111] [Beyond Feature and Structure Alignment: Learning Transferable Propagation Knowledge for Graph Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28980)
*Yi Wang,Jitao Zhao,Di Jin,Dongxiao He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProGFM introduces a propagation-aware graph foundation model that treats edge–feature dimension propagation relationships as transferable knowledge units via a prototype bank, enabling adaptive, cross-domain information aggregation and improved generalization compared to existing GFMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graphs lack unified transferable representation units and fixed propagation mechanisms; existing GFMs focus on alignment but neglect transferable propagation knowledge across domains, hindering cross-domain generalization.

Method: ProGFM constructs a propagation relationship prototype bank that captures transferable propagation patterns between edges and feature dimensions; these prototypes guide adaptive information aggregation in unseen graph domains, treating propagation relationships as transferable knowledge units.

Result: Extensive cross-domain experiments show that ProGFM achieves superior cross-domain knowledge transfer and generalization compared to existing graph foundation models.

Conclusion: Modeling propagation relationships as transferable units via a prototype bank enables adaptive aggregation and strong cross-domain generalization in GFMs, suggesting a new direction for graph foundation learning.

Abstract: Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) have recently emerged as a promising paradigm for enabling knowledge transfer across diverse domains. Unlike traditional graph learning methods that are typically designed for in-domain settings, GFMs aim to learn transferable knowledge that can generalize to unseen graph domains. However, unlike language or visual data, graphs lack intrinsic and unified representation units, such as tokens in language and patches in vision, making it challenging to identify transferable knowledge units for building graph foundation models. Existing graph foundation models mainly focus on mitigating domain discrepancies through feature alignment and structure alignment, while overlooking the exploration of transferable knowledge units underlying graph data. Moreover, these methods generally rely on fixed propagation mechanisms during message passing, overlooking the heterogeneity in propagation patterns, as different edges may exhibit distinct propagation patterns for different feature dimensions. To address these limitations, we propose a Propagation-aware Graph Foundation Model (ProGFM), which regards the propagation relationships between edges and feature dimensions as transferable knowledge units. Through a propagation relationship prototype bank, ProGFM learns cross-domain transferable propagation knowledge, enabling adaptive information aggregation in unseen graph domains. Extensive experiments across various cross-domain transfer scenarios demonstrate that ProGFM possesses strong cross-domain knowledge transfer capability and exhibits superior generalization performance compared with existing methods.

</details>


### [112] [SILVA Networks as Structured Implicit Layers and Vector Attractors via Dynamic Interaction Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28989)
*Jose Luis Lima de Jesus Silva*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SILVA introduces Structured Implicit Layers and Vector Attractors via Dynamic Interaction Fields to disentangle stimulus, local/global interactions, damping, and readout inside a fixed-point architecture across domains, enabling interpretable, trainable internal dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the opacity of fixed-point implicit layers by isolating distinct influence channels (stimulus, local context, global context, solver dynamics) and diagnose their contributions across tasks.

Method: Propose SILVA framework that partitions a fixed-point update into explicit terms for stimulus, local interactions, global interactions, damping, and readout, and instantiate the template for images, molecules, citation networks, and long-range graphs with domain-specific definitions; perform ablations and visualizations.

Result: Task-dependent roles observed: local interactions drive performance in graph tasks; MNIST shows limited benefit from recurrence at the tested capacity; the clearest global advantage occurs in long-range node-classification benchmarks; the framework yields interpretable, trainable internal dynamics.

Conclusion: SILVA provides a transparent, trainable implicit representation where the contributions of stimulus, local and global interactions, damping, and readout can be analyzed, ablated, visualized, and diagnosed across diverse domains.

Abstract: Many learning problems require representations that reconcile direct input, nearby structure, and broader context. In implicit neural layers, these influences are usually absorbed into a single fixed-point update, making it hard to identify what enters from the stimulus, what propagates locally, what comes from global context, and what is produced by solver dynamics. Here we introduce SILVA Networks, Structured Implicit Layers and Vector Attractors via Dynamic Interaction Fields. SILVA separates stimulus, local interaction, global interaction, damping, and readout inside one fixed-point architecture. The same template is instantiated for images, molecules, citation networks, and long-range graph benchmarks through domain-specific definitions of nodes, neighborhoods, and global summaries. Experiments and ablations show task-dependent roles for these terms: local interactions are load-bearing in the graph tasks, MNIST gains little from recurrence at the tested capacity, and the clearest global benefit appears in a long-range node-classification benchmark. SILVA therefore provides an implicit representation whose internal interaction dynamics can be trained, ablated, visualized, and diagnosed.

</details>


### [113] [Dynamics-aware identification of governing equations from sparse and noisy data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29036)
*Pongpisit Thanasutives,Yoshinobu Kawahara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Koopman-based upsampling with DMD/EDMD/optDMD improves derivative estimation for sparse, noisy data in SINDy/PDE-FIND; ODEs benefit most from polynomial EDMD; PDE results vary by system; generally outperforms non-dynamical interpolation; useful as a dynamics-aware preprocessing step when low-rank observable structure applies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate unreliable derivative estimates from sparse/noisy time-series used in sparse identification of nonlinear dynamics (SINDy) and PDE-FIND by learning a dynamics-aware upsampling/interpolation via Koopman operators.

Method: Train finite-dimensional Koopman models using DMD, EDMD, and optimized DMD on selected observables to interpolate and denoise snapshots within the observed time window. Use the denoised data for derivative estimation and subsequent sparse regression. Benchmark against linear and smoothing-spline interpolants across two ODEs (Lorenz-63, Van der Pol) and three PDEs (Burgers, Fisher-KPP, linear advection-diffusion).

Result: Polynomial EDMD provides the strongest coefficient accuracy for the ODEs. PDE results are system-dependent: low-rank DMD-assisted reconstructions improve discovery for Burgers and advection-diffusion, while the raw baseline can be competitive for Fisher-KPP. Compared with non-dynamical interpolation, Koopman preprocessors generally yield performance gains. DMD-assisted upsampling can stabilize Pareto-based non-oracle selection of support size. Overall, it's best viewed as a dynamics-aware preprocessing step to reduce derivative estimation error when suitable observable representations and low-rank structure exist.

Conclusion: Koopman-based upsampling is a valuable preprocessing approach for sparse identification tasks under noisy data, contingent on the dynamics admitting a suitable low-rank observable embedding; performance gains are most evident for ODEs with polynomial EDMD and depend on the specific PDE and observable choice.

Abstract: Sparse identification of nonlinear dynamics (SINDy) and PDE functional identification (PDE-FIND) recover parsimonious ordinary and partial differential equations (ODEs and PDEs) from data. However, sparse and noisy temporal measurements can make derivative estimates unreliable. To address this problem, we evaluate Koopman-based upsampling techniques implemented with dynamic mode decomposition (DMD), extended DMD (EDMD), and optimized DMD. These methods learn finite-dimensional approximations of Koopman evolution on selected observables and are used to interpolate and denoise snapshots inside the observed time window before derivative estimation and sparse regression. The empirical benchmark comprises two ODE systems, Lorenz-63 and Van der Pol, and three periodic PDE systems, Burgers, Fisher-Kolmogorov-Petrovskii-Piskunov (Fisher-KPP), and linear advection-diffusion, over sparse and noisy sampling regimes. Polynomial EDMD gives the strongest ODE results, especially in coefficient accuracy. The PDE results are system-dependent: low-rank DMD-assisted reconstructions improve Burgers and advection-diffusion discovery, while the raw baseline (without upsampling) remains competitive for the Fisher-KPP data. A comparison against linear and smoothing-spline interpolation techniques shows that the selected Koopman-based preprocessors provide overall performance gains over these non-dynamical alternatives. We also demonstrate that DMD-assisted upsampling can stabilize Pareto-based non-oracle support-size selection. Overall, Koopman-based upsampling is best viewed as a dynamics-aware preprocessing step that can reduce derivative-estimation error when its observable representation and low-rank structure are appropriate for the data.

</details>


### [114] [DFSC: Error-Controlled Differentiable Mittag-Leffler Propagation for Fractional Scientific Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29038)
*Ning Hu,Haitao Duan,Shuqun Li,Chuyang Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DFSC: a PyTorch environment with the Mittag-Leffler Spectral Layer that separates known fractional propagation from learnable corrections, using adaptive truncation/Lanczos acceleration and certified error bounds to enable differentiable, scalable fractional ML.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fractional scientific ML requires differentiable, batched, and efficient operators; repeatedly reconstructing fractional responses is costly when the primary propagation is known. A modular layer that handles the known Mittag-Leffler part and learns only unresolved dynamics can improve efficiency and integration with neural nets.

Method: Introduce the Mittag-Leffler Spectral Layer (MLSL) within DFSC, separating known fractional propagation from data-driven corrections; jointly optimize fractional orders and residual-network parameters. Use an adaptive algorithm to increase truncation depth or Lanczos dimension until a given tolerance is met. In negative-real alternating-series regimes, provide a certified first-omitted-term bound; in other regimes, label estimates as empirical. Support dense/sparse/matrix-free/self-adjoint/generalized/complex operator paths, trainable fractional orders, inverse problems, residual neural composition, and CPU/GPU execution. Reuse a prepared batched Lanczos basis to reduce query time; provide fixed-path values. Include external solver and mixed real-data results to position DFSC as an error-aware primitive rather than a universal solver or neural model.

Result: Certified series bounds cover all 59 eligible reference cases with a median bound/error effectivity of 1.246 for resolved errors. Reusing a batched Lanczos basis yields speedups of 4.61–7.11× on CPU and 13.07–16.22× on an RTX 5070 (excluding one-time prep). A 27-case inverse matrix yields full-rank local curvature; results remain model-conditional. External solver and mixed data corroborate DFSC as an error-aware specialized primitive for matched fractional structure, not a blanket replacement for solvers or neural models.

Conclusion: DFSC provides an error-aware, specialized primitive that leverages known fractional propagation to learn only unresolved dynamics, with adaptive accuracy control and performance gains. It is suited for matched fractional-structure problems and various operator classes, but with caveats: bounds are guaranteed primarily in the negative-real alternating-series regime, there is one-time prep cost, and gains depend on problem and hardware.

Abstract: Fractional scientific machine learning requires numerical operators that can be differentiated, batched, accelerated, and composed with neural networks. When the dominant linear fractional evolution is known through a Mittag-Leffler propagator, repeatedly reconstructing that response with a history solver or relearning it from data is unnecessary. We present DFSC, a PyTorch environment organized around the Mittag-Leffler Spectral Layer (MLSL). The layer separates known fractional propagation from data-driven corrections, so neural modules learn only unresolved dynamics while fractional orders and residual-network parameters are optimized jointly. Its adaptive algorithm increases special-function truncation depth or Lanczos dimension until successive differentiable evaluations satisfy a requested tolerance. In the negative-real alternating-series regime, DFSC additionally returns a certified first-omitted-term bound; outside that regime it explicitly labels estimates as empirical.
  DFSC supports dense, sparse, matrix-free, self-adjoint, generalized, and controlled complex operator paths; trainable fractional orders; direct inverse problems; residual neural composition; and CPU/GPU execution. The certified series bound covers all 59 eligible reference cases, with median bound/error effectivity 1.246 for resolved errors. Reusing a prepared batched Lanczos basis gives identical fixed-path values and reduces repeated-query time by 4.61--7.11 times on CPU and 13.07--16.22 times on an RTX 5070, excluding one-time preparation. A 27-case inverse matrix finds full-rank local curvature throughout, while remaining explicitly model-conditional. External solver and mixed real-data results support DFSC as an error-aware optional primitive for matched fractional structure, rather than a general replacement for fractional solvers or neural models.

</details>


### [115] [Learning Lookahead Lemmas for Neural Network Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29051)
*Liam Davis,Haoze Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lookahead-driven inprocessing framework for neural network verification that derives lemmas over unstable ReLUs, builds an implication graph to prune the search space and enrich boolean cuts, and is instantiated in Marabou and αβ-CROWN to prove up to 34% more instances UNSAT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance the efficiency and effectiveness of branch-and-bound-based neural network verifiers by generating forward-looking lemmas during inprocessing, thereby pruning search and strengthening cuts.

Method: An inprocessing framework driven by lookahead that generates lemmas for unstable ReLU phases, gathers them into an implication graph, and uses this graph to prune the search and vivify boolean cuts. The framework is instantiated in two state-of-the-art verifiers, Marabou and αβ-CROWN.

Result: The approach improves performance in both verifiers, achieving up to 34% more instances proven UNSAT.

Conclusion: A lookahead-based inprocessing framework is a viable and effective enhancement for NN verification, compatible with leading verifiers and capable of substantially increasing unsatisfiability proofs.

Abstract: State-of-the-art neural network verifiers use the branch-and-bound procedure as their core solving mechanism. We introduce an inprocessing framework for neural network verification driven by the lookahead procedure. Under this framework, lookahead derives new lemmas over the phases of unstable ReLUs, which are collected into an implication graph that is used to prune the search space and vivify boolean cuts. We instantiate the framework in two state-of-the-art verifiers, Marabou and $α$-$β$-CROWN, and demonstrate that it improves performance in both, proving up to 34% more instances unsatisfiable.

</details>


### [116] [Who Wins Where? Conformal Model Comparison for Local Superiority](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29053)
*Yi Zhou,Baishi Li,Xuan Yao,Ke-Wei Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Conformalized local model comparison (CLMC): a split-sample framework for calibrated local best-model maps with finite-sample guarantees, uncovering heterogeneous winners and abstaining when uncertain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global model comparison hides regional performance heterogeneity; need local, calibrated decisions with controlled error rates.

Method: Three-disjoint-splits approach to fit competing models, estimate local centers/scales from out-of-sample scores, and conformally calibrate residual uncertainty. At a target point, declare a local winner only if a one-sided conformal bound excludes a tie; the sign of the score selects the favored model.

Result: Finite-sample marginal control for one-sided erroneous declarations on the realized future score; pointwise consistency away from tie boundaries; aggregate comparisons can conflict with the prevalence of local superiority; squared-loss bias–variance decomposition clarifies how model structure affects local wins; synthetic and real-data experiments show recovery of heterogeneous winners, abstention under uncertainty, and higher conditional gain.

Conclusion: The proposed conformalized local framework provides calibrated, locally optimal decisions with strong theoretical guarantees and practical benefits over global model selection, particularly in heterogeneous settings.

Abstract: Standard model comparison is global, aggregating losses across the covariate space to declare a single winner. This can obscure heterogeneous performance, where different models are preferable in different regions. We introduce conformalized local model comparison, a split-sample framework for constructing calibrated local best-model maps. Given a model comparison score, such as the difference between two squared losses, the method uses three disjoint splits to fit competing models, estimate local centers and scales from out-of-sample scores, and conformally calibrate residual uncertainty. At a target point, the procedure declares a local winner only when a one-sided conformal bound excludes a tie, with the score's sign determining the favored model. We prove finite-sample marginal control for one-sided erroneous declarations on the realized future comparison score, establish pointwise consistency of the localized mean-score estimator away from tie boundaries, show that aggregate comparison can disagree sharply with the prevalence of local superiority, and derive a squared-loss bias--variance decomposition that clarifies how model structure affects local wins. Synthetic and real-data experiments show that the method recovers heterogeneous winner regions, abstains under uncertainty, and yields higher conditional gain than global selection.

</details>


### [117] [Autonomous Repair for Multi-Agent Systems via Monte-Carlo Tree Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29055)
*Hanxiao Lu,Tianyi Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MARS uses Monte Carlo Tree Search with diagnosis-guided expansion and partial rollouts to repair multi-agent system trajectories, achieving significant improvements over baselines on a large StateMAS benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated patching of MAS outputs is underexplored; current workflows rely on manual failure attribution and repair, motivating a scalable automated repair mechanism.

Method: Introduce MARS, a search-based repair framework that casts repairs as MCTS with diagnosis-guided exploration and taxonomy-augmented evaluation; uses partial rollouts to reduce token consumption; evaluated on StateMAS, a large-scale MAS repair benchmark of 1,310 failure trajectories across four agent architectures and four LLM backbones.

Result: MARS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods, with absolute improvements from 3.0% to 12.1% across settings, and similar token costs; ablation confirms taxonomy-augmented evaluation and diagnosis-guided expansion as critical components.

Conclusion: MARS is effective for MAS repair; its diagnosis-guided expansion and taxonomy-augmented evaluation drive the gains; StateMAS provides a robust benchmark for evaluating MAS repair across architectures and backbones.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems (MAS) are increasingly deployed to solve complex tasks. In case of incorrect or unsatisfactory outputs, users have to manually locate agent mistakes by inspecting agent trajectories (i.e., {\em failure attribution}) and provide feedback to refine the outputs (i.e., {\em repair}). Despite some recent work in MAS failure attribution, automated mechanisms to recover from such mistakes remain largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we propose MARS, a search-based framework that formulates MAS repair as a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) process and navigates the vast space of potential repairs via diagnosis-guided expansion with taxonomy-augmented evaluation. Unlike standard MCTS, which evaluates a complete simulation via full rollout, MARS evaluates the agent trajectory using partial rollout to reduce token consumption. Furthermore, we introduce StateMAS, a large-scale MAS repair benchmark with 1,310 replayable multi-agent failure trajectories spanning four types of agent architectures and four LLM backbones. Experiments on StateMAS demonstrate that MARS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods, achieving an absolute improvement from 3.0\% to 12.1\% across all settings, while maintaining a comparable token consumption cost. The ablation study further confirms that taxonomy-augmented evaluation and diagnosis-guided expansion are critical to achieving these performance gains.

</details>


### [118] [Benchmarking Frontier Large Language Models Against Official Crash Database Coding Using Police Crash Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29064)
*Sudhir Bharati,Rajendra K C Khatri,Sudip Bharati*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Benchmark of six frontier LLMs for coding crash narratives into official attributes (six attributes). Found GPT-5.5 High worst attribute alignment; always-majority and keyword-rule baselines often outperform LLMs on raw metrics; attribute performance varies more than model differences. Suggest attribute-specific evaluation with baselines and human review.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses whether LLMs can reproduce official crash coding from police narratives; fills gap in labor-intensive manual coding and potential gaps in narrative data supplementation.

Method: Six LLMs evaluated with a identical zero-shot prompt to assign six crash attributes (manner, non-motorist relation, intersection type, work-zone relation, roadway surface condition, light condition) to 5,587 fatal-crash narratives linked to 5,889 records (4,194 matched). Metrics: agreement, macro-F1, Cohen's kappa, coverage, selective agreement; baselines: always-majority, always-Unknown, keyword-rule. Analyses: repeated-measures and GEEs to compare models/attributes.

Result: GPT-5.5 High had highest inter-model agreement; however, always-majority baseline yielded higher raw agreement; keyword-rule baseline achieved macro-F1 and Cohen's kappa similar to best LLM. Agreement was highest for non-motorist relation and crash manner; lowest for light condition, roadway surface condition, and work-zone relation. Attribute differences exceeded model differences.

Conclusion: Provides a benchmark for LLM-based crash coding; deployment should be attribute-specific with transparent baselines and human review; significant attribute-level variation necessitates careful evaluation beyond overall model performance.

Abstract: Police crash narratives contain information that may supplement structured crash databases, but manual review is labor-intensive and it remains unclear how well large language models (LLMs) reproduce official crash coding. This study benchmarked six frontier LLMs by comparing narrative-derived crash attribute codes with corresponding fields in the Arkansas fatal-crash database. The analysis linked 5,587 fatal-crash narratives with 5,889 structured crash records from Arkansas (2015-2025), yielding 4,194 matched crashes. Six LLMs were evaluated using an identical zero-shot prompt to code crash manner, non-motorist relation, intersection type, work-zone relation, roadway surface condition, and light condition. Performance was evaluated using agreement, macro-averaged F1 score, Cohen's kappa, coverage, selective agreement, and comparisons with always-majority, always-Unknown, and keyword-rule baselines. Repeated-measures analyses and a generalized estimating equations model assessed differences among models and attributes. GPT-5.5 High achieved the highest agreement among the evaluated LLMs, but the always-majority baseline produced higher raw agreement and the keyword-rule baseline achieved macro-averaged F1 score and Cohen's kappa comparable to the best-performing LLM. Agreement was highest for non-motorist relation and crash manner and lowest for light condition, roadway surface condition, and work-zone relation. Differences across crash attributes exceeded differences across models. These results provide a benchmark for evaluating LLM-based crash coding and show that deployment should be evaluated on an attribute-specific basis using transparent baselines and human review.

</details>


### [119] [Federated Foundation Models Fine-Tuning with Heterogeneous Compressed Clients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29071)
*Shengkun Zhu,Jinshan Zeng,Zhihua Allen-Zhao,Mayi Xu,Quanqing Xu,Wei Ren,Qiang Yang,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedSLM is a parameter-centric federated fine-tuning framework for heterogeneous compressed clients, using SVD-based decompositions to create self-contained, aggregatable models. It coordinates adapters within compression groups and fuses full-rank reconstructions across groups via structural alignment, plus a weak-to-strong elicitation with confidence loss and bias–variance control. The approach yields memory-efficient client models (~50% of full-model GPU memory) and outperforms baselines on IID and non-IID NLP and vision-language tasks, with theoretical guarantees on adapter aggregation, subspace alignment, and noise mitigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resource asymmetry in federated learning for foundation models: the entities with valuable domain data cannot host billion-parameter models. Existing solutions trade memory, self-containment, or fidelity. A parameter-centric, mixed-compression approach that preserves as much fidelity as possible while enabling aggregation is needed.

Method: 1) SVD-based decomposition to create self-contained client models whose low-rank subspaces form nested manifolds; 2) two-stage protocol: synchronize lightweight adapters within compression groups, and fuse full-rank reconstructions across groups via structural alignment; 3) a weak-to-strong elicitation step with auxiliary confidence loss transferring aggregated knowledge to the full-scale server; 4) explicit bias–variance trade-off to mitigate compression artifacts; 5) theoretical guarantees for adapter-level aggregation, subspace-alignment bounds for cross-group fusion, and analysis of confidence loss against weak-supervision noise.

Result: Empirical evaluation on natural language and vision-language benchmarks shows FedSLM outperforms existing federated baselines under both IID and non-IID partitions; client models operate at roughly 50% of the GPU memory required by the full model.

Conclusion: FedSLM provides a principled, memory-efficient, and aggregation-friendly framework for federated fine-tuning of foundation models under heterogeneous compression, supported by theoretical guarantees and superior empirical performance.

Abstract: Federated learning of foundation models faces a fundamental resource-asymmetry challenge: the institutions holding the most valuable domain-specific data cannot host billion-parameter models. Existing heterogeneous federated approaches attempt to bridge this gap through parameter-efficient tuning, model pruning, or knowledge distillation, yet each trades away a critical property, whether full-model memory reduction, architectural self-containedness, or representational fidelity, leaving the core tension unresolved. We propose FedSLM, a parameter-centric framework for federated fine-tuning with heterogeneous compressed clients. FedSLM uses SVD-based decomposition to produce self-contained client models, whose low-rank subspaces form nested manifolds that are structurally compatible for aggregation. It then applies a two-stage protocol that synchronizes lightweight adapters within compression groups and fuses full-rank reconstructions across groups via structural alignment. Finally, a weak-to-strong elicitation step with auxiliary confidence loss transfers the aggregated knowledge to the full-scale server, while an explicit bias--variance trade-off mitigates compression artifacts. We provide theoretical guarantees for adapter-level aggregation, subspace-alignment bounds for cross-group fusion, and a characterization of how the confidence loss mitigates weak-supervision noise. Experiments on natural language and vision--language benchmarks show that FedSLM outperforms existing federated baselines under both IID and non-IID partitions, while client models operate at roughly 50% of the GPU memory required by the full model.

</details>


### [120] [DASH-OPD: Discrepancy-Aware Switching with Hysteresis for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29078)
*Yuchen Xia,Qianguo Sun,Chao Song,Junlong Wu,Yiyan Qi,Yunjian Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DASH-OPD is a discrepancy-aware, hysteresis-based switching method for on-policy distillation that adaptively switches between student and teacher executors in multi-turn settings to mitigate drift and recovery, showing improved performance and efficiency on ALFWorld; currently a work in progress with code to be released later.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address exposure bias in on-policy distillation (OPD) for multi-turn agents and the limitation of existing curricula which cannot determine when to provide teacher guidance; propose a dynamic, discrepancy-driven switching mechanism to balance learning from the teacher and student rollouts.

Method: Compute mean log-probability ratio between student and teacher executors per turn as a discrepancy metric. Use student-to-teacher ratios on student turns as drift signals and teacher-to-student ratios on teacher turns as recovery signals. Normalize and accumulate across turns into drift and recovery evidence. Switch executors when respective evidence exceeds predefined thresholds, with hysteresis to prevent rapid flipping.

Result: Empirical results on ALFWorld show that DASH-OPD outperforms all baselines in both training efficiency and deployment performance.

Conclusion: Discrepancy-aware, hysteresis-based switching provides a robust mechanism for adaptive, bidirectional OPD; reduces maladaptive drift, improves stability and efficiency; further development and code release expected.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains student models on their own rollouts to reduce exposure bias. However, in multi-turn agent scenarios, early student errors can lead a trajectory away from the teacher's familiar domain. Existing curriculum learning methods regulate how much teacher support is used according to training progress, but cannot determine when it is needed. In light of this, we propose DASH-OPD, Discrepancy-Aware Switching with Hysteresis for OPD, a new agentic OPD method that can switch executors adaptively and bidirectionally. On each turn, DASH-OPD calculates a mean log-probability ratio between the two executors over action tokens as their discrepancy. Student-to-teacher ratios on student turns form drift signals, while teacher-to-student ratios on teacher turns form recovery signals. These signals are normalized and accumulated over multiple turns into drift and recovery evidence. DASH-OPD switches executors when the evidence exceeds its corresponding switching threshold. This multi turn accumulation makes the switching hysteretic, preventing high-frequency switches caused by transient fluctuations. On ALFWorld, DASH-OPD outperforms all the baselines and demonstrates superior training and deployment efficiency. This paper is a work in progress. Code, training logs, and model checkpoints will be released later.

</details>


### [121] [What Is Missing in Surgical Risk Stratification and Outcome Prediction: A Scoping Review of End-to-End Machine Learning Approaches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29090)
*Yizhi Dong,Yuhe Ke,Hairil Rizal Abdullah,Yucheng Xing,Kevan Kai Bing Teo,Ling Huang,Mengling Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A scoping review of ML pipelines for surgical risk stratification using EHR data, identifying widespread methodological gaps that limit generalizability and clinical impact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Postoperative adverse events are a major global burden; ML on EHR data offers potential for early risk identification, but study designs are heterogeneous and often underreport crucial preprocessing and evaluation practices.

Method: Systematic scoping review of 190 studies evaluating ML workflows (data preprocessing, algorithm selection, model evaluation, explainability) in perioperative risk prediction using EHR data.

Result: Findings show predominance of single-center private datasets with limited data modalities and poor reporting on preprocessing, missing data handling, feature selection, and class imbalance. Conventional ML and simple neural networks prevail; deep learning and multimodal approaches are rare. Benchmark datasets and standardized evaluation protocols are scarce, hindering cross-study comparisons. Only ~1/3 incorporated explainability.

Conclusion: Methodological gaps hinder clinically robust postoperative ML tools. The review provides a structured reference to promote rigorous, reproducible, and clinically meaningful ML development for perioperative care.

Abstract: Postoperative adverse events, including mortality and morbidity, remain a major global burden, many of which are preventable through early identification of high-risk patients and targeted perioperative care. Accurate risk stratification is therefore essential. With the growing availability of large-scale electronic health records (EHRs), machine learning (ML) provides a data-driven approach to model complex clinical patterns. However, existing studies vary widely in design, and methodological practices remain fragmented. This scoping review characterizes ML pipelines for surgical risk stratification and outcome prediction using EHR data. We reviewed 190 studies covering the ML workflow, including data preprocessing, algorithm selection, model evaluation, and explainability. Most studies relied on single-center private datasets with limited data modalities, while the scarcity of open-access surgical datasets constrained reproducibility and generalizability. Reporting of key preprocessing steps, including missing data handling, feature selection, and class imbalance, was often incomplete. Conventional ML models and simple neural networks predominated, whereas deep learning and multimodal approaches remained uncommon. Benchmark datasets and standardized evaluation protocols were largely absent, hindering cross-study comparisons. Only about one-third of studies incorporated explainability methods. This review identifies methodological gaps limiting clinically robust postoperative ML tools and provides a structured reference to support more rigorous, reproducible, and clinically meaningful ML development for perioperative care.

</details>


### [122] [PiDDM: Physics-Informed Differentiable Degradation Modeling for Lithium-Ion Battery State-of-Health Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29095)
*Zeping Chen,Ruda Jian,Sachin Sigdel,Guoping Xiong,Jian-Xun Wang,Tengfei Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physics-informed differentiable degradation model (PiDDM) improves lithium-ion battery SOH prediction by embedding Arrhenius-based degradation kinetics into neural training, achieving better accuracy and physical consistency, especially in extrapolation, across a diverse dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-driven models alone can struggle with generalization across cycling protocols and may exhibit nonphysical behavior during long-term extrapolation; embedding physics helps ensure plausible degradation trends.

Method: Develop PiDDM that incorporates empirical Arrhenius degradation kinetics for SEI growth and loss of lithium inventory into the training objective; differentiable degradation model integrated with neural network predictions.

Result: On 55 batteries across six operating protocols, PiDDM achieves lowest mean prediction error and substantially lowers MSE relative to MLP and baseline PINN. In extrapolation (train on 90% life, test on final 10%), PiDDM captures accelerated end-of-life degradation and avoids nonphysical capacity regeneration observed in baselines.

Conclusion: Incorporating degradation physics into NN training yields more accurate and physically consistent SOH predictions, offering a practical approach for battery health monitoring.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of lithium-ion battery state of health (SOH) is essential for reliable energy storage operation. However, purely data-driven models may generalize poorly across cycling protocols and produce physically implausible behavior during long-term extrapolation. We developed a physics-informed differentiable degradation modeling framework (PiDDM) for battery SOH prediction. PiDDM incorporates empirical Arrhenius degradation kinetics associated with solid electrolyte interphase growth and loss of lithium inventory into the training objective, encouraging physically consistent capacity fade under diverse operating conditions. The framework was evaluated using a public dataset of 55 batteries cycled under six operating protocols. PiDDM achieved the lowest average prediction error among the evaluated models and substantially reduced mean squared error relative to a multilayer perceptron and a baseline physics-informed neural network. For extrapolation, the models were trained on the first 90% of each battery's cycle life and evaluated on the unseen final 10%. PiDDM captured accelerated end-of-life degradation while avoiding the nonphysical capacity regeneration produced by the baseline models. These results show that incorporating degradation physics into neural network training improves predictive accuracy and physical consistency, providing a promising approach for practical battery health monitoring.

</details>


### [123] [StraightDP: Geometry-Aware Differential Privacy for Rectified-Flow Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29100)
*Xujun Che,Depeng Xu,Xintao Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A DP training framework (StraightDP) uses end-to-end heterogeneity in rectified-flow interpolation between noise and data: release whitened class-conditional moments early, rely on DP-SGD for the rest, and inject at sampling time. This yields strong utility under tight privacy on MNIST and across a multimodal latent space, outperforming DP-LoRA at similar budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DP training for text-conditioned generative models suffers a sharp utility cliff under strong privacy. The geometry of rectified flows implies that sensitivity is concentrated in early/noise regions and becomes more data-specific near the end, suggesting a hybrid strategy that partially offloads utility-critical information while preserving DP.

Method: Introduce StraightDP: release whitened class-conditional moments once (privacy-budgeted) to be distilled into model weights or injected at sampling; allocate remaining budget to DP-SGD targeting the data end. Add per-token stream-norm constraints to stabilize training. Validate on MNIST and with frozen SD3-medium backbones, including a public latent-space pipeline and DP-LoRA baselines.

Result: With ε=1 on MNIST: released moments alone yield 0.76 accuracy (prototype-like samples) and FID 237; DP-SGD alone yields 0.21 accuracy. A pipeline using these moments in a public latent space achieves 0.81 accuracy with FID 56. Enforcing per-token stream norms improves downstream accuracy in extreme-noise pixel-space regimes, and benefits grow as privacy tightens.
