The moments transfer to frozen SD3-medium, where sampling-time injection beats DP-LoRA at a fraction of the budget.

Conclusion: Leveraging the geometry of rectified flows by partially releasing class-conditional moments and performing DP-SGD on the remainder mitigates the DP utility cliff. Sampling-time injection and norm-constrained backbones further enhance robustness, with demonstrated gains across backbones and privacy regimes and transferability to different model families.

Abstract: Differentially private (DP) training of text-conditioned generative models suffers a utility cliff at strong privacy. We revisit this problem through the geometry of rectified flows: along the straight interpolation between noise and data, the Bayes-optimal velocity is governed to leading order at the noise end by a few class-conditional moments, and increasingly sample-specific structure matters toward the data end. StraightDP exploits this heterogeneity end to end. A small budget share releases whitened class-conditional moments once, to be distilled into the weights or injected at sampling time. The rest is spent by pre-declared DP-SGD toward the data end, beyond the moments' reach. At $\varepsilon=1$ on MNIST, the released moments alone already attain $0.76$ downstream accuracy with prototype-like samples and an FID of $237$, and uniform DP-SGD attains $0.21$. The pipeline built on the release reaches $0.81$ accuracy at FID $56$ in a public latent space. Constraining per-token stream norms of the multimodal backbone leaves the pretraining loss unchanged yet improves downstream accuracy in the extreme-noise pixel-space regime, and its accuracy effect becomes monotonically more favorable as privacy strengthens. The released moments also port to frozen SD3-medium, where sampling-time injection beats DP-LoRA training at a fraction of the budget.

</details>


### [124] [Curriculum Matters: Data-Efficient Relational PFN Pretraining with Synthetic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29120)
*Mohammad Sadeq Abolhasani,Viswanath Ganapathy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Curriculum-guided synthetic data and diverse single-table generators can achieve relational PFN-level performance with far less data and without complex relational generators; data presentation order and pretraining curriculum are critical.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether an alternative synthetic generator (PluRel) can replace the RDB-PFN prior, how data ordering affects PFN performance, and how much relational reasoning can be learned from single-table pretraining before any relational data is used.

Method: Use PluRel as sole synthetic data across experiments. (i) Progressive single-table curriculum widening schema from 7 to 17 columns; measure average ROC-AUC on 23-task tabular benchmark. (ii) Relational curriculum trained from scratch on ~5,500 PluRel databases for RelBench/4DBInfer benchmark. (iii) Evaluate single-table curriculum on relational benchmark without relational adaptation.

Result: (i) 0.703 average ROC-AUC on 23 tasks with ~13,300 synthetic tables (≈45x fewer single-table datasets than prior warm-up); all-at-once training yields 0.541. (ii) 0.638 average ROC-AUC on 19 tasks RelBench/4DBInfer, recovering 88% of RDB-PFN's performance with ~220x less relational data. (iii) 0.631 ROC-AUC on relational benchmark without relational adaptation, nearly matching the relational pipeline.

Conclusion: Curriculum design and synthetic data diversity can be more influential than the choice of relational generator or sheer data scale for pretraining PFNs. Single-table pretraining can confer substantial relational reasoning capabilities, and relational data efficiency can be greatly increased via targeted curricula.

Abstract: Relational Prior-Data Fitted Networks (PFNs) such as RDB-PFN approximate Bayesian inference over multi-table relational databases by pretraining on millions of synthetic tasks. We investigate three intertwined questions about this paradigm. First, can a structurally different synthetic generator PluRel substitute for RDB-PFN's prior? Second, how much does the order in which synthetic data is presented to the PFN affect downstream performance? Third, how much relational reasoning can a PFN acquire from single-table synthetic pretraining alone, before any relational data is introduced? Using PluRel as the sole synthetic data source across all experiments, we find: (i) a progressive single-table curriculum that gradually widens schema complexity from 7 to 17 columns reaches 0.703 average ROC-AUC on the 23-task tabular benchmark using only approximately 13,300 synthetic tables (approximately 45x fewer single-table datasets than RDB-PFN's reported warm-up recipe), while the same data trained all-at-once collapses to 0.541 ROC-AUC; (ii) a relational curriculum trained from scratch on only approximately 5,500 PluRel databases reaches 0.638 average ROC-AUC on the 19-task RelBench/4DBInfer benchmark, recovering 88% of RDB-PFN's reported performance with approximately 220x less relational synthetic data; and (iii) the single-table curriculum model, evaluated directly on the relational benchmark without any relational adaptation, achieves 0.631, nearly matching the dedicated relational pipeline. Together, these findings suggest that curriculum design and synthetic data diversity may matter more for relational PFN pretraining than the specific relational generator or raw synthetic scale alone.

</details>


### [125] [PluRel-to-RDB-PFN: Schema-Guided Synthetic Relational Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29129)
*Mohammad Sadeq Abolhasani,Viswanath Ganapathy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: External synthetic relational data (PluRel) can effectively pretrain a relational foundation model (RDB-PFN) when paired with curriculum design; the best strategy (SCHEMA-GUIDED FIRST) uses only ~33K tasks from ~5.5K databases and achieves 0.6346 ROC-AUC on 19 real tasks at 1024-shot, recovering 87.6% of the original 0.7245 performance, with 64-shot context at 93.8%. Early exposure to real-world schemas is more effective than late adaptation, demonstrating that external synthetic generators can provide valuable pretraining signals for RFMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RFMs need large-scale synthetic relational data for pretraining, but coupling data generation with training is inefficient. This work tests whether an external generator (PluRel) can decouple data synthesis from training and still yield strong performance, exploring curriculum strategies to maximize transfer to real benchmarks.

Method: Convert PluRel-generated databases (including externally constructed binary tasks) into the RDB-PFN training format. Compare three curricula: SCHEMA-GUIDED FIRST (real schemas then synthetic), FULLY SYNTHETIC (synthetic schemas throughout), SCHEMA-GUIDED LAST (synthetic then real schemas). Train with ~5.5k databases (~33k tasks), no single-table warm-up, evaluate on 19 real tasks at 1024- and 64-shot contexts.

Result: Best curriculum (SCHEMA-GUIDED FIRST) achieves 0.6346 average ROC-AUC on 19 real benchmarks at 1024-shot, ~87.6% of the original RDB-PFN performance (0.7245). At 64-shot context, it reaches 0.6116, 93.8% of 0.6517. Overall, 55x fewer tasks were used than the original protocol, demonstrating that external synthetic generators, with proper curriculum design, can provide effective pretraining signals for RFMs.

Conclusion: External synthetic data sources can substitute or complement large-scale, tightly coupled pretraining pipelines for RFMs when paired with curriculum strategies that emphasize real-world schema exposure early in training.

Abstract: Relational Foundation Models (RFMs) require large-scale synthetic relational databases for pretraining, but existing approaches tightly couple data generation with the model training pipeline. We study whether PluRel, a general-purpose synthetic relational database generator, can serve as an external data source for RDB-PFN, a relational in-context learner originally pretrained with a 600K-task single-table warm-up followed by an approximately 1.8M-task adaptation stage.
  We build a conversion pipeline that maps PluRel-generated databases, including externally constructed binary prediction tasks, into the RDB-PFN training format and evaluate three curriculum strategies: SCHEMA-GUIDED FIRST (real-world schema then fully synthetic), FULLY SYNTHETIC (diverse synthetic schemas throughout), and SCHEMA-GUIDED LAST (fully synthetic then real-world schema). Using only approximately 5,500 relational databases (approximately 33K tasks), roughly 55x fewer tasks than the original protocol, and no single-table warm-up, our best curriculum (SCHEMA-GUIDED FIRST) achieves 0.6346 average ROC-AUC across 19 real benchmark tasks at 1024-shot context, recovering 87.6% of the published RDB-PFN performance (0.7245). At 64-shot context, the gap narrows to 93.8% (0.6116 vs. 0.6517). Our results demonstrate that external synthetic generators can provide useful pretraining signals for RFMs when combined with appropriate curriculum design and that exposure to a real-world schema early in training is substantially more effective than late-stage schema adaptation.

</details>


### [126] [HERO: History-Enriched Rollout Training for Long-Horizon Autoregressive Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29135)
*Jiaquan Zhang,Shuxu Chen,Haifan Meng,Yi Lu,Zhihan Lyu,Fan Mo,Wei Dong,Yang Yang,Chaoning Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HERO improves long-horizon neural operator rollouts for time-dependent PDEs by using history-based relative supervision; ranks candidate rollouts via multiple failure metrics against a lagged and perturbed references, uses a margin-based objective to reweight ground-truth gradients; shows improved long-horizon accuracy, stability, and robustness with no inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive neural operators accumulate errors over long horizons; existing rollout training uses only absolute trajectory loss and misses signals about long-horizon failure behaviors; need supervision that reflects optimization history and long-term stability.

Method: Introduce history-enriched rollout training (HERO). Create a pool of candidate rollouts from lagged operator, current model, and perturbed inputs. Rank them by rollout error, spectral discrepancy, energy drift, and error growth. Select the strongest failure trajectory as reference and form a margin-based objective that uses this reference as a fixed baseline to reweight the gradient of the ground-truth rollout, providing relative supervision. The approach is theoretically analyzed. Experiments on nine PDE benchmarks with spectral and attention-based backbones show improved long-horizon accuracy, rollout stability, and OOD robustness without added inference cost.

Result: Consistent improvements in long-horizon accuracy, stable rollout length, and out-of-distribution robustness across nine PDE benchmarks; no inference-time cost; validates that history-enriched relative supervision stabilizes autoregressive neural operator predictions.

Conclusion: History-enriched relative supervision is effective for stabilizing long-horizon autoregressive predictions in neural operators, enabling more reliable surrogates for time-dependent PDEs.

Abstract: Neural operators provide fast surrogates for time-dependent partial differential equations (PDEs) by applying a learned evolution operator recursively to its own predictions, but this autoregressive rollout feeds every prediction error back as input, so local errors accumulate. Existing rollout-training strategies reduce the mismatch between training inputs and self-generated states, yet their supervision still measures only the absolute discrepancy from the ground-truth trajectory. Such supervision is therefore uninformative about whether the operator has overcome the long-horizon failure behaviors it exhibited earlier during optimization. We propose history-enriched rollout training (HERO), which augments conventional absolute trajectory supervision with relative supervision derived from the model's optimization history. HERO ranks detached candidate rollouts from a periodically refreshed lagged operator, the current model, and a perturbed input by rollout error, spectral discrepancy, energy drift, and error growth, and selects the strongest failure trajectory as reference. This reference enters a margin-based objective as a fixed comparison baseline, inducing a bounded, sample-dependent reweighting of the ground-truth rollout gradient rather than an independent gradient direction, which we further analyze theoretically. Experiments on nine PDE benchmarks with spectral and attention-based backbones show that HERO consistently improves long-horizon accuracy, stable rollout length, and out-of-distribution robustness at no inference-time cost. These results indicate that history-enriched relative supervision is effective for stabilizing long-horizon autoregressive prediction.

</details>


### [127] [Implicit Machine Learning Force Fields Accelerate Molecular Dynamics Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29158)
*Johannes Maeß,Leon Werner,J. Thorben Frank,Winfried Ripken,Martin Michajlow,Joshua Futterer,Klaus-Robert Müller,Stefan Chmiela*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Implicit ML force fields (I-MLFFs) use self-consistent fixed-point equations to reuse intermediate representations across MD timesteps, delivering deep-network accuracy with the compute footprint of shallow models, achieving 2–5× reductions in compute and memory across several GNN architectures without losing atomistic detail or timestep.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the computational and memory inefficiency of stacking deep neural networks in ML force fields, while preserving accuracy and atomistic resolution; enable longer trajectories and larger systems within fixed computational budgets; provide architecture-agnostic efficiency gains.

Method: Replace explicit neural-network stacks with self-consistent fixed-point equations that enable warm-starting force evaluations by reusing intermediate representations across timesteps. Validate across three GNN families—invariant, Cartesian tensor, and SO(3)-equivariant spherical-tensor architectures—achieving substantial compute/memory savings without coarse-graining or timestep changes.

Result: Achieves a two- to five-fold reduction in compute and memory footprint across the three GNN classes while retaining full atomistic resolution and the original integration timestep.

Conclusion: This work broadens the scalable frontier of quantum-mechanically faithful molecular simulation, enabling longer trajectories and larger atomistic systems within fixed GPU budgets and fostering new insights in biomolecular and materials research.

Abstract: We introduce implicit machine learning force fields (I-MLFFs), which replace explicit stacks of neural network layers with self-consistent fixed-point equations. In molecular simulations, this formulation enables intermediate representations to be reused across successive timesteps, thereby warm-starting force evaluation. The resulting models effectively combine the computational footprint of a shallow, single-layer MLFF with the representational capacity and accuracy of a deep neural network. Our approach unlocks architecture-agnostic efficiency gains that are inaccessible when force prediction and trajectory integration are considered separately. We demonstrate this across three major classes of graph neural networks: invariant, equivariant Cartesian tensor, and SO(3)-equivariant spherical-tensor architectures. Each yields a two- to five-fold reduction in compute and memory footprint. Crucially, these gains are achieved while retaining full atomistic resolution and the original integration timestep, avoiding spatial or temporal coarse graining. Our contribution therefore advances the scaling frontier of quantum-mechanically faithful molecular simulation, enabling longer trajectories and larger atomistic systems within fixed GPU memory and compute budgets, and thereby opening access to new insights across biomolecular and material systems.

</details>


### [128] [MBDiff: Multi-view Behavior-aware Diffusion Model for Probabilistic Utility Data Imputation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29177)
*Rongchao Xu,Lin Jiang,Dahai Yu,Ximiao Li,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MBDiff introduces a multi-view, behavior-aware diffusion model for probabilistic utility data imputation, leveraging global/local/instance-level user behavior to guide imputation and outperform baselines on electricity and water datasets under block missingness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Utility sensor data often has large missing values due to device failures and transmission issues, harming billing, forecasting, and supply management. Existing imputation methods rely on aggregated data and ignore rich user behavior. Capturing and using multi-faceted user behavior for imputation is challenging due to data incompleteness and indirect correlations.

Method: Proposed two main components: (1) a multi-view User Behavior Extraction module that learns global, local, and instance-level user behavior from long-term, diverse, incomplete data; (2) a behavior-aware conditional diffusion model with a reference selection module and a conditional attentional denoising network to impute data efficiently.

Result: Empirical results on real utility datasets (electricity and water) show MBDiff outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving 7.04% improvement for electricity and 29.1% for water under block missingness imputation.

Conclusion: MBDiff demonstrates that incorporating rich user behavior through a multi-view extraction and a behavior-aware diffusion imputation framework can significantly improve utility data imputation accuracy, with practical applicability demonstrated via collaboration with a major utility provider.

Abstract: Utility data (e.g., electricity, water, and gas consumption), collected by ubiquitous sensors and embedded devices, often contains substantial missing values due to various factors such as device failures and data transmission issues. The data missingness can severely impact utility billing accuracy, hinder demand forecasting, and disrupt efficient utility supply management. As a result, utility data imputation has attracted much interest from both industry and academia. While many studies have attempted to address this issue, most of them rely on aggregated datasets for training, overlooking rich user behavior information, which could provide valuable insights for more accurate imputation. However, learning comprehensive user behavior from long-term, diverse, and incomplete utility data remains a significant challenge. Moreover, leveraging user behavior information to guide imputation is nontrivial due to the indirect nature of the correlations. To address these challenges, we propose MBDiff, a Multi-view Behavior-aware Diffusion Model for Probabilistic Utility Data Imputation. MBDiff incorporates two key technical components: (i) a multi-view User Behavior Extraction module that learns comprehensive user behavior from multiple perspectives, including global, local, and instance-level views; and (ii) a behavior-aware conditional diffusion model consisting of a reference selection module and a conditional attentional denoising network to impute utility data in a computationally efficient manner. We implement and evaluate MBDiff by collaborating with one of the largest municipal utility providers in Florida. Experimental results demonstrate our proposed MBDiff effectively outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, e.g., it improves 7.04% and 29.1% on the electricity and water usage datasets for block missingness imputation, respectively.

</details>


### [129] [SERUM: State Extraction and Refinement for User Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29181)
*Andy J. Phu,James Mooney,Karin de Langis,Khanh Chi Le,Dongyeop Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SERUM is a multi-pass framework that builds interpretable finite-state models from unstructured egocentric screen video using hierarchical VLM annotation, yielding a compact taxonomy and improved predictive models across domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need to derive structured user intent and workflow models from raw, unstructured screen activity, but current methods struggle with hallucination and temporal conflation in single-pass annotation and require manual labeling.

Method: Process screen recordings with a sliding window. Alternate between activity-recognition and intent-inference passes; refine labels using accumulated prior context to reduce hallucination. Merge synonymous states via sentence embeddings and calibrated thresholds to obtain a compact taxonomy. Evaluate by fitting first-order Markov models on action+intent sequences and compare to frequency baselines across 61 egocentric videos in coding, cooking, physical activity, and daily life.

Result: Iterative refinement reaches a stable state vocabulary (schematic equilibrium). Normalized Markov models show lower perplexity and better action prediction than baselines, especially in structured tasks like coding. Human annotators rate final-pass labels as accurate and improved over initial labels.

Conclusion: SERUM is the first system to automatically produce interpretable process models from unstructured egocentric screen video without manual annotation, enabling scalable user modeling; demo, code, and results are publicly available.

Abstract: Agentic assistants capable of proactive, personalized interactions require structured models of user intent and workflow. However, building these models from raw, unstructured screen activity remains an open challenge. We present SERUM, a multi-pass framework that extracts finite-state behavioral models directly from unstructured egocentric video using hierarchical VLM annotation. Processing screen recordings through a sliding window, SERUM alternates between activity-recognition and intent-inference passes, with each pass refining labels using accumulated prior context to reduce hallucination and temporal conflation seen in single-pass annotation. Synonymous states are then merged via sentence embeddings and human-calibrated thresholds into a compact, coherent taxonomy. We evaluate behavioral structure by fitting first-order Markov models over the resulting label sequences (both actions and intents) and measuring predictive accuracy against frequency baselines. Across 61 egocentric videos in four domains (coding, cooking, physical activities, and daily life), we find: (1) iterative label refinement converges to a stable state vocabulary, which we term schematic equilibrium, after several passes; (2) normalized Markov models achieve substantially lower perplexity and higher action predictions than frequency baselines, with the largest gains on structured tasks like coding; and (3) human annotators rate final-pass labels as accurate and meaningfully improved over first-pass labels. To our knowledge, SERUM is the first system to produce interpretable process models from unstructured egocentric screen video without manual annotation, opening a scalable pathway for user modeling and behavioral understanding in the wild. Our demo, code, and results are publicly available

</details>


### [130] [SAF-OPD: Stable Advantage Fusion for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29209)
*Yifan Ding,Xincheng Wei,Yoshua Y. Li,Ziheng Li,Yuquan Lu,Siyu Zhang,Dongsheng Ma,Rongxiang Weng,Xunliang Cai,Yun Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAF provides a stable fusion of RLVR and OPD by addressing magnitude and temporal miscalibrations. It introduces a four-stage sparsify-compress and warm-up-anneal pipeline that operates on the OPD advantage, enabling robust gains over fixed-coefficient fusion across multiple models and benchmarks with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR and OPD are complementary but fixed-coefficient fusion causes entropy collapse due to token-level magnitude spikes and persistent teacher guidance. A targeted, lightweight fusion strategy is needed to preserve exploration and signal.

Method: Apply SAF: a four-stage pipeline (two modules: sparsify-then-compress for magnitude control; warm-up-then-anneal for temporal control) to the OPD advantage; each stage is independently switchable and incurs negligible overhead. Use GRPO to instantiate RLVR with OPD and evaluate on seven math and code-generation benchmarks across Qwen 3-1.7B/4B/8B.

Result: SAF prevents entropy collapse, outperforms fixed-coefficient GRPO+OPD fusion; aggregate score gains 0.51–2.70% across six model-domain settings; demonstrates more stable training.

Conclusion: SAF provides a lightweight, plug-in solution for stable advantage fusion that resolves magnitude and temporal miscalibrations, enabling consistent gains over prior fusion approaches across diverse tasks and models.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) broadcasts a single response-level reward to every token, while on-policy distillation (OPD) scores each token against a stronger teacher for a dense advantage but caps performance at teacher quality and discourages exploration beyond it. Their complementarity makes combining RLVR and OPD promising, but we find that fusing the two advantages with a fixed coefficient triggers entropy collapse from two miscalibrations: a magnitude mismatch, where token-level OPD advantages can spike far beyond the bounded RLVR advantage and erase its signal, and a temporal mismatch, where sustained full-strength OPD keeps pulling the student toward the teacher and limits exploration needed to surpass it. We propose SAF, a Stable Advantage Fusion framework that resolves both issues via a lightweight, four-stage pipeline applied only to the OPD advantage: a sparsify-then-compress mechanism for magnitude control paired with a warm-up-then-anneal mechanism for temporal control, with each stage independently switchable and adding negligible overhead. Instantiating RLVR with GRPO, we evaluate SAF across seven mathematical reasoning and code generation benchmarks with Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B: SAF avoids entropy collapse and consistently outperforms fixed-coefficient GRPO+OPD fusion, improving the aggregate score by 0.51-2.70% across all six model-domain settings while achieving more stable training.

</details>


### [131] [Frugal Bayesian Optimization: Scalable Surrogates for Data- and Resource-Limited Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29225)
*Panagiotis Krokidas,Christoforos Rekatsinas,Vassilis Sioros,Grigorios M. Chatziathanasiou,Efi-Maria Papia,George Giannakopoulos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A compute-aware benchmarking of Bayesian Optimization reveals Gaussian Processes are often the most costly without giving the best optimization; scalable surrogates perform as well or better at lower cost, leading to FruBO—a surrogate-recommendation framework and compute-aware baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BO research emphasizes optimization performance but rarely accounts for compute cost; a compute-aware evaluation across diverse domains is needed to guide surrogate choice under budget constraints.

Method: Benchmark four surrogates (Gaussian Processes, Random Forests, NGBoost, Bayesian Adaptive Spline Surfaces) over eight benchmarks and nine real-world datasets spanning materials science, mechanics, robotics, chemistry, and ML; measure optimization quality and computational cost; develop a surrogate-recommendation framework to predict the best surrogate from inexpensive dataset characteristics; propose FruBO as a reproducible compute-aware baseline.

Result: Gaussian Process-based BO shows the highest time and memory overhead and does not deliver superior optimization or sample efficiency; scalable alternatives achieve equal or better performance at a fraction of the computational cost; a surrogate-recommendation framework can predict the most suitable surrogate, enabling FruBO as a practical compute-aware baseline.

Conclusion: For compute-constrained BO, adopt scalable surrogates over GPs; use the surrogate-recommendation framework to select surrogates under budget; FruBO provides a reproducible baseline and practical guidance for surrogate choice under limited compute budgets.

Abstract: Bayesian Optimization (BO) is widely adopted for data-efficient optimization in scientific and engineering applications, yet its computational cost is rarely evaluated alongside optimization performance. Here we present a systematic, compute-aware study of BO that evaluates surrogate models along two axes: optimization quality and computational frugality. Across eight benchmark functions and nine real-world datasets spanning materials science, mechanics, robotics, chemistry, and machine learning, we benchmark four surrogate models: Gaussian Processes, Random Forests, NGBoost, and Bayesian Adaptive Spline Surfaces. We show that Gaussian Process-based BO consistently incurs the highest time and memory overhead without delivering superior optimization or sample efficiency. In contrast, scalable alternatives achieve equal or better performance at a fraction of the computational cost. Motivated by these findings, we introduce a surrogate-recommendation framework that predicts the most suitable BO surrogate from inexpensive dataset characteristics. Together, these results establish FruBO as a reproducible, compute-aware baseline for Bayesian Optimization and provide practical guidance for surrogate selection under limited computational and experimental budgets.

</details>


### [132] [UniPolymer: A Unified Framework for Property Prediction, Structure Recommendation, and Evaluation in Polyimide Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29256)
*Junquan Hu,Zhihui Wang,Peng Xu,Xinru Guo,Xintong Li,Kun Lu,Ben Fei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UniPolymer is a unified framework combining property prediction, target-conditioned generation, candidate evaluation, and structure recommendation for polyimide Tg design, leveraging self-supervised chemical semantic learning, multi-scale information fusion, and a continuous-discrete Tg representation to guide SELFIES-based generation; it achieves R^2=0.93 for Tg prediction and a 73.79% candidate-evaluation pass rate, with Tg predictions aligning with MD simulations to reduce costly experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing polyimide structures with specific Tg is challenging due to mismatch between generated structures and target Tg in existing target-conditioned generation methods; a consistent, end-to-end framework is needed to reduce invalid experiments and shorten development cycles.

Method: 1) Build a structure–property mapping via self-supervised chemical semantic learning, structural-consistency enhancement, and multi-scale information fusion. 2) Use a continuous–discrete joint Tg representation to guide autoregressive SELFIES generation. 3) Evaluate generated candidates with a frozen Tg predictor and polyimide-specific structural constraints, then rank by deviation from target Tg to filter for validation.

Result: UniPolymer achieves Tg-prediction R^2=0.93 and a candidate-evaluation pass rate of 73.79%, both outperforming the best baseline by ~2% and ~1.21% respectively. Predicted Tg values for recommended candidates agree with molecular dynamics simulations, reducing the number of high-cost experiments.

Conclusion: A unified, consistency-focused design pipeline that aligns generated polyimide structures with target Tg, improving design quality and reducing experimental burden in Tg-targeted material discovery.

Abstract: Designing polyimide structures with specific glass transition temperatures (Tg) is highly challenging. Existing methods primarily focus on target-conditioned generation, lacking an assessment of the consistency between the generated structure and the target properties. This leads to low-quality candidates deviating from the design objective entering subsequent processes, increasing invalid experiments and prolonging the development cycle. To address this issue, we propose UniPolymer, a unified framework for property prediction, target-conditioned generation, candidate evaluation, and structure recommendation in polyimide design and a dataset containing 10066 deduplicated polyimide repeating units with Tg tags (PITg-Curated) was constructed. To improve the consistency between generated candidate structures and the target Tg, UniPolymer first establishes a reliable structure-property relationship mapping through self-supervised chemical semantic learning, structural consistency enhancement, and multi-scale information fusion. Subsequently, the model employs a continuous-discrete joint Tg representation to guide the autoregressive generation of SELFIES. The generated candidate structures are further evaluated using a frozen property predictor and polyimide-specific structural constraints, and ranked according to their deviation from the target Tg, thereby preventing structures deviating from the target from entering the subsequent validation stage. Experimental results show that UniPolymer achieved a property prediction accuracy of R^2=0.93 and a candidate structure evaluation pass rate of 73.79%, which are 2% and 1.21% higher than the best baseline, respectively. Meanwhile, the predicted Tg values of the recommended candidates are in high agreement with the results of molecular dynamics simulations, thereby reducing the number of candidates that enter the high-cost experimental stage.

</details>


### [133] [Assessing the Generalization of Graph Neural Networks for Fault Location Across Increasing Distributed Energy Resource Penetration Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29293)
*Burak Karabulut,Olayiwola Arowolo,Carlo Manna,Chris Develder,Jochen L. Cremer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STGATv2-based fault location for distribution networks with DERs outperforms GRU, GATv2, and traditional baselines; robust to DER penetration shifts and measurement noise, due to spatio-temporal topology awareness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DER penetration and bidirectional flows alter fault signatures, creating need for models that capture spatial-temporal dependencies to improve fault location accuracy and robustness.

Method: Benchmark STGATv2 against GRU, GATv2, and traditional ML baselines on a reconfigured IEEE 123-bus feeder with multiple DER injections; evaluate at DER penetration levels 10, 25, 50%; test under realistic measurement noise.

Result: STGATv2 achieves 92-94% macro F1 in-distribution; generalization across penetration is asymmetric: training at 50% generalizes well to lower levels; training at 10% fails at higher levels, though STGATv2 retains 81-84% F1; under noise, STGATv2 >85% F1 while GRU drops to 33.5%; STGATv2's topological awareness is key for robustness.

Conclusion: STGATv2 provides robust fault location under rising DER penetration; spatio-temporal graphs outperform temporal/spatial baselines; design should consider DER penetration distributions and noise resilience.

Abstract: Accurate fault location is critical for distribution network reliability. However, increasing distributed energy resource (DER) penetration complicates fault location due to intermittent generation and bidirectional power flows that reshape fault signatures. Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks (STGNNs) have shown promise by jointly modeling spatial and temporal dependencies, but their behavior under increasing DER penetration has not been studied rigorously. In this paper, we (i) systematically benchmark spatio-temporal graph attention network (STGATv2) against purely temporal (gated recurrent unit, GRU), purely spatial (GATv2) and traditional machine learning baselines, and (ii) evaluate how well models generalize across increasing DER penetration levels (10%, 25%, 50%) on a reconfigured IEEE 123-bus feeder with multiple DER injection points and moderate-to-high impedance faults. Results show that STGATv2 consistently outperforms neural baselines, achieving 92-94% macro F1 in-distribution. Notably, generalization across penetration levels is asymmetric: training at 50% penetration retains near in-distribution F1 score at lower levels, whereas training at 10% degrades considerably at 50% - with STGATv2 retaining 81-84% F1 under these drastic shifts, substantially higher than GATv2 and GRU which drop to 69-74% F1 and 73-75% F1 respectively. Under realistic measurement noise, STGATv2 maintains > 85% F1, while GRU drops as low as 33.5% F1, highlighting the critical role of topological awareness for robust fault location in active distribution networks.

</details>


### [134] [Sample Efficient Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning via Best Policy Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29294)
*Anders Jonsson,Emilie Kaufmann,Gianmarco Tedeschi,Lorenzo Steccanella*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HBPI-UCRL is a model-based HRL algorithm that learns high- and low-level policies in parallel; under two low-level dynamics conditions, achieves polynomial sample complexity; for sparse-reward goal-directed tasks, its complexity bound improves over non-hierarchical methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enhance sample efficiency in hierarchical RL by parallel learning and exploiting the correspondence between high-level transitions and multi-step low-level transitions; provide theoretical guarantees for learnability.

Method: HBPI-UCRL uses a model-based, optimistic planning (UCRL-style) framework to learn high- and low-level policies in parallel. It introduces two sufficient conditions on low-level dynamics that ensure the high-level multi-step transition correctly captures behavior across levels, enabling efficient, joint learning and analysis of sample complexity.

Result: Under the stated conditions, HBPI-UCRL achieves polynomial sample complexity in problem parameters. In sparse-reward, goal-directed settings, its upper bound on sample complexity is strictly lower than that of a non-hierarchical counterpart, offering theoretical justification for HRL’s empirical success.

Conclusion: The framework provides not only a practical HRL algorithm but also a rigorous theoretical basis for when parallel HRL is learnable and how hierarchy reduces sample complexity in challenging sparse-reward tasks.

Abstract: We present HBPI-UCRL, a model-based algorithm for hierarchical reinforcement learning (HRL) that learns high-level and low-level policies in parallel. HBPI-UCRL exploits the fact that a high-level transition corresponds to a multi-step transition at the low level. We introduce two conditions on the low-level dynamics that are sufficient to make parallel HRL learnable. When these conditions hold, we prove that HBPI-UCRL has a polynomial sample complexity in the problem parameters. In the sparse-reward, goal-directed setting, our sample complexity upper bound for HBPI-UCRL is strictly lower than that of its non-hierarchical counterpart, providing theoretical justification for the empirical success of HRL.

</details>


### [135] [Analysing User Reviews to Identify User Concerns Around Permissions in AI Apps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29343)
*Babar Shah,Faheem Ullah,Myles Watkinson,Muhammad Moiz Khalid,Tehmina Karamat Khan,Muhammad Junaid*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A ML model classifies permission-related reviews in AI-enabled mobile apps by leveraging AI-generated security reviews to label training data from a large pool of human-written reviews, achieving 82% accuracy; users' concerns align with sentiment toward the app rather than specific permissions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI features proliferate in mobile apps, users rely on reviews to gauge privacy and permission practices; manual annotation for training classifiers is costly; synthetic labeling offers scalable supervision.

Method: Train a classifier on a mixed dataset where AI-generated security/permission reviews are used to identify relevant training instances from a large corpus of human reviews; target permission-related categories; evaluate on held-out reviews achieving 82% accuracy.

Result: The classifier achieved 82% accuracy in labeling permission-related reviews; analysis shows user concerns cluster by sentiment toward the requesting app rather than by individual permission types.

Conclusion: The approach reduces annotation effort, supports better privacy-preserving insights for users and platform operators, and highlights the importance of sentiment-aware analysis in permission reviews.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence is increasingly embedded in everyday software, making its integration into mobile apps inevitable. However, AI mobile app developers are not always versed in security and privacy best practices, leaving users to monitor their own security and understand how apps use their data. App reviews capture real user experiences, helping others make informed decisions before downloading. This paper presents a machine learning model for classifying AI app reviews into permission-related categories. Because user reviews are unstructured, assembling a conventional labeled training set is difficult. To address this, AI-generated security and permission reviews are used to identify relevant training examples from a large corpus of human-written reviews, eliminating the need for manual annotation. The proposed approach classified permission reviews with an accuracy of 82%. Analysis shows that users organise their concerns by sentiment toward the requesting app rather than specific permission types, with implications for users, developers, and platform administrators.

</details>


### [136] [Versatile On-device Adaptation at the Edge by Unifying Few-shot, Zero-shot, Continual, and In-context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29353)
*Douwe den Blanken,Martin Lefebvre,Charlotte Frenkel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Embedder-centric learning (ECL) unifies four online on-device learning paradigms (FSL, continual learning, zero-shot learning, and in-context learning) and demonstrates silicon-based implementations across four edge use cases, achieving state-of-the-art/FIRST hardware baselines for several tasks and enabling edge adaptation without cloud.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge devices need on-device personalization with low energy, latency, and privacy costs. Existing systems are siloed to specific learning scenarios or rely on cloud retraining, limiting real-time adaptation.

Method: Proposes ECL framework that integrates four online learning paradigms. Validates on hardware (silicon) across four representative use cases covering FSL, CL, ZSL, and ICL. Demonstrates power-efficient operation from micro- to milli-watts and provides hardware demonstrations of ZSL and ICL as well as FSL and CL baselines.

Result: State-of-the-art FSL results for Omniglot (96.8% 5-way 1-shot; 83.3% 32-way 1-shot). First hardware baseline for CL in keyword spotting (71.8% for 200-way 5-shot). First hardware demonstrations of ZSL (60.6% for 5-way spoken sentence classification) and ICL (46.2% at 500th token) on edge with very low power budgets.

Conclusion: Unifying multiple learning paradigms enables versatile, privacy-preserving, on-device adaptation for edge applications, reducing reliance on cloud and enabling real-time personalization.

Abstract: With the ever-increasing pervasiveness of smart edge devices, the demand is growing for applications that can be tailored to users (e.g., custom keyword spotting) or patients (e.g., adaptive health monitoring). Yet, most edge devices rely on fixed inference algorithms and thus cannot learn on-device to personalize predictions. When they can, devices typically support only a specific learning scenario, such as few-shot learning (FSL): going beyond this requires resorting either to another specialized device or to cloud-based retraining, which implies significant energy and latency overheads, a lack of real-time capabilities, and privacy concerns. In this work, we introduce embedder-centric learning (ECL), a framework that unifies four different online learning scenarios: FSL for on-the-fly customization, continual learning (CL) for knowledge accumulation, zero-shot learning (ZSL) for leveraging semantic data, and in-context learning (ICL) for adapting beyond classification. We demonstrate in silicon that ECL can be deployed on resource-constrained devices across four real-world use cases representative of the aforementioned learning scenarios. Our approach establishes a new state-of-the-art performance for FSL character recognition (Omniglot: 96.8% for 5-way 1-shot, 83.3% for 32-way 1-shot), and the first hardware baseline for CL in keyword spotting (NeuroBench keyword FSCIL: 71.8% for 200-way 5-shot). Moreover, we present the first hardware demonstrations of ZSL with semantic data (60.6% for 5-way spoken sentence classification) and ICL (46.2% at the 500th token of RegBench) operating at micro-to-milliwatt power budgets. Therefore, by unifying multiple learning scenarios, we pave the way for smart and versatile devices that can adapt right at the edge, without reliance on the cloud.

</details>


### [137] [Cross-Resolution Semantic Learning for Graph Domain Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29365)
*Yingxu Wang,Haoze Huang,Zhongkai Zheng,Shangsong Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CReSL introduces cross-resolution semantic learning for graph domain adaptation, learning multi-resolution representations with resolution embeddings, a resolution-indexed expert, cross-resolution prototype transport, and target grafting to align source and target across propagation resolutions under semantic resolution shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GDA literature often aligns representations or regularizes structure but neglects how class-discriminative evidence is routed across different propagation resolutions. Semantic resolution shift—cross-domain changes in the resolutions at which class-discriminative signals are strongest—can cause suboptimal fixed-resolution pairing and increase negative transfer.

Method: Builds a multi-resolution representation bank via a shared Graph Neural Network with learnable resolution embeddings and a resolution-indexed expert per source resolution. Introduces Cross-Resolution Prototype Transport to form class-resolution prototypes from source labels and soft target posteriors, converting prototype discrepancies into expert-specific routing across target resolutions. Adds Cross-Resolution Target Grafting to construct posterior-weighted target-to-source prototype displacements and enforce correspondence-weighted prediction consistency for instance-level adaptation under class uncertainty.

Result: Experiments on graph benchmarks under diverse domain shifts show CReSL outperforms strong baselines across most settings.

Conclusion: CReSL provides a new mechanism to handle semantic resolution shift in graph domain adaptation, effectively aligning class structure across resolutions and reducing negative transfer; the approach demonstrates robustness across diverse domain shifts and suggests potential applicability to other cross-resolution transfer problems.

Abstract: Graph Domain Adaptation (GDA) transfers predictive knowledge from labeled source graphs to unlabeled target graphs under distribution shift. Existing methods align representations or regularize graph structures, but do not explicitly model how class-discriminative knowledge learned at different source neighborhood ranges should be routed across target ranges. We call the neighborhood range encoded by a graph representation its propagation resolution and define semantic resolution shift as a cross-domain change in the propagation resolutions at which class-discriminative evidence is strongest. Such shifts can make fixed same-resolution pairing suboptimal and increase the risk of negative transfer. To address this issue, we propose Cross-Resolution Semantic Learning (CReSL), a GDA method that learns soft sourceto-target resolution correspondence from cross-domain class structure. First, CReSL constructs a multi-resolution representation bank using a shared Graph Neural Network and learnable resolution embeddings, with a resolution-indexed expert for each source resolution. Second, CReSL introduces Cross-Resolution Prototype Transport, which constructs class-resolution prototypes from source labels and soft target posteriors and converts cross-domain prototype discrepancies into expert-specific routing over target resolutions. Third, CReSL introduces Cross-Resolution Target Grafting, which constructs posterior-weighted target-to-source prototype displacements and enforces correspondence-weighted prediction consistency for instance-level adaptation under class uncertainty. Extensive experiments on graph benchmarks under diverse domain shifts show that CReSL outperforms strong representative baselines across most settings.

</details>


### [138] [Exploring Block Anomaly Detection In HDFS Log Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29383)
*WenYang Zhong,Tutut Herawan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A streaming workflow for HDFS log block anomaly detection using a LLM–BiLSTM hybrid model with a Kafka-based real-time pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HDFS logs are abundant, noisy, and semi-structured. Manual inspection is impractical; an automated, scalable solution is needed to detect anomalies in real time to quickly locate and fix failures.

Method: Proposes a streaming anomaly-detection workflow: parallel processing of historical logs; a LLM–BiLSTM hybrid deep learning model for anomaly detection; a Kafka-based streaming pipeline for real-time detection.

Result: Abstract presents the workflow and model approach but does not report empirical results or evaluations.

Conclusion: An end-to-end real-time HDFS log anomaly detection solution is proposed, combining NLP/LLM features with a sequence model in a streaming architecture; further empirical validation is needed.

Abstract: In recent years, with the development of big data technology, increasingly more companies use HDFS for data processing and storage. As a result, the maintenance of distributed file systems has become an extremely important part of data management. As the function of server systems is becoming increasingly diversified and their services are becoming complex, the logs, recording real-time events make it easier for system operators to locate the failures and errors that happened in the server systems to make server always available. HDFS, a distributed file system, which contains large data sets, will record a large number of logs. Moreover, the logs are not always structured data, they are not stable as well. However, to detect the problems that occur in the system by checking one log by one log, it's complicated and boring work for the system operators. Using machine learning techniques and natural language processing techniques to detect the HDFS block anomaly will help the system operators to locate and fix the anomaly rapidly and accurately. This paper proposes a streaming HDFS log block anomaly workflow. It helps maintenance practitioners to use parallel computing network in processing historical log, and construct LLM-BiLSTM hybrid deep learning model to detect anomaly block in HDFS, then build streaming log pipeline based on Kafka to give one real-time HDFS log block anomaly detection solution.

</details>


### [139] [Simulation Code Generation for Fluid Systems using Large Language Models: Benchmarking Models and Prompting Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29389)
*Jan Marius Stürmer,Jascha Knack,Tobias Koch,Andreas Weinmann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs can translate a neutral graph model into executable code for WNTR and Modelica across 10 models and 6 prompts; syntactic quality is generally acceptable but substantial gaps in simulation fidelity remain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess feasibility of LLM-driven code synthesis for fluid-system modeling in two simulation environments and provide guidance for integrating such approaches into model-based design pipelines.

Method: Encode fluid system graphs; prompt 10 state-of-the-art LLMs with 6 prompting strategies varying contextual information; generate code for WNTR/Modelica; evaluate code with software-quality metrics; validate by reproducing benchmark scenarios.

Result: Best configurations yield acceptable syntactic quality; notable gaps in simulation fidelity persist; the study offers practical guidance but reveals limitations.

Conclusion: LLM-driven code synthesis can assist but cannot yet replace robust verification; requires careful prompting and validation; suitable for integration into MBSE workflows with explicit fidelity checks.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated a strong ability to generate syntactically correct code from natural-language specifications. In this study, we explore how LLMs can be harnessed to automatically translate a neutral graph representation of fluid system models into executable code for two widely adopted simulation environments: the Python library WNTR and the Modelica Standard Library. We conduct a systematic comparison of ten state-of-the-art LLMs and six prompting strategies that differ in the contextual information supplied (e.g., code or documentation). For each configuration we assess the generated code using a suite of software-quality metrics and we validate the functional fidelity of the resulting simulation models by reproducing benchmark fluid system scenarios. Our findings offer concrete guidance for researchers and engineers seeking to integrate LLM-driven code synthesis into model-based design pipelines. While the best-performing configurations achieve acceptable syntactic quality, we observe substantial gaps remain in simulation fidelity.

</details>


### [140] [OnlineCache: Learning Dynamic Caching Policies with Error Correction for Efficient Diffusion Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29398)
*Zhikang Xie,Xichen Ye,Yifan Wu,Haoshen Yu,Li chenan,Peizhu Gong,Weizhong Zhang,Cheng Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OnlineCache enables dynamic, per-sample and per-timestep caching in diffusion-model inference via a policy-gradient-learned caching decision and a learnable corrector, optimized in a bilevel framework to balance speed and fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static, sample-agnostic caching policies waste computation by not accounting for varying generation difficulty across prompts and changing error sensitivity across timesteps; adaptive, learned caching can allocate resources where needed while preserving quality.

Method: Joint optimization of (1) a lightweight policy network that decides when to cache based on sample and timestep (trained with policy gradient for speed-quality trade-offs) and (2) a learnable corrector to mitigate caching-induced errors. These modules are trained in a bilevel framework: the outer objective optimizes global generation quality, while the inner/local objective minimizes per-step errors. Inference proceeds online, with resource allocation across samples and timesteps.

Result: Empirical results show substantial acceleration with preserved fidelity: on the FLUX.1-dev model, ~3x speedup with maintained quality; on DiT and CogVideoX, competitive acceleration without quality loss; across scenarios, outperforming existing cache-based baselines.

Conclusion: Dynamic OnlineCache allocates computational resources adaptively across prompts and timesteps, enabling faster diffusion-based generation while maintaining quality and surpassing static caching approaches.

Abstract: Diffusion models have revolutionized generative tasks but incur high latency due to iterative denoising. While cache-based strategies accelerate inference by reusing intermediate features, they largely rely on static, sample-agnostic schedules. We argue that this rigidity overlooks two facts empirically validated in this paper: (i) generation difficulty varies across prompts, requiring adaptive resource allocation--complex inputs demand more computation while simpler ones require less; (ii) error sensitivity fluctuates across timesteps, where static policies may cache high-error steps or waste computation on low-error ones. We therefore propose OnlineCache, a dynamic caching framework that jointly learns when to cache and how to correct approximation errors. We leverage policy gradient to train a lightweight network for adaptive speed-quality trade-offs, and incorporate a learnable corrector to mitigate caching-induced errors. Both modules are jointly optimized under a bilevel optimization framework, with the policy targeting global generation quality and the corrector minimizing local errors. Our method automatically allocates computational resources across both samples and timesteps, improving overall generation quality. Extensive experiments demonstrate clear superiority. On FLUX.1-dev model, OnlineCache achieves nearly 3 speedup while preserving generation fidelity. On DiT and CogVideoX, it similarly delivers competitive acceleration without compromising quality; across all scenarios, it consistently outperforms existing cache-based acceleration baselines.

</details>


### [141] [ALIVE: Warnings Before Exclusion in Budgeted Multi-Source Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29400)
*Xiyang Zhang,Hongzhi Wang,Yuanhe Tian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ALIVE provides an auditable, evidence-driven routing control that governs a persistent exclusion mechanism under budget-constrained auditing; results show substantial reductions in required evidence and mixed accuracy gains, illustrating a restraint–power–cost–utility trade-off for evidence-based persistent decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formalize when unequal-persistence routing actions are warranted under finite-population auditing and learning budgets, and to design an auditable control layer (ALIVE) that bounds risk while enabling evidence-based routing decisions.

Method: Introduce ALIVE: an Action-Layered Intervention via Evidence framework where a randomized without-replacement prefix supplies cached evidence; heuristic warnings drive non-latching, floor-bounded routing; two fresh certificate separations may latch an exclusion request within capacity constraints; analysis assumes fixed support and labels under an ideal uniform audit permutation; establishes an anytime familywise bound δ for predictable controllers preserving the interface.

Result: Median evidence counts drop substantially (304→96 identities in e40; 171→62 in e60), with both engines using 48 in e80. In the CIFAR controller, the persistent-action layer yields +0.1935 accuracy-AUBC points over routing-only across ten seed clusters; the full-system improvement vs CBR is +0.1954 but fails the Holm-adjusted multiplicity criterion (reference value 0.097656). On a fixed natural panel, exploratory PPR shows a median closure prefix of 95 (vs 105 for Serfling/FPC) yet exposes 88.0% of the panel and has no downstream task. Overall, results delineate a boundary among restraint, power, cost, and utility: an action contract fixes a persistent decision, with net value depending on evidence margin, audit cost, and budget regime.

Conclusion: The action contract enables a defined persistent decision; the value of such persistence hinges on evidence margin, audit cost, and budget regime, mapping a restraint–power–cost–utility boundary for evidence-driven persistent routing decisions.

Abstract: A routing decision can be revised at the next transaction, but a latched source exclusion persists across later decisions. We ask what evidence should authorize these unequal-persistence actions when finite-population auditing and learning share a budget. ALIVE (Action-Layered Intervention via Evidence) is an auditable control layer: one randomized without-replacement prefix supplies cached evidence, heuristic warnings drive non-latching floor-bounded routing, and only two fresh simultaneous certificate separations may latch an exclusion request subject to capacity-feasible activation. Conditional on fixed support and labels under an ideal uniform audit permutation, any predictable controller preserving this interface inherits an anytime familywise bound of δon acting against a source that fails the pre-fixed absolute or relative strict-majority-disagreement predicate. With a published known-size, all-strict-majority PPR engine, median evidence count fell from 304 to 96 identities in e40 and from 171 to 62 in e60, while both engines used 48 in e80. In the matched CIFAR controller, the persistent-action layer added +0.1935 accuracy-AUBC percentage points over routing-only in all ten paired seed clusters. The +0.1954-point full-system contrast against CBR was also positive but did not meet the predeclared multiplicity-adjusted criterion (conditional Holm-adjusted sign-flip reference value =.097656). On a fixed natural panel, exploratory PPR used a median closure prefix of 95 rather than 105 for exploratory Serfling/FPC, but still exposed 88.0% of the panel and had no downstream task. Together these results map a restraint--power--cost--utility boundary: the action contract controls a defined persistent decision, while net value depends on evidence margin, audit cost, and budget regime.

</details>


### [142] [Explore Beyond the Boundary Using Entropic Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29419)
*Bumgeun Park,Donghwan Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ENTINEX introduces intrinsic rewards at the state distribution boundaries using entropic information to boost exploration in sparse, delayed-reward RL, outperforming existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exploration under sparse and delayed rewards suffers from limited feedback; agents must explore widely to discover valuable reward signals and rewards. Boundary exploration can reveal novel regions of the state space that yield information-rich signals.

Method: Propose Entropic Information for Exploration (ENTINEX), which assigns intrinsic rewards to the boundaries of the state distribution by leveraging entropic information to identify boundary states and incentivize exploration beyond these boundaries within a reinforcement learning framework.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows ENTINEX consistently improves exploration performance in environments with sparse and delayed rewards and outperforms existing exploration methods.

Conclusion: Boundary-focused exploration driven by entropic information is effective for tackling sparse/delayed reward settings, making ENTINEX a competitive approach for robust exploration.

Abstract: In reinforcement learning, exploration with sparse and delayed rewards presents a significant challenge due to the limited feedback available for guiding the learning process. Addressing this issue requires extensive exploration in the state space to discover valuable reward signals. In this paper, we propose Entropic Information for Exploration (ENTINEX), a novel method that enhances exploration by incentivizing agents to explore beyond the boundaries of the state distribution. ENTINEX achieves this by assigning intrinsic rewards to these boundaries, leveraging entropic information to identify them effectively. Through extensive experimentation, we demonstrate that ENTINEX consistently improves exploration performance in environments characterized by sparse and delayed rewards. Our experimental results show that ENTINEX outperforms existing exploration methods, highlighting its effectiveness in both sparse and delayed reward scenarios.

</details>


### [143] [End-to-End Fairness Optimization with Fair Decision-Focused Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29441)
*Yu Wang,Violet,Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified end-to-end fairness optimization framework (E2EFO) for prediction-to-decision pipelines in resource allocation, paired with Fair Decision-Focused Learning (FDFL). It jointly optimizes prediction accuracy and fairness with decision fairness (via a group-based alpha-fairness objective), derives the decision Jacobian for gradient-based training, provides a generalization bound, and validates on healthcare and synthetic allocation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fairness concerns permeate both prediction and decision stages in decision-support systems. Existing approaches typically treat prediction or decision fairness in isolation; a unified, end-to-end framework is needed to minimize downstream decision regret while bounding group-based disparities in both stages, especially in resource allocation problems.

Method: Propose End-to-End Fairness Optimization (E2EFO) as a unified framework tying prediction and decision modules. Introduce Fair Decision-Focused Learning (FDFL), training the predictor with a multi-task loss that combines (i) prediction accuracy, (ii) prediction fairness, and (iii) decision regret (loss in decision fairness due to imperfect predictions). Derive exact closed-form decision Jacobians for a tractable fair allocation class; apply a differentiable optimization layer for the general case; prove a finite-sample generalization bound for the scalarized FDFL objective.

Result: Numerical experiments on a healthcare-based single-resource allocation and a synthetic multiple-resource allocation demonstrate the practical benefits of jointly accounting for prediction fairness and decision fairness, illustrating improved fairness outcomes without sacrificing predictive performance.

Conclusion: End-to-end fairness optimization (E2EFO) with FDFL offers a principled, tractable approach to fairness in prediction-informed decision-making. The framework provides theoretical guarantees (decision Jacobian handling and generalization bound) and practical validation in resource allocation, supporting broader applicability to prediction-to-decision pipelines.

Abstract: Many real-world systems rely on predictive models to inform decisions, and fairness concerns arise in both the prediction and decision stages. We introduce end-to-end fairness optimization (E2EFO) as a unifying framework that integrates fairness across the prediction-to-decision pipeline. We focus on resource allocation with group-based fairness: the prediction task estimates allocation impacts while limiting accuracy disparity across groups, and the decision task distributes those impacts equitably by optimizing a group-based alpha-fairness measure. Within this framework, we propose fair decision-focused learning (FDFL), a training paradigm that jointly accounts for prediction accuracy, prediction fairness, and decision regret -- the loss in decision fairness due to imperfect predictions. FDFL trains the predictor by gradient descent, combining the objective gradients through multi-task learning techniques. The core computational challenge is the decision Jacobian with respect to the predictor parameters: we derive exact closed-form formulas for a tractable class of fair allocation and apply a differentiable optimization layer in the general case. We further establish a finite-sample generalization bound for the scalarized FDFL objective. Numerical experiments on a healthcare-based single resource allocation and a synthetic multiple resource allocation illustrate the value of jointly accounting for prediction fairness and decision fairness in prediction-informed decision-making.

</details>


### [144] [TFGformer: Multivariate Time Series Forecasting via Time-Frequency Graph Learning and Covariate Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29459)
*Yu Sun,Yuan Chang,Xiaohou Shi,Yan Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CrossRAG is a retrieval-augmented forecasting framework for large-scale heterogeneous IoT multivariate time series that integrates Shape-Aware Memory (SAM) with RevIN normalization, Future-Consistent Contrastive (FCC) learning, and Cross-Attention Temporal Fusion (CATF) to achieve robust, high-accuracy long-term forecasting across seven benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-series foundation models struggle to access external historical patterns during inference, especially with heterogenous sources and varying magnitudes. Retrieval-augmented strategies could provide adaptive, externally sourced references, but face challenges ensuring retrieved patterns align in magnitude and future behavior with the target series.

Method: 1) Shape-Aware Memory (SAM) with RevIN normalization to enable magnitude-robust, shape-level retrieval across heterogeneous sources. 2) Future-Consistent Contrastive (FCC) learning to distinguish informative references from hard negatives that have similar histories but different futures. 3) Cross-Attention Temporal Fusion (CATF) to fuse retrieved historical–future reference pairs into the backbone model's representations at the representation level.

Result: Empirical evaluation on seven public benchmarks shows CrossRAG consistently outperforms both parametric-only baselines and existing retrieval-augmented forecasting methods.

Conclusion: Incorporating retrieval-augmented components with shape-aware memory, magnitude-normalized retrieval, future-consistent learning, and cross-attention fusion yields superior long-horizon forecasting for heterogeneous IoT time series.

Abstract: Large-scale multivariate time series from heterogeneous IoT sensors demand accurate long-term forecasting for resource scheduling and predictive maintenance. While recent time series foundation models exhibit strong generalization, they rely on static parametric knowledge and lack dynamic access to external historical patterns during inference. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) offers a potential remedy, yet its application to time series forecasting is challenged by magnitude variations across heterogeneous sources and the mismatch between historical similarity and future consistency. We propose CrossRAG, a retrieval-augmented forecasting framework that integrates Shape-Aware Memory (SAM) with RevIN normalization for magnitude-robust shape-level retrieval, Future-Consistent Contrastive (FCC) learning to distinguish informative references from hard negatives with similar history but divergent futures, and Cross-Attention Temporal Fusion (CATF) to fuse retrieved historical--future reference pairs into the backbone's representations at the representation level. Experiments on seven public benchmarks show that CrossRAG consistently outperforms both parametric-only baselines and existing retrieval-augmented forecasting methods.

</details>


### [145] [Parameter-Free Heavy-Tailed Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29460)
*Gianmarco Genalti,Alberto Maria Metelli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This work studies adaptation to unknown heavy-tail parameters in bandit problems, proving a trade-off between distribution-dependent and distribution-free regret when the tail parameter u is unknown; it delivers a scheduled-exploration algorithm that adapts to u without prior knowledge and, by tuning to ε=1, adapts to the endpoint ε=1; sublinear regret achievable for every fixed ε>0 but not uniformly over ε∈(0,1], thereby resolving a COLT 2025 open problem and quantifying the cost of adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In heavy-tailed settings rewards may exhibit rare extreme outcomes. Existing regret minimization methods often require knowing tail parameters ε and u; realism demands parameter-free methods that adapt to unknown tails. The COLT open problem asks whether adaptation is possible and at what cost.

Method: First derive a lower bound showing a sharp trade-off between distribution-dependent and distribution-free regret when u is unknown for fixed ε. Then design a scheduled-exploration algorithm that does not require knowledge of u and matches the adaptation frontier up to log factors. Finally, instantiate the algorithm without knowing ε by calibrating exploration to the ε=1 endpoint, achieving sublinear regret for any fixed ε>0, while proving impossibility of sublinear regret uniformly over ε∈(0,1].

Result: The paper establishes the adaptation frontier and delivers an algorithm that attains it up to log factors. It achieves sublinear regret for every fixed ε>0 without prior tail parameter knowledge, and proves that no algorithm can guarantee sublinear regret uniformly across all ε in (0,1].

Conclusion: The study resolves the COLT 2025 open problem on parameter-free adaptation for heavy-tailed bandits, providing a precise cost of adaptation to unknown tail parameters and a practical algorithm that adapts to both u and ε (to the extent possible), with tight regret guarantees.

Abstract: Heavy-tailed distributions arise naturally in sequential decision-making problems such as financial investment, online advertising, and network management, where rare but extreme outcomes can dominate performance. Heavy-tailed bandits model online decision-making in these settings by assuming only that rewards $X$ satisfy $\mathbb{E}[|X|^{1+ε}]\leq u$, for some tail exponent $ε\in(0,1]$ and moment bound $u<+\infty$. However, most existing regret minimization algorithms require these parameters to be known. This assumption is particularly restrictive in practice: $ε$ and $u$ govern the frequency and magnitude of rare events and are therefore precisely the quantities that are hardest to infer reliably from limited observations.
  Motivated by an open problem posed by Genalti and Metelli at COLT 2025, we resolve the assumption-free adaptation problem for heavy-tailed bandits and characterize the price in the regret of not knowing the tail parameters. We first study adaptation to the moment bound $u$ for a fixed tail exponent $ε$. We prove that every algorithm unaware of $u$, or of any upper bound on it, must obey a sharp trade-off between its distribution-dependent and distribution-free regret guarantees. We then introduce a scheduled-exploration algorithm that requires no knowledge of $u$ and matches the resulting adaptation frontier up to logarithmic factors. Finally, we show that the same algorithm can be instanced without knowing $ε$ by calibrating its exploration schedule to the endpoint $ε=1$. It achieves sublinear regret for every fixed $ε>0$, while no algorithm can guarantee sublinear regret uniformly over all $ε\in(0,1]$. Altogether, our results resolve the COLT open problem without additional distributional assumptions and provide a sharp characterization of the statistical cost of adapting to unknown heavy tails.

</details>


### [146] [MolGVR: A Chemistry-Grounded Framework for Text-to-Molecule Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29479)
*Qian Tan,Xuanyu Zhu,Lei Jiang,Zhonghang Yuan,Chen Zhang,Yuqiang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MolGVR introduces a chemistry-grounded Generator--Verifier--Refiner architecture for text-to-molecule generation. It shows improved exact-match accuracy by adding executable chemical verification and feedback-guided refinement to generation, demonstrated on ChEBI-20 and PCDes datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-molecule generation often treats as one-shot sequence learning, but descriptions encode structural constraints that, if violated, change molecular identity. Chemical verification and error correction are underexplored, limiting reliability of downstream applications.

Method: A Generator proposes candidate molecules by inferring structural evidence from descriptions. A Verifier converts descriptions into chemical constraints and checks generated candidates for constraint satisfaction. A Refiner revises candidates rejected by the Verifier, enabling iterative improvement.

Result: On ChEBI-20 and PCDes, MolGVR achieves higher exact-match performance than baselines, demonstrating that executable verification and feedback-driven refinement improve text-to-molecule generation.

Conclusion: Coupling generation with executable chemical verification and iterative refinement is an effective strategy to improve reliability and accuracy in text-to-molecule generation.

Abstract: Text-to-molecule generation is typically formulated as a one-shot sequence generation problem, where a model directly maps target descriptions to molecular representations. However, molecular descriptions often contain informative structural constraints, and violating such constraints can change the molecular identity. This makes chemical verification and error correction important but underexplored. To fill this gap, we propose MolGVR, a chemistry-grounded Generator--Verifier--Refiner framework. The Generator infers structural evidence and generates candidate molecules. The Verifier addresses the lack of chemical validation by converting descriptions into chemical constraints and checking candidates against them. The Refiner addresses generation failures by revising candidates rejected by the Verifier. Experiments on ChEBI-20 and PCDes show that MolGVR improves exact-match performance. These results suggest that coupling generation with executable verification and feedback-guided refinement is an effective way to improve text-to-molecule generation.

</details>


### [147] [DreamQAS: Learning a Decision-Useful World Model for VQE-Efficient Quantum Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29491)
*Jiayang Niu,Yan Wang,Jie Li,Ke Deng,Azadeh Alavi,Muhammad Usman,Yongli Ren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DreamQAS is a model-based RL framework for quantum architecture search that preserves circuit dynamics, learns from post-VQE feedback via a world-model, and achieves higher data efficiency and reliability across molecular tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL-based quantum architecture search (QAS) faces high sample costs due to expensive post-VQE evaluations; existing methods may alter circuit dynamics and rely on imperfect energy predictions. DreamQAS preserves exact circuit dynamics and learns from costly feedback, aiming for reliable decision-useful guidance rather than precise energy forecasting.

Method: DreamQAS uses a recurrent randomized-prior ensemble as a world model to predict an oracle-free score relative to an empirical energy frontier and supports multi-step imagined policy learning over explicit legal circuits. It employs ranking-based activation, uncertainty-aware pessimism and truncation, and selective real-VQE verification within a reliability-controlled learning loop.

Result: Under a 15,000-episode budget and frozen evaluation, DreamQAS achieves the lowest mean frozen-policy energy error on four of five molecular tasks (second-lowest on the fifth). It uses 1.6x–2.0x fewer real VQE calls on four tasks and 10.6x fewer on BeH2-8q to reach fine-error targets (relative to baselines). Counterfactual action-ranking utility increases across all five tasks (mean 0.346; 95% CI [0.185, 0.507]); direct greedy and beam search with the same model fail to replicate these gains. Ensemble disagreement also improves risk-coverage. The study argues that the value of a world-model in QAS lies in producing decision-useful feedback rather than exact energy prediction.
