Conclusion: DreamQAS establishes a world-model design for QAS that achieves reliable, data-efficient learning by leveraging imagined policy optimization and uncertainty-aware strategies, focusing on decision usefulness of feedback rather than precise energy prediction.

Abstract: Reinforcement-learning-based quantum architecture search (RL-QAS) repeatedly optimizes a variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) after extending a circuit, although circuit construction and action legality are deterministic and known. We introduce DreamQAS, a model-based RL framework that preserves these exact circuit dynamics and learns only the expensive post-VQE feedback. A recurrent randomized-prior ensemble predicts an oracle-free score relative to an empirical energy frontier and supports multi-step imagined policy learning over explicit legal circuits. Ranking-based activation, uncertainty-aware pessimism and truncation, and selective real-VQE verification form a reliability-controlled learning loop. Under a common 15,000-episode budget and frozen evaluation for the RL methods, DreamQAS has the lowest mean frozen-policy energy error on four of five molecular tasks and the second-lowest on one. At fine-error targets reached by all seeds of both methods, it uses 1.6x to 2.0x fewer real VQE calls on four tasks and 10.6x fewer on BeH2-8q. Counterfactual action-ranking utility increases across all five tasks, with a mean increase of 0.346 and a 95 percent confidence interval of [0.185, 0.507], while direct greedy and beam use of the same model does not recover the gains of imagined policy learning. Ensemble disagreement also improves risk-coverage over random rejection on all three probed tasks. These results establish a world-model design for QAS whose value lies in decision-useful feedback rather than exact energy prediction.

</details>


### [148] [Adaptive FastOPD: Progress-Aware Rollout Horizon Expansion for Efficient On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29494)
*Qian Tan,Huaifei Liang,Xuanyu Zhu,Lei Jiang,Yuqiang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive FastOPD reduces online rollout cost in on-policy distillation by adaptively expanding the rollout horizon based on learning progress signals, achieving higher average performance and 49.1–71.2% training time reduction across two teacher–student pairs, with robustness to hyperparameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OPD's online rollouts are computationally expensive. Fixed budgets/thresholds for rollout length do not reflect progress across models/training stages, motivating a progress-aware scheme to balance learning gains with compute.

Method: Introduce Adaptive FastOPD that expands the rollout horizon only when learning near the current boundary shows plateauing and current horizon is efficiently utilized. Expansion is guided by four teacher–student signals measured relative to their values at horizon entry, making expansion stage-aware rather than based on fixed steps or raw agreement thresholds. Prevents small long responses from triggering cost increases. Evaluation on two teacher–student pairs, comparing to OPD 15K.

Result: Adaptive FastOPD achieves the highest average performance and reduces training time by 49.1–71.2% relative to OPD 15K across two pairs, with robustness across a range of hyperparameters.

Conclusion: A progress-aware horizon expansion strategy effectively aligns rollout cost with learning progress in on-policy distillation, offering substantial speedups without sacrificing performance and showing robustness across settings.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) provides dense teacher supervision along student-generated trajectories, but its online rollout process incurs substantial computational cost, particularly when a few long responses delay batch completion. Existing acceleration methods typically control rollout length using fixed budgets or absolute teacher--student agreement thresholds, which may not reflect learning progress across different models and training stages. We propose Adaptive FastOPD, a progress-aware strategy that expands the rollout horizon only when learning near the current boundary region has plateaued and the current horizon is sufficiently utilized. The former is determined from four teacher--student signals measured relative to their values upon entering each horizon, making expansion responsive to stage-specific progress rather than a predefined step interval or an absolute threshold on the raw agreement signals, while the latter prevents a small number of long responses from triggering increases in rollout cost. Across two teacher--student pairs, Adaptive FastOPD achieves the highest average performance while reducing training time by 49.1--71.2\% relative to OPD 15K, and remains robust across a range of hyperparameter settings.

</details>


### [149] [Transcript-Managed Transformers: Monotone Multi-Agent Collapse and Universality with Two Pop-Enabled Transcripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29496)
*Sergey Salishev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Transcript-Managed Transducers (TMT) with k channels to model fixed-precision causal Transformers; shows that pop-free RTMT_k equals deterministic finite-state transductions (DFST); with pop operations P_c, power escalates to DCFL (k=1) and RE (k>=2); two pop-enabled transcripts suffice for universality; discusses simulation costs, fixed-block/invariant parameters, and state growth via Spawn.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify how block-wise transcript management in fixed-precision, causal transformers maps to classical automata/transducer theory, and identify the computational power under various restrictions and agent-population settings.

Method: Formalize a family of models: Transcript-Managed Transducer TMT_n^k with one controller, k channels, per-round actions {stay, push, pop}, and a fixed visible window; introduce pop-free Restricted TMT RTMT_n^k (append-only); analyze realizable transductions under fixed k and monotone agent protocols; connect to Hopcroft–Ullman presentations; study universality with orchestrated agents and pop-enabled transcripts; discuss costs and invariances to block size/visibility; outline state-growth via Spawn.

Result: RTMT_n^k implements exactly the DFST class for fixed k; for monotone fixed populations this remains true; enabling pop operations via {P_c} enlarges the class to DCFL for k=1 and to RE for k>=2; two pop-enabled transcripts suffice for universality (in one or two transcripts/agents); there are stated simulation costs and invariances with respect to block size and visible radius; bounds fix parameters (precision, alphabets, blocks, visibility, controller state, population) while extending context, hidden-block access, writable stores, and unbounded Spawn add further state.

Conclusion: The framework yields a clean automata-theoretic account of transcript management in a fixed-precision transformer setting, showing how block-level operations control computational power and connect to classical language classes; pop-enabled capabilities yield richer expressiveness (DCFL and RE), and universality is achievable with two transcripts; state growth mechanisms (Spawn) further extend the model, while fixed bounds provide controllable analysis.

Abstract: We study transcript management for fixed, finite-precision causal Transformers. A transcript is partitioned into channels of bounded blocks. Each transition consults a fixed visible suffix and may append one block, leaving the model, weights, and token protocol unchanged. The operation $P_c:=\PopContext(c)$ deletes the newest block on channel $c$ and exposes its predecessor.
  We model the layer by the Transcript-Managed Transducer $\TMTn{k}$: one finite controller, $k$ channels, and per-round actions from stay, push, and pop under a caller-driven status map. Fixed visible windows encode as finite symbols. The pop-free Restricted Transcript-Managed Transducer $\RTMTn{k}$ is the standard append-only layer and, for every fixed $k$, realizes exactly the deterministic finite-state transductions. The same holds for every fixed finite agent population under a monotone protocol that appends, routes, and copies visible blocks.
  Admitting $\{P_c\}_{c=1}^k$ restores pop. Newest-first, a pop-enabled channel is a stack; compiling to the Hopcroft--Ullman presentation transfers the classical hierarchy: $\DCFL$ for $k=1$ and $\RE$ for every $k\ge2$. Orchestrated one-channel agents match one controller with $k$ channels, so two pop-enabled transcripts---in one agent or two---suffice for universality. Simulation costs and invariance to fixed block size and visible radius are stated. The bounds fix precision, alphabets, blocks, visibility, controller state, and population; growing exact context, hidden-block access, writable stores, and unbounded \textbf{Spawn} add further state.

</details>


### [150] [The Grokked Illusion: True Equilibrium Mitigates Catastrophic Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29503)
*Xiaotian Zhang,Lai Shun Chan,Yue Shang,Entao Yang,Ge Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: High-entropy models maintain robustness to interference beyond perfect generalization; they resist catastrophic forgetting better than AdamW-trained transformers when retraining with random labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test whether the high-entropy (Boltzmann entropy) advantage extends from generalization to robustness against forgetting during continual learning, using a controlled grokking-in-modular arithmetic setting.

Method: Compare AdamW-trained transformers with high-entropy models sampled via Wang-Landau Molecular Dynamics, matched for saturated performance; perform noise injection by forcing memorization of data with random labels; measure forgetting via test accuracy; analyze weight representations via singular value decomposition to assess effective rank in attention and MLP layers.

Result: AdamW models exhibit catastrophic forgetting (test accuracy falls from 100% to <75%), while high-entropy models retain ~95% accuracy; high-entropy networks show higher effective rank in weights pre- and post-noise.

Conclusion: Perfect generalization does not guarantee robustness to interference; the hidden fragility shows that higher-entropy representations confer robustness to forgetting through richer, higher-rank feature representations.

Abstract: While neural networks are typically evaluated by their training and test performance, these metrics do not reveal how robust a learned representation is. Recent studies have shown that solutions occupying larger volumes in parameter space, as quantified by Boltzmann entropy, often exhibit superior generalizability compared to those reached by conventional optimization, a phenomenon known as the high entropy advantage. Here we ask whether this advantage persists beyond generalization. Specifically, we investigate models' robustness, the ability to retain the learned knowledge when the model is subsequently trained to acquire new information. Using grokking in modular arithmetic as a controlled setting, we design a noise injection experiment to evaluate the robustness difference between AdamW-trained transformers and high-entropy model sampled from Wang-Landau Molecular Dynamics with identical saturated performance. By forcing both models to fully remember new data with random labels, we find that AdamW-trained models suffer from catastrophic forgetting, with original task test accuracy dropping from 100% to below 75%, whereas the high-entropy models maintain approximately 95% test accuracy. We term this hidden fragility behind apparent generalization the "grokked illusion." Through singular value decomposition of the neural network weights, we discover that high-entropy neural networks possess significantly higher effective rank in attention and MLP layers both before and after noise injection, indicating richer feature representations can serve as a buffer against catastrophic forgetting. Our findings demonstrate that perfect generalization does not imply equal robustness, offering a new perspective on what makes a trained model robust to interference.

</details>


### [151] [TerraNova: A Foundation Model for the Anthropocene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29527)
*Carlos Rodriguez-Pardo,Massimo Tavoni*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TerraNova fuses 512 gridded Earth-system fields and 512 national indicators into a unified foundation model with cross-modal encoders and a per-query decoder, enabling border-aware, country-level, spatiotemporal predictions with uncertainty estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome geometry mismatch: physical Earth data are continuous fields while societal data are reported in administrative units. A unified representation should span both domains, support country-level capabilities, and quantify uncertainty across time and space.

Method: Use 1,024 records: 512 gridded Earth-system fields and 512 national indicators. Dedicated encoders capture location, country, time, and task. Cross-modal transformers fuse into a shared spatiotemporal state. A hypernetwork generates a per-query decoder whose evidential head outputs a predictive distribution. Two contrastive objectives: (1) population-weighted alignment between each country and coordinates within its territory; (2) alignment with pretrained geospatial embeddings from image-derived semantics. A frozen backbone reconstructs dense fields from sparse observations and rapidly adapts to unseen variables on consumer hardware.

Result: The model is competitive with purpose-built geospatial encoders while spanning axes they do not represent (time, oceans, uncertainty). It supports country-level analytics, handles unseen variables quickly, and reconstructs dense fields from sparse data.

Conclusion: TerraNova demonstrates a scalable, border-aware foundation-model approach to integrate physical and societal geographies, enabling cross-domain spatiotemporal reasoning with rapid adaptation and uncertainty quantification.

Abstract: A defining problem of the Anthropocene is to model the physical Earth and human societies as one coupled system, yet no learned representation spans their observational breadth. We argue the obstacle is geometric: the physical Earth is measured as continuous fields that ignore political borders, whereas societies are reported for administrative units. Earth-system foundation models serve the first geometry; coupling it to the second has required lossy averaging over borders. We introduce TerraNova, a foundation model trained on 1,024 physical and societal records in their native geometries: 512 gridded Earth-system fields and 512 national indicators. Dedicated encoders represent location, country, time and task, cross-modal transformers fuse them into a shared spatiotemporal state, and a hypernetwork generates a per-query decoder whose evidential head returns a predictive distribution. Two contrastive objectives couple the representation: a population-weighted alignment between each country and coordinates in its territory, and one to pretrained geospatial embeddings carrying image-derived semantics. Read out through that decoder, the representation is competitive with purpose-built geospatial encoders while spanning axes they do not represent (time, oceans and uncertainty) and supporting country-level capabilities. The frozen backbone reconstructs dense fields from sparse observations and adapts to unseen variables in minutes on consumer hardware.

</details>


### [152] [A Neurosymbolic Approach for Explainable Early Diagnosis of Alzheimer's Disease](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29530)
*Ranveer Singh,Pranuthi Tenali,Saurabh Mathur,Ameet Soni,Vaishali Phatak,Karla Lynch,Daniel Murman,Matthew Rizzo,Sriraam Natarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Automates extraction of qualitative AD progression indicators from audio via foundation models to build a Bayesian Network for reasoning about relationships; scales analysis and uncovers novel linguistic-marker links.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual transcription and expert analysis of verbal fluency tests are labor-intensive and limit scalable AD marker identification; an automated pipeline from audio could yield interpretable clinical variables.

Method: Use pretrained foundation models to process raw audio and extract clinically relevant linguistic variables; construct a Bayesian Network to encode relationships among markers; reason about progression and infer qualitative relations; evaluate by recovering known clinical knowledge and discovering new links.

Result: The BN-based approach recovers established clinical knowledge and reveals novel relationships between linguistic markers.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility of end-to-end automated extraction and reasoning about AD progression markers from audio; enables scalable analysis and potential discovery of new indicators; future work may validate clinically and extend to other cognitive domains.

Abstract: Identifying reliable Alzheimer's disease (AD) markers typically requires manual, labor-intensive transcription and expert analysis, limiting its scale. We introduce an automated pipeline that extracts qualitative knowledge about potential AD progression indicators directly from audio recordings of verbal fluency tests. Our method uses pretrained foundation models to process raw audio and extract clinically relevant variables to construct a Bayesian Network (BN); this BN is used to reason about the AD progression markers and infer their qualitative relationships. Our system successfully recovers known clinical knowledge and identifies novel relationships between linguistic markers.

</details>


### [153] [Pyramidal Width Can Increase Under Vertex Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29555)
*Jinze Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Counterexample showing pyramidal width can increase with vertex insertion. In R^3, six integer points yield P = conv{v0..v4} and Q = conv{v0..v5}, with PWidth^2=48/353 and QWidth^2=36/133; ratio sqrt(1059/532) ≈ 1.4109. The proof uses the equivalence between pyramidal width and facial distance, certifies face lattices via integer supporting hyperplanes, and evaluates facial distances through finite rational calculations, with an exact dependency-free verifier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test and potentially refute Lacoste-Julien and Jaggi's 2015 conjecture that pyramidal width does not increase upon adding a vertex (while preserving old vertices). Provide an exact, checkable counterexample and illustrate the computational approach via rational certificates.

Method: Explicit construction of P and Q as convex hulls of specified integer points; compute pyramidal width via the known equivalence with facial distance; certify both face lattices with integer supporting hyperplanes; evaluate each facial distance by finite rational calculation; accompany with a dependency-free exact verifier.

Result: Exact numerical values: PWidth(P)^2 = 48/353, PWidth(Q)^2 = 36/133. Consequently PWidth(P) < PWidth(Q); the insertion increases pyramidal width by factor sqrt(1059/532) ≈ 1.4109.

Conclusion: The conjecture is false in general; the paper provides a precise, verifiable counterexample and demonstrates the tractability of exact rational certification for pyramidal width via facial distance.

Abstract: Lacoste-Julien and Jaggi conjectured in 2015 that the pyramidal width of a polytope cannot increase when a vertex is added, provided that every old point remains a vertex. We give an exact counterexample with six integer points in $\R^3$. For \[ P=\conv\{v_0,\ldots,v_4\},\qquad Q=\conv\{v_0,\ldots,v_5\}, \] where \[ \begin{aligned} v_0&=(-1,-3,-1), & v_1&=(3,2,-2), & v_2&=(0,2,1),\\ v_3&=(-1,-3,3), & v_4&=(-2,0,1), & v_5&=(-1,0,-2), \end{aligned} \] all five vertices of $P$ remain vertices of $Q$, but \[ \PWidth(P)^2=\frac{48}{353} \quad\text{and}\quad \PWidth(Q)^2=\frac{36}{133}. \] Thus vertex insertion increases pyramidal width by the factor $\sqrt{1059/532}\approx 1.410886779$. The proof uses the equivalence between pyramidal width and facial distance, certifies both face lattices by integer supporting hyperplanes, and evaluates every facial distance by a finite rational calculation. A dependency-free exact verifier accompanies the paper.

</details>


### [154] [MOT-SR: Multi-Objective Tool-Augmented Scientific Equation Discovery with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29561)
*Boxiao Wang,Runxiang Wang,Kai Li,Chongming Li,Zhiwei Chen,Yifan Zhang,Jian Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multi-Objective Tool-augmented Symbolic Regression (MOT-SR) uses external analytical tools and two collaborative LLM modules to jointly optimize accuracy, complexity, and generalization in symbolic regression. It maintains a dynamic Pareto front and shows improved accuracy, generalization, and efficiency across 40 tasks, including EMRI orbital modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based SR methods struggle with discovering variable dependencies and typically optimize a single objective (fit error), which can trap models in local optima and ignore structural complexity and generalization.

Method: Integrates external analytical tools to provide structural priors; deploys two LLM modules (Meta Strategy Generator and Equation Generator) in a closed-loop system; maintains a dynamic Pareto front over accuracy, complexity, and generalization; evaluates candidates with multi-objective metrics; validated on 40 standard SR tasks and on EMRI orbital dynamics.

Result: Outperforms existing SR methods in accuracy, generalization, and efficiency on 40 tasks; achieves interpretable corrections for EMRI with the lowest trajectory-level integration error on held-out configurations.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that tool-augmented, multi-objective, collaboration-driven SR can reliably model long-horizon scientific dynamics and improve the exploration of the equation space by incorporating structural priors and Pareto-based optimization.

Abstract: Symbolic Regression (SR) aims to discover analytical equations from observational data and plays a central role in scientific modeling. While recent Large Language Model (LLM) based approaches show promise, they face two limitations. First, they lack data analysis mechanisms for uncovering variable dependencies, which reduces the efficiency of equation discovery. Second, most methods rely on single-objective evaluation focused solely on fitting error. This neglect of structural complexity and generalization often causes models to converge prematurely to local optima, limiting their ability to explore the broader equation space. We propose Multi-Objective Tool-augmented Symbolic Regression (MOT-SR), a unified framework that integrates external analytical tools to extract structural priors and guide equation generation, while jointly optimizing for accuracy, complexity, and generalization via a multi-objective evaluation module that maintains a dynamic Pareto front. MOT-SR employs two collaborative LLM modules: a Meta Strategy Generator, which selects tools and synthesizes structural optimization strategies based on Pareto-optimal equations, and an Equation Generator, which produces new candidate equations accordingly. The system operates in a closed-loop manner, continuously refining both strategies and equation structures. Across 40 standard tasks, MOT-SR outperforms existing SR methods in accuracy, generalization, and efficiency. We further validate MOT-SR on extreme mass-ratio inspiral (EMRI) orbital modeling, an important problem in space-based gravitational-wave astronomy where small local errors can accumulate substantially over long-term evolution. The discovered interpretable correction achieves the lowest trajectory-level integration error on held-out configurations. These results demonstrate the potential of MOT-SR to enable reliable modeling of long-horizon scientific dynamics.

</details>


### [155] [Convergence and Regret of the Policy Gradient for Multi-Armed Bandits in Diffusion Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29593)
*Yanwei Jia,Du Ouyang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Policy gradient in a diffusion-based multi-armed bandit converges almost surely to the best arm with a constant learning rate; achieves logarithmic regret when the rate is below a threshold; a novel Lyapunov function unifies SDE-based analysis and extends to discrete-time updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address theoretical understanding of policy gradient methods in continuous-time diffusion environments, establish convergence and regret guarantees with constant learning rates, and bridge continuous-time SDE analysis with discrete-time policy gradient.

Method: Model the bandit with an SDE under a logit policy parameterization. Prove almost-sure convergence to the optimal arm for any constant learning rate. Develop a novel Lyapunov function to conduct non-asymptotic regret analysis, yielding an O(log T) bound when the learning rate is below a time-invariant threshold. Leverage tools from stochastic differential equations and extend insights to discrete-time policy gradient.

Result: Almost-sure convergence to the optimal arm for arbitrary constant learning rate. Non-asymptotic regret bound of order O(log T) when the constant learning rate is below a threshold. A novel Lyapunov function facilitates transparent SDE-based analysis and also informs discrete-time policy gradient analysis.

Conclusion: The Lyapunov-based approach provides a clear, rigorous framework for policy-gradient analysis in diffusion settings, improves upon prior SDE analyses, and yields results that extend to discrete-time implementations.

Abstract: This paper studies the policy gradient update for a multi-arm bandit problem in diffusion environment that is described by a stochastic differential equation (SDE) under the continuous-time reinforcement learning framework by Wang et al. (2020), Jia and Zhou (2022b). With the logit parameterization for the stochastic policy, we show that it converges almost surely to the optimal arm under an arbitrary constant learning rate. Furthermore, we derive the non-asymptotic regret upper bound when the constant learning rate is below a time-invariant threshold; and the regret bound has order $O(\log T)$. We improve the analysis in Lattimore (2026a) for the same SDE by constructing a novel Lyapunov function and demonstrate the transparency of analyzing policy gradient using the tools in SDEs. In addition, the same Lyapunov function is also helpful in analyzing the discrete-time policy gradient algorithm.

</details>


### [156] [The Parts Are Greater Than the Sum: Automated Task Sequencing for Efficient Training of Multi-Policy LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29601)
*Jiajia Tang,Sizhe Yuen,Francisco Gomez Medina,Yali Du,Adam Sobey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An automatic multi-policy PEFT framework decouples task adaptation by organizing optimization paths into independent QLoRA adapters, improving performance on heterogeneous task sequences under a fixed parameter budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Shared optimization paths in single-policy PEFT (LoRA) cause interference across heterogeneous tasks, limiting transfer and causing forgetting; existing work increases capacity or stacks adapters but remains centralized.

Method: Automatic multi-policy PEFT: group tasks and sequence their adaptation under a fixed parameter budget; implement as independent Quantized Low-Rank Adapters (QLoRA) to create decoupled adaptation spaces while preserving positive transfer among compatible tasks.

Result: On TRACE benchmark, multi-policy PEFT consistently outperforms single-policy PEFT, achieving best performance of 44.78 under the same trainable capacity, indicating optimization-path organization can outperform mere capacity expansion.

Conclusion: Optimization-path organization via automatic multi-policy PEFT and decoupled QLoRA adapters effectively mitigate interference among heterogeneous tasks and enhance transfer, suggesting a new direction beyond simply increasing adapter capacity.

Abstract: Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) commonly adapts large language models using a single shared Low-Rank Adapter (LoRA). This shared optimization space often suffers from interference when adapting heterogeneous task sequences, leading to poor transfer and catastrophic forgetting. Existing approaches mainly improve adapter expressiveness by increasing parameter capacity or composing multiple adapters, yet they still rely on a shared optimization path. In this paper, we propose an optimization-path organization framework for parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large language models, implemented as an automatic multi-policy PEFT architecture. Specifically, optimization-compatible adaptation paths are automatically organized through task grouping and task sequencing under a fixed parameter budget. The organized optimization paths are implemented as independent Quantized Low-Rank Adapters (QLoRA), enabling heterogeneous tasks to be optimized in decoupled adaptation spaces while preserving positive transfer among compatible tasks. Experiments on the TRACE benchmark demonstrate that performance consistently improves from conventional single-policy PEFT to multi-policy PEFT, with the proposed automatic multi-policy framework achieving the best performance of 44.78 under the same trainable capacity. This suggests that optimization-path organization is more effective than simply increasing adapter capacity for heterogeneous parameter-efficient fine-tuning.

</details>


### [157] [A Human-Centered Validation of the Explainability-Performance Coefficient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29614)
*Christian Oliva,Luis F. Lago-Fernández*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A model-agnostic EPC score extends Explainability-Performance Coefficient to quantify explanation quality by balancing feature sparsity and preserved model performance; validated across tabular, text, and image data, and aligned with human explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Objective, human-aligned evaluation of explanations is needed in XAI, especially in high-risk domains, to assess why models make decisions and to compare explainer methods.

Method: Introduce EPC score as a model-agnostic metric that trades off sparsity of feature selections with retained predictive performance. Validate across tabular, text, and image modalities to study how network activations, data dimensionality, and explainer quality interact. Compare EPC scores with independent human explanations (lexical sentiment judgments, spatial annotations).

Result: EPC captures operational dependencies among network activations, data dimensionality, and explainer performance; higher EPC scores correlate with human explanations, indicating alignment with human judgments on lexical sentiment and spatial regions.

Conclusion: The EPC score is a robust, model-agnostic metric for explanation quality that aligns with human understanding and can guide the selection and development of XAI methods across modalities.

Abstract: The rapid adoption of deep learning models in high-risk domains has intensified the need for trustworthy Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI). However, objectively evaluating explanation fidelity and aligning XAI metrics with human-centered understanding remain critical open challenges. In this work, we propose a model-agnostic metric, the EPC score, which is an extension of the Explainability-Performance Coefficient (EPC), that quantifies explanation quality by explicitly balancing the trade-off between feature selection sparsity and preserved model performance. Through an empirical validation across tabular, text, and image modalities, we show that the EPC score effectively uncovers operational dependencies among network activations, data dimensionality, and explainer performance. Furthermore, we validate the EPC score against independent human-based explanations, proving that higher EPC scores strongly align with human lexical sentiment judgments and spatial visual annotations.

</details>


### [158] [When Does On-Policy Interaction Help? Representational Tradeoffs in Value-Based Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29617)
*Luca Viano,Antoine Moulin,Audrey Huang,Volkan Cevher,Philip Amortila,Dylan J. Foster*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Expert interaction in imitation learning relaxes the need to exactly realize an expert policy; by focusing on the expert's value function, the proposed OVI method achieves data and computation efficiency, with negative results showing interaction is necessary; empirically, OVI outperforms BC, DAgger, and offline value-based IL, especially when the learner is underparameterized.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand why interactive expert queries and value-function-based objectives improve imitation learning, and identify the theoretical and practical limits of these interventions; determine when interaction reduces representational demands and how it interacts with policy vs value-based IL.

Method: Propose OVI, an on-policy interactive IL algorithm. Theoretical results show statistical efficiency under expert-value realizability and computational efficiency with a linear maximization oracle; include a negative result proving interaction is necessary beyond expert-value realizability; empirical evaluation compares OVI against BC, DAgger, and offline value-based IL.

Result: OVI outperforms baselines across settings, with the largest gains when the learner network is substantially less expressive than the expert; results support the claim that interaction lowers the representational demands to the level of the expert value function.

Conclusion: Interacting with an expert shifts the IL goal from exactly imitating the policy to realizing the value function, enabling efficient learning even with limited model capacity; interaction is necessary for favorable sample efficiency; offline IL is constrained by the complexity of the expert policy class.

Abstract: Imitation learning (IL)---training an agent to replicate expert behavior from demonstrations---underpins applications from robotics to language model training. Standard approaches such as Behavior Cloning (BC) are known to suffer from compounding errors and performance plateaus, particularly when the learner cannot perfectly represent the expert's policy (as is typical, e.g., in distillation). Two interventions are widely understood empirically to improve performance: querying the expert interactively along the learner's own trajectories, and using value function estimation en route to generating a policy rather than directly fitting the expert's full action distribution.
  We investigate the nature of these improvements and their potentially surprising interplay. Our main finding is that expert interaction relaxes the representational demands on the learner: one only needs a model capable of realizing the expert's value function, bypassing the (often stricter) requirement of realizing the expert's policy itself. Concretely, we introduce OVI, an interactive on-policy IL algorithm that is statistically efficient whenever the learner can represent the expert's value function and computationally efficient given access to a linear maximization oracle. We complement this with a negative result showing that interaction is necessary. Namely, without stronger assumptions beyond expert-value realizability alone, any offline IL algorithm must scale with the complexity of the expert policy class. Our findings bear out empirically. OVI outperforms offline policy-based (BC), interactive policy-based (DAgger), and offline value-based IL methods, with the largest gains when the learner network is substantially less expressive than the expert's.

</details>


### [159] [CENDRe: Concept Extraction with Natural Domain Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29621)
*Antonia Holzapfel,Andres Felipe Posada Moreno,Sebastian Trimpe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CENDRe is a two-stage, frequency-aware concept extraction method for CNNs on time-series. It discovers concepts via silhouette-guided clustering of per-timestep latent representations, localizes them in time and through a Fourier-based frequency mapping, and assigns relevance to classes. It outperforms baselines on representation and importance correctness, and reveals frequency bands relevant to bearing-fault diagnosis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing time-series concept extraction methods operate only in the time domain, require a fixed, predefined number of concepts, and yield localizations misaligned with the model's actual usage. There is a need for a method that (i) captures both temporal and spectral patterns, (ii) automatically determines the number of concepts, and (iii) provides aligned, globalizable localizations with class relevance.

Method: CENDRe discovers concepts by clustering per-timestep latent representations in two stages with silhouette-guided aggregation to determine the number of concepts. Localization uses gradients of a presence score contrasting latent representations with prototypes to produce concept-focused masks in time. These gradients are propagated through a differentiable invertible mapping (e.g., Fourier transform) to obtain frequency-domain localizations. Each concept is assigned a relevance score quantifying its contribution to each class.

Result: On synthetic benchmarks, CENDRe achieves representation correctness comparable to state-of-the-art CE methods and significantly higher importance correctness. On real bearing-fault data, it identifies frequency bands driving predictions, aligning with regions routinely inspected for fault diagnosis and providing evidence beyond time-domain CE methods.

Conclusion: CENDRe extends concept extraction to time-series by jointly modeling temporal and spectral patterns, enabling automatic concept-count determination, and producing time- and frequency-aligned localizations with class relevance. It improves interpretability and offers practical diagnostic evidence for fault diagnosis.

Abstract: Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) are widely used for time-series classification, but their deployment in critical domains requires understanding the temporal and spectral patterns that drive their predictions. Concept extraction (CE) methods identify such patterns by analyzing representations within the models' latent space. However, existing time-series CE methods have three limitations: they operate only in the time domain and overlook frequency features, predefine the number of concepts, and produce localizations misaligned with the regions the model uses. We address these limitations by proposing CENDRe, a concept extraction method for CNNs. It first discovers concepts by clustering per-timestep latent representations in two stages, where silhouette-guided aggregation selects the number of concepts automatically. Then, it localizes each concept through gradients of a presence score that contrasts the latent representations with their prototypes, producing masks that concentrate on the regions driving the concept. These gradients, propagated through a differentiable invertible mapping of the input such as a Fourier transform, yield localizations for the same concepts in the frequency domain. Finally, each concept receives a relevance score that quantifies its contribution to each class. On synthetic benchmarks, CENDRe achieves representation correctness comparable to state-of-the-art CE methods and significantly higher importance correctness. On real bearing-fault data, CENDRe extracts the frequency bands driving the model's predictions, located in regions commonly inspected for fault diagnosis, producing evidence to assess the model that time-domain CE methods cannot.

</details>


### [160] [GQ-FSL: Green Quantized Federated Split Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29659)
*Idan Roth,Lutz Lampe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Green quantized Federated Split Learning (GQ-FSL) uses stochastic quantization and asymmetric precision to reduce energy in local training and wireless transmissions, with a theoretical convergence bound and a joint split/precision optimization, achieving better energy efficiency than quantized FL and full-precision FSL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate energy and resource bottlenecks of deploying state-of-the-art DNNs at the wireless edge by reducing both on-device computation and communication overhead in FSL.

Method: Introduce GQ-FSL framework with stochastic quantization for local and transmission processes; allow asymmetric precision between client and server submodels; develop energy models for the split architecture; derive convergence bound under data heterogeneity; formulate joint optimization to choose split point and precisions under a target accuracy.

Result: Theoretical convergence bound established; optimization reveals energy-efficient configurations; experiments show GQ-FSL outperforms quantized FL and full-precision FSL in energy efficiency for large-scale DNN on resource-constrained devices.

Conclusion: GQ-FSL enables scalable, energy-efficient edge deployment of large DNNs by decoupling device energy constraints from global convergence and enabling flexible precision/split-point design, outperforming baselines in energy efficiency.

Abstract: Deploying state-of-the-art deep neural networks (DNNs) at the wireless edge is severely bottlenecked by the strict energy and resource constraints of mobile devices. While federated split learning (FSL) mitigates on-device computation by offloading workloads to an edge server, this may introduce systemic overheads, while the continuous exchange of cut-layer data, and submodels still incurs significant energy consumption (EC). To address this, we propose a green quantized FSL (GQ-FSL) framework that incorporates stochastic quantization for both local collaborative training and wireless transmissions. Notably, GQ-FSL supports asymmetric precision levels for the client- and server-side submodels, effectively decoupling device energy constraints from global convergence degradation. To quantify these tradeoffs, we develop parameterized energy models for the split architecture and derive a theoretical convergence bound under statistically heterogeneous data. Building on that, we formulate a joint optimization problem to configure the DNN split point and precision levels, minimizing the total system EC while satisfying a strict target accuracy constraint. Ultimately, we demonstrate that GQ-FSL enables large-scale DNN deployment on resource-constrained devices, achieving superior energy efficiency compared to quantized federated learning and full-precision FSL.

</details>


### [161] [Freeze, Then Select: Structured Field Adapters and Stability-Validated Weak Selection for PDE Discovery from Sparse Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29665)
*Juncheng Zhong,Chenghuang Shen,Jianfeng Liu,Zhengdong Xiao,Longjiu Luo,Qianrong Wang,Wenjun Xu,Wenlian Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Freeze-then-select framework for robust PDE discovery from sparse data, decoupling equation selection from neural optimization. Demonstrates state-of-the-art exact support recovery across six sparse MDBench regimes, notably on Kuramoto–Sivashinsky dynamics; extends to GP-generated expressions and nonlinear diffusion functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PDE discovery from sparse observations requires reconstructing a continuous field and correctly identifying differential terms. Optimization paths in coupled neural PDE discovery can cause exact support to persist, appear transiently, or fail, complicating reliable equation selection.

Method: Introduce a freeze-then-select pipeline: a structured field adapter factorizes the field into learned spatial features and temporal coefficients (cubic splines). After freezing the field, Stability-Validated Weak Selection (SVWS) identifies recurrent terms across independent weak-form systems, refits candidate supports, and selects the final equation on held-out weak-form systems. Applies the same principle to expressions generated by genetic programming and recovers a power-law nonlinear diffusion function from sparse, noisy observations. Evaluated across six sparse MDBench regimes, compared to classical and neural baselines.

Result: Attains the highest exact support recovery rate across all six sparse MDBench regimes; shows clearest gains over both classical and neural baselines on challenging KS dynamics.

Conclusion: Decoupling equation selection from neural optimization via freeze-then-select and SVWS yields robust PDE discovery from sparse data and adaptable expression discovery, offering improved exact support recovery and generalizing to symbolic GP-generated expressions.

Abstract: PDE discovery from sparse observations requires reconstructing a continuous field and selecting the correct differential terms. Our analysis of optimization paths in coupled neural PDE discovery reveals three behaviors: the exact support can persist to the end of training, appear only transiently, or fail to emerge. To decouple equation selection from neural optimization, we develop a freeze-then-select method combining a structured field adapter with Stability-Validated Weak Selection (SVWS). Trained from observations without a PDE residual, the adapter factorizes the field into learned spatial features and temporal coefficients represented by cubic splines. After freezing the field, SVWS identifies recurrent terms across independent weak-form systems, refits candidate supports, and selects the final equation on held-out weak-form systems. Beyond fixed libraries, we apply the same principle to expressions generated by genetic programming and recover the power-law form of an unknown nonlinear diffusion function from sparse, noisy observations. Across all six sparse MDBench regimes, our method attains the highest exact support recovery rate, with its clearest gains over classical and neural baselines on challenging Kuramoto-Sivashinsky dynamics.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [162] [Advances, challenges, and opportunities for legged robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28952)
*Jonas Frey,Matías Mattamala,Hae-Won Park,Mayank Mittal,Georg Martius,Maike Osborne,Robert Sparrow,Marco Hutter*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A landscape-level synthesis of humanoid and quadrupedal robots: summarizing capabilities across hardware, locomotion, autonomy, data, and applications; highlighting advances, open challenges, and a forward-looking view on societal, ethical, economic, and policy implications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how legged robots can transform work, interaction, and scientific discovery, and to anticipate the societal and policy-related implications of their adoption.

Method: Literature synthesis and cross-domain assessment across hardware, locomotion, autonomy, data, and applications; identifies advances and persistent challenges; develops an outlook on ethics, economics, policy, and societal impact.

Result: Identifies key recent advances and major open challenges hindering widespread adoption; provides a roadmap for enabling new use cases; outlines expected societal and economic effects and policy considerations.

Conclusion: Legged robots have transformative potential, but realizing it requires coordinated advances across technical, ethical, and policy dimensions; proactive consideration of societal impact is essential for responsible deployment.

Abstract: Humanoid and quadrupedal robots have the potential to revolutionize the way we work, interact, and coexist with intelligent machines. To understand their effects on society and how they can enable scientific discovery, we assess the current capabilities of these systems along hardware, locomotion, autonomy, data, and applications. We identify recent advances and key open challenges that must be overcome to enable widespread adoption and new use cases for legged robots. Last, we provide an outlook on the future of legged robots, exploring their ethical considerations, economic potential, policy implications, and broader societal effects.

</details>


### [163] [ST-WAM: Semantic-Temporal World Action Model for Robust Manipulation under Visual Distribution Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28993)
*Mingxin Wang,Bin Hu,Bin Qian,Kaitao Jiang,Haoning Wu,Feng Yan,Bowen Jing,Ruiyang Hao,Enyi Wang,Kangning Niu,Yandan Yang,Mu Xu,Yan Wang,Houde Liu,Tianlun Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ST-WAM uses semantic-temporal modeling to boost robustness of World Action Models by integrating semantic DINOv3 features with pixel-level VAE dynamics via Dual-Space Future Experts and Current-Anchored Intent Retrieval, mitigating Training-Distribution Hallucination and achieving strong gains under visual shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pixel-based future supervision in WAMs can entangle action-relevant dynamics with task-irrelevant visual content, especially under visual distribution shifts; there is a need for stable semantic representations to maintain task-state fidelity.

Method: Propose Semantic-Temporal WAM (ST-WAM) with DINOv3 as a shared semantic representation. Dual-Space Future Experts (DSFE) jointly predict future VAE latents and DINO features. Current-Anchored Intent Retrieval (CAIR) retrieves task-relevant evidence from recent DINO history under the current visual-language context. End-to-end training without extra embodied pretraining or explicit future generation at inference.

Result: ST-WAM achieves 98.7% on LIBERO and 92.8% on RoboTwin 2.0. It improves zero-shot LIBERO-Plus performance by 21.3 percentage points over Fast-WAM and more than doubles real-world success under visual shifts (25.8% to 61.5%).

Conclusion: Semantic-temporal modeling complements pixel-generative dynamics in WAMs, yielding robust manipulation under visual distribution shifts without requiring additional annotations or explicit future generation at inference.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm by jointly modeling robot actions and future visual dynamics. However, their reliance on pixel-generative future supervision can entangle action-relevant state transitions with task-irrelevant visual content, limiting robustness under visual distribution shifts. We identify Training-Distribution Hallucination, a recurring phenomenon in which futures conditioned on visually shifted observations hallucinate training-domain content rather than remain faithful to the current scene. A controlled frame-triplet diagnosis further shows that DINOv3 features remain more stable across visual shifts while better preserving task-state distinctions than Wan-VAE latents. Rather than correcting the predicted futures, we propose Semantic-Temporal WAM (ST-WAM) to improve action robustness by using DINOv3 as a shared semantic representation for future prediction and history retrieval while retaining fine-grained VAE dynamics. Its Dual-Space Future Experts (DSFE) jointly predict future VAE latents and DINO features, while Current-Anchored Intent Retrieval (CAIR) retrieves task-relevant evidence from recent DINO history under the current visual-language context. ST-WAM is trained end-to-end without additional embodied pretraining or task-specific annotations, and requires no explicit future generation at inference. It achieves 98.7% on LIBERO and 92.8% on RoboTwin 2.0; more importantly, compared with Fast-WAM, it improves zero-shot LIBERO-Plus performance by 21.3 percentage points and more than doubles real-world success under visual shifts from 25.8% to 61.5%. These results demonstrate that semantic-temporal modeling effectively complements pixel-generative dynamics for robust manipulation.

</details>


### [164] [Receding-Horizon Next-Best-View Planner for Autonomous Leaf Surface Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28995)
*Arif Ahmed,Sajal K. Das,Parikshit Maini*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes Centroid-based Information Gain NBV planning with a receding-horizon variant for leaf surface reconstruction under travel constraints; demonstrates improvements over an attention-driven baseline on LAST-STRAW dataset, achieving up to 10% error reduction, especially under inter-leaf occlusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient, task-driven NBV planning for large-scale field leaf reconstruction under limited planning budgets and computation resources; addresses limitations of prior work focusing on rigid objects, point-cloud coverage, or general plant reconstruction.

Method: Introduce Centroid-based Information Gain (CIG) to quantify viewpoint utility via spatial distribution of observed points relative to point-cloud centroid; develop receding-horizon variant that optimizes over future viewpoints; evaluate on LAST-STRAW strawberry plant dataset; compare to attention-driven NBV using visibility-based information gain.

Result: Receding-horizon approach consistently reduces surface reconstruction error and improves geometric fidelity across growth stages, especially under higher inter-leaf occlusion; improvement up to 10% over baseline.

Conclusion: CIG-based NBV with a receding-horizon framework provides more effective viewpoint planning for leaf surface reconstruction under travel constraints, enhancing accuracy and robustness to occlusion in autonomous field robotics.

Abstract: Accurate plant leaf modeling is fundamental to downstream tasks such as plant growth monitoring, and phenotyping for yield estimation. Autonomous robotic reconstruction for large-scale field deployment must address limitations on robot planning budget and computation resources while optimizing viewpoint utility for leaf surface reconstruction. Existing approaches either focus on rigid objects, point-cloud coverage or plant reconstruction without fully addressing the system limitations or exploiting task-driven point cloud utility. In this work, we study next-best-view (NBV) planning for leaf surface reconstruction under travel constraints. We develop a novel Centroid-based Information Gain (CIG) function that measures the spatial distribution of observed points relative to the centroid of the existing point cloud to compute viewpoint utility. We also develop a receding-horizon variant that reasons over future viewpoints. To benchmark our work, we use the LAST-STRAW [1] public dataset that includes point clouds of strawberry plants over different growth stages and compare our method with attention-driven NBV [2] that uses a visibility-based information gain approach. The proposed receding-horizon approach consistently reduces surface reconstruction error and improves geometric fidelity across multiple growth stages, especially under increased inter-leaf occlusion. Results demonstrate that our approach is able to visit viewpoints that reduce surface reconstruction error and improves reconstruc-tion accuracy as compared to the baseline by upto 10%.

</details>


### [165] [D-VLC: Decentralized Vision-Language Collaboration for Heterogeneous Embodied Multi-Robot Systems in Unknown Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29009)
*Yuan Zhou,Ruitong Lin,Shen Wang,Weiqi Gai,Mo zhu,Xin Zhou,Yuze Wu,Fei Gao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Decentralized asynchronous VLM/LLM-based framework enabling heterogeneous robot swarms to interpret instructions, decompose tasks, and generate robot-specific actions without task/robot-specific training; achieves >70% success and up to ~55.8% faster completion vs geometric greedy baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome limitations of rule-based, map-dependent, centralized decision making; leverage semantic reasoning and visual perception to handle complex, unseen tasks and heterogeneous agents.

Method: Decentralized asynchronous reasoning; lightweight information sharing; capability-aware collaboration; unified action interface; actions executed by learning-free experts; no task/robot-specific training; evaluation across diverse scenarios with multiple VLMs.

Result: Experimental results show success rates above 70% and completion time reductions up to 55.8% relative to geometric greedy baseline.

Conclusion: Framework generalizes to heterogeneous robots and unseen tasks without task-specific training, improving efficiency; scalable and less dependent on maps or centralized control.

Abstract: Multi-robot systems, particularly heterogeneous robot swarms, can improve the efficiency of complex task execution through parallel collaboration and complementary capabilities. However, conventional rule-based methods rely on predefined task models and specialized decision making programs, making it difficult to understand complex semantic instructions and coordinate heterogeneous robots. LLMs introduce strong language understanding and task reasoning capabilities, allowing multi-robot systems to interpret instructions, decompose tasks, and assign roles according to task semantics. VLMs further incorporate visual perception, enabling robots to reason about objects, regions, and spatial relationships in physical environments. Nevertheless, existing LLM/VLM based methods often depend on known maps, centralized and synchronized decision making, limiting their generalization to heterogeneous robots and unseen tasks. We therefore propose a framework that combines decentralized asynchronous reasoning, lightweight information sharing, capability aware collaboration, and a unified action interface, enabling general purpose VLMs to generate robot specific actions executed by learning free experts without task or robot specific training. Experiments across diverse scenarios and multiple VLMs show success rates above 70\%, with completion time reduced by up to 55.8\% relative to the geometric greedy baseline.

</details>


### [166] [DART: Dual-Axis Airborne Reachability-Gated Torque-Reaction for Off-Road Vehicle Jumps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29011)
*Yu Hu,Fangzhou Zhao,Mingyuan Sang,Cheng Min,Liang Chen,Wei Li,Wenyu Kuang,Shican Chen,Jinwei Li,Baolei Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DART improves control during airborne phase of high-speed traversal, back-propagating landing constraints into a conservative feasible-takeoff set and in-flight wheel-reaction torque control. In BeamNG.tech simulations, it outperforms RW-PD and TOBB, reducing touchdown speeds and increasing on-target landings; hardware validation is pending.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify airborne-phase instability and crash hazard during rough-terrain takeoffs/landings; derive certified takeoff envelopes and robust in-flight control to mitigate crash risk.

Method: 1) Quantify wheel angular-momentum budget to establish recoverable pitch-rate ceilings under typical takeoff wheel speeds and drivetrain limits. 2) Develop DART (Dual-Axis Airborne Reachability-Gated Torque-Reaction) to back-propagate landing constraints into a feasible-takeoff set and derive a pre-takeoff speed-shaping law. 3) Implement steer-resolved wheel-reaction torque with a per-flight roll latch derived from yaw-coupling analysis for in-flight control. 4) Validate via deterministic full-scale simulation in BeamNG.tech, comparing DART against RW-PD and TOBB. 5) Analyze banked run-ups and disturbance regimes; assess pitch/roll allocation and yaw coupling. 6) Note that all results are simulation-based; hardware validation remains open.

Result: Airborne phase is barely controllable: wheel angular-momentum budget caps recoverable pitch-rate change at roughly 9–13 deg/s nose-up under typical takeoff wheel speeds, about twice that in braking direction; driving wheels to hard limit raises the ceiling to 16–18 deg/s. Takeoff disturbances beyond these budgets are unrecoverable, so leverage is pre-takeoff. DART back-propagates landing constraints into a closed-form feasible-takeoff set, providing a conservative go/no-go and a pre-takeoff speed-shaping law. In BeamNG.tech, a calibrated pre-takeoff regulator reduces touchdown speed by 36% and raises on-target landings from 0/30 to 30/30. Under steep-lip approach, airborne law yields 29/30 safe landings vs 0/30 for RW-PD and TOBB. On banked run-ups, median pitch error ≤ 2 degrees; largest baseline separation at γ=12 degrees. Across disturbances, the latch preserves pitch-only allocation in low-disturbance entries and enables dual-axis control when roll binds. All results are from simulation; hardware validation remains open.

Conclusion: DART provides a conservative, certified-feasible-takeoff set and robust in-flight coordinated pitch/roll control via steer-resolved wheel-reaction torque, improving landing safety and performance in simulation. Hardware validation is needed to confirm real-world applicability.

Abstract: Traversing crests, ledges, and ditches at high speed often launches vehicles into the air, and a mishandled landing presents a substantial crash hazard. We show that the airborne phase is barely controllable: on a 1383 kg platform the wheel angular-momentum budget caps the recoverable pitch-rate change at roughly $9$-$13^\circ$/s in the tighter nose-up direction under drive at typical takeoff wheel speeds, and at about twice that in the reverse-inclusive braking direction; driving the wheels to their drivetrain hard limit raises the measured nose-up ceiling to only $16$-$18^\circ$/s. Takeoff pitch-rate disturbances beyond this directional budget are physically unrecoverable in flight, so the decisive leverage lies before takeoff. DART (Dual-Axis Airborne Reachability-Gated Torque-Reaction) back-propagates the landing constraint into a closed-form certified feasible-takeoff set, which supplies a conservative go/no-go condition and a pre-takeoff speed-shaping law. In flight, DART regulates pitch and roll via steer-resolved wheel-reaction torque, governed by a per-flight roll latch derived from the yaw-coupling analysis. In deterministic full-scale simulation in BeamNG.tech, a calibrated pre-takeoff speed regulator reduces touchdown speed by 36% and raises on-target landings from 0/30 to 30/30. Under the same steep-lip approach the airborne law completes 29/30 safe landings under crash-avoidance bounds versus 0/30 for reaction-wheel-style PD (RW-PD) and time-optimal bang-bang (TOBB). On banked run-ups DART holds the median pitch error at or below $2^\circ$ at every cross-slope, with the largest baseline separation at $γ=12^\circ$. Across disturbance regimes, the latch preserves pitch-only allocation on low-disturbance entries and enables dual-axis control when roll becomes binding. All results are from simulation; hardware validation remains open.

</details>


### [167] [Auto-JEPA: A Latent World Model of Continuous Intent for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29031)
*Jiwei Yang,Zhengxian Chen,Chaosheng Huang,Jun Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Auto-JEPA reframes autonomous planning as predicting a future intent embedding rather than reconstructing the full future world; it aligns a latent intent with future ego trajectories, retrieves trajectories from a fixed memory, and uses a scene-conditioned scorer for selection, achieving strong planning metrics while keeping a frozen visual encoder and no explicit perception labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autonomous-driving world models emphasize dense future predictions, which are costly and may include non-essential information for planning. The authors posit that planning hinges on features that influence ego actions, enabling more efficient, action-oriented representations without full-s future world reconstruction.

Method: Inputs: visual observations, egomotion history, and navigation commands. The model learns a continuous future driving intent by joint-embedding prediction, aligning an intent embedding with the latent representation of the future ego trajectory. Retrieved executable trajectories come from a fixed trajectory memory and are ranked by a scene-conditioned candidate selection module. The visual encoder is frozen; no perception annotations or learned trajectory generator are used; optimization targets only trajectory representation, intent prediction, and candidate selection modules.

Result: Achieves 91.3 PDMS on NAVSIM v1 and 89.1 EPDMS on NAVSIM v2. Semantic occlusion experiments show masking dynamic-agent regions causes ~2.97x greater intent change than random masks, and occluding influential vehicles substantially alters predicted intent and selected trajectory while occluding non-influential ones does not. Indicates future-intent prediction focuses on planning-relevant features and enables high-quality planning with less dense future-world modeling.

Conclusion: Shifting focus to future intent rather than full future-world reconstruction yields planning-efficient models that depend less on dense perception annotations or trajectory generation, while producing robust planning performance and interpretable sensitivity to influential agents.

Abstract: Existing autonomous-driving world models typically perform dense prediction of future videos, occupancy states, BEV representations, or agent motion. We argue that planning need not reconstruct the complete future world, but only focus on scene features that affect future ego action. Based on this perspective, we propose Auto-JEPA, an action-oriented latent world model that learns continuous future driving intent through joint-embedding prediction. Given visual observations, egomotion history, and navigation commands, Auto-JEPA predicts an intent embedding aligned with the latent representation of the future ego trajectory. The predicted intent retrieves executable trajectories from a fixed trajectory memory, which are then ranked by a scene-conditioned candidate selection module. Auto-JEPA keeps the visual encoder frozen, requires no explicit perception annotations, and uses no learned trajectory generator. By optimizing only task-specific modules for trajectory representation, intent prediction, and candidate selection, Auto-JEPA achieves 91.3 PDMS on NAVSIM v1 and 89.1 EPDMS on NAVSIM v2. Semantic occlusion experiments show that masking dynamic-agent regions induces an average intent change 2.97x that of equal-area random masking. Moreover, occluding vehicles that affect future driving substantially changes the predicted intent and selected trajectory, whereas both remain essentially unchanged when non-influential vehicles are occluded. These results show that future-intent prediction encourages the model to focus on planning-relevant visual features and supports high-quality planning without dense future-world modeling.

</details>


### [168] [Outcome-Guided Distillation: A Teacher-Student Framework to Advance VLM Reasoning in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29052)
*Zeyu Dong,Yimin Zhu,Yu Wu,Yu Sun*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A teacher-student framework for reasoning-guided end-to-end autonomous driving using Vision-Language Models (VLMs). The teacher performs reflective reasoning with VLM explanations refined by action supervision; a student distills this reasoning. A separate waypoint decoder converts textual reasoning into trajectories. The approach aims for interpretable, robust driving with zero-shot generalization and improved efficiency; ~24% gain over a non-reasoning baseline on Waymo.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tackle the black-box nature and labeling burden of E2E driving by injecting structured, interpretable reasoning via VLMs while enabling zero-shot generalization without intermediate labels. Combine geometry-aware trajectory decoding with reasoning to improve robustness and efficiency.

Method: A teacher model generates reflective reasoning: the VLM provides logical explanations that are refined under supervision of ground-truth actions. This enables zero-shot generalization without intermediate labels. A student model is trained by supervised fine-tuning to distill the teacher's reasoning. A separate waypoint decoder translates textual reasoning into continuous trajectories. A staged inference engine integrates reasoning into driving prediction.

Result: On Waymo benchmarks, the framework outperforms classical reasoning-based baselines in zero-shot reasoning, waypoint accuracy, and inference efficiency. The reasoning text contributes significantly to driving inference, yielding about a 24% improvement over an identical model that lacks reasoning.

Conclusion: The approach advances reasoning-driven autonomous driving by achieving interpretable and deployable systems that leverage explicit reasoning to guide driving predictions, with demonstrated performance gains.

Abstract: End-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving aims to learn a direct mapping from visual observations to control actions. However, these E2E models often act as black boxes and struggle with complex scenarios. To address this, recent works incorporate Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to provide explicit reasoning, enhancing both interpretability and driving robustness. These approaches typically rely on pre-generated annotations, which suffer from potentially flawed labels and require costly human labor. In this work, we propose a new framework that integrates structured reasoning and geometric precision through a teacher-student architecture. The teacher model introduces reflective reasoning, where the VLM generates logical explanations and then reflectively refines the reasoning under the supervision of ground-truth action. This enhances zero-shot generalization without intermediate labels. The student model distills the teacher's reasoning capabilities via supervised fine-tuning. We also design a separate waypoint decoder that interprets textual reasoning into continuous trajectories. Our proposed solution integrates two goals: providing explicit reasoning for interpretability and delivering robust and accurate driving performance. It leverages the synergy between these two objectives within a staged inference engine to enhance driving performance and explicitly uses the reasoning to guide driving prediction. Evaluated on Waymo benchmarks, our framework outperforms classical reasoning-based baselines in zero-shot reasoning, waypoint accuracy, and inference efficiency. Our experiments validate this design, demonstrating that the reasoning text makes a significant contribution to driving inference, resulting in around a 24% improvement in performance compared to an identical model that lacks reasoning. Our work advances reasoning-driven autonomous driving toward interpretable and deployable systems.

</details>


### [169] [VSTaI: Design and Characterization of Variable-Stiffness Tactile Interfaces Based on 3D-Printed Structured Fabrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29102)
*Yiting Mo,Xinyuan Mao,Jashan Preet Singh,Fernando Bello*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A vacuum-induced jamming tactile interface using structured fabrics yields tunable stiffness for realistic palpation, with four fabric patterns tested; vacuum increases stiffness, enabling a wide modulus range and uniformity in certain patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Realistic soft-tissue palpation requires real-time, tunable stiffness rendering in compact, conformable devices; conventional simulators struggle to reproduce stiffness changes accurately.

Method: A vacuum-sealed fabric layer is sandwiched between two silicone layers to form a variable-stiffness interface (VSTaI). Stiffness is controlled by internal pressure. Four fabric patterns with different geometric parameters were fabricated and evaluated via force–indentation tests under atmospheric and vacuum conditions. Patterned fabrics include circular chainmail and other geometries with varying density.

Result: Vacuum jamming substantially increases stiffness across patterns and geometries, producing an effective modulus ranging from sub-megapascal to megapascal levels. One configuration showed up to a 140% stiffness increase in the jammed state. Circular chainmail patterns provided the most uniform spatial distribution of tactile stiffness, while denser geometries achieved higher peak stiffness. The device conformed well to underlying geometry.

Conclusion: Structured-fabric jamming is a practical approach for creating shape-conformable, tunable-stiffness displays suitable for physical examination training.

Abstract: Realistic palpation training requires reliable rendering of soft tissue stiffness changes in real time, which is difficult to achieve with conventional simulators. This paper presents a compact, variable-stiffness tactile interface (VSTaI) based on vacuum-induced jamming of 3D-printed structured fabrics. A vacuum-sealed fabric layer is sandwiched between two silicone layers, and stiffness is tuned by regulating internal pressure. Four fabric patterns with different geometric parameters were fabricated and evaluated using force-indentation tests under atmospheric and vacuum conditions. Across the tested pattern and geometry combinations, vacuum jamming increased stiffness significantly, producing an effective modulus from sub-megapascal to megapascal levels. Specifically, one configuration exhibited a stiffness increase of up to 140% under the jammed state. Circular chainmail patterns provided the most spatially uniform distribution of tactile stiffness, while denser geometries reached higher peak stiffness. VSTaI was also shown to exhibit excellent conformability to the underlying geometry. These results support structured-fabric jamming as a practical approach for shape-conformable, tunable-stiffness displays aimed at physical examination training.

</details>


### [170] [ActFovea: Runtime Safeguarding for VLA Policies via Spatiotemporal Visual-Action Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29169)
*Wenda Yu,Tianshi Wang,Fengling Li,Xin Li,Jingjing Li,Lei Zhu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ActFovea is a plug-and-play safeguarding framework for vision-language-action policies that detects and mitigates runtime disturbances by enforcing action-conditioned, spatiotemporal consistency between visuals, proprioception, and actions, without retraining, and proven effective in LIBERO suites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA policies suffer from runtime disturbances that break temporal alignment among vision, state, and actions; need runtime safeguarding without modifying the underlying policy.

Method: Construct action-conditioned foveated regions using robot kinematics, proprioceptive states, and recent actions to preserve contact-relevant areas and predicted motion corridors while suppressing task-irrelevant visual content. Detect runtime risks by assessing consistency between visual motion, observation freshness, and geometric/proprioceptive/action transitions. For recoverable disturbances, generate disturbance-specific candidate observations and validate the resulting action chunk before acceptance; if recovery is impossible, trigger a bounded safe-failure procedure. Evaluate π0 across LIBERO suites in closed-loop settings.

Result: Localized visual overlays: success rises from 49.3% to 90.3%, closing 93.7% of the gap to clean performance. Improvements under action drift (+7.0 pp) and visual delay (+9.8 pp) while preserving clean-task performance. Under frozen-observation replay, safe-failure triggers in all trials with no unprotected failures.

Conclusion: Spatiotemporal visual-action consistency is an effective basis for runtime safeguarding of VLA policies.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies achieve strong performance in robotic manipulation but remain vulnerable to runtime disturbances that break the temporal alignment among visual observations, robot states, and executed actions. We introduce ActFovea, a plug-and-play safeguarding framework that detects and mitigates such failures without retraining or modifying the underlying VLA policy. ActFovea uses robot kinematics, proprioceptive states, and recent actions to construct action-conditioned foveated regions that retain contact-relevant areas and predicted motion corridors while suppressing task-irrelevant visual content. It detects runtime risks by evaluating whether visual motion and observation freshness remain consistent with geometric, proprioceptive, and action transitions. For recoverable disturbances, ActFovea constructs disturbance-specific candidate observations and accepts a recovery only after verifying the resulting action chunk. When stale or replayed observations make reliable recovery impossible, it invokes a bounded safe-failure procedure. In closed-loop evaluations of $π_0$ across multiple LIBERO suites, ActFovea increases success under localized visual overlays from 49.3\% to 90.3\%, closing 93.7\% of the gap to clean performance. It further improves success under action drift and visual delay by 7.0 and 9.8 percentage points, respectively, while preserving clean-task performance. Under frozen-observation replay, ActFovea triggers timely safe failure in all trials, with no unprotected failures. These results demonstrate that spatiotemporal visual-action consistency provides an effective basis for runtime safeguarding of VLA policies.

</details>


### [171] [CLIFT: Turning Gemini Robotics On-Device into Humanoid Specialists via Non-Invasive Closed-Loop Iterative Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29172)
*Yuxin Chen,Hari Srikanth,Nathan Jew,Menglin Wu,Pengcheng Wang,Junli Ren,Masayoshi Tomizuka,Peng Xu,Jinyu Xie,Thomas Tian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Managed-API fine-tuning for closed-weight robot foundation models can outperform open-weight baselines but may not reach deployment-level mastery; introducing CLIFT, a closed-loop method that converts deployment rewards into API-compatible data to achieve near-perfect humanoid manipulation after two iterations without accessing model internals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prop proprietary, closed-weight robotics limit adaptation to new tasks and settings; managed SFT APIs enable policy tuning without exposing weights, but constrain learning to imitation and hinder closed-loop improvements essential for agile, contact-rich humanoids.

Method: Empirical study on a real humanoid using Gemini Robotics On-Device (GROD). Compare direct API-based SFT against an open-weight VLA trained on identical demonstrations; develop CLIFT to transform deployment-time reward feedback into supervised data consumable by the API, enabling closed-loop updates without weights/gradients/likelihoods/losses. Evaluate after two flywheel cycles.

Result: API-based SFT beats the open-weight VLA on the same demos but does not reach deployment-level mastery; CLIFT closes the gap, driving GROD to near-perfect success after two cycles.

Conclusion: Closed-loop improvement within a managed-API regime is feasible and effective for humanoid control, enabling substantial performance gains without model access, and offering a practical path toward deployment-ready policies using proprietary foundation models.
