Abstract: While robot foundation models are growing increasingly capable, the strongest models are typically trained on proprietary data and remain closed-source, limiting downstream users' ability to adapt them to new tasks, embodiments, and deployment settings. Following the LLM community, an emerging access paradigm for closed-weight robot foundation models is the managed supervised fine-tuning (SFT) API, where users submit training data and receive a tuned policy without access to model weights, gradients, or training internals. While such APIs let downstream users leverage powerful proprietary foundation models, they restrict policy improvement to pure imitation, ruling out reinforcement learning and other closed-loop methods that rely on internal training signals. This limitation is particularly acute for agile, contact-rich humanoid manipulation, where the gap between policy outputs and deployed behavior is large due to novel states, action tracking dynamics, latency, and controller-specific failure modes. We study how effective this managed-API regime is for humanoid adaptation, and how closed-loop improvement can be realized within it to push policies toward task mastery. We conduct one of the first empirical studies of managed-API adaptation on a real humanoid, instantiated on Gemini Robotics On-Device (GROD). We find that direct SFT through the API substantially outperforms a leading open-weight VLA trained on the same demonstrations, yet still falls short of deployment-level mastery on agile, contact-rich tasks. To close this gap, we introduce CLIFT: Closed-Loop Iterative Fine-Tuning, which turns deployment-time reward feedback into API-compatible supervised data and enables closed-loop policy improvement without accessing weights, gradients, likelihoods, or losses-pushing GROD to near-perfect success after two flywheel cycles, all without "opening the model box."

</details>


### [172] [MROPE: A Multi-Robot Safe Cooperative Strategy via combined Predictive Safety Filters and Ellipse-based Constraint Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29203)
*Alice Rosetti,Lorenzo Pichierri,Domenico Cappello,Fabrizio Schiano,Giuseppe Notarstefano*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A hierarchical drone-swarm framework (MROPE) decouples mission performance from local safety, aggregates obstacles into per-drone safe ellipses to reduce computation, and combines distributed optimization, decentralized safety consensus, and local predictive safety filters to enable scalable, real-time multi-robot tracking in cluttered spaces; validated by simulations and real-world experiments with superior efficiency over centralized methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tracking a dynamic target with drone swarms in cluttered environments imposes high computational load for safe path planning and strict, local collision avoidance; existing centralized or dense-space methods struggle with scalability and real-time performance.

Method: Introduce MROPE, a hierarchical architecture that (1) aggregates obstacle geometry into a single safe bounding ellipse per drone, (2) uses distributed aggregative optimization for high-level swarm coordination, (3) applies a decentralized consensus scheme to compute safe areas, and (4) employs local Predictive Safety Filters for real-time collision avoidance.

Result: Virtual and real-world experiments demonstrate superior real-time efficiency and scalability of MROPE compared to centralized approaches, confirming the approach's practicality in dynamic, cluttered environments.

Conclusion: Decoupling mission coordination from local safety and compacting complex obstacles into safe ellipses, integrated with distributed optimization, decentralized safety consensus, and PSFs, enables scalable, real-time drone-swarm monitoring in cluttered spaces.

Abstract: Deploying drone swarms to track a dynamic target in cluttered environments presents severe computational and safety challenges. We propose MROPE, a hierarchical strategy that decouples the cooperative monitoring mission from strict local safety requirements. To overcome the computational bottlenecks typical of dense spaces, our approach dynamically aggregates complex obstacle geometries into a single safe bounding ellipse for each drone. Methodologically, this architecture is realized by combining distributed aggregative optimization for high-level swarm coordination, a decentralized consensus scheme for the safe area computation, and local Predictive Safety Filters (PSF) for real-time collision avoidance. Virtual and real-world experiments validate the framework, demonstrating superior real-time efficiency and scalability compared to centralized approaches.

</details>


### [173] [Event-Based Upper-Body Humanoid Teleoperation Under Challenging Illumination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29227)
*Haoyu Fu,Zhou Ge,Chengze Li,Chenzhao Sun,Ze Cui,Wenjing Zhou,Xulei Qin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Neuromorphic event-based vision enables real-time upper-body motion imitation with low latency and robust tracking in HDR/low-light, outperforming RGB baselines in fast/poorly lit scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome frame-based RGB limitations in high dynamic range scenes and rapid motions; leverage asynchronous, high-temporal-resolution event data and inertial fusion for real-time teleoperation.

Method: Perception Module with optimized event accumulation and gravity-aligned inertial fusion; Motion Module TWIST for online kinematic retargeting; hardware: Prophesee EVK4, NVIDIA Booster T1; 18-DoF humanoid upper body; end-to-end latency 23-34 ms.

Result: Demonstrates stable tracking and low latency; RGB baselines perform worse under tested HDR/low-light conditions; events advantageous for fast or poorly lit tasks, while RGB/hybrid sensing may excel in well-lit static scenarios.

Conclusion: Event-based sensing is a viable option for real-time teleoperation under challenging lighting and motion; validated on embedded hardware with practical latency, suggesting hybrid sensing strategies depending on scene dynamics.

Abstract: We present a real-time upper-body human-to-humanoid motion imitation framework driven by neuromorphic event-based vision. This work addresses practical perceptual bottlenecks of conventional frame-based RGB sensors, specifically their difficulty in high dynamic range (HDR) scenes and rapid motions due to fixed integration times. By leveraging the Prophesee EVK4 event camera, which operates asynchronously with high temporal resolution and a dynamic range exceeding 120 dB, our system supports stable tracking in conditions where standard vision pipelines degrade, such as severe backlighting and very low light environments below 5 lux. The architecture integrates a low-latency Perception Module, utilizing optimized event accumulation and gravity-aligned inertial fusion, with a causal Motion Module (TWIST) that performs online kinematic retargeting. We validate the system on an embedded NVIDIA Booster T1 platform and an 18-DoF humanoid upper-body setup, demonstrating an end-to-end photon-to-action latency of 23-34 ms and advantages over RGB baselines under our experimental setup. The results indicate a practical trade-off: events can be preferable for fast or poorly lit upper-body teleoperation, whereas well-lit static scenes may favor RGB or hybrid sensing.

</details>


### [174] [TacPrint: A Wearable Fingertip Tactile Sensor for Human-to-Robot Contact Reproduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29231)
*Yongxi Liu,Chaofan Zhang,Xingyu Zhang,Xiangyin Bao,Boyue Zhang,Shaowei Cui,Shuo Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Wearable 24-taxel fingertip tactile sensor (TacPrint) uses localized capacitive sensing with one-to-one taxel alignment and a real-to-sim-to-real pipeline to predict dense contact-depth maps, enabling improved manipulation in contact-rich tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for low-cost, scalable tactile sensing to enhance reliability of human-to-robot transfer in contact-rich tasks without hindering natural operation.

Method: Design of a wearable fingertip skin with inner-surface protrusions aligned to 24 capacitive taxels. A real-to-sim-to-real pipeline estimates a 35×26 contact-depth map from 24 capacitive signals. Network trained to predict depth and contact regions using simulation-generated labels and measured inputs. Evaluations include RMSE, IoU, centroid error, and contact-position error; closed-loop grasping with dense-depth feedback and tactile-guided compensation.

Result: Key performance: contact-region RMSE 0.223 ± 0.161 mm; weighted-centroid error 1.213 ± 2.379 pixels; IoU 0.829 ± 0.169. Depth error at contact center 0.085 ± 0.057 mm over 40 trials; contact-position error 0.250 ± 0.208 mm over 37 trials. Human-to-robot replay: grasping success 91.67%, wiping 90%. Closed-loop grasping: 87.5% overall, 85% edge-contact vs 67.5% and 45% with raw-taxel feedback.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates that a low-cost tactile sensor with dense-depth estimation and real-to-sim-to-real calibration substantially improves manipulation performance in contact-rich tasks, highlighting the viability of scalable tactile sensing for robot skill acquisition.

Abstract: Human-centric data collection is emerging as a significant paradigm for robot skill acquisition, but seamlessly integrating low-cost, scalable tactile sensing systems that capture fine-grained fingertip interactions without compromising natural operation remains a key challenge. This reduces the reliability of human-to-robot transfer in contact-rich tasks. In this work, we present TacPrint, a wearable fingertip tactile sensor, where protrusions on the inner surface of the silicone skin are aligned one-to-one with 24 capacitive taxels to enable localized capacitive responses. A real-to-sim-to-real pipeline estimates a 35 $\times$ 26 contact-depth map from 24-channel capacitive signals. Against simulation-generated labels, the model achieved a contact-region RMSE of 0.223 $\pm$ 0.161 mm, a weighted-centroid error of 1.213 $\pm$ 2.379 pixels, and an IoU of 0.829 $\pm$ 0.169. With measured capacitive inputs, the network-predicted depth evaluated at the guide-calibrated contact center showed a mean absolute error of 0.085 $\pm$ 0.057 mm across all 40 controlled trials, while the mean contact-position error was 0.250 $\pm$ 0.208 mm across the 37 trials whose reference contact regions were not truncated by the sensing boundary. In human-to-robot replay, tactile-guided compensation increased grasping and wiping success rates from 0% to 91.67% and 90%, respectively. In closed-loop grasping, dense-depth feedback achieved success rates of 87.5% over all tested positions and 85% under edge-contact conditions, compared with 67.5% and 45% for raw-taxel feedback.

</details>


### [175] [FBFM: A Training-Free Asynchronous Feedback Mechanism for Flow-Matching in World-Action Models Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29235)
*Peize Li,Ruimeng Zhang,Ru Zhang,Cong Huang,Kai Chen,Shanghang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces Feedback Flow Matching (FBFM), a training-free, online correction mechanism for world-action models that re-ground inside generated chunks via masked pseudoinverse corrections, reducing drift and improving responsiveness. Evaluated on DreamZero/LingBot-VA with LIBERO and RoboTwin2.0 tasks, yielding modest but consistent gains (~>5% in favorable settings) and better real-world tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon world-action models suffer drift and rely on coarse chunk-level grounding. There is a need for fine-grained, training-free online correction that can be applied within active prediction chunks to maintain accuracy between chunk boundaries.

Method: FBFM performs flow matching by applying a masked pseudoinverse correction to the conditional velocity field. It uses the preceding action chunk to influence the generation of the next chunk and leverages the observed image after the preceding chunk to guide the next frame prediction. This creates an asynchronous loop where feedback from one chunk informs the next, without training.

Result: On selected LIBERO and RoboTwin2.0 tasks, FBFM improves success rates by over 5% in favorable settings and yields notably better tracking in real-world observation-prediction diagnostics for both DreamZero (joint-generation) and LingBot-VA (stage-wise).

Conclusion: FBFM provides a training-free, fine-grained online correction paradigm that bridges open-loop flow generation with closed-loop real-world dynamics, enhancing responsiveness to unexpected events and reducing long-horizon drift.

Abstract: Although world-action models (WAMs) enhance long-horizon robot control by predicting visual evolution before acting, long-horizon reliability demands repeated re-grounding in real observations--not recursive rollout. Existing WAMs address this by refreshing history or KV cache with ground-truth data between chunks. However, such chunk-wise feedback operates at a coarse temporal granularity and thus fails to correct prediction errors at the individual time-step level. To address this, we propose Feedback Flow Matching (FBFM), a training-free inference mechanism that pushes re-grounding inside the actively generated chunk. During flow matching, FBFM applies a masked pseudoinverse correction to the conditional velocity field: it leverages the preceding action chunk to guide generation of the next action chunk, and uses the image observed after executing that preceding chunk to guide the next frame prediction. This cross-chunk pairing--where feedback from one chunk arrives in time to shape the next--creates an asynchronous loop that corrects errors without waiting for chunk boundaries. Being training-free, the mechanism improves responsiveness to unexpected events and suppresses drift in long-horizon tasks. We evaluate FBFM on both a joint-generation WAM (DreamZero) and a stage-wise WAM (LingBot-VA). On selected LIBERO and RoboTwin2.0 tasks, it improves success rates by over 5% in favorable settings, and real-world robot observation-prediction diagnostics show notably better tracking. We argue that FBFM offers a new paradigm for fine-grained online correction, bridging open-loop flow generation with closed-loop real-world dynamics.

</details>


### [176] [MDIR: A Task-Manifold Impedance Retargeting Method for Contact-Rich Teleoperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29271)
*Liu Jiahao,Kento Kawaharazuka,Tasuku Makabe,Kei Okada*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MDIR deterministically retargets a fixed Cartesian impedance into a manifold of task-channel variable-impedance commands, yielding safer, lower-variance contact-rich teleoperation from a single demonstration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of fixed Cartesian impedance in single-demonstration teleoperation by preserving local task-channel responses while reducing impact, force variability, and aggressiveness.

Method: Decompose the source impedance response via a passive residual in a control-chain metric, compute a Cartesian-to-Manifold (C2M) baseline, and use Manifold-Constrained Parameter Optimization (MPO) to select a feasible representative on the impedance manifold. Evaluate on Franka Panda with planar wiping, pick-and-place, and pushing across 15 closed-loop trials.

Result: MDIR passes Task Check in all 15 executions and reduces all four aggressiveness metrics (wrist-force peaks, impulse, force variability, and nominal controller power) compared with the fixed-impedance demonstrations.

Conclusion: MDIR enables deterministic, safer, and less aggressive execution for contact-rich tasks from a single demonstration by retargeting impedance onto a manifold with optimized parameters.

Abstract: Fixed Cartesian impedance makes contact-rich teleoperation demonstrations practical, but gains that secure progress and contact support also determine impact and force variability. We study single-demonstration controller-to-controller impedance retargeting. Given one fixed Cartesian impedance command sequence {K0, D0, xcmd}, Manifold-Decomposed Impedance Retargeting (MDIR) deterministically reparameterizes the recorded controller into an executable task-channel variable-impedance command. MDIR targets this local retargeting problem by preserving projected task-channel responses near the demonstrated trajectory. It represents the source response in operational work, exertion, and support channels with a passive residual complement under a control-chain metric, computes an executable Cartesian-to-Manifold Retargeting (C2M) baseline, and applies Manifold-Constrained Parameter Optimization (MPO) to select a feasible representative with lower wrist-force peaks, impulse, force variability, and nominal controller power. Across planar wiping, pick-and-place, and pushing on a Franka Panda, the full MDIR controller passes Task Check in all 15 closed-loop executions and reduces all four aggressiveness metrics relative to the fixed-impedance demonstrations.

</details>


### [177] [TRACT: Temporally Routed Action Chunks with Chronological Phase Authority for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29285)
*Jiahao Liu,Kento Kawaharazuka,Tasuku Makabe,Kei Okada*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TRACT: a phase-structured action chunking approach that uses a single boundary inside the future horizon to route action queries, achieving full-sequence success across six robots and reducing phase ambiguity and stalls compared with a flat package.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve temporal misalignment when action chunks cross procedural boundaries in imitation learning and improve robustness in contact execution by distinguishing current phase from boundary events.

Method: TRACT factorizes phase-structured chunking into an accepted current phase plus a single CURRENT-to-NEXT boundary within the horizon; uses a task-local graph to constrain chronological phase authority; employs a cumulative boundary distribution to route future queries along phase-specific paths; integrates a causal response-deficit mechanism for contact execution to compare policy intent with subsequent motion and accumulate compensation with decay after recovery.

Result: Empirical evaluation on six real-robot variants (10 trials each) yields 10/10 full-sequence success, 99.00 median wipe completion, zero phase ambiguity, zero stalls. Compared to a flat package, TRACT improves from 3/10 to 6/10 success and from 8.03% to 77.08% median wipe. Chronological authority reduces phase ambiguity from 8/10 to 0/10; response integration eliminates stalls from 4/10 to 0/10. The package comparison limitations prevent isolating routing effects.

Conclusion: TRACT delivers robust phase-aware action chunking with boundary routing that eliminates phase ambiguity and stalls and improves task success, though further isolation of routing effects and broader evaluation would strengthen claims.

Abstract: Action chunking shortens the effective decision horizon of robot imitation learning by predicting multiple future actions, while conventional phase conditioning describes the current control instant. When a predicted horizon crosses a procedural boundary, assigning the current phase to the entire chunk creates a structural temporal mismatch. We present TRACT, which factorizes phase-structured action chunking into an accepted current phase and a single CURRENT-to-NEXT boundary inside the future horizon. A task-local graph constrains chronological phase authority, and a cumulative boundary distribution monotonically routes future queries through phase-specific query and action paths. For contact execution, a causal response-deficit integrator compares policy intent with ACK-eligible subsequent motion, accumulates arm compensation when directional response is suppressed, and decays after confirmed recovery. Across six real-robot variants with ten trials each, full TRACT achieves 10/10 full-sequence success, 99.00 [88.75, 100.00]% median [min, max] wipe completion, zero observed phase ambiguity, and zero stalls. Under the current complete method package and evaluation setting, the routed representation obtains better observed task results than the flat package (6/10 vs. 3/10 success; 77.08% vs. 8.03% median wipe completion). Chronological authority reduces observed phase ambiguity from 8/10 to 0/10, and response integration reduces stalls from 4/10 to 0/10. The package comparison does not isolate routing from other generator-package differences.

</details>


### [178] [BWM: A Low-Cost High-Fidelity World Simulator for Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29302)
*BWM Team*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A low-cost, open-source action-conditioned world model (Boundless World Model) for robot manipulation that improves simulator fidelity, augments imitation-learning data with action-aligned rollouts, and enables policy evaluation; achieved top results on WorldArena.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitations of traditional physics simulators (asset-intensive calibration, sim-to-real gap) and video generators (poor fine-grained control) by providing an inexpensive, high-fidelity, open-source world model that can generate data and evaluate policies.

Method: An action-conditioned autoregressive world model integrating initial-environment guidance, dynamic visual history, and temporally aligned robot-action conditioning. It constructs action-aligned training clips via trajectory replay, overlapping clip sampling, and initial-observation enhancement. It serves as a data engine to augment imitation-learning data with action-aligned rollouts and as a policy evaluator for closed-loop assessment, risk anticipation, and policy ranking.

Result: Demonstrates improved simulator fidelity and functional utility in both data-engine and policy-evaluator settings. Achieves first place in the WorldArena Challenge across Track 1 and two Track 2 applications. Open-source release includes model checkpoints, training/inference code, and interfaces for data generation and policy evaluation.

Conclusion: BWM provides a practical, scalable solution to bring high-fidelity, controllable simulation to robot manipulation, enabling better data generation and policy evaluation while reducing development costs; its open-source ecosystem facilitates broader adoption and benchmarking.

Abstract: Reliable robot learning requires a world simulator that can predict action consequences before execution on physical hardware, including risky and failure-prone outcomes. Existing physics simulators require substantial asset construction and calibration and still face a sim-to-real gap, while video generators often lack precise control over their responses to fine-grained robot actions. In this paper, we present the Boundless World Model (BWM), an open-source, low-cost, high-fidelity world simulator for robot manipulation. BWM is an action-conditioned world model that combines initial-environment guidance, dynamic visual history, and temporally aligned robot-action conditioning for stateful autoregressive prediction of future observations. We construct action-aligned training clips through trajectory replay, overlapping clip sampling, and initial-observation enhancement. BWM serves as a data engine that augments imitation-learning data with action-aligned rollouts, and as a policy evaluator for closed-loop assessment, risk anticipation, and policy ranking. Experiments on the WorldArena benchmark and physical robots demonstrate improved simulator fidelity and functional utility across the data-engine and policy-evaluator settings. BWM ranks first overall in the WorldArena Challenge across Track 1 and its two Track 2 applications. We release the BWM open-source ecosystem, including model checkpoints, training and inference code, and interfaces for data generation and policy evaluation.

</details>


### [179] [SAGP: Semantic Affordance-Guided Grasp Planning via Coarse-Zone VLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29374)
*Muhayy Ud Din,Irfan Hussain*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A training-free pipeline that uses coarse-zone segmentation of object geometry and a VLM-based zero-shot evaluation to re-rank geometric grasps by functional appropriateness, bridging geometry and semantics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geometry-based grasp planning ensures validity but often neglects functional semantics; existing VLM approaches either require fine-grained segmentation or risk spatial hallucinations; there is no practical, training-free method to connect high-level semantics with geometric grasping.

Method: Partition the object point cloud into coarse spatial zones (top, middle, bottom, lateral sides, protrusions) using PCA alignment followed by distance-driven DBSCAN. A pre-trained vision-language model is queried in a zero-shot manner to assess grasp quality per zone. Zone scores are fused with geometric, reachability, and task-alignment cues to re-rank antipodal grasp candidates, without learned segmentation.

Result: On YCB objects in PyBullet with a Franka Panda robot, SAGP preserves the high success rate of geometry-only planning while substantially improving the functional appropriateness of selected grasps, notably for asymmetric, handle-bearing objects where geometry alone is uninformative.

Conclusion: The coarse-zone abstraction provides a training-free bridge between semantic reasoning and geometric grasp planning, avoiding fine-grained segmentation while enhancing functional grasp selection.

Abstract: Geometry-based grasp planners ensure physically valid grasps but ignore functional semantics, often generating grasps that are antipodal and collision-free yet practically inappropriate, for example, gripping a mug by its rim, a knife by the blade, or a bottle near its cap. These inconsistencies cause the downstream task to fail even when traditional grasp metrics are met. Existing vision-language model (VLM) approaches either depend on fine-grained, category-specific part segmentation or attempt to directly infer grasp poses, with the latter prone to spatial hallucinations. As a result, no practical, training-free framework has yet been proposed that robustly links high-level semantic reasoning to geometric grasp planning. We introduce Semantic Affordance-Guided Grasp Planning (SAGP), a training-free pipeline built on a coarse-zone abstraction layer. The method first partitions the object point cloud into spatial regions (top, middle, bottom, lateral sides, and protrusions) by applying PCA-based alignment followed by distance-driven DBSCAN clustering, entirely bypassing learned segmentation. A pre-trained VLM then assesses the grasp quality of each region through a structured zero-shot query, and the resulting zone-wise scores are fused with geometric, reachability, and task-alignment signals to re-rank antipodal grasp candidates. Experiments on YCB objects in PyBullet with a Franka Panda robot show that SAGP preserves the high success rate of geometry-only planning while substantially improving the functional appropriateness of selected grasps, particularly on asymmetric, handle-bearing objects where geometry alone is uninformative. The introduced coarse-zone abstraction offers an effective, training-free bridge between VLM-based reasoning and geometric grasp planning, without the need for fine-grained part segmentation.

</details>


### [180] [AquaJEPA: Action-Conditioned Multimodal Predictive Representations for Underwater Robot Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29393)
*Alan-Barsag Gazzaev,Alexey Gavrilov,Sergey Muravyov*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AquaJEPA is an action-conditioned, multimodal predictive model that fuses RGB, sonar, and proprioception with explicit sensor validity to predict future targets, velocity, and sonar profiles for receding-horizon planning, achieving robust closed-loop performance in underwater navigation and outperforming several baselines in a 120-environment replication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underwater robotic sensing is highly variable: visibility, viewpoint, and motion disrupt sensor reliability. There is a need for robust, action-conditioned, multimodal prediction that explicitly reasons about sensor validity to improve planning under uncertainty.

Method: Introduce AquaJEPA, an action-conditioned joint-embedding predictive model that fuses RGB, forward-looking sonar, and proprioception with explicit sensor validity. It predicts a future latent target conditioned on eight-thruster commands and provides velocity and sonar-profile predictions to a shared receding-horizon planner. The method includes ablations isolating EMA target, action margin, masks, and modality dropout; evaluated on Stonefish against baselines (reactive, state-only, ordinary multimodal, supervised dynamics, and recurrent world-model). A preregistered 120-environment replication tests generalization to unseen obstacle maps, varying water visibility, dynamic shifts, with intermittent DVL loss.

Result: AquaJEPA achieves 74 goals in 120 environments with scheduled DVL loss, vs 68 for state-only and recurrent world model baselines; it also attains the lowest mean final error (0.906 m). Paired final-error reductions relative to ordinary multimodal prediction, supervised dynamics, and recurrent world model are 0.273 m, 0.364 m, and 0.106 m (with CIs provided). It offers best aggregate closed-loop performance and significant gains over three baselines; however its advantage over state-only is not statistically resolved.

Conclusion: Explicit sensor-validated, action-conditioned multimodal fusion yields superior closed-loop navigation performance in underwater settings across diverse conditions, outperforming several baselines. While gains over state-only are not statistically significant in this study, AquaJEPA demonstrates robust, generalizable planning improvements and a principled framework for sensor reliability-aware prediction.

Abstract: Underwater robots combine complementary sensors whose reliability changes abruptly with water visibility, viewpoint, and vehicle motion. We introduce AquaJEPA, an action-conditioned joint-embedding predictive model that fuses an RGB camera, forward-looking sonar, and proprioception with explicit sensor validity. It predicts a future latent target conditioned on eight-thruster commands and supplies velocity and sonar-profile predictions to a shared receding-horizon planner. We study the method in Stonefish against reactive, state-only, ordinary multimodal, supervised dynamics, and recurrent world-model baselines. We further isolate the EMA target, action margin, masks, and modality dropout. A preregistered 120-environment replication comprises five independent replicates of a grid crossing three unseen obstacle maps, four water-visibility coefficients, and nominal versus shifted dynamics, while intermittently removing DVL observations. In 120 fresh paired environments with scheduled DVL loss, AquaJEPA reaches 74 goals, versus 68 for both state-only and the recurrent world model, and attains the lowest mean final error (0.906 m). Paired final-error reductions relative to ordinary multimodal prediction, supervised dynamics, and the recurrent world model are 0.273 m (95% CI: 0.190-0.356), 0.364 m (0.260-0.468), and 0.106 m (0.025-0.187), respectively. AquaJEPA therefore achieves the best aggregate closed-loop performance and significantly outperforms three action-conditioned predictive baselines in paired final error; its advantage over state-only remains statistically unresolved.

</details>


### [181] [Automated Straight-line Sewing of Stretchable Fabrics with Different Lengths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29464)
*Bingchen Jin,Akinari Kobayashi,Dipankar Bhattacharya,Akira Seino,Fuyuki Tokuda,Norman Chihnan Tien,Kazuhiro Kosuge*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A roller-based 6-DoF robotic system (DLRoSS) automates Different Length Alignment Sewing (DLAS) via four phases: wrap, real-time tension-based sewing, secure finishing with a press roller, and release, achieving consistent sewing across varying materials and lengths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DLAS is difficult to automate due to tension and alignment challenges when stretching shorter fabric to match a longer counterpart; a robust robotic solution is needed to ensure quality and repeatability across diverse fabrics and length ratios.

Method: A 6-DoF manipulator carries a roller-type end-effector with active shorter/longer fabric rollers and a passive press roller. The process comprises four phases: (P1) wrap and attach fabrics to feed rollers; (P2) real-time alignment via roller velocity control guided by sewing speed and known length ratio; (P3) final sewing with press roller to prevent slip under tension; (P4) release as fabric edge passes the press roller. 

Result: Experimental results show that DLRoSS achieves consistent, high-quality sewing of stretchable fabrics across different materials and lengths.

Conclusion: DLRoSS provides a viable, automated solution for DLAS by integrating roller-based tension control with a multi-phase sewing workflow, enabling reliable stitching of mismatched-length fabrics.

Abstract: Different Length Alignment Sewing (DLAS), which involves stretching the shorter fabric to match the longer one and sewing them together in a straight line, is a challenging task that needs to satisfy several requirements when automating the sewing process. To address the challenges, this research proposes a novel robotic sewing system, Different Length Robotic Sewing System (DLRoSS), which consists of a roller type end-effector, attached to a 6-DoF manipulator. The end-effector composed of active shorter and longer fabric rollers, and a passive press-roller attached to the shorter-fabric roller. Assuming that one end of the two fabric layers are initially positioned under the sewing machine's presser foot, the system automates DLAS by operating in four distinct phases. (P1) Fabric wrapping: Individual fabric layers are picked, held, and wrapped from the other end onto the feed rollers. (P2) Sewing: During the sewing, the shorter fabric is stretched and aligned with the longer fabric in real-time using roller velocity control based on the sewing speed and apriori known length ratio. (P3) Sewing completion: In the final sewing round on the fabric rollers, the press roller is engaged to prevent the stretched fabric from slipping off due to internal tension. (P4) Sewing fabric release: At the end of sewing, the fabric edge moves past the press roller, and the fabric releases from the rollers. Experimental results demonstrate that DLRoSS achieves consistent, high-quality sewing of stretchable fabrics of different materials and lengths.

</details>


### [182] [Temporal Policy: History-Initialized Action Generation for Robotic Learning from Demonstration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29482)
*Dylan Miller,Martin Jagersand*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Temporal Policy reframes action generation as a temporally coupled transport problem using stochastic interpolants, initializing from recent history to reduce transport cost and enable high-frequency closed-loop control in visuomotor tasks, with competitive success rates and low inference latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard diffusion and flow matching with uninformative Gaussian priors enforce high-cost vector fields, hindering multimodal LfD and real-time control; a data-dependent, efficient transport geometry is needed to achieve fast, accurate action generation.

Method: Introduce Temporal Policy: a generative framework based on stochastic interpolants that couples past states to future action sequences by initializing the flow at the robot's recent history, forming a data-dependent transport. Evaluate on visuomotor benchmarks and Barrett WAM 2x 7DoF teleoperation; compare to noise-initialized baselines; measure transport cost and inference latency; report code release.

Result: Transport costs reduced by nearly an order of magnitude relative to baselines; inference latency ~19.1 ms on RTX 4080; preserved state-of-the-art success rates; validated on simulation benchmarks and real platform; enabled high-frequency, closed-loop control; code released.

Conclusion: Temporal Policy achieves geometric and computational efficiency by bypassing independent Gaussian priors, enabling high-frequency, closed-loop visuomotor control while maintaining performance; represents a practical improvement for LfD in robotic systems.

Abstract: By relying on independent couplings from uninformative Gaussian priors, standard diffusion and flow matching models are forced to learn complex, high-cost vector fields to reach the physical action space. Generative models excel at capturing multimodal behaviors for robotic Learning from Demonstration (LfD), but often suffer from high inference cost. This paper introduces Temporal Policy, a generative framework based on stochastic interpolants that formulates action generation as a temporally coupled transport problem. By initializing the generative flow at the robot's recent history, we explicitly couple past states to future action sequences. This data-dependent coupling reduces transport cost and produces straight vector fields. We validate Temporal Policy across visuomotor simulation benchmarks and on a physical Barrett WAM 2x 7DoF teleoperation platform. Our approach reduces transport costs by nearly an order of magnitude compared to noise-initialized baselines, achieving a 19.1 ms inference latency on a single NVIDIA RTX 4080. Crucially, these geometric and computational efficiencies are achieved while matching the success rates of state-of-the-art baselines. This simplified transport geometry bypasses the computational bottleneck of independent Gaussian priors, helping enable high-frequency, closed-loop control. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/dmiller12/TemporalPolicy.

</details>


### [183] [Tri-Space Operational Control of Redundant Multilink and Hybrid Cable-Driven Parallel Robots Using an Iterative-Learning based Reactive Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29500)
*Dipankar Bhattacharya,Yin Pok Chan,Siqi Shang,Yuen Shan Chan,Ying Tan,Darwin Lau*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A tri-space control framework for cable-driven parallel robots (CDPRs) that fuses Reactive Control (RC) and Iterative-Learning Control (ILC) to perform online operational-space tracking under tri-space constraints, with a novel null-space parameterization to enhance ILC across repetitive tasks; validated via simulation and hardware on Diverse Multilink and Hybrid CDPRs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CDPRs exhibit two levels of redundancy and numerous constraints in joint, cable, and operational spaces (tri-space). Existing tri-space control methods are limited in robustness and applicability across architectures; a unified, real-time framework is needed to track operational trajectories while respecting tri-space constraints.

Method: Introduce a tri-space control framework combining Reactive Control (online trajectory tracking with feasible cable forces and avoidance of cable-link and joint interference and loss of manipulability) and Iterative-Learning Control (ILC) that optimizes a null-space parameter through a novel null-space vector parameterization to improve performance over repeated tasks.

Result: Simulation and hardware experiments on various Multilink CDPRs (MCDRs) and Hybrid CDPRs (HCDRs) demonstrate that the framework can track operational-space trajectories online with feasible cable forces while avoiding tri-space conflicts; ILC further improves performance across repetitions via the null-space parameterization.

Conclusion: The proposed tri-space framework provides a convenient and effective approach for real-time control of different CDPR architectures, addressing tri-space constraints and redundancy; the novel null-space parameterization enables performance gains through iterative refinement on repetitive tasks.

Abstract: Cable-Driven Parallel Robots (CDPRs) are a type of parallel mechanism in which cables are used as actuators. Due to the two levels of redundancy and numerous constraints within the CDPR actuation, joint and operational spaces (together known as the tri-space), tracking a given trajectory in the operational space while satisfying constraints in tri-space simultaneously is challenging. To the best of the authors' knowledge, there does not exist any tri-space control framework, which is robust, effective, and directly applicable to several architectures of redundantly actuated CDPRs. This paper proposes a tri-space control framework that combines Reactive Control (RC) and Iterative-Learning Control (ILC) to perform repetitive tasks in the operational space. The framework allows the tracking of operational space trajectories online with feasible cable forces, while avoiding undesirable situations such as cable-link interference, joint interference, and loss of manipulability. On the other hand, by finding an optimal parameter in the null space using a novel parameterization of a null space vector, the performance can be improved through ILC when the task is repeatedly executed. Simulation and hardware results on various Multilink Cable-Driven Robot (MCDRs) and Hybrid Cable-Driven Robots (HCDRs) show that the proposed tri-space control framework can be conveniently and effectively applied to the real-time control of different CDPRs.

</details>


### [184] [Homotopy-Aware Corridor Generation without Predefined Reference Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29513)
*Haoze Dong,Minghan Li,Meng Guo,Zhongkui Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A path-free corridor generation framework on graphs of convex sets (GCS) with adaptive multi-scale representation enabling homotopy-aware exploration and incremental updates; validated in simulation and hardware, yielding shorter, more robust trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing corridor generation methods rely on predefined reference paths, which bias corridor geometry and restrict the set of homotopy classes that can be explored. A path-free, adaptive representation derived from free space is needed to allow topologically diverse corridors and robust updates in uncertain environments.

Method: Develop a GCS-based corridor generation framework that constructs corridors as sequences of convex sets. Extend visibility-based deformation from paths to convex-set sequences to fuse topologically redundant corridors while preserving distinct alternatives. Introduce an adaptive multi-scale GCS with a sampling-based fine-scale graph for localized updates and a visibility-based coarse-scale graph for compact global exploration, ensuring topological consistency for incremental updates without full graph reconstruction.

Result: Efficient GCS construction and corridor generation; stable trajectory-level performance and shorter homotopy-aware trajectories compared with baselines; hardware validation on ground and aerial robots with onboard localization, demonstrating robustness to translated and unknown obstacles.

Conclusion: The framework enables reference-path-free corridor generation with adaptive, topologically consistent representations, allowing robust, real-time operation in uncertain environments and improving homotopy-aware planning compared with existing methods.

Abstract: Generating safe corridors is essential for collision-free robotic motion planning, yet most existing methods rely on predefined reference paths, which bias corridor geometry and implicitly limit the homotopy classes that can be explored. We propose a reference-path-free corridor generation framework on graphs of convex sets (GCS) that constructs corridors directly as sequences of convex sets, allowing corridor structure to emerge from the free-space representation rather than from a guiding path. To reason about similarity among corridors, we extend visibility-based deformation from paths to convex-set sequences, enabling the fusion of topologically redundant corridors while preserving distinct alternatives. To overcome the limited adaptability of existing GCS methods based on static global decompositions, we further develop an adaptive multi-scale GCS, in which a sampling-based fine-scale graph supports localized updates and a visibility-based coarse-scale graph enables compact global exploration. The two levels maintain topological consistency, allowing incremental updates without full graph reconstruction under environmental uncertainty. Numerical experiments characterize GCS construction, corridor generation, homotopy-aware exploration, and local updates, showing efficient graph construction, stable trajectory-level performance, and shorter-duration homotopy-aware trajectories than existing baselines. Hardware experiments on ground and aerial robots, including deployment with onboard localization, further validate the framework under translated and previously unknown obstacles.

</details>


### [185] [STAGE: STyle-controllable Action GEneration for personalized autonomous driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29517)
*Zihao Liu,Xing Liu,Yizhai Zhang,Panfeng Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A style-controllable imitation-learning framework (STAGE) for driving that allows continuous user-specified driving style values to generate action patterns, yielding behavior that aligns with human expectations and improves safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Drivers want autonomous systems to reflect their own driving style; current systems lack fine-grained style controllability. Reducing human-in-the-loop labeling cost via rule-based preference comparisons.

Method: Imitation learning-based training with style value and latent action modality encoding; preference learning to map user style to a monotonic continuous style value; rule-based data-pair comparisons to reduce labeling effort; inference uses a user-provided style value to generate actions.

Result: The STAGE framework achieves style-controlled action generation in typical road scenarios that aligns with human expectations, demonstrating style controllability, continuity, alignment with user preferences, and safety; comparisons show its unique advantages over alternative approaches; code is available at the provided GitHub link.

Conclusion: STAGE enables effective, user-aligned, and safe driving style control in autonomous systems, with reduced burden for preference labeling; practical via available implementation.

Abstract: Driving style refers to the behavioral preferences that drivers maintain during driving, shaped by their diverse experiences, habits, and needs, and is typically reflected in varying levels of aggressiveness. If humans choose to use autonomous driving systems, they would expect the driving style of the systems to closely resemble their own habit. However, this is challenging for current industrial autonomous driving systems. To address this, we developed a style controllable action generation method, STAGE, for driving tasks. Its training process is based on imitation learning, incorporating both style value and latent value action modality encoding. Preference learning is then used to identify the user's driving style as a continuous, monotonic style value. And to reduce the cost of human involvement in the preference training process, we also developed a set of rules to compare driving style in data pairs. Then, during inference, the user inputs the style value to control the generated action patterns, dynamically meeting the user's expectations. Using the STAGE method, we verified that the style-controlled action generation results in several typical road scenarios significantly align with human expectations. Furthermore, through comparisons between the STAGE method and various other approaches, we reveal the unique functionalities of STAGE, including its style controllability, style continuity, driving style alignment capability and driving safety. The code for this work is available at: https://github.com/CarlDegio/STAGE

</details>


### [186] [TransGraspNet: Physically and Geometrically Consistent Manipulation of Transparent Labware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29567)
*Hailing Hu,Mingyi Zhu,Yiquan An,Yifei Tian,Tianyou Zuo,Lifeng Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TransGraspNet is a geometry-physics consistent framework for robust manipulation of transparent glassware, combining boundary, surface, and physics consistency to improve perception fidelity and grasp reliability, achieving high success and zero spillage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-stage inconsistencies in transparent-object perception and grasping cause depth bleeding, distorted normals, and misaligned grasps, posing safety risks in liquid handling.

Method: Three coupled principles: boundary consistency for structurally reliable contours; surface consistency to preserve depth and surface normal fidelity during depth reconstruction; physics consistency to refine grasp selection via centroid alignment and wrench-space stability; evaluated on public benchmarks, a dedicated transparent glassware dataset, and a real robotic platform.

Result: Improved boundary quality and surface normal fidelity; strong task-level performance in cluttered scenes; high grasp success rates with zero spillage during high-speed liquid transport.

Conclusion: Geometry-physics consistent framework enabling reliable real-world manipulation of transparent objects by enforcing perception-execution consistency.

Abstract: Manipulating transparent laboratory glassware that contains liquid is inherently safety-critical: even small geometric errors can cause unstable grasps and hazardous spillage. Although recent progress has been made in transparent object perception and robotic grasping, most existing systems optimize detection, depth reconstruction, and grasp planning independently, which leads to cross-stage inconsistency imperfect boundaries induce depth bleeding, distorted surfaces corrupt normal estimation, and task agnostic grasp scoring yields tilted or off-center grasps that fail under dynamic motion. In this paper, we propose TransGraspNet, a geometry physics consistent framework that explicitly enforces consistency from perception to execution through three coupled principles: boundary consistency to produce structurally reliable object contours as downstream priors, surface consistency to preserve geometric fidelity and surface normal accuracy during depth reconstruction, and physics consistency to refine grasp selection with centroid alignment and wrench-space stability for upright and dynamically robust manipulation. We evaluate TransGraspNet on public benchmarks, a dedicated transparent glassware dataset, and a real robotic platform. The results show improved boundary quality and surface normal fidelity, and demonstrate strong task-level performance in cluttered transparent scenes. Most importantly, the proposed system achieves reliable real-world operation, including high grasp success rates in clutter and zero spillage during high speed liquid transport, highlighting the effectiveness of our method.

</details>


### [187] [Safe Vision Language Action Models via Barrier Enhanced Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29569)
*Kasra Sinaei,Hung-Chieh Wu,Donald Ebeigbe*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A modular inference framework combines Flow Matching generative models with formal Control Barrier Function safety guarantees by embedding safety into the denoising process, using a smooth Log-Sum-Exponential barrier over action chunks to enforce safety with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable safe, end-to-end generative control without post-hoc safety filters, costly retraining, or safety-specific data, while preserving model fidelity and success rates.

Method: Integrate Flow Matching denoising with a smooth Log-Sum-Exponential aggregate barrier to enforce safety across action chunks; prove that the generated distribution remains within a bounded 2-Wasserstein distance to the target; avoids external safety filters and retraining.

Result: Empirical validation on two robotic manipulation platforms and a 2D navigation task showing reliable safety without degrading model success rate and with minimal computational overhead.

Conclusion: The approach provides a versatile, safe-inference framework for flow-based generative control that maintains performance while guaranteeing safety, without requiring safety datasets or retraining.

Abstract: This article presents a modular inference framework that integrates Flow Matching generative models with formal Control Barrier Function (CBF) safety guarantees. Unlike existing methods that apply external safety filters to a model's final output, our approach modifies the Flow Matching denoising process within the model to inherently generate safe trajectories. By employing a smooth Log-Sum-Exponential aggregate barrier, we enforce safety over entire action chunks. This aggregate barrier ensures a minimal increase in computational overhead and does not alter the semantic intent of the model. We show that, within the proposed framework, the 2-Wasserstein distance between the generated distribution and the target distribution remains bounded. Our method eliminates the need for safety-specific datasets or costly model retraining, providing a versatile solution for safe inference. We validate the approach on two robotic manipulation platforms and a 2D navigation benchmark, verifying that our framework achieves reliable safety without degrading the success rate of the model.

</details>


### [188] [FibVLA: An Efficient Temporal Vision-Language-Action Model with Fibonacci Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29596)
*Li Lin,Wujun Xu,Weiwei Meng,Kaiwen Xia,Kang Hao Cheong,Shuai Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FibVLA is an efficient vision-language-action framework that captures long-context temporal information with minimal redundancy, achieving smoother, more successful actions and real-time responsiveness without retraining large encoders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reconcile the need for temporal context in VLAs with inference efficiency, addressing the efficiency drop from encoding long histories.

Method: Use logarithmic hindsight sampling of proprioceptive states and visual frames; adopt flow matching to model action distributions; employ Fibonacci recurrent inference for long-range planning with real-time closed-loop feedback.

Result: Significant improvements in action smoothness and task success rates, plus favorable efficiency compared to video-based baselines; real-world evaluations show real-time responsiveness without fine-tuning encoders.

Conclusion: FibVLA provides a scalable, efficient approach to long-context perception in VLAs, enabling improved embodied AI performance with maintained computational efficiency.

Abstract: Vision-language-action models (VLAs), which leverage the cognition of multimodal information to infer physical-world actions, provide a generalized solution for embodied AI applications. Conventional VLAs usually concentrate on current digital cognition. While some efforts are made to enhance VLAs' reasoning capabilities by capturing temporal information, encoding the long-context history causes an efficiency-decreasing issue. To reconcile the conflict between capturing temporal information and maintaining inference efficiency in VLAs, this paper introduces FibVLA, an efficient framework featuring temporal perception of long-context history. Specifically, we leverage logarithmic hindsight sampling to both proprioceptive states and visual frames to capture long-term temporal dependencies with minimal redundancy. For the action expert, we introduce the flow matching to produce action distributions, and the Fibonacci recurrent inference strategy to generate long-range planning steps based on real-time closed-loop feedback. Experiments demonstrate that FibVLA significantly improves action smoothness and success rates without retraining large-scale visual encoders. Efficiency analysis demonstrates superior real-time responsiveness compared to video-based baselines in real-world evaluations.

</details>


### [189] [HAM-VLN: Harnessing Hierarchical Agentic Memory for Zero-Shot Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29600)
*An Liu,Bingxi Liu,Hongyu Ding,Yixuan Jiang,Yaran Chen,Fulin Tang,Cong Leng,Hong Zhang,Jian Cheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HAM-VLN introduces a persistent, depth-grounded world graph with decision-coupled memory that stores semantic and reflective data during the action decision, enabling retrieval-based history without extra LLM calls and achieving strong, training-free VLN results with substantially reduced context length.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon vision-and-language navigation suffers from growing memory and reasoning bottlenecks when using continuous image streams or dense maps; a scalable, persistent memory structure is needed to maintain context without exponential LLM usage.

Method: HAM-VLN employs a single model call for action selection that also records memory into a depth-grounded world graph. Recent waypoints are kept verbatim within a bounded window; older history is retained via retrieval scored by relevance, recency, and salience, together with one-hop topological expansion. No additional LLM calls are made beyond the per-waypoint decision.

Result: Context length is reduced by more than 65%. The approach yields competitive SR on multiple VLN benchmarks without training: VLN-CE R2R 61.0% SR, VLN-CE RxR 52.7% SR, HM3D-v2 ObjectNav 79.7% SR.

Conclusion: A memory-efficient, training-free navigation framework that couples decision with persistent, depth-grounded memory; it achieves strong navigation performance while substantially reducing context length and avoiding extra LLM calls, outperforming prior methods on key VLN benchmarks.

Abstract: Vision-and-language navigation (VLN) enables robots to follow instructions in previously unseen environments. Recently, a training-free paradigm has emerged: the robot queries a multimodal LLM to understand its observations and plan the next action. However, long-horizon navigation based on either image streams or dense map inevitably introduces a growing memory and reasoning bottleneck. We present HAM-VLN, a decision-coupled, agent-authored memory that equips the robot with a persistent, depth-grounded world graph. In the same model call used to select the next action, HAM-VLN also records semantic and reflective information---including room type, objects, navigation progress, and failure notes. Recent waypoints remain verbatim within a bounded window, while older history re-enters the context only through retrieval scored by relevance, recency, and salience, together with one-hop topological expansion. This design requires no additional LLM calls beyond the per-waypoint decision. Compared to previous methods, HAM-VLN not only improves various navigation metrics but also reduces the context length by more than 65%. Specifically, HAM-VLN achieves 61.0% Success Rate (SR) on VLN-CE R2R, 52.7% SR on VLN-CE RxR, and 79.7% SR on HM3D-v2 ObjectNav without any training.

</details>


### [190] [WCM: A World Critic Model for Vision-Language-Action Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29613)
*Senyu Fei,Xiaopeng Yu,Siyin Wang,Xianzhong Zhao,Jingjing Gong,Xipeng Qiu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: World Critic Model (WCM) introduces explicit world modeling into RL for Vision-Language-Action robotic manipulation by jointly predicting future latent state and value estimates, addressing temporal dynamics beyond scalar returns; achieves SOTA across 149 tasks and real-world settings, compatible with Pi0, Pi0.5, OpenVLA-OFT, with on-/off-policy training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critics in RL for partially observable robotic control rely on single-frame observations, leading to poor temporal reasoning; naive history integration causes combinatorial complexity and scalar return supervision is insufficient to learn cross-temporal dynamics; need an explicit world-model objective for temporal structure.

Method: WCM uses LeJEPA-based lightweight architecture to jointly predict future latent state and estimate values; integrates into both on-policy and off-policy RL pipelines; compatible with existing VLA backbones (Pi0, Pi0.5, OpenVLA-OFT).

Result: Empirical evaluation on 149 tasks across four benchmarks shows state-of-the-art performance in in-distribution and out-of-distribution settings with strong generalization; seven real-world manipulation tasks validate stable deployment.

Conclusion: Explicit temporal/world modeling within the critic via WCM mitigates state approximation issues and enables robust, generalizable value estimation in VLA robotic manipulation across simulators and real hardware; a versatile approach compatible with current backbones and training schemes.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training of Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models has shown strong promise for robotic manipulation. Among RL methods, critic-based approaches rely on a value estimator that predominantly operates on single-frame observations or single-frame VLM backbone latents, which is a fundamental mismatch with the partially observable nature of robot control. A naive approach to incorporate observation history into the critic incurs exponential complexity with high-dimensional visual space, and still fails because pure scalar-return regression provides insufficient supervision for learning cross-temporal dynamics. We identify the root cause as a state approximation problem: without an explicit world modeling objective, the critic's representation cannot capture the temporal structure needed for accurate value estimation. To address this, we propose the World Critic Model (WCM), built on a lightweight LeJEPA architecture; WCM jointly predicts future latent state and estimates values, such that the critic's representation is explicitly trained to capture temporal dynamics rather than merely regress scalar returns. WCM integrates seamlessly into both on-policy and off-policy training pipelines and is compatible with state-of-the-art VLA backbones including Pi0, Pi0.5, and OpenVLA-OFT. Extensive experiments on 149 tasks across four benchmarks demonstrate that WCM consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance in both in-distribution and out-of-distribution settings, with particularly strong generalization gains. We further validate WCM on seven real-world manipulation tasks using OpenVLA-OFT and Pi0.5 with off-policy RL, confirming stable deployment across diverse settings.

</details>


### [191] [RayViT: Ray-Conditioned Visual Representations for Viewpoint-Robust Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29622)
*Qian Wang,Longrui Chen,Peiran Sun,Aleksandar Taranovic,Niklas Freymuth,Ge Li,Weiran Liao,C. F. Maximilian Nagy,Yucheng Tan,Tao Chen,Gerhard Neumann*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A lightweight RayViT architecture injects camera geometry into pretrained ViT backbones using a Plücker ray map and ray-conditioned tokens, improving robustness to camera perturbations in visuomotor learning and achieving measurable gains in sim and real robot tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RGB-based vision policies lack explicit geometry, making them sensitive to camera perturbations; incorporating camera geometry into vision transformers can yield more robust, geometry-aware representations for visuomotor control.

Method: Represent camera geometry as a Plücker ray map, patchify into ray features, and apply gated cross-attention to produce a ray-conditioned class token. Add ray features as dense positional embeddings and replace the ViT class token with a ray class token. Train with an auxiliary cosine similarity loss to align geometry-aware tokens.

Result: Empirical gains: ~13 percentage points improvement under camera perturbations in the RoboCasa benchmark (multi-task) and +1.78 average completed stages in real-world multi-task success rate, compared to baselines.

Conclusion: RayViT provides a geometry-aware,Robustified representation for visuomotor learning by integrating camera geometry into ViT backbones; the approach is lightweight and transferable to sim and real tasks, offering improved robustness and performance.

Abstract: Visual imitation learning enables robots to acquire visuomotor skills directly from images, yet RGB observations lack explicit geometric cues, making learned policies brittle to camera perturbations. To address this, we propose \textbf{Ray-conditioned Vision Transformer Encoder (RayViT)}, a lightweight architecture that injects camera geometry into pretrained ViT backbones. RayViT represents camera geometry as a Plücker ray map, patchifies it into ray features, and uses gated cross-attention to produce a ray-conditioned class token. These ray features are added as dense positional embeddings, while the ray class token replaces the original ViT class token to provide a geometry-aware summary representation. We combine this approach with an auxiliary cosine similarity loss to consistently improve the performance and robustness for geometry-aware tokens. Experiments on sim- and real-robot tasks demonstrate that RayViT improves robustness by approximately 13 percentage points under camera perturbations in multi-task RoboCasa benchmark and by 1.78 average completed stages in real-world multi-task success rate compared to baselines.

</details>


### [192] [Balancing of Humanoid with Object Mass: Trade-off Analyses and Lifting Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29625)
*Hyunjong Song,William Z. Peng,Joo H. Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A whole-body dynamics framework incorporating object mass is developed to analyze balance in humanoid loco-manipulation. It introduces the balanced state basin/boundary (BSB), a COM-space partition that yields systematic predictions and trajectory-threshold constraints for stable object lifting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current humanoid balance controllers for loco-manipulation rely on heuristics or machine learning. There is a need for rigorous analysis of how object mass affects balance and for principled control methods.

Method: Formulate object mass parameters within the whole-body dynamics using distributed contact wrenches and centers of pressure. Define and compute the balanced state basin/boundary (BSB) as a partition of the COM state space. Analyze the impact of base of support, actuation, and pose on BSBs. Identify two mass-related quantities (critical mass and transition mass). Establish sufficient trajectory-level conditions to impose balanced states using BSB, and enforce them as explicit constraints in whole-body trajectory optimization for stable object lifting. Validate via simulations and experiments on a humanoid and a reduced-order mechanism.

Result: The approach quantifies nonlinear effects of object mass on system momenta and constraints, characterizes how BSBs change with support, actuation, and pose, and reveals trade-offs between momentum regulation and other limiting factors. It identifies a critical mass at which balance capability is maximized and a transition mass that activates different limits. It provides explicit threshold constraints for lift-and-hold and lift-and-release tasks in trajectory optimization, demonstrated in simulations and experiments.

Conclusion: The BSB framework offers a principled, predictive basis for balance in humanoid loco-manipulation with objects, enabling systematic analysis of mass effects and providing practical trajectory-optimization constraints for stable lifting and manipulation.

Abstract: The demand for humanoid loco-manipulation tasks with an object has recently increased, and most existing control approaches for stability in such tasks rely on heuristics or machine-learning techniques. This study rigorously analyzes and exploits the dynamic effects of the object mass on balance stability. By formulating the object mass parameters in the whole-body dynamics with distributed contact wrenches and centers of pressure at the stance contacts, their nonlinear effects on the system momenta and constraints are quantified. The dynamic models and constraints are incorporated into the construction of the balanced state basin/boundary (BSB), a partition of the center-of-mass state space for a biped system to maintain balance in its desired contacts. The implications of the BSB for prediction and control are highlighted using a humanoid robot and an analytically tractable reduced-order mechanism. The BSBs under different conditions of base of support, actuation capacity, and pose provide systematic analyses of the effects of object mass on the balancing capability of a system. In particular, the trade-off relationships between momentum regulation and limiting factors in balancing are characterized, introducing two key quantities of the object: the critical mass, at which the system's balancing capability is maximum, and the transition mass, which activates different limiting factors. In addition, sufficient conditions for imposing balanced states on a trajectory are established and implemented with BSBs as explicit threshold constraints in the whole-body trajectory optimization for stable object-lifting control of the humanoid, demonstrating the lift-and-hold and lift-and-release tasks with distinct mass properties in simulations and experiments.

</details>


### [193] [Bootstrapping Self-Supervised Learning of Binary Classification Using Error Bounds: A Case Study on a Robotic Insertion Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29640)
*Zebin Duan,Norbert Krüger,Juan Heredia,Thorbjørn Mosekjær Iversen,Frederik Hagelskjær*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A self-improving data engine for flexible manufacturing using a fast predictor with optional expensive verification, governed by confidence bounds, demonstrated on a force-based robotic insertion task with UMAP and Wilson-score to control error rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce deployment delays and setup effort in flexible manufacturing by enabling autonomous learning of the error-tolerance and reducing reliance on costly verifications, while maintaining a user-specified error-rate.

Method: Two-classifier pipeline: a fast model for initial predictions and an expensive verifier for uncertain cases. The system triggers expensive verification based on the confidence of the fast prediction, with a tunable confidence threshold to set the tolerable error. The engine learns during task execution, reducing the need for expensive verifications over time. Implemented on a real-world robotic insertion task using force data; applies UMAP for dimensionality reduction and Wilson-score to compute confidence bounds for predictions.

Result: The approach learns to reduce reliance on expensive verifications over time while staying within the predefined error-rate. Confidence bounds contribute to improved reliability in robotic classification tasks.

Conclusion: Confidence-bounded self-improving models can enhance reliability and reduce verification costs in robotic and manufacturing contexts, enabling faster, more economical deployment of adaptive systems.

Abstract: Flexible manufacturing requires rapid deployment of solutions and minimal setup time to remain competitive. An essential attribute is the ability to control error levels, as failures can range from minor performance degradation to severe equipment damage. However, conventional deployment often involves extensive setup, data collection, model training or parameter tuning, and system testing, resulting in significant delays that hinder commercial feasibility. We propose a data engine which gathers data and improves its performance while executing the task. The data engine consists of two classifiers, a fast model prediction and expensive verification. First, a model prediction is performed and based on the confidence level of the prediction, the expensive verification can be used. By adjusting the confidence level, users can control the level of tolerable error.
  Our method is implemented on a real-world robotic insertion task, which uses force data for the model prediction. The system applies UMAP dimensionality reduction and uses Wilson-Score to compute the confidence bounds of the prediction. Results demonstrate the ability to learn and reduce the need for expensive verifications over time, while staying within the set error-rate. The results highlight the potential of confidence bounds in self-improving models to enhance reliability in robotic classification task.

</details>


### [194] [Diagnosing Compositional Generalization in Sequential Robot Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29687)
*Yixiao Wang,Cheng-En Wu,Lingfeng Sun,Pengcheng Wang,Xiang Ji,Boyuan Liang,Guojian Zhan,Masayoshi Tomizuka*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Investigates compositional generalization in sequential robot manipulation by decomposing the generalization gap into three instruction-related shifts; shows structured, smaller instruction-space coverage suffices for out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization, and that improving instruction steering via minimal per-task finetuning yields large OOD gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diagnose how instruction-space coverage and structural dependencies affect compositional generalization in robotics, and identify data-collection principles that enable reliable OOD action prediction without exhaustive task enumeration.

Method: Decompose the generalization gap into marginal instruction shift, instruction-compositional shift, and context–action shift. Analyze training coverage requirements, test with a structured subset of the task space (as small as one quarter of full space) that preserves action-relevant dependencies, and assess OOD performance. Investigate the impact of sparse training and finetuning, showing that updating a single demonstration per task can markedly improve OOD success. Consider semantically dependent tasks to emphasize relational versus elemental diversity.

Result: Exhaustive enumeration is unnecessary; a structured subset capturing action-relevant dependencies suffices to achieve strong OOD performance. Sparse training often fails due to instruction steering rather than missing low-level skills, with finetuning a single demonstration per task raising OOD success from 0.4% to 54.7%. For semantically dependent tasks, coverage must reflect relational structure rather than only factor diversity.

Conclusion: Prioritize dependency coverage in instruction space over exhaustive task expansion for efficient robot data collection. Ensure training data captures action-relevant dependencies and relational structure to support robust compositional generalization; supplementary material and project site provide further results.

Abstract: Sequential robot manipulation requires policies to execute novel combinations of familiar instruction components. However, collecting demonstrations for all possible instruction tuples is combinatorially expensive, while sparsely covered datasets often fail under out-of-distribution recombination. This paper studies compositional generalization through the lens of instruction-space coverage. We decompose the generalization gap into three sources: \textit{marginal instruction shift}, \textit{instruction-compositional shift}, and \textit{context--action shift}. This decomposition allows us to diagnose when sparse training coverage is sufficient, and what structure the training set must preserve for reliable action prediction. Our results show that exhaustive tuple enumeration is unnecessary: a structured subset, as small as one quarter of the full task space, can recover strong out-of-distribution performance when it covers action-relevant dependencies. We further find that sparse training often fails due to instruction steering rather than missing low-level skills; finetuning only one demonstration per task improves OOD success from \(0.4\%\) to \(54.7\%\). For semantically dependent tasks, effective coverage must capture relational structure rather than only factor diversity. These findings suggest that efficient robot data collection should prioritize dependency coverage in instruction space over exhaustive task expansion. More results are available in the supplementary material. Project website: https://yixiaowang7.github.io/Diagnosing_Compositional_Generalization_Robot_Page/.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [195] [OpenClaw and Ollama in Agentic AI: Toward Fully Autonomous and Scalable AI Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28629)
*Konstantinos I. Roumeliotis,Ranjan Sapkota*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a layered, full-stack architecture for Agentic AI, separating inference, orchestration, and execution; evaluates OpenClaw and Ollama as a prototype; demonstrates system-level integration yields persistence, memory, tool use, and adaptive decision-making; discusses challenges and future directions; releases artifacts for reproducibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address critical gaps in Agentic AI architecture, moving beyond reactive LLMs to persistent, goal-driven agents with memory, planning, and execution; establish a framework for designing, evaluating, and benchmarking full-stack agent systems.

Method: Proposes a layered architecture and analyzes OpenClaw (orchestrator) and Ollama (inference layer) as a full-stack Agentic AI system; implements a prototype integrating reasoning, tool use, and action execution; benchmarks and discusses scalability, security, privacy, governance, and evaluation; outlines future directions and provides public artifacts.

Result: Demonstrates that persistent memory, tool utilization, and adaptive decision-making arise from system-level integration rather than isolated models; performance improves as architectural complexity increases; provides a roadmap and supports reproducibility.

Conclusion: Establishes a unified architectural foundation for Agentic AI, validates the viability of full-stack autonomous agents, and highlights challenges and future directions (scalability, security, governance, evaluation); offers guidance for building scalable, secure, and trustworthy agentic systems and releases all models, code, and datasets for benchmarking.

Abstract: The rapid transition from reactive large language models (LLMs) to persistent, action-capable systems has exposed critical gaps in the architectural understanding of Agentic AI, particularly in separating inference, orchestration, and execution layers for autonomous AI agents. Despite recent advances, unified frameworks for designing and evaluating full-stack agentic systems remain limited. This paper presents a comprehensive, layered architecture for Agentic AI, outlining the evolution from reactive LLM interfaces to persistent, goal-driven autonomous AI agents with memory, planning, and continuous execution.
