Abstract: Counterfactual explanations (CEs) enhance the interpretability of machine learning models by identifying the smallest change to an input required to obtain a desired output. Although CEs are conventionally formulated as a distance-minimization problem, the theoretical basis of this formulation has received limited attention. We show that a distance-minimization-based CE is mathematically equivalent to the maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimate of a Gibbs posterior within the generalized Bayes framework, specifically when a distance-based prior is used. We call this formulation the Distance-Prior Generalized Bayes CE (DP-GBCE). Building on this posterior perspective, we introduce two decision rules beyond MAP within a unified framework: a Bayes decision that minimizes expected decision loss and CVaR-CE, a risk-averse decision rule. We also propose an extension that uses Bayesian model weights to mix the posterior distributions of multiple models, thereby accounting for model multiplicity, where several models have comparable predictive performance. Finally, we define metrics for evaluating both individual CEs and the posterior distribution as a whole, and use experiments on simulated data and Google Trends data to quantify the trade-offs among the decision rules.

</details>


### [221] [Harnessing the Wisdom of LLM Crowds through Complementarity-Driven Iterative Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29087)
*Yanbin Fang,Xuan Wei,Wei Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WILC enables sequential, relay-style coordination among multiple LLMs to overcome model-specific bottlenecks using PCF/PCG gating, achieving superior performance and lower cost with self-hosted deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address heterogenous capability boundaries of individual LLMs and untapped complementarity; move beyond fixed combination strategies to dynamic, state-aware collaboration; align with wisdom-of-crowds to improve problem solving.

Method: Two design principles: iterative reflection-refinement with a state-preserving workflow; dual-gate model selection: prospective complementarity fit (PCF) to pick the best worker for the current bottleneck, and posterior complementarity gain (PCG) to assess whether the transition improves the evolving solution.

Result: WILC outperforms single-model self-refinement, ensembles, and query-routing across four benchmarks; under standardized pricing, matches the average benchmark of GPT-5.2 at ~7x lower estimated per-query cost; supports data sovereignty via self-hosted deployment.

Conclusion: Extends wisdom-of-crowds from static aggregation to sequential AI complementarity and provides transferable design principles for multi-AI coordination.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in enterprise settings, yet individual models remain bounded by model-specific capability limitations. These heterogeneous boundaries pose a deployment challenge, but also create an opportunity: strategically coordinating multiple LLMs may unlock collective intelligence exceeding any single model. Existing approaches fix how models are combined in advance, overlooking the dynamic, state-dependent role of complementarity in complex problem solving. Drawing on the wisdom-of-crowds paradigm, we reconceptualize collective LLM intelligence as relay-style complementarity: a sequential process in which each successor model is selected to address the specific bottleneck identified in its predecessor's output. To operationalize this, we propose WILC (Wisdom Integration of LLM Crowds), a framework grounded in two design principles. First, iterative reflection-and-refinement establishes a state-preserving workflow through which models diagnose and refine prior outputs. Second, complementarity-driven model selection governs transitions via a dual-gate mechanism: prospective complementarity fit (PCF) identifies the worker most suited to the current bottleneck, while posterior complementarity gain (PCG) evaluates whether the selected transition improves the evolving solution. Experiments across four diverse benchmarks show that WILC outperforms existing approaches, including single-model self-refinement, ensemble methods, and query-routing methods. Under standardized pricing assumptions, WILC matches the average benchmark performance of GPT-5.2 at roughly 7 times lower estimated per-query cost, while facilitating data sovereignty through self-hosted deployment. This study extends wisdom-of-crowds theory from static aggregation to sequential AI complementarity and provides transferable design principles for multi-AI coordination.

</details>


### [222] [CAGE: Certified Authorization under Typed-Return Uncertainty for Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29190)
*Blaise Delattre,Cong Wang,Yang Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Joint certification for tool-using LLM agents: CAGE certifies the joint neighborhood of plausible returns by combining discrete binding faults and continuous numerical drift, proving per-channel certs do not compose; it enumerates branches exactly and certifies drift within each branch. Variants include CAGE-Exact for executable policies and CAGE-Lip/CAGE-RS for learned gates with fidelity assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Runtime permission gates guard tool returns in LLMs, but small binding errors and drift can bypass protections if certs treat categorical and numerical channels separately. Non-composability motivates a joint certification approach to prevent false approvals while preserving autonomous operation within budgets.

Method: Formulate a joint neighborhood over (binding fault, numerical drift); enumerate discrete branches representing binding faults exactly; within each branch certify continuous perturbations; apply across synthetic, policy-as-code, regulatory, and real-transaction settings. Introduce CAGE variants: CAGE-Exact (policy-executable certifies the policy), CAGE-Lip and CAGE-RS (learned gates with fidelity assumptions).

Result: Joint certification removes in-budget false allows that arise when per-channel certs are applied independently, while retaining a meaningful subset of autonomous decisions. The method demonstrates effectiveness across diverse settings, with exact policy certification achievable when the policy is executable and practical, measured certification otherwise.

Conclusion: Joint channels certification is necessary; CAGE provides a direct, exact or approximate joint certification framework for tool-using LLM agents, enabling safer autonomous decision-making with bounded perturbations across both provenance and numerical channels.

Abstract: Tool-using LLM agents act on typed tool returns, records pairing provenance and categorical fields with numerical values. Runtime permission gates generally authorize the observed return and action, leaving the decision unprotected against small errors in how the return was bound to its source. We ask whether a candidate action stays authorized over a declared neighborhood of plausible correctly bound returns: one admissible binding fault plus bounded numerical drift. We prove that certifying the categorical and numerical channels separately does not compose: perturbations that are safe on each channel alone can jointly turn the same action unsafe. CAGE certifies this joint neighborhood directly, enumerating the discrete branches exactly and certifying the continuous perturbation within each branch. Across synthetic, policy-as-code, regulatory, and real-transaction settings, CAGE removes the in-budget false allows that accurate pointwise gates admit, while keeping a useful fraction of decisions autonomous. When the policy is executable, CAGE-Exact certifies the policy itself; otherwise CAGE-Lip and CAGE-RS certify a learned gate under an explicit, measured fidelity assumption.

</details>


### [223] [MirrorCraft: Paired Evaluation under Hidden Rule Changes in Minecraft](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29218)
*Jianxin Gao,Beini Hu,Runze Li,Wanli Peng,Ruohan Lei,Jinyuan Zhang,Linna Deng,Tianyi Yu,Zining Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MirrorCraft introduces a paired Vanilla–Mirror Minecraft benchmark to evaluate LLM-based agents under hidden rule changes, using Rule Intervention Effect (RIE) to quantify performance shifts across rule suites and objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess robustness and generalization of LLM-driven agents when game rules change, moving beyond fixed mechanics.

Method: Construct paired Vanilla and Mirror worlds with identical terrain, resources, objectives, interfaces, and budgets; modify server-side rules via datapacks; evaluate across 5 biomes, 6 rule suites, 3 progression objectives, 2 model families, 6 agent configurations via Mineflayer; measure progress with deterministic milestones, success rate, and Rule Intervention Effect (RIE).

Result: Hidden rule changes produce diverse effects across rule suites; ReAct performs best among rule-descriptive-free configurations; providing exact rules yields modest gains in average progress and completion across objectives.

Conclusion: MirrorCraft enables evaluation beyond fixed mechanics, offering a controlled setting to study how agents adapt when world rules differ from familiar ones.

Abstract: With the prosperity of the large language models (LLMs), it has become an interesting topic: how do LLM-based agents work in Minecraft? Unfortunately, most existing benchmarks evaluate them under fixed game mechanics. High performance in these settings does not show whether an agent can continue making progress when familiar recipes, drops, and other rules change. In this paper, we introduce MirrorCraft, a paired benchmark for evaluating agents under hidden rule changes in Minecraft. Each Mirror world is a copy of its paired Vanilla world, with selected server-side rules modified by the corresponding datapack. Terrain, spawn, resource placement, objective, interface, and action budget remain matched within every Vanilla-Mirror pair. MirrorCraft includes five controlled biomes, six rule suites, three progression objectives, two model families, and six agent configurations under a shared Mineflayer interface. We evaluate task progress with deterministic advancement milestones and success rate and use the Rule Intervention Effect (RIE) to measure the performance change between matched Vanilla and Mirror worlds. The experiments show that hidden rule changes have strongly different effects across suites. Among the configurations evaluated without rule descriptions, ReAct achieves the highest pooled Mirror score. Providing the exact rules yields modest gains in average progress and completion across all three objectives. MirrorCraft extends Minecraft evaluation beyond fixed mechanics and provides a controlled setting for studying how agents use gameplay outcomes when the rules of the current world differ from familiar ones.

</details>


### [224] [Don't Mix Rewards, Mix Policies: Policy Decomposition and Optimization for Multi-Reward RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29246)
*Ruiming Liang,Yi Zhong,Yizhen Yuan,Yinan Zheng,Tianyi Tan,Tianyue Wang,Haiyun Guo,Jinqiao Wang,Xianyuan Zhan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PRISM introduces a policy-space decomposition approach for multi-reward RL in LLMs, training standalone positive policies plus a global negative policy, to reduce objective conflicts and enable inference-time policy composition, and shows improved performance and controllability over baselines on reasoning and safety tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-reward RL for LLMs often suffers alignment tax where conflicting rewards cause unstable/training inefficiency. A decomposition of policy space aims to minimize cross-objective interference and allow controllable policy combination at inference time.

Method: Train multiple independent (positive) policies corresponding to different rewards and a global negative policy. At inference, compose these policies to meet preferences, mitigating conflicts during optimization and enabling customizable behavior via policy combination.

Result: PRISM reportedly outperforms existing multi-reward RL baselines on tasks in scientific reasoning, tool-use reasoning, and helpfulness-safety alignment, while providing extra controllability for inference-time preference control.

Conclusion: Policy-space decomposition with a global negative policy can alleviate alignment tax in multi-reward RL for LLMs and offer controllable inference-time behavior, at the cost of potential added training/inference complexity and the need for robust policy composition mechanisms.

Abstract: Modern large language models (LLMs) are expected not just to answer correctly, but to adapt their behavior to different human values and use cases. As a result, multi-reward reinforcement learning (RL) has become an increasingly important problem for LLMs, where each reward captures a different aspect of desired behavior. However, optimizing with multiple rewards suffers from a more severe alignment tax issue, where different optimization objectives can trade off or even conflict with each other, leading to unstable and inefficient post-training. In this work, we propose PRISM, a new multi-reward RL framework built upon the idea of policy-space decomposition and composition. Instead of compositing different rewards, PRISM optimizes a set of standalone positive policies and a global negative policy. This alleviates the potential conflict during multi-reward policy optimization, while enabling controllability during inference by flexible policy composition. Experiments on scientific reasoning, tool-use reasoning, and helpfulness-safety alignment show that PRISM consistently outperforms existing multi-reward RL baselines, with extra controllability for inference-time preference control.

</details>


### [225] [Tool Specifications Matter: Uncovering and Mitigating Safety Risks in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29254)
*Minghui Pan,Jiayuxuan Yang,Yuanyuan Yuan,Yu Jiang,Zhenpeng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SafeKeep decouples safety judgments from tool execution by using flattened textual tool specifications for assessment while preserving original schema-formatted specs for execution, improving safety of LLM agents with tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Schema-formatted tool specifications are a key source of safety degradation in LLM-powered agents; these specs weaken internal refusal signals and enable unsafe tool use. A runtime safeguard is needed to separate safety evaluation from tool execution.

Method: Inference-time safeguard SafeKeep; at evaluation time, the model’s request is assessed against flattened textual representations of tool specs to decide safety, while the original schema-formatted specifications are retained for actual tool use during execution.

Result: Across two benchmarks and four LLMs (including white-box and black-box models), SafeKeep raises average refusal rate for harmful requests from 23.8% to 70.6% and cuts average attack success rate under observation-level prompt injection from 25.6% to 2.5%; it outperforms existing safeguards and maintains task performance.

Conclusion: SafeKeep effectively improves the safety of LLM agents using tools by separating safety judgments from tool execution, offering strong protection against prompt-injection attacks without sacrificing usefulness; code and data are released.

Abstract: AI agents extend large language models (LLMs) with external tools, enabling them to perform complex tasks and translate model outputs into consequential real-world actions. Yet LLMs often become substantially less safe when deployed as agents, and the source of this degradation remains poorly understood. In this paper, we identify schema-formatted tool specifications as a primary source of agent safety degradation and show, through white-box representation analysis, that they weaken the model's internal refusal signals and contribute to unsafe tool execution. Building on this finding, we propose SafeKeep, an inference-time safeguard that decouples safety judgment from tool execution: it assesses requests using flattened textual tool specifications while retaining the original schema-formatted specifications for execution. Across two representative benchmarks and four LLMs, including both white-box and black-box models, SafeKeep increases the average refusal rate for harmful requests from 23.8% to 70.6% and reduces the average attack success rate under observation-level prompt injection from 25.6% to 2.5%. It also outperforms existing safeguards and preserves task-handling capability. We release the code and data at https://github.com/snowcatsmoking/SafeKeep .

</details>


### [226] [MAGA: Multi-Platform Self-Fusion of GUI Agents via Structured Action Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29320)
*Hang Yan,Zhangxuan GU,Beitong Zhou,Jiaxuan Chen,Runze Li,Yusong Hu,Shuheng Shen,Changhua Meng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAGA is a cross-environment GUI agent framework that re-allocates distillation signals by action structure to fuse domain-specific experts, achieving higher mean success than baselines at 8B, with training-only hints aligning supervision with actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address fragmentation and limited deployment of GUI agents by unifying domain-specific models into a single cross-environment policy; existing approaches (weight merging, on-policy distillation) either corrupt actions under disagreement or treat all tokens equally, ignoring the action-token as the interface to the environment.

Method: Introduce MAGA: re-allocate training signal according to the structured action. Suppress unnecessary/invalid distillation signals based on action correctness; emphasize learning from erroneous actions. Introduce a training-only hint to optimize supervision from domain-specific teachers without changing the student input.

Result: Evaluated on two model scales; MAGA achieves the highest mean success rate, outperforming the strongest baseline by 2.0% at 8B, and attains almost the same average performance as the teachers.

Conclusion: Action-aware, action-structured distillation with training-only hints enables effective consolidation of domain-specific GUI agents into a cross-environment policy, improving performance and maintaining parity with teacher guidance.

Abstract: Graphical user interface (GUI) agents based on large language models are increasingly deployed across mobile, web, and desktop environments. However, existing agents are typically domain-specific, limiting the deployment and user experience. This motivates the consolidation of specialized models into a single cross-environment policy. Weight merging directly merges domain-specific experts but can corrupt executable actions under expert disagreement, while on-policy distillation (OPD) avoids conflicting teacher supervision yet still treats all response tokens equally during distillation, ignoring that action tokens are the only interface between the environment and the agent. To address this, We introduce MAGA that re-allocates training signal according to the structured action. Based on the correctness of the generated action, it suppresses unnecessary or invalid distillation signals and focuses learning on erroneous actions. Besides, a training-only hint optimizes the supervision signal provided by domain-specific teachers without changing the student input. Across two model scales, MAGA achieves the highest mean success rate, outperforming the strongest baseline by 2.0% at 8B and achieves almost the same average performance with teachers.

</details>


### [227] [Beyond Component Testing: Validating Agentic AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29405)
*Fabio Orazio Mirto,Luca D'Agati,Giuseppe Tricomi,Stefano Silvestri,Francesco Longo,Antonio Puliafito,Giovanni Merlino*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Survey of 257 papers to define a five-dimension taxonomy for validating agentic AI trajectories; finds behavioral evaluation is mature, but temporal validity, runtime evidence, regulatory legibility, and multi-agent assurance are underdeveloped; presents three cross-domain case studies and proposes a lifecycle-oriented research agenda focused on bounded-autonomy specs, adversarial trajectory generation, runtime monitoring, and audit-ready evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems operate via multi-step trajectories that unfold decisions over time in changing environments. Validation must assess trajectories in context, not just isolated components, to ensure safety and reliability.

Method: Systematic literature review across agent evaluation, software assurance, cyber-physical systems, runtime monitoring, and regulatory guidance (257 papers). Development of a five-dimension taxonomy (behavioral, safety, temporal, regulatory, multi-agent), mapping of current approaches, identification of gaps, and presentation of cross-domain case studies, culminating in a lifecycle-oriented research agenda.

Result: Mapped landscape shows maturity in behavioral evaluation; significant gaps in temporal validity, runtime evidence maintenance, regulatory legibility, and open-ended multi-agent systems assurance. Three case studies (medical care, industrial operations, smart-mobility) illustrate how taxonomy dimensions recur in safety-critical contexts. Provides a path toward auditable, trajectory-centered validation and runtime evidence structures.

Conclusion: Trustworthy deployment of agentic AI requires validating decision trajectories within context rather than evaluating isolated components; advances should focus on bounded-autonomy specifications, adversarial trajectory generation, runtime monitoring, and audit-ready evidence to enable regulatory compliance and safety assurance.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems act through multi-step trajectories that combine planning, tool use, memory, interaction, and adaptation. This behavior stretches validation practice beyond component testing and one-shot input--output evaluation, because acceptable system behavior now depends on how decisions unfold over time and under changing environmental conditions. This survey synthesizes 257 papers spanning agent evaluation, software assurance, cyber-physical systems, runtime monitoring, and regulatory guidance in order to characterize the validation problem for agentic systems. The review is organized around a five-dimension taxonomy covering behavioral, safety, temporal, regulatory, and multi-agent concerns, and uses that taxonomy to map current approaches and expose recurrent coverage gaps. The analysis shows that behavioral evaluation is comparatively mature, while temporal validity, runtime evidence maintenance, regulatory legibility, and open-ended multi-agent systems assurance remain under-developed. Three cross-domain case studies (medical care, industrial operations, smart-mobility systems) provide operational illustrations of how the five taxonomy dimensions recur in safety-critical settings, grounded in the failure patterns documented in the reviewed literature. The paper concludes with a lifecycle-oriented research agenda centered on bounded-autonomy specifications, adversarial trajectory generation, runtime monitoring, and audit-ready evidence structures. The central claim is that trustworthy deployment of agentic AI depends on validating trajectories in context rather than assessing isolated components alone.

</details>


### [228] [ModelEquivBench: Certifying Multi-Relational Evaluation of LLM-Generated Optimization Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29431)
*Penglin Zhu,Jungang Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ModelEquivBench: a certifying, multi-relational evaluation framework for optimization-model generation by LLMs, with per-pair semantics E0–E6 and verifiable evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations collapse model equivalence to binary or success metrics, lacking independently verifiable and multi-faceted judgments across reformulations; need granular, certifiable evidence.

Method: Define six relations E0–E6 capturing model construction, representation alignment, feasible-set relations, objective order, optimal value, and optimizer-set equivalence; provide replayable traces, exact maps, rational certificates, and witnesses; handle UNKNOWN/N/A when incomplete; apply to 3 models on 173 problems under a no-repair protocol.

Result: Profiles reveal significant distinctions not captured by coarse baselines: executable candidates that are negative on some relations; structural rejections where E2 indicates feasible-set equality; models fail at different stages and cannot be summarized by a single accuracy score.

Conclusion: The multi-relational, certifiable evaluation provides richer, actionably different insights than single-score metrics; incomplete mapping and resource limits are explicitly reported; more discriminative evaluation is possible for LLM-specified optimization formulations.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly generate optimization models from natural language, but existing evaluation often reduces a generated model and its ground truth to a single equivalent/not-equivalent verdict or an execution-success rate--labels that are neither independently checkable nor faithful to the multiple distinct senses in which two formulations can agree. We present ModelEquivBench, a certifying, multi-relational evaluation system that reports a per-pair semantic profile E0--E6: model construction and exact ingestion (E0), verified representation alignment (E1), same-space and projected feasible-set relations (E2, E3), objective-order equivalence (E4), optimal-value equality (E5), and optimizer-set equivalence (E6). Each decided entry carries relation-appropriate, independently re-checkable evidence: replayable traces or explicit maps for E0--E1, exact-rational certificates for positive E2--E6 conclusions, and explicit witnesses for supported negatives. Incomplete mapping search, unsupported structure, and resource limits produce typed UNKNOWN or N/A outcomes rather than guesses, while unmet prerequisites are reported as ABSENT. Using ModelEquivBench to evaluate three model snapshots--GPT-5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.6, and Qwen3.5-397B-A17B--on the same frozen cohort of 173 base problems (346 cells per model) under a no-repair protocol, the resulting profiles expose distinctions that coarse baselines do not represent: 49, 35, and 25 cells contain executable candidates that are nevertheless certified negative on at least one supported relation, and 25, 8, and 18 structural rejections occur on pairs for which E2 certifies mapped feasible-set equality under a verified map. The three model snapshots fail at different stages of the profile and therefore cannot be meaningfully reduced to a single accuracy score.

</details>


### [229] [Beyond Retrieval: Analytic Memory for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29440)
*Zhoujin Tian,Yao Tian,Hao Zhang,Cheng Chen,Yakun Li,Lei Zhang,Xiaofang Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AdaMM introduces analytic memory to complement retrieval memory for long-term multimodal memory, enabling analytics on recurring attribute-value observations. A memory-aware planner decomposes queries into retrieval and analytic steps, routing them to suitable tools, yielding gains on MemEye and MemGallery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current long-term multimodal systems emphasize retrieval memory, organizing histories with summaries/indexes. There is a need to compute over accumulated observations—filtering, aggregation, ranking, and temporal comparison—across modalities.

Method: AdaMM is a framework that jointly supports retrieval and analytic memory. It extracts provenance-linked attribute-value observations from dialogue, images, and metadata, discovers recurring field structures, and materializes them for analytics. At inference, a memory-aware planner decomposes queries into retrieval and analytic operations and routes each operation to appropriate tools.

Result: Experiments on two benchmarks, MemEye and MemGallery, show AdaMM improves performance by up to 11.3% and 7.3%, respectively.

Conclusion: AdaMM demonstrates that coupling retrieval memory with analytic memory and employing a memory-aware planner for operation routing can enhance long-term multimodal memory systems, enabling structured analytics over accumulated observations.

Abstract: Long-term multimodal memory must support not only retrieving relevant information but also computing over observations accumulated across interactions. Existing systems largely emphasize \emph{retrieval memory}, organizing interaction histories through summaries and indexes to return query-relevant information at multiple granularities, from high-level abstractions to underlying records. In this paper, we formulate \emph{analytic memory} as a complementary abstraction that organizes recurring multimodal observations into queryable structures supporting filtering, aggregation, ranking, and temporal comparison. We present AdaMM, a framework that jointly supports retrieval and analytic memory. Rather than relying on application-defined schemas, AdaMM extracts provenance-linked attribute-value observations from dialogue, images, and contextual metadata, discovers recurring field structures, and materializes them for analytical access. At inference time, a memory-aware planner decomposes queries into retrieval and analytic operations and routes each operation to the appropriate tools. Experiments on two long-term multimodal memory benchmarks, MemEye and MemGallery, show that AdaMM improves performance by up to 11.3\% and 7.3\%, respectively.

</details>


### [230] [LEMUR: Learning to Align with Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning from Preference Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29559)
*Manith Adikari,Bei Peng,Samuele Vinanzi,Angelo Cangelosi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LEMUR introduces an interactive, preference-based approach to multi-objective RL, learning both multi-objective policies and objective-specific reward models from human feedback to balance competing objectives without predefined reward functions; evaluated on MORL benchmarks with superior performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world tasks involve multiple conflicting objectives and ground-truth reward functions are hard to specify. MORL typically assumes access to well-defined scalar rewards; PbRL can learn from human feedback but is mostly studied in single-objective settings. There is a need to learn from human preferences across multiple objectives to align policies with several criteria.

Method: The framework jointly learns policies and multiple objective-specific reward models from human feedback. It uses an interactive loop where human preferences guide the shaping of reward models for each objective, enabling the agent to balance competing objectives during learning without predefined reward functions.

Result: Empirical evaluation on benchmark multi-objective tasks shows superior performance of LEMUR over baseline methods, indicating effective handling of trade-offs and alignment with human preferences across multiple objectives.

Conclusion: LEPUR demonstrates a promising direction for solving multi-objective decision-making without pre-defined reward functions through preference-based learning across multiple objectives.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) systems are typically trained using a single, well-specified scalar reward function. However, real-world decision-making tasks often involve multiple, competing objectives, such as performance versus efficiency, where ground-truth reward functions are difficult to specify or inaccessible. While Multi-Objective RL (MORL) addresses such trade-offs by modeling rewards as vectors, existing approaches typically assume access to a well-specified reward function for each objective, inheriting the same challenges faced by single-objective RL. Meanwhile, Preference-based RL (PbRL) has shown great potential in solving complex tasks without access to a pre-defined reward function through reward learning from human feedback, yet has largely been studied in single-objective settings. In this work, we bridge this gap with LEMUR: Learning to Align with Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning with Preference feedback, a novel framework where an agent interactively learns from the preferences of multiple humans to learn optimal multi-objective policies. Our approach jointly learns policies and multiple objective-specific reward models from human feedback, enabling agents to effectively balance competing objectives during learning. We evaluate LEMUR on a variety of benchmark multi-objective tasks, and empirical results demonstrate its superior performance over baseline methods. Our method presents a promising direction for solving multi-objective decision-making tasks without pre-defined reward functions.

</details>


### [231] [Self-Play Meets Skill Evolution: Self-Evolving Search Agents that Pose, Solve, and Remember](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29468)
*Zenghuang Fu,Zhaoyang Li,Qiuyuan Ai,Haoyu Wu,Minghui Wu,Chenxu Zhao,Ante Wang,Guannan He,Changwei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SESA introduces a bidirectional, self-evolving skill-augmented agent that treats procedural memory as an evolving state in a tool-augmented self-play loop, enabling memory to influence task generation and policy learning; achieves consistent accuracy gains across open-domain and multi-hop QA benchmarks and supports memory-free deployment via optional inference-time retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the absence of long-horizon, persistent state in self-play curricula and the limitations of fixed external skill memories by coupling task generation with an evolving skill bank that informs both training and inference.

Method: Propose SESA: a challenger poses problems while a solver retrieves skills from a separately parameterized, evolving memory. Failures are distilled into reusable skills and written back to memory, creating a bidirectional loop where memory updates alter solver behavior and future problem distributions. Evaluated across seven open-domain and multi-hop QA benchmarks on multiple backbones; includes an Ablation comparing SESA-off and final skill bank.

Result: SESA improves average accuracy over SSP by 1.2–3.2 points across multiple backbones; also surpasses SkillRL baseline by ~0.9 points under unified evaluation. On Qwen3 models, SESA-Off improves SSP by 1.8–2.2 points; the final skill bank adds 0.5–1.0 points. Memory can influence policy learning and future training distributions while remaining usable as external memory or for optional inference.

Conclusion: Evolving skill memory is not merely an inference-time plug-in; it actively shapes policy learning and the trajectory of future tasks, enabling both memory-based benefits and memory-free deployment. The approach demonstrates robust gains across diverse QA benchmarks and supports reusable skills that adapt as the task distribution evolves.

Abstract: Self-play agents can generate training problems without questions from target benchmarks, but their curricula lack persistent state: failures affect gradients yet do not explicitly shape future practice. External skill memories preserve procedural experience but are typically learned from fixed task distributions. We introduce \textbf{SESA} (Self-Evolving Skill-Augmented Agent), which makes procedural memory an evolving state of tool-augmented search self-play. A challenger poses problems, while a separately parameterized solver alone retrieves skills. Informative failures are distilled into reusable skills and written back to memory. The updated memory changes solver behavior and success, which changes the challenger's reward and the distribution of future problems; the resulting frontier produces new failures that rewrite memory. This bidirectional loop makes task generation and skill memory co-evolve. Because retrieved skills shape on-policy training trajectories, their benefits can enter the model parameters as well as remain in the external bank, enabling memory-free deployment and optional inference-time retrieval. Across seven open-domain and multi-hop question-answering benchmarks, SESA improves average accuracy over SSP by 1.2--3.2 points across multiple backbones and surpasses the skill-augmented SkillRL baseline by 0.9 points under a unified evaluation protocol. On Qwen3 models, SESA-Off retains 1.8--2.2 points of improvement over SSP, while the final skill bank adds a further 0.5--1.0 points. These results show that evolving skill memory is not merely an inference-time plug-in: it changes policy learning and the future training distribution while retaining value as optional external memory. Our code is available at https://github.com/Zenghuang-Fu/SESA-Self-Evolving-Search-Agents.

</details>


### [232] [AMTFV: Agentic Mathematical Tool-Flow Verification for LLM Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29549)
*Rui Zou,Yutao Zhu,Mengqi Wei,Ji-Rong Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AMTFV introduces a decoupled, interrupt-execute-resume verification flow (MTF) that uses a math toolbox to perform exact computations, improving LLM verification accuracy on math problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Verification of large language model (LLM) mathematical answers is unreliable when relying on natural-language reflection alone or on embedding verification logic into the reasoning process; existing methods either can't guarantee exact computation or couple modeling with low-level execution. A decoupled, exact-computation verification framework is needed.

Method: Propose Mathematical Tool Flow (MTF) as an interrupt--execute--resume interface. A verification agent constructs a verification workflow and encodes mathematical objects and computational intent in an MTF request sent to a mathematical toolbox agent, which parses the request, generates executable calls, and dispatches them to a backend for exact computation. Tool outputs support candidate adjudication, answer revision, and verification-workflow revision.

Result: Evaluated on five challenging mathematical reasoning datasets with seven model configurations from DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini. AMTFV outperforms representative baselines; under an individual model configuration, average accuracy improved by up to 8.3 percentage points, with larger gains on samples of medium/high verification complexity.

Conclusion: Decoupling verification modeling from execution and enabling exact computation via a mathematical toolbox improves verification reliability for complex math problems, suggesting broad applicability to robust problem-solving in LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models have demonstrated strong mathematical problem-solving capabilities, yet reliably verifying their candidate answers remains challenging. Existing representative methods mainly revise outputs through natural-language reflection or assist verification by directly generating verification programs; the former may not reliably support exact computation, whereas the latter prematurely couples mathematical modeling with low-level implementation. We propose AMTFV (Agentic Mathematical Tool-Flow Verification). By introducing Mathematical Tool Flow (MTF) as an interrupt--execute--resume interface, AMTFV decouples verification modeling from concrete execution and supports exact computation through a mathematical toolbox. Specifically, the verification agent first constructs a verification workflow, encodes the mathematical objects and computational intent requiring reliable execution in an MTF request, and sends it to the mathematical toolbox agent. The latter parses the request, generates executable calls, and dispatches them to the backend for exact computation. Tool outputs then support candidate-answer adjudication, answer revision, and verification-workflow revision. We evaluate AMTFV on five challenging mathematical reasoning datasets with seven model configurations from DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini. Experimental results show that AMTFV outperforms the representative baselines evaluated in this study overall; under an individual model configuration, it improves average accuracy over the strongest baseline by up to 8.3 percentage points, with larger gains on samples of medium and high verification complexity.

</details>


### [233] [COntExt: Towards Context-Aware Ontology Extension from Operational Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29553)
*Hussain Hussain,Stefan Schöberl,Angelika Schneider,Verena Geist*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COntExt: a framework that extends ontologies by using structured metric definitions to predict parent classes, relation types, and data properties; evaluated on four cybersecurity ontologies, showing improvements over baselines and enabling cost-effective ontology maintenance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Operational metric catalogs increasingly encode domain knowledge beyond formal ontologies, yet integration with ontologies is manual and costly. Automating extension can reduce effort and improve consistency.

Method: Formulates ontology extension as three sub-tasks (parent class prediction, relation type prediction, data property assignment) using metric-derived context from structured metric definitions as input; evaluates multiple algorithms across four cybersecurity ontologies; compares to ontology-context baselines.

Result: Metric-derived context improves predictions for relation types and data properties over baselines; demonstrates that metric catalogs are a practical, underexploited source for ontology extension across multiple ontologies.

Conclusion: Operational metric catalogs can support scalable, low-cost ontology extension; COntExt provides a practical framework to leverage structured metrics for maintaining and extending ontologies in organizational settings.

Abstract: Organizations increasingly define operational metrics in structured, machine-readable formats to monitor systems, processes, and compliance. These metric definitions implicitly encode domain knowledge, such as referencing concepts, properties, and relationships, that often extends what is captured in formal ontologies. Yet the connection between operational metric catalogues and ontological knowledge remains manual, ad-hoc, and labor-intensive. We present COntExt, a framework for context-aware ontology extension that takes structured metric definitions as input and suggests how referenced concepts and properties should be integrated into an existing ontology, utilizing the context of these metrics. The framework defines the extension problem as three sub-tasks: parent class prediction, relation type prediction, and data property assignment. Across four cybersecurity ontologies, we evaluate different algorithms for each task. Our results show that metric-derived context improves the suggestions over ontology-context baselines for relation type prediction and data property assignment. Our work demonstrates that operational metric catalogues are a practical and underexploited source for ontology extension. This work enables organizations to maintain their ontologies at a significantly lower cost than manual engineering.

</details>


### [234] [DungeonBench: A Benchmark for Rules-Rich Tactical Reasoning in Dungeons & Dragons Combat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29577)
*Ismayil Ismayilov,Atakan Kara,Kaan Oktay*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DungeonBench is a benchmark for tactical reasoning in D&D combat, exposing complete tactical observations and option sets across two tracks (Encounter and Day) to evaluate decision-making under action economy, geometry, timing, and resource management; frontier language-model policies are promising for single fights but struggle with long-horizon budgeting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks emphasize rule-rich tactical reasoning but often abstract away essential constraints; a realistic, D&D-inspired benchmark is needed to test policies under action economy, resource management, and multi-encounter planning.

Method: Build DungeonBench exposing step-by-step observations, decisions, and executable options; support both encounters and day-long campaigns; use 2014 SRD content; run a shared decision stream through heuristic controllers, language models, ranking policies, and masked-action RL; evaluate frontier language-model policies.

Result: Language-model policies perform well on direct encounters but fail on resource budgeting and rest timing when encounters are linked; the benchmark reveals limitations of current models in long-horizon tactical discipline; the engine supports diverse policy types.

Conclusion: DungeonBench provides a comprehensive, adaptable benchmark for tactical reasoning in structured combat, highlighting the gap between single-fight success and sustained strategic play; it enables systematic evaluation of rule-aware planning and resource management in complex tactical domains.

Abstract: Games and simulators make valuable benchmarks by turning decisions into measurable outcomes, but many current suites under-test rules-rich tactical reasoning: the ability to choose well when geometry, timing, resources, objectives, and rule interactions all matter at once. We introduce DungeonBench, a benchmark for tactical reasoning in Dungeons & Dragons combat, built to cover the vast majority of combat-relevant 2014 System Reference Document content whose effects can be resolved by the simulator while retaining mechanics that simplified combat simulators often abstract away. At each step, DungeonBench exposes a complete tactical observation, a pending decision, and an indexed list of executable options spanning movement, attacks, spells, reactions, objectives, preparation, and scarce resources. The task is to value legal choices whose consequences depend on action economy, creature traits, battlefield geometry, timing windows, and future encounters. DungeonBench has two tracks: Encounter, which evaluates local tactical play in single fights, and Day, which links encounters through persistent hit points, spell slots, consumables, preparation, and short-rest timing, forcing policies to trade off immediate tactical advantage against future survivability. The same engine-generated decision stream supports heuristic controllers, language-model policies, learned option rankers, and masked-action reinforcement-learning agents. We evaluate frontier language-model policies on this shared decision stream. Results show that full tactical observations do not saturate the benchmark: frontier policies often win direct encounters, but linked encounter days expose failures in resource budgeting, rest timing, and rule-aware tactical discipline.

</details>


### [235] [AgentHPOBench: A Benchmark For Evaluating LLM Agents as Sequential Hyperparameter Optimizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29626)
*Tianyu Huai,Tingshuo Fan,Xinchi Chen,Yining Zheng,Yuxin Wang,Shuang Chen,Jie Zhou,Xuanjing Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentHPOBench is a 30-task sequential HPO benchmark that tests whether agents can interpret experimental evidence and iteratively guide hyperparameter choices; 12 agents evaluated; results show nontrivial optimization ability but notable limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a gap in evaluating autonomous agents' ability to learn from experimental traces and use that information to adjust hyperparameters, beyond static code generation, paper replication, or final answer accuracy.

Method: The benchmark comprises 30 executable ML tasks across seven research categories. Each task starts from a validated baseline run; an agent performs several sequential interventions, observing accumulated configurations, metrics, and logs before proposing the next valid configuration. A unified protocol evaluates 12 widely used agents and conventional HPO baselines.

Result: Agents exhibit measurable experimental optimization ability across domains but struggle with sustained iterative refinement, complex log diagnosis, and achieving consistent progress toward reported reference performance.

Conclusion: AgentHPOBench provides a unified platform to evaluate experimental optimization capability in autonomous agents; while current agents show some capability across tasks, significant improvements are needed in iterative refinement, log interpretation, and reliable progress toward reference performance.

Abstract: As LLMs evolve from code completion systems into autonomous scientific agents, evaluating their ability to conduct experiments has become increasingly important. Existing benchmarks typically focus on static code generation, paper replication, or final answer correctness, but do not directly assess whether agents can interpret experimental evidence and use it to guide subsequent hyperparameter decisions. To address this gap, we introduce AgentHPOBench, a sequential benchmark comprising 30 executable machine learning tasks across seven research categories. Each task begins with a validated baseline run, after which an agent performs several sequential interventions. At each step, the agent observes the accumulated configurations, metrics, and logs before proposing the next valid configuration. We evaluate 12 widely used agents and conventional HPO baselines under a unified protocol. The results show that current agents exhibit measurable experimental optimization ability across domains, but still face clear limitations in sustained iterative refinement, complex log diagnosis, and consistent progress toward reported reference performance.

</details>


### [236] [Development of FDD-ON: an Ontology for VAV HVAC System Fault Detection and Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29657)
*Yimin Chen,Brian Fricke,Bo Shen,Jamie Lian,Mingkan Zhang,James Lo,Yun Zhang,Shi Ye,Jiajing Huang,Han Hu,Chujie Lu,Rui Tang,George Zhuang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FDD-ON is a modular, extensible ontology for VAV HVAC fault detection and diagnosis that formalizes components, fault types, symptoms, and impacts with explicit causal relations to enable machine-interpretable querying, interoperability, and interoperable FDD applications; validated on public VAV datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome data interpretability and interoperability gaps in FDD, reduce information silos, and enable digital twin-enabled FDD and AI-driven maintenance decision-making.

Method: Develop a modular/extensible FDD ontology (FDD-ON) that formally represents VAV HVAC components, fault types, symptom statuses, fault impacts and attributes; provide controlled vocabulary and libraries for faults, symptoms, and impacts; define explicit cause–fault–symptom–impact relations; enable machine-interpretable querying and cross-system integration; validate on public VAV datasets and demonstrate in FDD development scenarios.

Result: FDD-ON provides a foundational semantic framework that supports scalable, transparent, and interoperable FDD solutions; includes comprehensive fault/symptom/impact libraries and explicit relations; demonstrated with public datasets and FDD development applications.

Conclusion: FDD-ON advances interoperable, data-sharing capable FDD practices and supports digital-twin–enabled and AI-driven maintenance through a formal, extensible semantic representation of VAV HVAC FDD knowledge.

Abstract: Fault detection and diagnosis (FDD) technology is essential for improving HVAC system reliability, energy efficiency, and maintenance effectiveness. However, effective deployment of FDD solutions in buildings requires structured domain knowledge that can bridge heterogeneous data sources, diverse equipment types, and varied diagnostic outputs. Limited data interpretability and interoperability within the FDD domain have led to fragmented information silos, hindering the implementation of FDD and related applications, such as the digital twin-enabled FDD frameworks and artificial intelligence (AI)-driven maintenance decision-making systems. This paper presents an FDD Ontology (FDD-ON), a modular and extensible ontology to formally represent variable air volume (VAV) HVAC system components, fault types, symptom statuses, fault impacts and associated attributes. FDD-ON integrates HVAC system FDD semantics to provide comprehensive representations of fault and symptom attributes, supported by the well-defined controlled vocabulary. Additionally, FDD-ON offers comprehensive fault, symptom, and impact libraries to capture a broad spectrum of operational abnormalities and their consequences in VAV HVAC systems. Through explicit contributing cause-fault-symptom-impact relations, FDD-ON serves as a machine-interpretable basis for querying diagnostic knowledge, mapping heterogeneous FDD outputs, and developing interoperable FDD-related applications. FDD-ON is evaluated using publicly available VAV HVAC system datasets and demonstrated through FDD development applications. Results indicate that FDD-ON provides a foundational semantic framework for advancing scalable, transparent, and interoperable FDD solutions across various applications.

</details>


### [237] [ExtractBench: A Benchmark for Schema-Guided Enterprise Document Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29677)
*Boyang Zhang,Adrian Lyjak,Eli Stewart,Zhaoqi Li,Simon Suo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ExtractBench is a new benchmark for schema-guided extraction that jointly evaluates value accuracy, record completeness at scale, grounding (source traceability), and cost. It covers 4,869 pages across 370 enterprise documents, 8 domains, and 67 types, with a scalable curation pipeline and three evaluation metrics. LlamaExtract Agentic Plus achieves top performance on all metrics, with accuracy similar to coding agents but at lower cost. Dataset and code are publicly available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for a comprehensive, scalable benchmark to assess schema-guided extraction across multiple axes (value accuracy, completeness, grounding, and cost) on real and synthetic data, enabling fair comparison of agents (commercial, coding, and Llama-based) especially for long documents where truncation and cost are critical.

Method: A scalable schema-guided extraction benchmark (ExtractBench) using 4,869 pages from 370 enterprise documents across 8 domains and 67 types. Ground truth is curated via independent-system agreement for real documents, known values for synthetic lists, and human verification for forms. Evaluation includes: (i) value accuracy via order-insensitive value F1, (ii) grounding via word- and page-level F1, and (iii) a cost aspect. Comparisons are made among commercial VLMs, coding agents, and LlamaExtract Agentic Plus.

Result: The study finds that commercial VLMs perform well on short documents but truncate records on long ones; coding agents maintain higher accuracy but at higher cost. LlamaExtract Agentic Plus ranks first on all metrics, achieving accuracy comparable to coding agents at a fraction of the cost.

Conclusion: ExtractBench provides a scalable, ground-truth benchmark for evaluating schema-guided extraction, enabling fair assessment of accuracy, completeness, grounding, and cost. The dataset and code are publicly available for broader adoption and benchmarking.

Abstract: Enterprise workflows increasingly rely on agents for \emph{schema-guided extraction}: given a document and a user-defined schema, the agent faithfully follows the schema to produce the correct output with source evidence as grounding metadata. We present ExtractBench, a benchmark for schema-guided extraction and, to our knowledge, the first to score value accuracy, record completeness at scale, grounding, and measured cost together. The evaluation system contains 4,869 pages across 370 enterprise documents, 8 business domains, and 67 document types, with clear tags differentiating their challenge scenarios. The scalable schema and ground-truth curation pipeline combines independent-system agreement for real documents, known values for synthetic lists, and human verification for forms. We report order-insensitive value F1 for value accuracy, plus two grounding metrics for source traceability: word- and page-level F1. Commercial VLMs perform well on short documents but often truncate record lists on long ones, while coding agents retain higher accuracy at much higher cost. LlamaExtract Agentic Plus ranks first on all three metrics, with accuracy comparable to coding agents at a fraction of the cost. Dataset and evaluation code are available on \href{https://huggingface.co/datasets/llamaindex/ExtractBench}{HuggingFace} and \href{https://github.com/run-llama/ExtractBench}{GitHub}.

</details>


### [238] [SVR: Self-Verifying Refinement via Joint Verdict-Confidence Reinforcement Learning for Adaptive Test-Time Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28457)
*Hongyu Chen,Liang Lin,Guangrun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SVR introduces an oracle-free, multi-turn RL framework where a model uses internal self-verification as a compute-control signal to decide whether to retain an answer or continue refinement. Trained with GRPO using fixed-horizon trajectories, it optimizes correctness and calibration-aware self-verification, enabling adaptive stopping at inference. Empirically, SVR achieves 0.563 macro accuracy on seven math benchmarks with 2.99 turns on average, outperforming baselines and reducing compute relative to fixed ten-turn inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniform test-time budgets waste computation on easy inputs and external verifier feedback is not always available. There is a need for an internal, learnable mechanism to allocate compute adaptively without leaking ground-truth information at inference.

Method: At each turn, the model outputs a solution, a discrete correctness verdict, and a confidence score. The current answer is retained only if the verdict is Correct and confidence exceeds a threshold; otherwise refinement proceeds using the model's own self-verification. Ground-truth correctness informs training rewards but is not exposed at inference. Training uses GRPO on fixed-horizon trajectories with rewards for solution correctness, calibration-aware self-verification, and stop-ready correct states; adaptive stopping is activated only during inference.

Result: SVR achieves macro-average accuracy of 0.563 on seven mathematical reasoning benchmarks using Qwen3.5-2B with an average of 2.99 inference turns. It exceeds standard GRPO, strong multi-turn baselines, and a fixed-budget oracle-guided score-feedback reference, while using substantially fewer turns than a fixed ten-turn inference.

Conclusion: Learned self-verification offers an effective internal control signal for answer retention and adaptive test-time compute allocation, enabling more efficient and reliable multi-turn reasoning without external feedback.

Abstract: Scaling test-time computation can improve language-model reasoning, but uniform budgets waste computation on easy inputs, while verifier-guided refinement relies on external feedback. We introduce Self-Verifying Refinement (SVR), an oracle-free multi-turn reinforcement learning framework that learns to use self-verification as a compute-control policy. At each turn, the model produces a solution together with a discrete correctness verdict and a confidence score; it retains the current answer only when the verdict is Correct and confidence exceeds a threshold, and otherwise continues refinement using its own self-verification. Ground-truth correctness is used only to construct training rewards and is never exposed to the policy through refinement prompts or required at inference. SVR is trained with GRPO on fixed-horizon trajectories using rewards that promote solution correctness, calibration-aware self-verification, and stop-ready correct states; adaptive stopping is activated only at inference. On seven mathematical reasoning benchmarks with Qwen3.5-2B, SVR achieves a macro-average accuracy of 0.563 with only 2.99 inference turns on average. In the evaluated complete-system comparison, it exceeds standard GRPO, strong multi-turn baselines, and a fixed-budget oracle-guided score-feedback reference while requiring substantially fewer turns than fixed ten-turn inference. These results demonstrate that learned self-verification can serve as an effective internal control signal for answer retention and adaptive test-time compute allocation.

</details>
